## 导读

> 本导读整理自 Socratopia 网站本书介绍页：https://www.socratopia.app/library/close-listening-bach-en

This is the fifth volume of Close Listening — a single-composer deep dive across the 65 years from Bach's 1685 birth in Eisenach to his 1750 death in Leipzig. The structural spine is Christoph Wolff's six-period framework (Eisenach → Lüneburg → Arnstadt → Mühlhausen → Weimar 1708-1717 → Köthen 1717-1723 → Leipzig 1723-1750 ), with about 50 anchor works placed inside the arc so each major category — the Weimar organ output, the Köthen secular instrumental peak (Brandenburgs, Cello Suites, Violin Sonatas and Partitas, WTC Book 1), the Leipzig sacred output (cantatas, two Passions, B Minor Mass , Christmas Oratorio ), and the late contrapuntal works ( Goldberg , Musical Offering , Art of Fugue , WTC Book 2) — gets concentrated listening. By Chapter 19 you can hear a fugue subject's voice-entries, follow a chorale-prelude's cantus firmus, and recognise a stile-antico texture without being told.

The book is conservative about the legends. Where modern scholarship has questioned a long-told story — the Toccata and Fugue BWV 565 attribution, the Forkel-Goldberg-Keyserlingk anecdote, the romantic "Bach died over Contrapunctus 14" image — the chapter names the dispute and does not adjudicate. The parallel HIP and modern-instrument performance traditions are treated equally seriously: Karl Richter and Nikolaus Harnoncourt as different valid readings, not as competing schools that must be ranked.

One thread runs the length of the volume — the Six Listening Habits to Unlearn (Bach Edition) . The first habit, "Bach is brain-music / mathematical," is dismantled by "Sheep May Safely Graze" (Ch 2), the St. Matthew Passion bass aria "Mache dich, mein Herze, rein" (Ch 13), and the Two-Violin Concerto slow movement — all carrying plain emotional weight. By Ch 22 the "mathematical Bach" turns out to be a 19th-century reception construction, not a description of the working composer. The volume opens the catalogue and hands it back to you knowing where to look.

**Target audience**：Adult amateur classical-music lovers who have completed Close Listening: Classical Music (B159), Beethoven (B201), Mozart (B202), and Chopin (B203), and now want a 65-year deep dive into J. S. Bach through ~50 specific anchor works across his catalogue.

**Prerequisites**：None formal. Familiarity with the four-section listening loop introduced in B159 ( Before You Listen / The Listening Window / What's Happening in the Music / The Stories ) helps but is re-introduced in Chapters 1-2.

**Study hours**：65-85 hours of listening + reading (one chapter per week sustained; one chapter per evening if pressed).

## Chapter 1: Why Bach and Why Now

Johann Sebastian Bach is one of those names everyone knows. Almost no one reads his catalogue end to end. The reason is not that his music is forbidding — most of it is not — but that there is, simply, too much of it. He left about 1,100 surviving works catalogued by their BWV numbers, plus another two or three hundred lost or fragmentary works whose existence is known from period records. About 200 of his sacred cantatas survive from a presumed total of roughly 300. There are four mature Passion settings, the _B Minor Mass_ , the _Christmas Oratorio_ , the _Magnificat_ , the six Brandenburg Concertos, the four Orchestral Suites, the six Cello Suites, the six Violin Sonatas and Partitas, the _Goldberg Variations_ , the _Art of Fugue_ , the _Musical Offering_ , the 48 Preludes and Fugues of the _Well-Tempered Clavier_ , the great organ works, the keyboard partitas, the Italian Concerto, the inventions, the chorale preludes. A reader who came to Bach without a guide could reasonably listen for a decade and still feel she had only sampled the catalogue.

The _Close Listening_ series exists to solve this. The Bach volume, the volume you are now beginning, picks about fifty specific works from those 1,100, organizes them along the 65 years of his life, and walks you through each one closely enough that by the end you will recognize them by ear and place them in the career-arc. By the end you will not have heard everything Bach wrote, but you will have heard the central spine, and you will be in a position to follow your own curiosity into the rest.

This chapter does the introductory work for the volume. It places the book in relation to the four earlier _Close Listening_ volumes (_Classical Music_ , _Beethoven_ , _Mozart_ , _Chopin_). It introduces the Wolff career-arc framework that the volume's biographical thread will follow. It introduces the Six Listening Habits to Unlearn for Bach specifically. It addresses the strange fact that Bach was largely forgotten for fifty years after his death, and only became "the supreme architect of Western music" through a slow nineteenth-century revival that culminated in the Mendelssohn 1829 _St. Matthew Passion_ revival in Berlin. By the end of this chapter you will have the volume's map.

### 1.1 Where This Volume Sits in the Series

This is the fifth _Close Listening_ volume, after the broad survey _Classical Music_ and the single-composer volumes on Beethoven, Mozart, and Chopin. The Bach volume now does the same work for the 65 years from the 1685 Eisenach birth to the 1750 Leipzig death.

You have already met Bach twice in the series.

In _Close Listening: Classical Music_ Chapter 7, the _Toccata and Fugue in D minor_ BWV 565 was the Baroque-warmup anchor. You learned the listening loop on its dramatic opening cascade, the chord-and-flourish gesture, the brief silence, the next cascade. You learned what a fugue subject sounds like when it first enters. The chapter also briefly used the Brandenburg Concerto No. 3 in G major BWV 1048 as a second anchor and showed the concerto grosso form Bach was working within.

In _Close Listening: Classical Music_ Chapter 8, the _Goldberg Variations_ BWV 988 were the variation-form anchor. You met the Aria + 30 Variations + Aria da capo architecture. You met the Glenn Gould 1955 CBS recording (the breakthrough debut at age 22) and the Gould 1981 CBS re-recording (the late stereo reading at slower tempos). You learned what it means for the same pianist to record the same work twice across 26 years and offer two readings that disagree with each other.

This volume will take you back through both works in the deep treatment. The _Toccata and Fugue_ returns in Chapter 5 with the modern scholarly question, Peter Williams and others have argued the work may not be by Bach at all, since the autograph is lost, the work survives only in a mid-eighteenth-century manuscript copy by Johannes Ringk, and some keyboard-writing features are atypical of Bach's organ idiom. The _Goldberg Variations_ return in Chapter 17 with the full late-period vantage and the full canonic-structure analysis (every third variation is a canon at successively rising intervals from the unison to the ninth).

But this volume does much more than revisit the _Classical Music_ survey's anchors. You will meet about fifty Bach works across 23 chapters, including ones the larger culture knows by name (the "Sheep May Safely Graze" aria; the Brandenburg Concertos; "Jesu, Joy of Man's Desiring"; the Cello Suite No. 1 Prelude; the _St. Matthew Passion_ ; the "Air on the G String") and ones whose names are unfamiliar but whose music is part of the central Bach achievement (the _Passacaglia and Fugue in C minor_ BWV 582; the chaconne from the D-minor Partita BWV 1004; the Cantata BWV 82 "Ich habe genug"; the late chorale prelude "Vor deinen Thron tret ich hiermit" BWV 668 that Bach may have dictated from his deathbed). The selection follows the Christoph Wolff biographical framework, which gives the volume its structural spine.

### 1.2 The Wolff Career-Arc Framework

Modern Bach scholarship has a contemporary single-volume gold standard: Christoph Wolff's _Johann Sebastian Bach: The Learned Musician_ (Norton 2000). The Wolff biography rests on twentieth-century discoveries, corrected dating of the cantata cycles by Alfred Dürr's chronology of the 1950s and 60s; new archival material from Leipzig and Köthen; reattribution work on doubtful pieces, and on Wolff's own decades of editorial work on the Neue Bach-Ausgabe. The volume you are reading uses Wolff's framework throughout, supplemented by John Eliot Gardiner's _Bach: Music in the Castle of Heaven_ (Knopf 2013) for the performer-biographer voice, Peter Williams's _The Organ Music of J. S. Bach_ (Cambridge 2003 second edition) for the organ-works analytical voice, Robert L. Marshall's _The Music of Johann Sebastian Bach_ (Schirmer 1989) for the catalogue-analytical voice, Daniel R. Melamed's _Hearing Bach's Passions_ (Oxford 2005) for the Passions, and Malcolm Boyd's _Bach_ (OUP Master Musicians 2000) for the single-volume scholarly biography.

The Wolff framework divides Bach's life into six periods. The volume follows this division.

**Eisenach 1685-1695** : the birthplace years. Bach was born March 21, 1685 in Eisenach, Thuringia (in the part of central Germany that was a patchwork of small Protestant duchies and principalities at the time). His father Johann Ambrosius Bach (1645-1695) was the town's _Stadtpfeifer_ (town musician). The Bach family was the most extensive musician dynasty in German history, six generations of professional musicians by Johann Sebastian's birth, the family so dense that "Bach" in Thuringia was almost a synonym for "town musician." The early Eisenach years end with the death of Bach's mother in 1694 and his father in 1695, leaving the nine-year-old as a partial orphan.

**Ohrdruf 1695-1700** : the elder-brother years. After the parents' deaths, J. S. Bach went to live with his elder brother Johann Christoph Bach (1671-1721, not the more famous cousin Johann Christoph Bach of Eisenach), organist in nearby Ohrdruf. Johann Christoph had been a pupil of Johann Pachelbel; he gave J. S. Bach his first systematic keyboard instruction. The Ohrdruf years are sparsely documented but probably formative.

**Lüneburg 1700-1702** : the choir-school years. The fifteen-year-old Bach traveled north to Lüneburg in Lower Saxony (a journey of about 200 miles, partly on foot) to take a place as a choir scholar at the Michaeliskirche choir-school. The Lüneburg years gave Bach access to a large music library and connected him with the North German organ school through Georg Böhm (the Lüneburg organist) and through trips to Hamburg to hear Johann Adam Reincken.

**Arnstadt and Mühlhausen 1703-1708** : the first organist posts. Bach took his first paid organist post in Arnstadt in 1703. The Arnstadt years included the famous 1705 winter trip to Lübeck (about 250 miles each way, mostly on foot) to hear Dietrich Buxtehude play the organ at the Marienkirche, a trip that lasted four months instead of the four weeks Bach had been granted leave for, drawing a written reprimand from the Arnstadt consistory upon his return. In 1707 Bach moved to Mühlhausen as organist at the Blasiuskirche and married his first wife Maria Barbara (a first cousin) in October of that year. The Mühlhausen tenure lasted less than a year before the Weimar offer arrived.

**Weimar 1708-1717** : the organ-virtuoso years. Bach took the post of court organist and chamber musician at the ducal court of Weimar in 1708, promoted to concertmaster (with the obligation to compose one new cantata per month for the court chapel) in 1714. The Weimar years are the period during which most of the great organ works were composed: the _Passacaglia and Fugue in C minor_ BWV 582; the _Toccata and Fugue in D minor_ BWV 565 (if it is by Bach at all); the _Orgelbüchlein_ chorale-prelude collection (a planned 164-chorale set of which 46 were completed); the Vivaldi-concerto transcriptions for solo keyboard and solo organ that brought the Italian-concerto language into Bach's writing. The Weimar period ended badly, Bach was imprisoned for a month in 1717 in the Weimar consistory before being dismissed for "stubbornly demanding" his release to take the Köthen offer.

**Köthen 1717-1723** : the secular-instrumental years. Bach moved to Köthen in late 1717 as Kapellmeister to Prince Leopold of Anhalt-Köthen. The Köthen court was Calvinist; no elaborate church music was required. Bach for the only time in his career devoted himself primarily to secular instrumental music. The Brandenburg Concertos (dedicated March 24, 1721), the Cello Suites BWV 1007-1012, the Violin Sonatas and Partitas BWV 1001-1006, the _Well-Tempered Clavier_ Book 1 (BWV 846-869, completed 1722), the orchestral suites, all date primarily from Köthen. Maria Barbara died in July 1720 while Bach was traveling with Prince Leopold in Carlsbad; Bach married his second wife Anna Magdalena (a court soprano twenty years younger than him) in December 1721. The Köthen years ended in 1723 when Bach took the Leipzig offer.

**Leipzig 1723-1750** : the cantata-and-Passion years. Bach moved to Leipzig in April 1723 as Thomaskantor, music director for the four main Leipzig churches and Latin-and-music teacher at the Thomasschule. He was the third choice for the post: Georg Philipp Telemann had been offered first and declined for a better Hamburg offer; Christoph Graupner had been offered second but his Darmstadt patron refused to release him; Bach was then offered the post. He held it for twenty-seven years until his death on July 28, 1750. The Leipzig output is the sacred core of the catalogue, the ~200 surviving cantatas, the four mature Passions (the _St. John Passion_ BWV 245, the _St. Matthew Passion_ BWV 244, and two lost Passion settings: the _St. Mark Passion_ BWV 247 which survives only in libretto, and a contested _St. Luke Passion_ BWV 246 generally now regarded as not by Bach), the _B Minor Mass_ , the _Christmas Oratorio_ , the _Magnificat_ , and the late contrapuntal peak (the _Goldberg Variations_ 1741, the _Musical Offering_ 1747, the _Art of Fugue_ c. 1740-1750, the _Well-Tempered Clavier_ Book 2 c. 1742).

The volume follows this six-period division. Chapters 3-4 cover Eisenach through Mühlhausen. Chapters 5-6 cover Weimar and the organ output. Chapters 7-10 cover Köthen and the secular instrumental output. Chapters 11-16 cover the Leipzig sacred output. Chapters 17-19 cover the late contrapuntal peak. Chapters 20-22 are synthesis (contrapuntal-voice spotting, recording history, reception history). Chapter 23 is the closing handoff.

### 1.3 The Catalogue Scale, Briefly

A few numbers to set scale.

Bach left roughly 1,100 BWV-numbered works that survive. The BWV (Bach-Werke-Verzeichnis) catalogue was compiled by Wolfgang Schmieder in 1950 and revised across the latter half of the twentieth century. The BWV numbers are organized by genre, BWV 1-224 are the cantatas; BWV 225-249 are the motets, masses, Passions, oratorios, and Magnificat; BWV 250-524 are the chorales and sacred songs; BWV 525-771 are the organ works; BWV 772-994 are the keyboard works (harpsichord, clavichord, fortepiano); BWV 995-1040 are the lute works; BWV 1041-1071 are the chamber works; BWV 1072-1078 are the canons; BWV 1079-1080 are the _Musical Offering_ and _Art of Fugue_ ; later numbers cover doubtful and lost works.

About 200 of the 224 cantata-numbered slots in BWV 1-224 contain surviving cantatas. Of these, roughly 195 are sacred cantatas for the Lutheran liturgical year; the remaining ~5 are wedding, funeral, and ceremonial pieces. Bach is known to have composed at least five complete annual cantata-cycles (the year-round Lutheran liturgy required about 60 cantatas per cycle), meaning he composed something like 300 cantatas total over his Leipzig career, of which the surviving ~200 represent perhaps two-thirds. About 100 are lost; some are known by title from the Leipzig church-bulletin records but the music itself has not survived.

The keyboard output is similarly large, about 220 keyboard works including the 48 preludes and fugues of the WTC, the _Goldberg Variations_ , the six English Suites, the six French Suites, the six Partitas (Clavier-Übung I), the Italian Concerto, the inventions, the sinfonias, the toccatas, the fantasias, and the doubtful and fragmentary pieces.

The organ output is about 240 works, the largest single-instrument body in the catalogue.

The instrumental ensemble works are smaller in number but include several of the most consequential pieces in the Western catalogue, the six Brandenburg Concertos, the four Orchestral Suites (BWV 1066-1069), the Double Concerto for Two Violins in D minor BWV 1043, the harpsichord concertos (mostly transcriptions of earlier works), and the Italianate concertos for keyboard.

The chamber music includes the six Cello Suites BWV 1007-1012 and the six Violin Sonatas and Partitas BWV 1001-1006 (both standing in the modern repertoire as the central unaccompanied-string works in Western music), the six flute sonatas, the three viola da gamba sonatas, the _Musical Offering_ trio sonata, and various smaller chamber works.

The vocal output beyond the cantatas includes the four mature Passions, the _B Minor Mass_ , the _Christmas Oratorio_ , the _Easter Oratorio_ , the _Ascension Oratorio_ , the _Magnificat_ , six motets BWV 225-230, secular cantatas (the _Coffee Cantata_ BWV 211, the _Peasant Cantata_ BWV 212, the _Hunting Cantata_ BWV 208, the one containing "Sheep May Safely Graze"), and a body of sacred songs and chorales.

This volume samples about fifty works from this catalogue. The principle of selection is the Wolff career-arc framework plus the criterion that the selected works are either canonical anchors (the _Brandenburg_ No. 3, the Cello Suite No. 1 Prelude, the _Goldberg Aria_ , the _St. Matthew Passion_ opening chorus, the _Toccata and Fugue_ BWV 565, the C-major WTC Prelude) or central representatives of a genre Bach worked in (the cantata BWV 140 represents the chorale-cantata cycle; the cantata BWV 82 represents the bass-and-oboe solo cantata; the Passacaglia BWV 582 represents the organ-passacaglia).

### 1.4 The Six Listening Habits to Unlearn (Bach Edition)

Listeners arriving at Bach often arrive with received ideas that get in the way of the music. The series has used this checklist in the Beethoven, Mozart, and Chopin volumes, each time calibrated to the specific composer. The Bach calibration is the following six.

**Habit 1: "Bach is brain-music. He is too mathematical. The music is dry."**

This is the dominant nineteenth-and-twentieth-century misconception. The construction is identifiable as reception history (more in Chapter 22): the nineteenth-century revival treated Bach as the supreme architect, the symbol of structural perfection, the master of pure contrapuntal abstraction. Aspects of the late catalogue support this reading (the _Art of Fugue_ is in a real sense a contrapuntal-technique textbook). But the reading flattens what Bach was, a working performer, improviser, teacher, husband, father of twenty children across two marriages (only ten survived to adulthood), Lutheran cantor responsible for one new cantata per week through the Leipzig church year. The music includes a great deal that is direct, emotional, immediate, even tender. The aria "Sheep May Safely Graze" (Chapter 2) is among the warmest pieces in the catalogue. The bass aria "Mache dich, mein Herze, rein" from the _St. Matthew Passion_ (Chapter 13) is among the most-grieving pieces in Western music. The slow movement of the Double Violin Concerto BWV 1043 in D minor is a love duet between two equal voices. The keyboard pieces composed for Anna Magdalena's notebook are domestic, written for the kitchen-table evenings of the Bach household.

When you encounter the "mathematical Bach" claim, in a program note, on a record sleeve, in a casual remark, register that the claim is partial. The contrapuntal architecture is real and is the volume's Thread B. But the emotional immediacy is equally real and is built into the foundation of the catalogue.

**Habit 2: "All the cantatas sound the same."**

This is the impression a listener gets when she dips into the Bach cantata catalogue at random without orientation. About 200 cantatas, across thirty years of Bach's compositional life, can blur together. The impression is wrong. The cantatas span at least three compositional periods (early Mühlhausen and Weimar; the first Leipzig years 1723-1727 of the great early cantata cycles; the later Leipzig years of the chorale-cantata cycle 1724-25 and the post-1727 mature cantatas) and several sub-genres (the chorale cantatas; the solo cantatas; the dialogue cantatas; the secular cantatas). The early cantata BWV 4 "Christ lag in Todesbanden" (composed c. 1707-08 at Mühlhausen) sounds like an early-eighteenth-century Lutheran chorale cantata. The mature chorale cantata BWV 140 "Wachet auf" (1731 Leipzig) sounds like a fully-developed Bach concerto-fugue-aria architecture. They are recognizably different musical worlds.

The volume's strategy on the cantatas is to anchor on a few specific representatives, BWV 4 in Chapter 11, BWV 140 + 147 + 80 + 82 in Chapter 12, and to teach the listening habits that let you go into the rest of the cantata catalogue with bearings. Alfred Dürr's standard cantata-by-cantata commentary (_The Cantatas of J. S. Bach_ , Oxford 2005 English edition) is the reference you can take into the catalogue afterward.

**Habit 3: "Bach was always recognized as the supreme master."**

He was not. After his death on July 28, 1750, Bach disappeared from the active musical scene for roughly fifty years. His sons, Wilhelm Friedemann Bach (1710-1784), Carl Philipp Emanuel Bach (1714-1788), Johann Christoph Friedrich Bach (1732-1795), Johann Christian Bach (1735-1782, the "London Bach"), moved past their father's contrapuntal idiom into the _Empfindsamer Stil_ (the sensitive style) and then the early Classical style. By the 1770s J. S. Bach was considered old-fashioned. The young Mozart and Beethoven studied his keyboard works (Mozart at Baron Gottfried van Swieten's Vienna circle from 1782; Beethoven studied the WTC under his teacher Christian Gottlob Neefe in Bonn from the 1780s, both treated in the Beethoven and Mozart volumes), but these were specialist activities. The broader public did not know the cantatas, the Passions, the _B Minor Mass_ , the _Christmas Oratorio_. Johann Nikolaus Forkel's 1802 biography (_Über Johann Sebastian Bachs Leben, Kunst, und Kunstwerke_) was the first major modern study and began the slow revival. Felix Mendelssohn's March 11, 1829 performance of the _St. Matthew Passion_ at the Berlin Singakademie, exactly 102 years after the work's 1727 premiere, was the public turning point. The full reception story takes the volume to Chapter 22.

When you hear someone treat Bach's supremacy as natural and inevitable, register that the supremacy is itself a constructed nineteenth-century fact. For fifty years after the composer's death, nothing of the kind was the case.

**Habit 4: "You need to be Christian, Lutheran, to appreciate Bach."**

You do not. The theological depth is real. The chapter that treats the Lutheran-theological context (Chapter 16) does not pretend otherwise. Bach was a devout Lutheran whose Leipzig cantatas were composed for liturgical use; the Passions are Holy Week music; the chorale preludes are settings of hymns the Leipzig congregation knew by heart. To follow the cantatas and Passions completely, a non-Lutheran listener has to learn some of the theological frame, what the chorales meant, what the Gospel readings the cantata texts engaged with, what the sermon-cantata-sermon liturgical sequence looked like in a 1730s Leipzig Sunday service. Chapter 16 provides this orientation.

But the volume's broader claim is that the music itself works without the theology. The opening of the _St. Matthew Passion_ chorus "Kommt, ihr Töchter, helft mir klagen" is a piece of Western art that does not depend on Lutheran confession for its force. The "Crucifixus" of the _B Minor Mass_ is grief on a scale that does not require denominational identification. The Cello Suites are dance suites without theological content; the Brandenburg Concertos are secular court music; the _Coffee Cantata_ is a comic vignette about a daughter who refuses to give up coffee. About half the catalogue is secular or proto-secular. The reader who is not Christian, or not Lutheran, or not religious at all, can approach the catalogue without obstacle.

**Habit 5: "I don't get fugue. The contrapuntal works are over my head."**

Many readers come to Bach holding this. The fear is reasonable, fugue is intimidating in the abstract. But it becomes much less intimidating in the specific. A fugue is a piece of music in which a short musical idea (the _subject_) is announced by one voice, answered by a second voice five notes higher (the _answer_), then taken up by a third voice (and possibly more), with each voice imitating the first in turn. Once all the voices have stated the subject, the texture is built, there are now four voices (or three, or six) moving simultaneously, each independent, each making sense as a line, each contributing to a combined whole.

The volume teaches fugue-listening only via specific anchor works that require it. Chapter 10 (the _Well-Tempered Clavier_ Book 1) introduces the subject-and-answer pattern via the Prelude and Fugue No. 1 in C major BWV 846, a four-voice fugue at gentle tempo where you can follow each voice's entrance clearly. Chapter 19 (the _Art of Fugue_) goes further with the three-voice and four-voice and six-voice fugues; by that point you will have the listening habit. The volume does not require you to read scores or to count voices on a first hearing. It requires only that you follow the _first_ voice when it enters, listen for the _second_ voice when it enters a few seconds later in a related key, and so on. The skill builds from Chapter 10 onward.

**Habit 6: "I don't know where to start with Bach."**

The starter list, the seven works to begin with if you are arriving at Bach essentially fresh, is in Chapter 23. Briefly, here, the list is: the _Goldberg Variations_ (in either Gould reading, or in any pianist's reading you can find easily, the Schiff, Perahia, or Hantaï harpsichord versions are well-recorded alternatives); Brandenburg Concerto No. 3 in G major BWV 1048 (the most-recorded Brandenburg, a tight ten-minute work for three violin-viola-cello soloist groups); Cello Suite No. 1 in G major BWV 1007 Prelude (Yo-Yo Ma's three cycles or Pablo Casals's 1930s recording); "Sheep May Safely Graze" from Cantata BWV 208 (any sung version, Magdalena Kožená, Anne Sofie von Otter, Renée Fleming, Emma Kirkby); the Prelude in C major from the _Well-Tempered Clavier_ Book 1 (the prelude Charles Gounod 1853 used as the accompaniment for his _Ave Maria_); the opening chorus of the _St. Matthew Passion_ "Kommt, ihr Töchter, helft mir klagen" (the John Eliot Gardiner 1988 Archiv recording or the Karl Richter 1958 Archiv recording); and the _Toccata and Fugue in D minor_ BWV 565 (any organ recording, Helmut Walcha's Archiv version is the modern-instrument standard).

These seven are the doorways. After you are in, the volume will take you to the rest.

### 1.5 The Volume's Two Threads, Briefly

The volume runs two main threads that you should be aware of from this opening chapter.

**Thread A: the 65-year biographical arc.** This is the Wolff framework already introduced in §1.2. The arc moves through six places (Eisenach, Ohrdruf, Lüneburg, Arnstadt/Mühlhausen, Weimar, Köthen, Leipzig) and three periods of mature output (Weimar organ, Köthen secular instrumental, Leipzig sacred + late contrapuntal). Thread A appears throughout the volume; each anchor work is placed at the moment of Bach's life when it was composed.

**Thread B: Bach's contrapuntal voice.** This is the volume's central listening-skill thread. By the end of the volume you will recognize Bach's principal contrapuntal devices when you hear them: the fugue subject and answer (Ch 5, 6, 10, 19); the chorale-prelude texture (Ch 6, 11, 12, 16); the double and triple fugue (Ch 6, 19); the canon at various intervals (Ch 17, 18, 19); the _stile antico_ (Ch 14); the permutation fugue (Ch 10); the Italianate concertante writing (Ch 8); the French-dance suite movements (Ch 9); the chromatic harmony (Ch 14, 19). Chapter 20 collects these into a single synthesis. The forward pointers are embedded throughout: when a feature first appears in an anchor chapter, the prose tells you where in Chapter 20 the synthesis will pick it up.

The volume's two threads run in parallel through the chapter sequence. Thread A gives you the biographical placement of each work; Thread B gives you the listening skill that lets you hear what is in the work.

### 1.5.5 What This Volume Argues

A reader is owed an honest statement of where the volume itself stands, beyond the framework. The position taken throughout these chapters: Bach is a Lutheran working musician whose religious commitments shaped his compositional output but whose works function as music without requiring confessional decoding to be experienced. This puts the volume between the strongly-mystical Schweitzer reading (which treats the music as fundamentally theological communication) and the strictly-secular concert-hall reading (which treats the religious texts as historical accident the music transcends). Chapter 16 develops the position in detail. The implications: the chorale-prelude texture and the cantata's liturgical context are presented as scaffolding that enriches the listening without gating it; the Crucifixus of the B Minor Mass works as grief whether or not the listener accepts the Crucifixion's theological premise; the cantatas can be approached either as art-music or as sacred-musical theology depending on what the listener brings. The volume is also more skeptical than most introductory treatments about specific biographical anecdotes (the Goldberg-Keyserling commission story, the Maria Barbara-Chaconne memorial reading, the Forkel moonlight-copying story) — these are treated as polished family traditions of uncertain factual basis, not as established history. Readers who prefer either the strongly-confessional or the strongly-secular reading will sometimes find the volume's middle position unsatisfying; both poles are coherent and the choice between them is finally personal.

### 1.6 What the Volume Does Not Do

A few things the volume explicitly does not attempt.

It is not a survey of all 1,100 BWV numbers. About fifty anchor works are treated closely. The rest are mentioned in passing where the structural argument requires, but not anchored. A reader who wants the catalogue end-to-end can use Wolff (Norton 2000) or the Marshall _Music of Johann Sebastian Bach_ (Schirmer 1989) as the next step.

It is not a sheet-music or music-theory course. You do not need to read a staff to follow the volume. The few technical terms used (fugue subject, answer, canon, ricercar, chorale prelude) are introduced in prose where they appear; there are no notated score examples. If you have musical training you will recognize the analytical claims; if you do not, the prose has been written to be intelligible without it.

It is not a Lutheran-theological treatise. Chapter 16 gives the theological orientation needed to follow the cantatas and Passions. The volume does not require you to endorse the theology to follow the music.

It is not a recording-discography reference. Chapter 21 sketches the principal recording lineages and gives recommended first recordings for each major work. The volume does not exhaustively review every recording in print. Recording-review periodicals (_Gramophone_ , _Fanfare_ , _International Record Review_ , _BBC Music Magazine_) and the David Hurwitz and Norman Lebrecht online recording-comparison voices are the references for that work.

It is not a biographical biography of Bach. The biographical thread runs through the volume but is subordinated to the music. Christoph Wolff's _Johann Sebastian Bach: The Learned Musician_ (Norton 2000), Malcolm Boyd's _Bach_ (OUP Master Musicians 2000), and Peter Williams's _J. S. Bach: A Life in Music_ (Cambridge 2007) are the single-volume biographies a reader can take alongside this volume or after it.

### 1.7 The Next Chapter

Chapter 2 begins the listening work itself. The first anchor is the aria "Sheep May Safely Graze", the soprano-and-two-recorders aria from the _Hunting Cantata_ BWV 208, composed at Weimar in 1713 for a small birthday celebration at the court of Saxe-Weissenfels. The aria runs about five minutes, sits well within a casual listener's familiarity (most readers have heard it without knowing what it is), and gives a deliberately gentle introduction to the Bach listening loop. The four-section pattern you will use across the entire volume, Before You Listen / The Listening Window / What's Happening in the Music / The Stories, is set up on this aria.

After Chapter 2 the biographical arc begins. Chapter 3 takes you back to the 1685 Eisenach birth and follows Bach through Mühlhausen 1708. Chapter 4 sets up the German-Baroque musical world Bach inherited. From Chapter 5 onward the chapters proceed through the career, Weimar, Köthen, Leipzig, with anchor works selected to represent each phase.

By Chapter 23 you will have heard about fifty Bach works closely, you will know the 65-year career-arc, you will be able to follow a Bach fugue, you will hear the chorale-prelude texture when it appears, and you will be in a position to walk into any Bach concert or recording with confident anticipation. That is what the _Close Listening_ series aims for in every volume; it is what this volume aims for with Bach.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The chapter argues that the 'mathematical Bach' image is partial, that Bach was also a working performer, husband, father, and Lutheran cantor whose music includes much that is direct and emotional. Where in your prior listening, before this volume, have you encountered the 'mathematical Bach' framing? Was it in a program note, a record sleeve, a music-appreciation course, a casual remark? What did the framing get right and what did it leave out, as best you can tell from what you have heard?"_

* _"Bach was largely forgotten for fifty years after his death and only became 'the supreme master of Western music' through a slow nineteenth-century revival culminating in Mendelssohn's 1829 St. Matthew Passion performance. What does this fact suggest about the relationship between an artist's intrinsic achievement and an artist's public reception? Are there other figures, composers, writers, painters, scientists, whose contemporary reputation was built decades after their death? What might Bach's case have in common with those, and what is specific to his?"_

* _"The chapter's starter list (the seven Bach works to begin with) is the Goldberg Variations, Brandenburg 3, Cello Suite 1 Prelude, 'Sheep May Safely Graze,' the C-major WTC Prelude, the St. Matthew Passion opening chorus, and the Toccata and Fugue D minor BWV 565. If you had to pick one of those to listen to first, before reading Chapter 2, which would it be and why? What does your choice tell you about the kind of Bach listener you already are?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

This is the framework chapter; it does not have an anchor work. Use the time you would otherwise spend on a re-listen to do one of the following.

  1. **Listen to the chapter's starter list, briefly.** Find each of the seven works on streaming or recording. Spend three to five minutes with each, no close listening yet, just orienting samples. Notice which ones you recognize without being told, which ones surprise you, and which ones you have no prior memory of. Make a note of which work you are most curious to spend more time with after the volume covers it.

  2. **Find a recording of the _Toccata and Fugue in D minor_ BWV 565 you already have or can locate quickly.** Listen to the opening 30 seconds, the cascade-of-chords-and-flourish gesture, the silence, the next cascade. Then skip to the closing 60 seconds, the pedal-point ending. The middle (the fugue) is what Chapter 5 will examine in detail; for now, the opening and closing alone are enough to set up the anticipatory context.

  3. **Find a recording of the Cello Suite No. 1 Prelude.** Yo-Yo Ma's 1983 Sony recording or his 2018 Sony recording, Pablo Casals's 1936-39 EMI recording, or Anner Bylsma's 1992 Sony period-instrument recording will all be easy to locate. Listen once. This is what Chapter 9's first anchor work sounds like. Notice the descending arpeggios, the broken chords, the way a single melodic line implies a chord progression. Chapter 9 will go into this in detail; here it is just the orientation.

### Going Deeper

**Read** : Christoph Wolff, _Johann Sebastian Bach: The Learned Musician_ (W. W. Norton, 2000). The contemporary single-volume biography in English. About 600 pages. Wolff's voice is conservative, archival, and dense with archival detail. The book is the reference for the framework this volume uses throughout. Read the prologue and the first chapter as orientation; the rest you can take chapter-by-chapter alongside the present volume.

**Read** (alternative): Malcolm Boyd, _Bach_ (Master Musicians, Oxford University Press, 2000, third edition). A shorter and more compact single-volume biography. About 300 pages. Boyd's voice is lucid and economical. Take this as your single-volume option if Wolff is too dense or too long.

**Listen** (orientation): a single Bach work of your choice from the starter list, played end-to-end without interruption, with no commentary. Pick the one you are least familiar with. Make no notes; do not read about the work; just listen. The point is to establish a baseline for what your current ear catches and what it misses. Chapters 2 onward will gradually add to what your ear catches; the baseline lets you measure the change.

---

## Chapter 2: The Listening Basics for Bach

"Sheep May Safely Graze", "Schafe können sicher weiden" in the original German, is the Bach piece you most likely already know without knowing you know it. It is the wedding-recessional choice, the late-night-radio cue, the soft-classical-album closer. It runs about five minutes; it is gentle from first measure to last; it has been recorded in dozens of versions by sopranos, mezzos, and even instrumental ensembles arranging it for solo flute, cello, or piano. If you have heard exactly one Bach aria in your life, this is a strong candidate for what it was.

This chapter does what the _Close Listening_ series always does on its first anchor work: it puts a familiar piece under closer attention than the larger culture usually grants it, and it uses the close attention to set up the listening pattern for the rest of the book. The anchor is one piece, five minutes long; the chapter around it is longer, because the listening pattern it establishes is the one every subsequent chapter will use.

The Bach-specific listening calibration is different from the Beethoven calibration you absorbed in the Beethoven volume or the Mozart calibration of the Mozart volume or the Chopin calibration of the Chopin volume. For Beethoven the listening calibration is: follow a thematic argument through orchestral or chamber forces, attend to the development sections, expect a dramatic struggle that resolves into affirmation. For Mozart the listening calibration is: follow an ensemble dialogue at high speed, attend to harmonic-rhythm density, accept that the texture is what carries the message. For Chopin the listening calibration is single-piano-with-two-hands: the right hand sings, the left hand accompanies, the texture-management between them is what you attend to.

For Bach the listening calibration is different again: you are listening to multiple independent musical lines moving at once, and the central skill is to follow them as lines rather than to fuse them into a single textural impression. Even on a piece as gentle as "Sheep May Safely Graze," there are at least four lines in play, the soprano singer, the two recorders (a wooden flute-family instrument, the early-eighteenth-century recorder rather than the modern transverse flute), and the continuo (the bass-line-plus-keyboard support that holds the harmony). The four lines weave together into the texture you hear. The question for the listener is: can you hear each line, or do they fuse?

This chapter sets up the listening loop that gives you the skill to hear them separately.

### 2.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

February 23, 1713. Bach is twenty-seven years old, three years into his Weimar concertmaster appointment (still six months from the promotion that would formalize the title), and traveling. He has gone to Weißenfels in Saxony, about sixty miles east of Weimar, to attend the thirty-first birthday of Duke Christian of Saxe-Weissenfels. The duke is a passionate hunter and a generous music-patron, his Weißenfels court runs one of the more substantial musical establishments in central Germany at the time. Bach has been commissioned to compose a cantata for the birthday celebration. He has composed _BWV 208_ , the work that the Bach-Werke-Verzeichnis catalogue would later christen the _Jagdkantate_ , the "Hunting Cantata", for performance at the birthday banquet.

The cantata is secular. It is a pastoral-mythological court entertainment, not a piece of sacred music. The text (by the Weimar court poet Salomon Franck, with whom Bach worked frequently in this period) puts five mythological figures into a small dramatic situation: Diana the goddess of hunting, Endymion her mortal lover, the shepherd-god Pan, and the shepherdess Pales celebrating the duke's birthday in pastoral terms. Pales (a Roman fertility goddess of shepherds and flocks) sings the ninth movement, a soprano aria with two recorders and continuo accompaniment. Her text, in Franck's German, is a four-line pastoral commonplace: _"Schafe können sicher weiden, wo ein guter Hirte wacht. Wo Regenten wohl regieren, kann man Ruh und Friede spüren und was Länder glücklich macht"_ , sheep can graze in safety where a good shepherd watches; where rulers govern well, one can feel quiet and peace, and what makes lands prosperous.

The poetic figure is utterly conventional. The shepherd-pastoral-good-ruler equation has been a commonplace of court poetry since the Renaissance and before. The duke is being flattered as the good shepherd-ruler whose well-governed land allows peace and prosperity. Bach takes the conventional text and produces about five minutes of music that has outlived the duke, the court, the Hunting Cantata's other eleven movements (most of which are now mostly performed only in complete-cantata cycles), and most of the seventeen hundred birthday cantatas composed across all German courts in the year 1713. Most of those other cantatas no longer exist; the music was performed once and the manuscripts discarded. The Hunting Cantata survived because Bach himself kept a copy, and the Pales aria survived independent of the cantata because by the late nineteenth century arrangers had started extracting it as a stand-alone piece.

You have probably heard "Sheep May Safely Graze" as an extracted stand-alone, at a wedding, in a film score, as background music, without knowing that it sat originally inside a twelve-movement Hunting Cantata for a Weißenfels duke's birthday. The chapter's first task is to hear the piece in its original soprano-with-two-recorders-and-continuo scoring, and to attend closely enough that the conventional pastoral text becomes a piece of music with specific compositional features.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The opening instrumental introduction (the ritornello).** Before the soprano enters, the two recorders and the continuo play a short instrumental introduction, what Italian opera of the period called a _ritornello_ (a returning passage). The ritornello is about thirty seconds long in most recordings. Notice that the two recorders are not playing in unison: they are playing two independent lines that weave around each other. One recorder sits a little higher; the other a little lower. They sometimes converge into thirds (the two notes a third apart, which is the most consonant interval after the unison and octave); they sometimes diverge into independent shapes. The continuo (in most recordings, a harpsichord and a cello or a bassoon doubling the bass line) provides the bass-and-harmonic support underneath. Listen to the recorders' two lines specifically.

  2. **The soprano's entrance.** When the soprano enters with her first line ("Schafe können sicher weiden") about thirty seconds in, her line joins the two recorder lines and the continuo, now there are four independent musical lines moving at once. Notice that the soprano is not simply singing on top of the accompaniment the way a romantic-period art-song singer would. She is one voice among four. The recorders continue their independent figures; the continuo continues its bass line; the soprano weaves through them. Listen for the moments where her line aligns with one of the recorder lines (sometimes they double her at the third or sixth) and the moments where she goes her own way.

  3. **The B major surprise (it happens late).** The aria is in B♭ major, a warm, settled key. About two-thirds of the way through, the music briefly touches a darker harmonic area before returning home. Listen for the moment where the soprano's line takes a shape that doesn't sound quite as bright as the opening, a brief shadow before the brightness returns. The shadow is the cloud-and-clearing pattern that Bach uses across his catalogue. It is the simplest version of what this chapter and subsequent chapters will call Bach's harmonic shading.

#### One-sentence background

Composed 1713 at Weimar as the ninth movement of the Hunting Cantata BWV 208 for the birthday celebration of Duke Christian of Saxe-Weissenfels; soprano-aria scoring for soprano, two recorders, and continuo, in B♭ major, in 12/8 meter, about 4 to 5 minutes; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Emma Kirkby with the Academy of Ancient Music conducted by Christopher Hogwood (recorded London, 1985, released as the Hyperion Bach Cantatas series volume 1, period instruments), chosen for the clarity of the line, the period-instrument sound, and the canonical-HIP status.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Bach Sheep May Safely Graze Emma Kirkby Hogwood" or "Bach Schafe können sicher weiden BWV 208" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Magdalena Kožená with the Berliner Philharmoniker conducted by Sir Simon Rattle (DG 2003, modern instruments), a warmer, more romantic reading; the modern-instrument alternative for listeners who find the period-instrument sound austere.
* Renée Fleming with the Orchestra of St. Luke's conducted by Charles Mackerras (Decca 1999, modern instruments), the operatic-voice reading; the line is sung as a major operatic soprano would sing it.
* Carolyn Sampson with The King's Consort conducted by Robert King (Hyperion 2007, period instruments), a quieter, more inward reading than Kirkby; the alternative HIP for listeners who want still more restraint.

### 2.2 The Listening Window

This section is for your second listen, the one where you know what is coming and can attend to specific moments. Timestamps below refer to the Kirkby / Hogwood / AAM 1985 Primary Recording named in §2.1. The aria in their reading runs about 4 minutes 50 seconds. If you are listening to a different recording, the score-structural moments still apply but the exact clock times will differ; in that case use the score-moment descriptions rather than the timestamps.

**0:00: The opening ritornello, recorders alone with continuo.** The two recorders enter together, playing the principal instrumental theme of the aria. The continuo (harpsichord plus a soft cello or bassoon doubling) lays down the bass line and the supporting harmonies. Notice immediately that the two recorder lines are not parallel, they have independent shapes, sometimes a third apart, sometimes diverging into independent figures. The meter is 12/8 (twelve eighth-notes per bar, grouped as four beats of three eighths each, a lilting, dance-like meter that Bach uses across his catalogue for pastoral and contemplative moments).

**About 0:18: The first cadence of the ritornello.** The recorders arrive at a momentary resting point (a cadence, a harmonic landing). The continuo confirms it. Then the music moves immediately to the next phrase. Notice that the cadence is not a full stop; the music breathes briefly and continues. This is the Italian-concerto practice, the ritornello is a self-contained instrumental statement that nonetheless leads into what follows.

**About 0:30: The soprano enters.** Pales (sung by Kirkby) enters with "Schafe können sicher weiden", sheep can graze in safety. The soprano line is gentle, mostly stepwise (small melodic intervals), set to long-held notes on the open vowels of "Schafe" (sheep) and "weiden" (graze). The two recorders continue weaving above and below. The continuo continues underneath. There are now four lines in play.

**About 0:50: The soprano completes the first phrase of text.** The first text-line ("Schafe können sicher weiden") arrives at a cadence. The recorders take a brief turn, a short instrumental interlude, before the soprano enters with the second text-line.

**About 1:00: The second phrase: "wo ein guter Hirte wacht."** Where a good shepherd watches. The soprano's line on "Hirte" (shepherd) holds longer than the surrounding words, foregrounding the central pastoral image. The recorders provide more active figuration during this phrase.

**About 1:20: A small instrumental episode.** The recorders take a moment of their own, the soprano rests; the instrumental pair plays an episode based on figures from the ritornello. Listen for the recorders' independent lines specifically here, with no vocal line covering them.

**About 1:35: The third phrase: "Wo Regenten wohl regieren."** Where rulers govern well. The text leaves the pastoral image and reaches up to the courtly flattery (the duke as a good ruler). The musical setting stays in the same gentle register; Bach does not change mode for the political compliment.

**About 2:00-2:30: The "Ruh und Friede" (quiet and peace) section.** The soprano takes the words "quiet" and "peace" and stretches them into long sustained notes. The two recorders weave around her with slowly-moving figures. This is the contemplative center of the aria, the moment where the pastoral image is at its most pictorial. Notice how the recorders' independence increases here: they are no longer doubling the soprano's line but moving in their own directions, creating a four-voice polyphony in which the central image of peace is generated by the simultaneous calm of all four voices.

**About 3:00: The harmonic shading begins.** Listen for a brief darkening, the music touches a minor-mode area (G minor, the relative minor of B♭ major) for about twenty seconds before returning. The soprano's line takes a slightly more chromatic shape; the harmony underneath develops a brief shadow. Bach does not stay in the shadow, the cloud passes; the major-key sunshine returns. This is the cloud-and-clearing pattern Chapter 1's §1.4 Habit 1 alluded to. The aria's surface is unbroken; the harmonic detail registers the brief shadow.

**About 3:30: The return of the opening text material.** The soprano returns to the opening "Schafe können sicher weiden" phrase with the original musical setting, often slightly elaborated with additional ornamental figures (Bach wrote out specific ornaments in the score; period-practice singers like Kirkby add a few more in the eighteenth-century improvised manner; modern-instrument singers like Fleming or Kožená take this varying degrees of liberty). The recorders' lines return alongside the soprano. The home-key warmth is fully restored.

**About 4:15: The closing ritornello.** The soprano falls silent; the recorders and continuo close the aria with a return of the opening ritornello material. The cadence arrives; the aria ends on the tonic B♭ major chord.

If your recording's total time differs from Kirkby's 4:50 by more than fifteen percent, you may be listening to a recording at a noticeably different tempo (Fleming's reading runs a bit slower; some inflated modern arrangements with full orchestra stretch past 6 minutes; some HIP readings tighten to closer to 4:00). The relative positions of the moments above should still hold; recalibrate by ear from the section landmarks.

### 2.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The four-voice texture: soprano, two recorders, continuo

The aria is scored for soprano, two treble recorders (in the period-instrument tradition; some modern recordings substitute transverse flutes, which produces a different timbre, thinner, less hollow), and continuo (in most recordings, harpsichord with a bass instrument doubling, cello, viola da gamba, or bassoon, depending on the performance tradition). The four voices comprise three melodic-treble lines (soprano, recorder 1, recorder 2) plus the continuo bass.

The texture is Bach's standard solo-aria scoring with one obbligato pair. _Obbligato_ , Italian for "obligatory", is the technical term in Baroque opera and cantata for an instrumental part written into the aria as an obligatory partner to the voice. Some Bach arias have a single obbligato instrument (the cantata BWV 82 "Ich habe genug", Chapter 12, has an obbligato oboe partnering the bass voice). Some have two obbligato instruments (this aria has two recorders; the aria "Erbarme dich" from the _St. Matthew Passion_ , Chapter 13, has a solo obbligato violin partnering the alto). The obbligato is not accompaniment; it is a partner, an equal voice in the polyphonic texture.

For the listener: the central skill is to hear the obbligato instruments as voices, not as decoration. When you listen to the Kirkby recording, your default tendency may be to attend to the soprano and let the recorders register as "soft pretty background." Resist this. The recorders are doing their own work. Listen, on a second pass, to the two recorder lines specifically, what shapes they trace, where they cross each other, where they converge into thirds or sixths, where they diverge into independent figures. You will find that the aria is much richer than the soprano-melody-with-accompaniment hearing suggests. This is the Bach listening habit being established. Every Bach cantata aria you will hear in this volume, and there will be many, uses this scoring principle. The skill you build on this aria is the skill you will use for the rest of the cantata-listening across the volume.

#### The pastoral genre: meter, register, instrumentation, affect

The aria is in _12/8 meter_ , twelve eighth-notes per bar grouped as four beats of three eighths each. This meter is one of the Baroque period's two principal "pastoral" meters (the other is 6/8). The connection between 12/8 meter and pastoral subjects is not arbitrary: the lilting compound-meter pattern resembles a slow gigue or a slow siciliana (an Italian shepherd-dance form), and by the early eighteenth century the convention was well-established. When a Baroque-era composer wrote in 12/8 with a slow tempo and a flute or recorder accompaniment, listeners would have recognized the pastoral genre immediately.

The aria's other pastoral markers are: the major key (B♭ major is bright and settled, not dramatic); the gentle dynamic (the score marks no sudden fortissimos; the music stays in a soft-to-moderate range throughout); the recorder instrumentation (recorders, with their hollow wooden tone, were the period's shepherd-pipe analog and carried the symbolic association); the lack of trumpets, timpani, or strident strings (which would have signaled the heroic-military mode); and the slow tempo (the aria lasts about five minutes on a text that could be sung in two, indicating that the music is meant to linger in the contemplative pastoral image rather than to move forward dramatically).

For the listener: when you encounter another Bach pastoral aria in this volume, and you will, in the cantata chapters and in the _Christmas Oratorio_ shepherds' Sinfonia (Chapter 15), the markers will be the same. 12/8 meter + recorders or flutes + major key + slow tempo + soft dynamic + lilting accompaniment = Bach in pastoral mode. The convention is part of the inherited Baroque vocabulary; Bach used it because his audience would recognize it.

#### Hearing four voices: a practical method

The central listening skill the aria sets up is hearing four independent voices at once. Most listeners' default hearing fuses the four voices into a single textural impression, "soprano with pretty accompaniment." The skill the chapter is teaching is to hear each voice separately while still registering the combined effect.

The practical method is to take a single hearing and assign your attention to one voice at a time. On your first hearing, attend to the soprano. You probably will anyway. On a second hearing, attend specifically to recorder 1 (the higher of the two recorders); try to follow its line for the full aria, ignoring everything else as best you can. On a third hearing, attend specifically to recorder 2 (the lower recorder); follow its line. On a fourth hearing, attend specifically to the continuo bass line (the lowest sound, the cello or bassoon doubling the harpsichord's bass); follow it. Four hearings, four different attentional foci.

This is unusual listening, most music is not designed to support this kind of voice-by-voice attention. Bach's music is. The four voices are independent compositions that happen to fit together. After the four single-voice hearings, take a fifth hearing in which you try to hold all four voices in attention at once. Most listeners cannot, your attention will drift to one or another. But the attempt builds the skill. Subsequent chapters in this volume will rely on it.

The four-voice-listening exercise on this gentle aria is the foundation of the more demanding contrapuntal listening you will do in Chapter 10 (the _Well-Tempered Clavier_) and Chapter 19 (the _Art of Fugue_). Begin building the habit now.

#### The harmonic shadow around 3:00

The single moment in this aria that most rewards close attention is the brief darkening near the middle. Around 3:00 in Kirkby's reading (the timing shifts in other recordings; locate by ear), the soprano sings the word _Friede_ — peace — and the harmony underneath touches G minor, the relative minor of B♭ major. The shift is brief, perhaps fifteen to twenty seconds; the soprano's line takes a chromatic side-step (an A-flat where you expected an A-natural, falling toward G); the continuo follows; the two recorders, which have been doubling and complementing the soprano in clear B-flat throughout, momentarily move into a more constrained harmonic space. Then it clears. The chromatic note resolves; the soprano returns to a fully diatonic shape; the recorders open back into B-flat major; the cloud has passed.

This is a small moment in a small aria, but it is the moment that lifts the piece from pretty pastoral to something more specifically Bach. The text is at its most consoling point — _peace_ — and the music gives you not pure consolation but consolation against the brief shadow of what consolation answers. The pastoral image is a Lutheran image; the good shepherd watches over flocks that could be otherwise. Bach does not state this theologically; he registers it in twenty seconds of harmonic shading. The cloud-and-clearing pattern recurs across the catalogue — in the slow movements of the Brandenburg concertos, in the contemplative arias of the cantatas, in the slow variations of the Goldbergs — and this aria is its gentlest demonstration. Listen for the moment specifically; once you have heard it once, you will hear similar moments elsewhere in Bach as variations on the same harmonic-rhetorical device.

#### The "good shepherd" rhetorical frame

A note on the text. The "good shepherd" image is one of the most heavily-loaded images in Western theology and political philosophy. In the Lutheran tradition Bach worked within, "the Good Shepherd" is Christ (John 10:11, "I am the good shepherd; the good shepherd lays down his life for the sheep"). In court poetry of the early eighteenth century, "the good ruler as good shepherd" was a standard image (Salomon Franck, the Weimar court poet who wrote BWV 208's text, was working within a thoroughly conventional vocabulary). The image carries both the religious and the political resonances simultaneously.

Bach's setting does not foreground either resonance. The music is pastoral-mode music; it does not signal "this is theological" or "this is political." A modern listener can hear the aria as a piece of pastoral atmosphere without registering either the theological or the political layer. A Weißenfels-court listener in 1713 would have registered both, the duke as good ruler, the duke as Christ-image figure, the duke as fortunate ground for the pastoral peace the aria depicts.

The volume does not require you to take on the theological frame to enjoy the music. Chapter 16 will give the orientation for readers who want to take it on for the cantata chapters; here, the aria stands on its own as music.

### 2.4 The Stories

#### What this piece is and is not

"Sheep May Safely Graze" is the ninth movement of a twelve-movement cantata. It is not the cantata's centerpiece (the cantata is constructed around the soprano-Pales-and-Diana mythological framing, with several arias for different characters; the Pales aria sits in the middle of the cantata's second half); it is not formally elaborate (the aria is a relatively simple ABA-like setting of a four-line text); it is not contrapuntally demanding by Bach's later standards (the four-voice polyphony is gentle compared to a fugue or to a Brandenburg movement). It is, simply, a beautifully-made pastoral aria, one of several gentle moments in a longer secular cantata composed for a Weißenfels birthday banquet in 1713.

What it is not is a deep work. The aria is a finely-made gentle song for soprano with obbligato recorders. The Bach catalogue contains works of vastly more ambition, the _St. Matthew Passion_ opening chorus, the _B Minor Mass_ Crucifixus, the Cello Suite No. 6 Sarabande, the _Art of Fugue_ Contrapunctus 14. The aria is not in that class. It is in the class of works that are perfect within their modest scope.

What the aria does is establish for this volume the listening habits the rest of the volume will build on: the four-voice attentional discipline, the obbligato-instrument recognition, the pastoral-genre markers, and the harmonic-shading attention (the cloud-and-clearing pattern). These habits will recur. Whenever a Bach piece in subsequent chapters has multiple independent voices (which is, basically, every Bach piece), the four-voice habit applies. Whenever a piece has an obbligato instrumental partner to a voice, the obbligato-recognition applies. Whenever a Bach piece briefly darkens harmonically before clearing, the shading-attention applies.

#### The Hunting Cantata, briefly

The full _Jagdkantate_ BWV 208 ("Was mir behagt, ist nur die muntre Jagd", "What pleases me is only the lively hunt") is twelve movements totaling about thirty-five minutes. It opens with a recitative and aria for Diana the huntress (mezzo-soprano or soprano) celebrating the hunt; it includes recitatives and arias for Endymion (tenor), Pan (bass), and Pales (soprano); it closes with a celebratory chorus. Bach composed the cantata in 1713 for the Weißenfels birthday; he also performed parts of it again in modified form at other occasions across his career, including a Leipzig performance in 1729. The cantata contains other notable moments, the tenor aria "Ein Fürst ist seines Landes Pan" is a charming setting; the celebratory closing chorus is bracing, but the Pales aria has had a life of its own beyond the cantata that no other movement has achieved.

The cantata's modern recording history is more limited than the Pales aria's. Complete recordings exist (the Helmuth Rilling Hänssler series; the John Eliot Gardiner Cantata Pilgrimage series; the Ton Koopman Erato/Antoine Marchand series; the Masaaki Suzuki BIS series all include BWV 208 in their complete-cantatas projects); but most listeners encounter only the Pales aria, in extracted form, in collections of Bach favorites or in soprano recital albums.

A reader who wants to hear the Pales aria in its cantata context can spend half an hour with one of the complete-cantata recordings (the Gardiner is the recommended starting point, Soli Deo Gloria recording of the Cantata Pilgrimage). It is worth doing once. The Pales aria sounds slightly different when you hear it as movement 9 of a sequence of twelve, prepared by the eight earlier movements (especially the Diana arias) and followed by the closing celebratory framework. Outside the cantata, "Sheep May Safely Graze" is a beautiful gentle aria; inside the cantata, it is the pastoral-contemplative center of a court entertainment that builds to a celebratory close.

#### Recording history briefly

The Pales aria is one of the most-recorded short Bach pieces in the catalogue. The major recording lineages divide as follows.

The Kirkby / Academy of Ancient Music lineage. Emma Kirkby (born 1949) is the central figure in the late-twentieth-century period-instrument soprano tradition. Her partnership with the Academy of Ancient Music under Christopher Hogwood (founded 1973) produced the 1980s Hyperion Bach Cantatas series that is the period-instrument touchstone for many Bach arias. The Kirkby reading of "Sheep May Safely Graze" is the most-canonical HIP version; the voice is light, clear, controlled, with minimal vibrato; the two recorders are clearly audible as independent lines; the continuo is discreet. The reading takes about 4 minutes 50 seconds and feels, to modern ears, almost austere compared to the late-Romantic readings that dominated mid-twentieth-century concert programming.

The mid-twentieth-century modern-instrument lineage. Karl Richter (1926-1981) recorded BWV 208 with the Munich Bach Orchestra in the late 1960s (Archiv); his soprano Edith Mathis sang the Pales aria. The Richter reading is fuller, warmer, slower, with a more substantial continuo (an organ in addition to harpsichord and bass instruments); the recorders are sometimes replaced by transverse flutes. The reading takes about 6 minutes. It is the canonical modern-instrument version for listeners who find the period-instrument sound too thin; some listeners prefer it; others find it heavy by comparison.

The crossover-modern lineage. Major modern-instrument sopranos, Magdalena Kožená with Simon Rattle and the Berlin Philharmonic (DG 2003), Renée Fleming with the Orchestra of St. Luke's (Decca 1999), Anne Sofie von Otter with the Berlin Philharmonic (DG 1990s), have recorded the aria in arrangements that emphasize the operatic-voice tradition. These readings treat the aria as a soprano-showcase piece; the line is sung with more vibrato, more dynamic range, more interpretive freedom; the accompanying ensemble is often a full modern chamber orchestra rather than the original soprano-plus-two-recorders-plus-continuo. The arrangements work as concert pieces but lose some of the four-voice polyphonic character of the original scoring.

The instrumental-arrangement tradition. The aria has been arranged for solo piano (Mary Howe's 1938 piano arrangement, used by Walter Gieseking and other twentieth-century pianists); for solo cello with piano (used by Yo-Yo Ma in his _Bach_ album in the late 1990s); for solo flute with continuo (where the soprano line is given to the flute and the original obbligato recorder pair becomes a single soloist); and for various chamber-orchestra arrangements. The instrumental arrangements are pleasant but, again, lose the four-voice polyphonic character that is the original aria's specific compositional achievement. For your listening, prefer the vocal versions in the original scoring or close to it.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The Pales aria connects forward to many later works in this volume.

To the cantata BWV 82 "Ich habe genug" (Chapter 12). Same scoring principle, solo voice (bass, in BWV 82's case) with one obbligato instrument (an oboe, in BWV 82). The four-voice texture you are learning here (counting continuo as one voice and the two recorders as two voices plus soprano) becomes a three-voice texture there (bass + oboe + continuo). The listening skill transfers directly.

To the alto aria "Erbarme dich" from the _St. Matthew Passion_ (Chapter 13). Same scoring principle, different scale and emotional weight. "Erbarme dich" is an alto aria with obbligato violin and continuo, a three-voice scoring directly analogous to BWV 82's. The "Erbarme dich" is one of the most-grieving pieces in the Bach catalogue; the pastoral peace of "Sheep May Safely Graze" is at the opposite emotional pole. The scoring principle and the four-voice attentional discipline carry across the emotional distance.

To the chorale prelude "Ich ruf zu dir, Herr Jesu Christ" BWV 639 from the _Orgelbüchlein_ (Chapter 6). Same kind of slow, contemplative, polyphonic-but-gentle music with multiple independent voices in a small-scale package. The chorale-prelude listening you will develop in Chapter 6 grows from the same root as the obbligato-aria listening you are building here.

When the volume's later chapters ask you to follow Bach's contrapuntal voice as a unifying thread, the listening habit you are building now on a gentle pastoral aria is what those chapters will rely on. The obbligato-aria pattern recurs throughout the Leipzig cantatas (Chapter 12) with countless voice-and-instrument combinations.

#### The Weimar 1713 context (Thread A note)

A brief note for the biographical thread (Thread A). February 1713 finds Bach in his fifth year at Weimar, three years into the court-organist-and-chamber-musician appointment (he would be promoted to concertmaster in March 1714, a year after the Hunting Cantata's Weißenfels performance). The Weimar years are productive years; most of the great organ works are being composed in this period (Chapter 5 will take up the Weimar story in detail). The Hunting Cantata is a side-commission, a piece composed for a neighboring duke's birthday, not for Weimar, and represents an early Bach excursion into the secular-cantata genre that would not become a major part of his output until the Leipzig years (the _Coffee Cantata_ and _Peasant Cantata_ of the 1730s are the famous later secular cantatas).

Bach was twenty-seven, married seven years to Maria Barbara (they would have four surviving children by 1713), with a household in the Weimar court town. The composition of the Hunting Cantata was a paid commission; the Weißenfels duke would have provided an honorarium plus travel expenses. The cantata was performed once at the birthday celebration on February 23, 1713; Bach took the manuscript home; he reused several movements (including the Pales aria, in modified form) in subsequent court cantatas across his career. The "Sheep May Safely Graze" aria itself was not extracted as a stand-alone until much later, well into the nineteenth century, when arrangers began publishing the aria separately for amateur performance.

The aria's modern fame rests on three nineteenth-and-twentieth-century developments: the late-nineteenth-century stand-alone-extract publication; the early-twentieth-century arrangement tradition (Mary Howe's piano arrangement was much-performed in the 1940s and 50s); and the late-twentieth-century period-instrument-recording cycle (Hogwood, Gardiner, Suzuki, Koopman all recorded the complete cantata in the cantata-cycle projects of the 1970s onward). Each generation has retransmitted the aria into the next; each has slightly recalibrated what the aria sounds like. The Kirkby / Hogwood reading you have just heard is one specific moment in that transmission history, a late-twentieth-century period-instrument reading that emphasizes the polyphonic clarity. A 1950s Edith Mathis / Karl Richter modern-instrument reading would have sounded fuller and slower. A 1900 amateur-soprano-with-piano-arrangement performance would have been an entirely different piece.

The aria you hear when you hit "play" on the Kirkby recording is a specific 1985 London product. The aria the Weißenfels banquet guests heard on the evening of February 23, 1713 is forever inaccessible; what you have is what scholarship, performance, and recording have transmitted. The chapter's listening exercise is to attend to what is on the recording, with what is on the recording understood as a particular instance of a much longer transmission history.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The chapter emphasizes that 'Sheep May Safely Graze' is a four-voice polyphony, with the two recorders functioning as independent voices alongside the soprano and the continuo. Listen to the Kirkby / Hogwood reading once more, but this time attend specifically to one of the two recorders, try to follow its line from start to finish, ignoring the soprano as best you can. What did you discover about the recorder's part that you missed when the soprano was your main focus? What is the recorder doing that the soprano is not?"_

* _"The text, 'sheep can graze in safety where a good shepherd watches, where rulers govern well one can feel quiet and peace, and what makes lands prosperous', is a conventional court flattery of the duke. The music does not foreground either the theological 'good shepherd as Christ' resonance or the political 'good ruler' resonance. Why might Bach have chosen to set such a loaded text in such a neutral pastoral mode? What does the choice cost or gain in expressive force?"_

* _"The aria has lived a strange double life: as the ninth movement of a twelve-movement birthday cantata for a 1713 Weißenfels duke that very few people now perform complete, and as a stand-alone wedding-and-radio favorite that millions know without knowing its origin. What does this kind of extraction do to a piece of music? Are there other works you can think of, pieces of literature, paintings, films, that have a similar 'famous excerpt vs forgotten whole' dynamic? What did you lose, or gain, by hearing 'Sheep May Safely Graze' inside its cantata context for the first time?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Emma Kirkby / Christopher Hogwood / Academy of Ancient Music 1985 Hyperion recording (or one of the alternatives listed in §2.1). Listen to "Sheep May Safely Graze" a second time and attend to these four concrete moments.

  1. **The opening ritornello (0:00-0:30).** Confirm: two recorders enter together, weaving independent lines around each other, with the continuo (harpsichord plus bass instrument) underneath. Try to hear the two recorders as two separate lines, not as a fused pair. Notice the moments where they converge into thirds (the most consonant interval after the unison) and the moments where they diverge into independent figures.

  2. **The soprano's first entrance (about 0:30).** When Kirkby enters with "Schafe können sicher weiden," the four-voice texture is suddenly in play. Listen for a moment to see whether your attention immediately collapses onto the soprano (the natural tendency) or whether you can still hear the recorders independently underneath. The volume will spend many chapters building the skill of holding multiple voices in attention; this is the first practice.

  3. **The harmonic shading around 3:00.** The music briefly darkens into a minor-mode area before returning to B♭ major. Listen for the moment of the shadow, it is brief, perhaps fifteen to twenty seconds, and the soprano's line takes a slightly more chromatic shape during it. The cloud-and-clearing pattern is the simplest version of Bach's harmonic-shading practice; you will hear elaborated versions in subsequent chapters.

  4. **The two recorders alone for one full hearing.** Listen one more time, but try to keep your attention on the two recorders throughout, ignore the soprano and the continuo as best you can. This is an unnatural way to listen; most music is not designed to support it. Bach's is. After a single hearing focused on the recorders, you will have a clearer sense of how much independent compositional work the obbligato pair is doing. The discovery is the foundation of the contrapuntal-listening skill the volume will build chapter by chapter.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : the complete Hunting Cantata BWV 208 in one of the complete-cantata recordings (the John Eliot Gardiner / Soli Deo Gloria Cantata Pilgrimage recording is the recommended starting point; the Ton Koopman / Erato cycle and the Masaaki Suzuki / BIS cycle are alternatives). About thirty-five minutes total. The Pales aria sits as movement 9; you will hear it differently in context than as an extracted stand-alone. Worth doing once, even if you do not return to the complete cantata afterward.

**Listen** (comparison): the alto aria "Erbarme dich, mein Gott" from the _St. Matthew Passion_ BWV 244. Janet Baker with Otto Klemperer (EMI 1961), Andreas Scholl with Philippe Herreweghe (Harmonia Mundi 1998), or Bernarda Fink with the Tölzer Knabenchor and the Concerto Köln (Harmonia Mundi 2008) are well-recorded versions. The aria is scored for alto voice, obbligato solo violin, and continuo: same scoring principle as "Sheep May Safely Graze," at far greater emotional weight. The four-voice listening skill transfers; the emotional register is different. The pair makes a useful first comparison across the volume's pastoral and Passion registers; Chapter 13 will return to the "Erbarme dich" in detail.

**Read** : Alfred Dürr, _The Cantatas of J. S. Bach_ (Oxford University Press, 2005 English edition, originally German 1971 with revisions). The cantata-by-cantata commentary that is the standard reference for the cantata literature. The entry on BWV 208 gives the compositional context, the text-and-music analysis, and the performance history. About one page per cantata; the BWV 208 entry is two or three pages. The reference is one you will return to throughout the volume; this is a good first dip in.

**Read** (alternative): John Eliot Gardiner, _Bach: Music in the Castle of Heaven_ (Knopf, 2013), pages covering the Weimar period. Gardiner (cf. Ch 1 §1.2) gives the cantata a fuller social-and-performance context than Dürr's strict commentary does.

---

## Chapter 3: Eisenach → Lüneburg → Arnstadt → Mühlhausen 1685-1708

The first twenty-three years of Bach's life are sparsely documented compared to the Weimar, Köthen, and Leipzig years that follow. There are no surviving letters from the young Bach; almost no extant compositions from before about 1703; only fragmentary records of the schools he attended, the teachers he studied with, and the daily life he led.
