What we do have, the parish records of births and deaths, the choir-school enrollment lists, the Arnstadt consistory's reprimand for the Lübeck trip, the Mühlhausen marriage register, is enough to sketch the biographical arc but not to fill it in with the kind of detail later periods provide.

The arc itself is one of the most consequential apprenticeships in Western music. The boy who was born in Eisenach in March 1685 to a father who was the town's _Stadtpfeifer_ , town musician, a guild-trained craftsman whose job mixed civic ceremonial fanfares, wedding-and-funeral music, and street-corner waits, would, by the time he took the Weimar court-organist post in 1708 at age twenty-three, have absorbed essentially the entire German musical inheritance of the seventeenth century. He would have studied with elder Bach relatives in Eisenach and Ohrdruf. He would have copied other composers' works by candlelight in his elder brother's house (the legend, from Forkel 1802, is that the eleven-year-old Bach was forbidden to read a particular collection his brother kept locked up, and copied it secretly by moonlight over six months, ruining his eyes in the process). He would have sung as a choir scholar in Lüneburg. He would have walked to Hamburg several times to hear Johann Adam Reincken at the Catharinenkirche, and walked to Lübeck once on a 250-mile trip to hear Dietrich Buxtehude at the Marienkirche. He would have served as an organist at Arnstadt and Mühlhausen. He would have married his first wife and composed his first surviving cantatas.

This chapter walks through those twenty-three years and anchors on one early keyboard work, the _Capriccio on the Departure of a Beloved Brother_ BWV 992, composed around 1704 at Arnstadt, the most-discussed of the young Bach's keyboard pieces. The Capriccio is short (about ten minutes), unmistakably the work of a young composer experimenting with a programmatic-keyboard genre, and biographically interesting (the title-page program is the most explicit narrative attached to any early Bach work). It is also a piece you can listen to in its entirety in less than the running time of an episode of any television show, which makes it a good early anchor: by the end of the chapter you will know one early Bach work intimately.

### 3.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Arnstadt, central Germany, 1704. Bach is nineteen years old, in the second of what will be four years as organist of the Neue Kirche (the "New Church"). The post is his first paid musical employment; he was appointed in August 1703 after a successful organ examination at age eighteen, on the basis of his work testing the church's new instrument. The salary is small but sufficient, about 50 gulden per year, plus accommodation. His duties are light by the standards he will later carry: he is to play for two services on Sundays and one weekday service, to lead the choir of choirboys from the Arnstadt grammar school, and to maintain the organ. The duties leave him significant free time for composition and for keyboard practice.

It is in this period, likely the summer or autumn of 1704, that an event occurs in the Bach family that may have prompted one of his earliest substantial keyboard works. His elder brother Johann Jakob Bach (1682-1722), three years older than Sebastian, has accepted a position as oboist in the Swedish royal guard under Charles XII (1682-1718), the young Swedish king then engaged in the Great Northern War (1700-1721) against a coalition of Denmark, Russia, and Poland-Saxony. Johann Jakob will be leaving Thuringia for an indefinite military assignment in Sweden; the brothers will likely not see each other again for years, perhaps ever. (In fact they would never meet again; Johann Jakob died in Stockholm in 1722, eighteen years after his departure.)

The young Bach composes a keyboard work to mark the brother's departure: the _Capriccio sopra la lontananza del fratello dilettissimo_ , "Capriccio on the Departure of a Beloved Brother", a six-section keyboard suite with explicit programmatic captions printed at the head of each section. The captions, in Italian, sketch a small narrative: friends try to dissuade the brother from leaving; the friends describe the dangers awaiting him abroad; the friends lament his departure; the friends gather to bid farewell; the post-coach horn sounds (the post-coach being the traveling-coach system that carried mail and passengers across central Germany); the fugue closes the work in a return to high spirits.

The Capriccio is in B♭ major (the same key as "Sheep May Safely Graze" from Chapter 2, a key Bach used frequently for warm, settled moods); it takes about ten minutes to play; it is one of the earliest works in the Bach catalogue that bears an unambiguously personal occasion as its prompt. Most of Bach's later music, even the cantatas, even the Passions, does not bear this kind of explicit autobiographical inscription. The Capriccio is a young man's piece in this respect: a not-yet-twenty-year-old composer using the keyboard to mark a family event.

Scholarship has questioned aspects of the standard story. The biographical detail that the Capriccio was specifically for Johann Jakob's 1704 Swedish departure traces to the late-eighteenth-century reception (Forkel 1802 mentions it; Spitta in the 1870s repeated and expanded it); Bach himself left no surviving letter explaining the work. The internal captions are present in the manuscript and clearly indicate a brother-departure program, but which brother and which departure is not as firm as a casual narrative might suggest. There is also a textual question about whether the captions in their present form are entirely Bach's (some scholars have suggested they may have been added or elaborated by copyists). The cautious modern reading is: the work is from about 1704; it is a programmatic farewell piece for a departing brother; the precise biographical details (Johann Jakob, Swedish royal guard, 1704 departure) are plausibly correct but not as solidly verified as the Wolff or Boyd biographies sometimes present them.

For listening purposes, the precise biographical anchor matters less than the programmatic structure. The Capriccio is a six-section keyboard work with a narrative spine; the narrative is a departure-of-a-loved-one drama; the music traces an emotional arc from persuasion-to-stay through lament to bittersweet farewell to a final fugue that closes the work in the major mode. You can hear it as the young Bach's experiment with programmatic keyboard writing, regardless of which brother or which year.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The six-section structure.** Most Bach pieces you will encounter in this volume, the Cello Suites, the Brandenburg Concertos, the cantatas, are works in multiple movements that flow together in performance with clear breaks between movements. The Capriccio is six sections played in succession with brief connecting moments. Each section has a different character: the first is conversational and persuasive; the second is more agitated; the third is a lament (an _adagissimo_ , "very slow"); the fourth is a more settled farewell; the fifth is the post-coach horn (an _aria di postiglione_ , a postilion's aria, depicting the horn-call of the coach driver); the sixth is a fugue. As you listen, try to register the section transitions, when one character ends and the next begins.

  2. **The lament (section 3).** Around the middle of the work, the music slows dramatically into a long lament with a descending bass line that repeats throughout (a _passacaglia_ -like ground-bass figure, though more freely treated than a strict passacaglia). The lament is the emotional center of the work; the rest of the Capriccio leads to it and away from it. Listen for the moment when the music shifts into the slow lament, the change from the prior energetic sections is unmistakable.

  3. **The post-horn (section 5).** The fifth section uses a specific musical figure to depict the horn of the traveling post-coach, a rapid repeated-note figure in the right hand that sounds quite literally like a horn-call. Listen for it; it is the most pictorial of the Capriccio's effects and the moment at which the work most clearly tips into program music. The horn-call signals that the brother's coach has arrived; the actual departure is imminent.

#### One-sentence background

Composed c. 1704 at Arnstadt, likely on the occasion of his elder brother Johann Jakob Bach's departure for Sweden; a six-section programmatic keyboard suite in B♭ major (or B♭ with shifting modes across sections), about 10 minutes; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Glenn Gould playing on piano (CBS, recorded 1979, released as part of Gould's mid-career Bach keyboard project, Gould's strict tempo and his clear articulation of the polyphonic lines make the work's structure unusually audible), chosen because the Gould reading on piano clarifies the Capriccio's structural divisions and the polyphonic texture more than most harpsichord readings do.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Bach Capriccio BWV 992 Glenn Gould" or "Bach Capriccio on the Departure Beloved Brother" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Andras Schiff on piano (Decca, recorded 1980s, released in his early Bach keyboard cycle), a warmer, more lyrical reading; the lament section is taken with more breathing space than Gould's stricter tempo.
* Christophe Rousset on harpsichord (Decca / L'Oiseau-Lyre, period-instrument, recorded 1990s), the harpsichord reading on a period instrument; gives the work its original timbre and is closer to what Bach himself would have played at the Arnstadt house instrument.
* Pierre Hantaï on harpsichord (Mirare, recorded 2000s), another well-recorded harpsichord version; Hantaï is the leading French Bach-harpsichordist of his generation.

### 3.2 The Listening Window

This section is for your second listen, the one where you know what is coming and can attend to specific moments. Timestamps below refer to Gould's 1979 CBS recording. Gould plays the Capriccio in about 10 minutes 15 seconds. If you are listening to a different recording, the section transitions still apply but the exact clock times will differ; in that case use the section-by-section descriptions rather than the timestamps.

**0:00-1:50: Section 1 (Arioso. Adagio): "Friends try to dissuade him from leaving."** The opening section is in a flowing arioso style (an Italian term for a half-melodic, half-recitative-like musical setting, neither strict aria nor strict recitative). The right hand sings a long, ornamented melody; the left hand provides harmonic support with broken chords. The mood is conversational and persuasive, the music is meant to depict the friends' calm initial attempt to talk the brother out of leaving. Gould takes the section at a slightly faster tempo than most performers; the conversational character is foregrounded rather than the persuasive one.

**About 1:50-3:00: Section 2 (Andante): "They picture for him the dangers awaiting him abroad."** The music becomes more agitated, more rapid passagework in both hands, more dissonant harmonic shifts, a sense of warning. Listen for the moment of transition out of section 1 into section 2; it is marked by a clear change in figuration (the right hand begins running scales and arpeggios instead of singing a long line). The friends are no longer pleading calmly; they are alarmed.

**About 3:00-5:30: Section 3 (Adagissimo): "The friends' general lament."** This is the longest and slowest section of the Capriccio and the emotional center. The music shifts into the minor mode (F minor in the principal lament passage). The bass line settles into a slow descending pattern that repeats with variation throughout the section (a ground-bass or passacaglia-like figure). The right hand sings a long, mournful melody above it. The lament is in the long-breathed Italian-lament tradition; Bach is working within a genre (the keyboard lament with descending ground bass) that had centuries of precedent in Italian and German Baroque music. Gould takes the lament unusually slowly, almost two and a half minutes for the section, which foregrounds its sustained gravity.

**About 5:30-6:30: Section 4: "Since they cannot stop him, they bid him farewell."** The music returns to a more flowing, less mournful character, the friends have accepted the brother's decision; they say goodbye. The tempo is moderate; the figuration is fluid; the mood is bittersweet rather than tragic. Listen for the moment of transition out of the lament (around 5:30), the descending bass pattern lifts; the right hand returns to a more singing line; the harmonic mode shifts back toward the major.

**About 6:30-7:30: Section 5 (Aria di postiglione): "The post-coach driver's call."** The post-horn enters. The right hand plays rapid repeated-note figures that imitate the sound of a brass horn-call (specifically the post-horn, the small valveless horn that mail-coach drivers used to announce their arrival in towns and along the road). The figure is unmistakable; once you have noticed it once, you will recognize it in subsequent hearings. Listen also for the left hand underneath, which continues the harmonic support while the right hand does the horn-imitation. The section is brief, perhaps a minute long; it is the most pictorial moment in the Capriccio.

**About 7:30-10:15: Section 6 (Fuga sopra il corno di postiglione): "Fugue on the post-horn theme."** The Capriccio closes with a fugue (the first fugue you will encounter in this volume, a foretaste of the much-greater fugues to come in later chapters). The fugue's subject is derived from the post-horn theme of section 5, the rapid repeated-note figure becomes the basis of a four-voice fugue. Listen for the subject's entries: the first voice states the subject; the second voice enters with the subject a few seconds later (transposed up or down by a fourth or fifth, the standard fugue answer); the third voice and (if the fugue is in four voices) the fourth voice follow. Gould's clear articulation of the polyphonic lines makes the entries unusually audible. The fugue lasts about 2 minutes 30 seconds and closes the work in the major mode, with the post-horn echo now turned into a celebration rather than a farewell signal.

If your recording's total time differs from Gould's 10:15 by more than fifteen percent, you may be hearing a substantially different tempo (Schiff takes the lament slower, bringing the total closer to 11 minutes; some HIP harpsichord readings tighten the work to about 8:30). The section transitions should still be in the same proportional locations.

### 3.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The programmatic-keyboard tradition

Program music, instrumental music with an attached narrative or descriptive program, has a long pre-history in the seventeenth century. The Italian harpsichord composer Bernardo Pasquini (1637-1710) wrote programmatic suites with battle-and-storm depictions. The French harpsichord school (François Couperin among others) wrote _pièces caractéristiques_ , character pieces with descriptive titles. The German harpsichord tradition Bach was working within included Johann Jakob Froberger (1616-1667) and Johann Kuhnau (1660-1722). Kuhnau, Bach's predecessor as Thomaskantor at Leipzig, published a set of _Biblische Historien_ (Biblical Histories) in 1700, keyboard sonatas depicting Old Testament narratives (David and Goliath, Saul cured by David's music, Jacob's death and burial) with explicit programmatic captions. The Kuhnau collection is one of the closest precedents for Bach's Capriccio: same genre (programmatic keyboard suite), same use of section captions, same period (1700 is four years before Bach's Capriccio).

What Bach does in the Capriccio is a younger composer working within the Kuhnau-Froberger-Pasquini tradition with skill but without (yet) the compositional distinction he will bring to later works. The lament section is the strongest part of the Capriccio, the descending-bass-line lament with ornamented melodic line above is something Bach will return to throughout his career (the Crucifixus of the _B Minor Mass_ , Chapter 14, uses a related ground-bass-lament device at much greater emotional weight). The fugue is competent but not contrapuntally virtuoso (the Bach of 1740 would have written a much denser fugue on the post-horn theme; the Bach of 1704 writes a clear, audible, but relatively simple four-voice fugue). The pictorial sections (the post-horn especially) are charming but pictorialism for its own sake is not what Bach's mature voice prioritizes, the mature Bach prefers to embed pictorial moments within larger structural arguments rather than letting the picture take over.

For the listener: the Capriccio is interesting as a young Bach exercise within a genre he would essentially abandon (mature Bach wrote very little explicitly programmatic instrumental music; the pictorial element gets folded into the cantatas and Passions where text drives the picture rather than the picture standing on its own). Hearing the Capriccio gives you a baseline for what the nineteen-year-old Bach could write, against which the rest of the catalogue can be measured.

#### The lament's descending bass

The Capriccio's central section, the _adagissimo_ lament, uses a compositional device called a descending tetrachord bass. A _tetrachord_ is a four-note descending step pattern (most often, in this period's usage, the four notes that descend from the tonic to the dominant, for example C-B♭-A-G in C minor, or F-E♭-D-C in F minor). The descending tetrachord, especially in minor mode, was a standard Baroque emblem of lament. Henry Purcell used it in "Dido's Lament" from _Dido and Aeneas_ (1689), the most-famous English-Baroque lament. Claudio Monteverdi used it in the _Lamento della Ninfa_ (1638). Bach's mature lament writing uses it many times: the Crucifixus of the _B Minor Mass_ , the opening chorus of the Cantata BWV 12 "Weinen, Klagen, Sorgen, Zagen" (which Bach later parodied as the Crucifixus), and many others.

In the Capriccio, the descending tetrachord underneath the lament gives the section its grief-stricken character. The bass repeats the descending pattern over and over (with some variation); the right hand sings a free-ornamented melody above. The listener's ear registers the repeated descent as a sustained sorrow; the melodic line above provides the specific personal feeling. The compositional principle, a fixed bass pattern with free elaboration above, is the same principle that drives the chaconne form (the D-minor violin Chaconne BWV 1004, Chapter 9) and the passacaglia (BWV 582, Chapter 6) and many of the Goldberg Variations (Chapter 17). The Capriccio lament is the young Bach's first surviving substantial encounter with the device.

For the listener: when you hear a slow Bach passage with a repeating bass figure underneath an elaborated melodic line, especially in minor mode, you are hearing a version of the descending-tetrachord lament tradition that the Capriccio anchors here. The pattern recurs across the catalogue.

#### Section transitions and the suite tradition

The Capriccio's six sections are played in succession with brief connecting moments, usually a held chord or a short transitional figure between sections, rather than a clean stop. This was standard practice for Baroque keyboard suites: the sections are individually conceived but the work as a whole is a continuous experience for the listener. The pause between sections is short, long enough to mark the transition, not so long as to break the work's flow.

The suite tradition Bach is working within in the Capriccio is the _German keyboard suite_ of the late seventeenth century, which itself derives from the French-clavecin suite (Louis Couperin, Jacques Champion de Chambonnières) and the German organ-suite tradition (Froberger especially). A typical German keyboard suite would have an opening prelude or fantasia, then a series of stylized dance movements (allemande, courante, sarabande, gigue), possibly with optional extra movements (a minuet, a bourrée, a gavotte). The Capriccio is not a strict dance suite, it has the programmatic captions rather than dance-form titles, but it inherits the suite's structural principle of multiple short sections played in succession.

The Bach who in 1717 would compose the French Suites and the English Suites at Köthen, and who would compose the six Partitas of _Clavier-Übung I_ in the 1730s at Leipzig, is the same Bach who in 1704 wrote the Capriccio. The suite-tradition skill is being built in the Capriccio; the great fruits will come later.

#### The fugue, briefly

The Capriccio closes with a fugue. The fugue is the first fugue you will hear in this volume, it is a good introduction to the genre because the subject (drawn from the post-horn figure of section 5) is short, audibly distinct, and easy to follow as it passes from voice to voice.

A fugue, briefly, is a piece of music in which one voice announces a short musical idea (the _subject_); a second voice answers with the same idea, usually transposed up or down by a fourth or fifth interval; a third voice (and possibly more) enters with the subject in turn. By the time all voices have stated the subject, the texture is built, multiple voices moving simultaneously, each independent, all derived from the same opening subject. The Capriccio's fugue is in four voices. Listen for the entrances: in Gould's recording, the first voice enters around 7:30 with the post-horn-derived subject; the second voice enters a few seconds later transposed up; the third and fourth voices follow.

The Capriccio fugue is a fugue for a young composer, clear, audible, well-constructed, but not pushing the form to its limits. The Bach of 1722 who composed the _Well-Tempered Clavier_ Book 1 (Chapter 10) would write twenty-four fugues of much greater contrapuntal density. The Bach of 1740-50 who composed the _Art of Fugue_ (Chapter 19) would write fugues that work in three, four, and even six independent voices simultaneously and at the highest possible contrapuntal sophistication. The Capriccio's fugue is the beginning of the arc; it is the young Bach trying the form and getting it competently right. Chapter 20 §20.1 will collect the fugue-subject-and-answer device as a unifying thread of the volume; the Capriccio fugue is the first example.

The forward pointer is here: when you hear later fugues in this volume, the C-major WTC Prelude and Fugue (Chapter 10), the Art of Fugue Contrapunctus 1 (Chapter 19), you should be able to trace them back to the Capriccio fugue's much-simpler post-horn-derived four-voice exposition.

### 3.4 The Stories

#### The 1685-1700 Eisenach-Ohrdruf years

Bach was born on March 21, 1685 in Eisenach, in the Duchy of Saxe-Eisenach, in the Thuringian region of central Germany. The town in 1685 had a population of about 8,000; it was a small Protestant town with one principal church (the Georgenkirche, where Bach was baptized), a town hall, a market square, and the Wartburg castle on the hill above where Martin Luther had translated the New Testament into German in 1521-22.

Bach's father Johann Ambrosius Bach (1645-1695) was the Eisenach _Stadtpfeifer_ , the head of the town's small group of professional musicians, responsible for playing fanfares at civic occasions, for performing wedding and funeral music, and for the daily _Türmer_ music played from the town hall tower at fixed hours. The Stadtpfeifer was a respected craftsman with guild-recognized status; the position carried a regular salary, accommodation in a town-owned house, and an apprentice-training role. Johann Ambrosius had married Maria Elisabeth Lämmerhirt in 1668; they had eight children of whom six survived past infancy. Johann Sebastian was the youngest.

The Bach family in Eisenach was part of an extraordinarily extensive musician dynasty. Karl Geiringer's _The Bach Family_ (Oxford 1954) traces six generations of professional musicians in Thuringia by Johann Sebastian's birth, town musicians, organists, cantors, instrument makers, all bearing the Bach surname and all interconnected through marriages and shared apprenticeships. The Bach family met annually in family-reunion gatherings (the so-called Bach-Familientage); at these reunions the Bachs would sing through their own compositions and through quodlibets (humorous medleys of folk tunes piled on top of each other; the _Goldberg Variations_ number 30, the Quodlibet, is Bach's nod to this family tradition). The boy Sebastian was born into a musical environment so saturated that "Bach" in Thuringia was effectively a synonym for "musician."

The Eisenach years end abruptly with the death of Sebastian's mother in May 1694 (he was nine) and his father in February 1695 (he was nine, going on ten). His father had remarried in late 1694 after the mother's death; the second wife became Sebastian's stepmother for only a few months before becoming his widowed stepmother for the second time. The two surviving Bach sons, Johann Jakob (twelve) and Johann Sebastian (ten), were sent to live with their elder brother Johann Christoph Bach (1671-1721), then organist at Ohrdruf, about 30 miles east of Eisenach. (This Johann Christoph should not be confused with their cousin Johann Christoph Bach of Eisenach, the composer of the celebrated motet "Es erhub sich ein Streit"; the family had many Johann Christophs.)

Johann Christoph of Ohrdruf had been a pupil of Johann Pachelbel (1653-1706), the South German organist and composer best known today for the Canon and Gigue in D major (which served as the Baroque-warmup anchor in _Close Listening: Classical Music_ Chapter 7). Through Johann Christoph, the young Sebastian Bach received his first systematic keyboard instruction in the Pachelbel tradition, a tradition oriented toward the South German keyboard style, more melodically straightforward and harmonically clear than the North German organ-school's free-rhapsodic style would be.

The Forkel 1802 biography preserves the famous Ohrdruf story: that Johann Christoph kept a particular collection of keyboard works (variously identified by later scholars as a manuscript of pieces by Pachelbel, Froberger, Kerll, and other South German keyboard composers) locked in a cabinet, refusing to let the young Sebastian study it; that Sebastian, determined to learn the pieces, took the manuscript out at night through the cabinet's grating, copied it secretly by moonlight over six months, and finished just as Johann Christoph discovered the project and confiscated the copy. The story has the texture of a piece of family lore polished into anecdote across decades of retelling; modern scholarship (Wolff, Boyd) treats it cautiously, the basic outline is plausible, the specific details (six months, moonlight, ruined eyesight) are likely embellished. What is clear is that the young Bach was assertive about his own musical education and that the Ohrdruf years gave him access to keyboard repertoire substantial enough to form the basis of his self-trained adolescence.

#### The 1700-1702 Lüneburg years

At age fifteen Bach left Ohrdruf for the choir school of the Michaeliskirche in Lüneburg, a town in Lower Saxony about 200 miles north of Thuringia. The journey was likely made on foot (Forkel reports this; though as with the moonlight-copying story the detail may be embellished, the practice of long foot-journeys was common in early-eighteenth-century Germany and is consistent with the surviving record). The Lüneburg choir school accepted scholarship boys whose voices were suitable; Bach was admitted on the strength of his voice (a soprano at age fifteen, before the voice change that would come within a year or two).

The Lüneburg years gave Bach access to a substantial music library, the choir-school maintained a collection of choral and instrumental music going back to the late sixteenth century, and to the North German organ tradition through Georg Böhm (1661-1733), the organist at the Johanniskirche in Lüneburg from 1698 onward. Böhm was a major figure in the North German organ school and one of the principal influences on Bach's developing organ style. Bach almost certainly studied informally with Böhm, though the relationship is not formally documented; the Wolff biography is cautious about the relationship's specifics.

From Lüneburg, Bach also made several trips to Hamburg (about 30 miles to the north) to hear Johann Adam Reincken (1623-1722, then in his late seventies, organist at the Catharinenkirche in Hamburg). Reincken was the senior North German organist of the period; his playing was the major touchstone for the rhapsodic-improvisatory _stylus phantasticus_ tradition that would become a fundamental influence on Bach's own organ writing. Bach copied out Reincken's _Hortus Musicus_ , a 1687 collection of instrumental works, and would return decades later (in 1720) to play for the by-then-97-year-old Reincken in Hamburg, drawing Reincken's reported remark that "I thought this art was dead, but I see it survives in you."

#### The 1703-1707 Arnstadt years

In August 1703 Bach was appointed organist of the Neue Kirche in Arnstadt, on the strength of his examination of the church's new organ (the _Wender_ organ, named for its builder Johann Friedrich Wender). The post paid 50 gulden plus accommodation; Bach was eighteen years old; it was his first paid musical employment. He held the post for just over four years.

The Arnstadt years are interesting biographically for two reasons. First, they include the Capriccio (composed there about 1704). Second, they include the famous 1705-06 Lübeck trip.

In October 1705 Bach requested four weeks of leave from his Arnstadt duties to travel to Lübeck, about 250 miles to the north, to hear Dietrich Buxtehude (c. 1637-1707), then the most celebrated organist in northern Germany. Buxtehude was organist at the Marienkirche in Lübeck and ran an annual _Abendmusik_ series of public concerts in the church on the Sunday afternoons before Christmas, a series that drew musicians from across Germany and beyond. Bach traveled to Lübeck (on foot, according to the standard account; the distance is consistent with a journey of about two weeks each way) to attend the November-December 1705 Abendmusiken.

Bach stayed in Lübeck for about three months, well beyond the four weeks he had been granted. He returned to Arnstadt in February 1706 to find the consistory waiting. The minutes of the February 21, 1706 consistory hearing survive in the Arnstadt church archives; they record the consistory's reprimand for the over-long absence and also (interestingly) a separate complaint that Bach was introducing "strange and remarkable variations" into the chorales he played, "mixing many strange notes into them" and "confusing the congregation." The complaints suggest that Bach's organ playing was becoming too elaborate for the conservative Arnstadt congregation; he was bringing back from Lübeck a more flamboyant, more contrapuntally adventurous style that did not suit a small parish church. The consistory also raised a separate issue, that Bach had been observed making music with a "strange maiden" (eine fremde Jungfer) in the church's choir gallery, but the matter was apparently dropped without resolution. (The "strange maiden" was almost certainly his cousin Maria Barbara Bach, whom he would marry the following year.)

Bach stayed at Arnstadt for another year-and-a-half but the relationship had soured. In summer 1707 he accepted the organist post at the Blasiuskirche in Mühlhausen, about 50 miles to the north.

#### The 1707-1708 Mühlhausen year

The Mühlhausen tenure lasted barely a year but contained two important events: the marriage to Maria Barbara, and the composition of the first surviving cantatas.

Bach married Maria Barbara Bach (1684-1720) on October 17, 1707, in the village of Dornheim (about 6 miles from Arnstadt). Maria Barbara was Bach's first cousin once removed, her father Johann Michael Bach (1648-1694) was a brother of Bach's grandfather Christoph Bach, making Maria Barbara and Sebastian related but at sufficient genealogical distance that the marriage was canonically permitted. Maria Barbara had been raised in Arnstadt; she was the "strange maiden" of the 1706 consistory complaint. The marriage was apparently happy and produced seven children, four of whom survived past infancy (including Wilhelm Friedemann Bach and Carl Philipp Emanuel Bach, both of whom would become major composers in their own right). Maria Barbara died suddenly in July 1720 while Bach was traveling with the Köthen court at Carlsbad; she was thirty-five. Bach would marry Anna Magdalena Wilcke in December 1721, eighteen months after Maria Barbara's death.

The Mühlhausen year also includes the composition of the earliest surviving Bach cantatas, among them BWV 4 "Christ lag in Todesbanden" (the chorale cantata that Chapter 11 will anchor on) and BWV 71 "Gott ist mein König" (a 1708 ceremonial cantata for the Mühlhausen town-council inauguration, one of the few Bach cantatas printed during his lifetime). The cantatas show Bach already in command of the Lutheran chorale-cantata genre at age 22-23; the BWV 4 in particular is regarded as one of the masterworks of the early cantata literature, despite its composer's youth.

In June 1708 Bach resigned the Mühlhausen post (citing in his resignation letter the "vexation and reluctance" of the local church politics, Mühlhausen at the time was riven by a controversy between Orthodox Lutheran and Pietist factions, with Bach's church on the Orthodox side and the Pietists hostile to elaborate church music) and accepted the post of court organist at the ducal court of Weimar. The Weimar appointment, which would last from July 1708 until December 1717, takes us out of the early period and into the chapter that follows.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The Capriccio connects forward to several later works in this volume.

To the _Passacaglia and Fugue in C minor_ BWV 582 (Chapter 6). The Capriccio's lament with descending-bass-line and ornamented melodic line above is a small-scale exercise in the same compositional principle as the Passacaglia's much-larger 20-variation structure over an 8-bar bass theme. The lament tradition with ground-bass support is the through-line.

To the Crucifixus of the _B Minor Mass_ (Chapter 14). The Crucifixus uses a descending chromatic bass that is a more elaborate cousin of the Capriccio's descending-tetrachord lament; the late G-minor Goldberg "Black Pearl" (Variation 25, Chapter 17) is the keyboard-late-period sibling. Same compositional family across three works; vastly different emotional weight.

To the fugues of the _Well-Tempered Clavier_ Book 1 (Chapter 10). The Capriccio's closing fugue is the youngest specimen of Bach's fugal writing in the volume; the WTC fugues represent the mature working-out of the form. Comparing the Capriccio fugue (clear, audible, simple) with the WTC C-major fugue or the WTC B♭-minor fugue (denser, more contrapuntally elaborate, more harmonically adventurous) gives you a sense of the eighteen years of compositional development between them.

The Capriccio is the earliest example in the volume of both the fugue thread and the ground-bass / passacaglia / chaconne family that Chapter 20 will collect.

#### A judgment on the Capriccio's importance

The Capriccio BWV 992 has been anchored in this chapter not because it is among the best Bach works — it is not — but because it gives the listener a concrete encounter with a specific moment in Bach's early development. The lament section is genuinely beautiful and represents the descending-tetrachord device Bach would carry through his career. The post-horn imitation and the closing fugue are competent young-composer work that does not push the form. A reader who comes to the Capriccio expecting one of Bach's masterworks will be disappointed; the Capriccio is not in the class of the Cello Suites or the Goldbergs. What it is, is the earliest substantial surviving Bach keyboard work, with explicit programmatic captions, providing a documentary anchor for the listening discipline the rest of the volume builds. Anchoring it here is a pedagogical choice that some volumes about Bach might skip; the choice is defensible, but readers should know what they are and are not getting. The mature voice begins to come into focus in Chapter 5 with the Weimar organ works; the Capriccio is the apprentice still finding it.

#### The biographical context: 1685-1708 in summary

Bach's first twenty-three years are an apprenticeship of extraordinary thoroughness. He absorbed the South German keyboard tradition through his elder brother and the Pachelbel inheritance. He absorbed the North German organ tradition through Georg Böhm in Lüneburg and Reincken in Hamburg and Buxtehude in Lübeck. He absorbed the Italian-concerto tradition (via Weimar later, but its seeds were planted in his early studies). He absorbed the Lutheran chorale tradition through his family's deep involvement in church music going back generations. He composed his first substantial keyboard works (the Capriccio among them) and his first surviving cantatas (BWV 4, BWV 71). He took two organist posts and held both successfully (with the Arnstadt incident notwithstanding). He married. He was twenty-three years old and ready for the Weimar appointment that would, over the next nine years, see him grow from a competent young organist into the unmistakable master of the organ repertoire.

Chapter 4 will turn from biography to musical context, the German Baroque inheritance Bach was working within, and specifically the Buxtehude question (what the young Bach found at Lübeck in 1705 and what he carried back with him). Chapter 5 will then enter the Weimar years themselves and the great organ output.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The chapter notes that the standard biographical anchor for the Capriccio, Johann Jakob Bach's 1704 departure for the Swedish royal guard, traces partly to late-eighteenth and nineteenth-century reception rather than to Bach's own surviving statements. Modern scholarship treats the specific biographical anchor cautiously. How does the strength or weakness of biographical evidence change the way you listen to a piece of program music? Does the Capriccio's emotional arc still work if you do not know which brother or which year prompted it?"_

* _"The Capriccio is structured as six sections of contrasting character, conversational, agitated, lamenting, farewell-giving, post-horn-imitating, fugal, played in succession. The work tells a small narrative. Most of Bach's mature music does not use this explicit programmatic structure; he largely abandoned the narrative-keyboard genre after his early years. Why might a young composer be drawn to program music, and why might a mature composer move away from it? What does narrative impose on instrumental music, and what does it offer?"_

* _"The lament section (around 3:00-5:30 in Gould's recording) uses a descending bass line that repeats throughout. The same compositional device, a descending bass under an elaborate melodic line, drives the Crucifixus of the B Minor Mass (Chapter 14), parts of the Goldberg Variations (Chapter 17), and the chaconne of the D-minor violin Partita (Chapter 9). Why might a fixed repeating bass be a particularly effective vehicle for lament or for variation? What does the repetition do for the listener?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Glenn Gould 1979 CBS recording of the Capriccio BWV 992 (or one of the alternatives listed in §3.1). Listen to the Capriccio a second time and attend to these four concrete moments.

  1. **The six section transitions.** Make a small written note of when, by your ear, each new section begins. Compare your notes with the timestamps in §3.2. You should be able to register the transitions even on a first careful listen; the character-changes between sections are large enough to be unmissable once you are listening for them.

  2. **The lament's descending bass (about 3:00-5:30).** Listen for the bass line specifically. Try to follow the descending pattern as it repeats with small variations. The pattern is what gives the lament its sustained grief, the constant downward motion underneath the elaborated melody above. This is the descending-tetrachord-lament device that the chapter discussed in §3.3.

  3. **The post-horn (about 6:30-7:30).** Listen for the rapid repeated-note figure in the right hand. The figure is the most pictorial moment in the Capriccio; once you have heard it once, you will recognize it instantly on subsequent hearings. Notice also how the post-horn figure becomes the basis of the closing fugue's subject, the same musical idea reappears in section 6.

  4. **The closing fugue's subject entries (about 7:30-8:00).** Listen for the entrances of the four fugue voices. The first voice states the subject; the second voice enters with the subject transposed up (the "answer"); the third and fourth voices follow. Gould's clear articulation of the polyphonic lines makes the entries unusually easy to follow. Try to count the entrances: how many of the four can you hear distinctly? This is the first fugue-listening exercise in the volume; the skill will be developed further in Chapter 10 (the WTC) and Chapter 19 (the Art of Fugue).

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : a Kuhnau _Biblical History_ : for example _La Battaglia di David e Goliath_ ("The Battle of David and Goliath") from the 1700 collection _Musicalische Vorstellung einiger Biblischer Historien_. Recordings by John Butt (Harmonia Mundi 1990s), Robert Hill (Hänssler 1990s), or Glen Wilson (Naxos 2000s) are available. The Kuhnau pieces are the closest pre-Bach parallel to the Capriccio's programmatic-keyboard genre; hearing one of them gives you the genre context the Capriccio was working within. The Kuhnau collection was published in 1700, four years before Bach's Capriccio.

**Listen** (comparison): a Buxtehude organ work: for example the Praeludium in G minor BuxWV 149 that Chapter 4 will use as the anchor for the German Baroque inheritance. Hans Davidsson (BIS 2000s), Helmut Walcha (Archiv 1959-71), or Ton Koopman (Challenge Classics 2000s) are well-recorded versions. Hearing Buxtehude before Chapter 4 gives you the music the young Bach traveled 250 miles each way to hear; you can register what the eighteen-year-old Bach found at Lübeck and what he brought back to his Arnstadt organ-playing.

**Read** : Karl Geiringer, _The Bach Family: Seven Generations of Creative Genius_ (Oxford University Press, 1954). The standard reference for the Bach family-musician dynasty, beginning with Veit Bach (c. 1550-1619) and tracing the family through six generations to Johann Sebastian, then forward through his sons (Wilhelm Friedemann, Carl Philipp Emanuel, Johann Christian) to the family's gradual fading-out in the late eighteenth century. About 500 pages. The book is dated in some scholarly particulars but remains the most thorough single-volume treatment of the family context; the chapters on Eisenach and Ohrdruf are particularly relevant to the present chapter.

**Read** (alternative, shorter): Malcolm Boyd's _Bach_ (Master Musicians, Oxford 2000, third edition), chapter 1 and chapter 2. About fifty pages covering the years to 1708. Boyd's treatment is compact and cautious where the documentary record is thin. A good first read if Geiringer is too dense.

---

## Chapter 4: Early Bach in Context, the German Baroque Inheritance and the Buxtehude Question

When the eighteen-year-old Bach walked from Arnstadt to Lübeck in the autumn of 1705 to hear Dietrich Buxtehude play the organ, he was not making an arbitrary cultural-tourism choice. He was traveling to the center of the most important organ-playing tradition in northern Europe to study with the seventy-year-old composer whose work was the touchstone of the _stylus phantasticus_ , the free-rhapsodic improvisatory style that defined North German organ music at the turn of the eighteenth century. The trip was a deliberate professional pilgrimage by a young organist who knew what he wanted to learn and where to find it.

This chapter sets up the German Baroque musical world Bach inherited. To follow Bach's mature work, the great Weimar organ pieces of Chapter 5 and Chapter 6, the cantatas of Chapter 11-12, the Brandenburg Concertos of Chapter 8, you need to know what was in the air around him as he grew up and as he made his early professional choices. Bach did not arrive at his mature voice through some private inspiration; he arrived at it by absorbing a specific set of musical traditions and combining them. The three principal traditions he combined were the North German organ school (with Buxtehude as the central figure), the South German keyboard tradition (Pachelbel as the central figure), and the Italian instrumental concerto (Vivaldi as the central figure, though Bach's exposure to Italian music began earlier through Corelli and others). The Lutheran chorale tradition underlay all three.

This chapter anchors on one Buxtehude organ work, the Praeludium in G minor BuxWV 149, that represents the North German style at full force, with brief contextual treatment of Pachelbel and Reincken alongside. By the end of the chapter you will have a sense of what the young Bach absorbed at Lübeck in 1705 and what he carried into the Weimar years that follow.

### 4.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Lübeck, late November 1705. The Marienkirche, the principal Lutheran church of the Hanseatic city, holds its annual _Abendmusik_ , "evening music", series in the long Sunday afternoons of late autumn before Advent. The Abendmusiken were a Lübeck institution: free public concerts in the Marienkirche on Sunday afternoons from late November to Christmas, organized and largely composed by the church's organist, drawing audiences of merchants, civic officials, visiting musicians, and curious townspeople. Buxtehude had been the Marienkirche organist since 1668, by 1705 he had held the post for 37 years. The Abendmusiken under his direction had become the most-celebrated public-music institution in northern Germany; musicians traveled from across Germany, Denmark, and the Netherlands to attend.

Bach is among the audience that autumn. He has walked from Arnstadt, 250 miles or so, mostly on foot, with intermediate stops, to hear what the great Buxtehude does at his own organ in his own church. He has presumably attended the early November Abendmusiken (two large-scale extended cantata-oratorio works, _Castrum Doloris_ BuxWV 134 and _Templum Honoris_ BuxWV 135, composed for the December 1705 commemoration of Emperor Leopold I's death and the accession of Joseph I); he has attended Buxtehude's organ playing at the regular Sunday services; he has spent time, perhaps, with Buxtehude personally in the organ loft.

Buxtehude in 1705 is sixty-eight years old. He has just over a year to live (he will die in May 1707). He is looking for a successor at the Marienkirche; the standard arrangement at the time involved the incoming organist marrying the outgoing organist's eldest unmarried daughter as a kind of dowry-and-succession arrangement. (George Frideric Handel had visited Lübeck three years earlier in 1703 with the same possibility in mind and decided against the arrangement; Buxtehude's eldest unmarried daughter Anna Margreta was then about 30.) Whether Buxtehude offered Bach the same arrangement, and whether Bach considered it, the documentary record does not say. Bach was twenty in 1705 and had not yet married Maria Barbara; the timing would have made the proposition theoretically open to him. What we know is that Bach extended his Arnstadt leave from the four authorized weeks to about sixteen weeks, returned to Arnstadt in February 1706, and did not become Buxtehude's successor, the Lübeck post went to Johann Christian Schieferdecker in mid-1707.

What Bach absorbed at Lübeck across those sixteen weeks shaped his subsequent organ playing. The Arnstadt consistory's February 1706 complaint that he was introducing "strange and remarkable variations" into the chorales he played, "mixing many strange notes into them," is a direct reflection of what he had absorbed at Lübeck. The North German style, free, rhapsodic, chromatically adventurous, contrapuntally dense, was incompatible with the conservative Arnstadt congregation's expectations for plain hymn accompaniment. Bach was bringing back not just techniques but a whole conception of what an organist's role could be; the conception would, over the next decade at Weimar, settle into the great Bach organ works of Chapter 5 and Chapter 6.

The Praeludium in G minor BuxWV 149, the anchor work of this chapter, is a representative example of what the young Bach would have heard Buxtehude play at the Marienkirche in 1705. The work is in five sections, in the _stylus phantasticus_ tradition: a free toccata-like opening; a strict fugue; a more sectional free passage; a second fugue; a closing free passage. The whole runs about ten minutes. It is the kind of piece Buxtehude played at the Abendmusiken's organ-recital portions, and the kind of piece Bach would write his own versions of in his Weimar years.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The free-rhapsodic opening (the toccata gestures).** The work opens with what sound like spontaneous improvisations, running scales, sudden chordal pauses, sudden silences, dramatic gestures. This is the _stylus phantasticus_ in action: deliberately unpredictable, deliberately rhetorical, deliberately closer to a speaker's rhetorical phrasing than to a strict musical form. Compare it to the opening of the _Toccata and Fugue in D minor_ BWV 565 if you have heard the BWV 565 from _Close Listening: Classical Music_ Chapter 7, Bach's opening gestures (the chord-then-flourish-then-silence pattern) are direct descendants of Buxtehude's North German practice. Listen to where Buxtehude pauses; listen to how each new gesture seems to surprise the one before; listen to how the music seems to be thinking aloud rather than executing a plan.

  2. **The fugues (the strict middle sections).** Within the five-section structure, two of the sections are strict fugues, a fugue subject is announced and answered in successive voices, building up the polyphonic texture in the manner Chapter 3's Capriccio closing fugue introduced. Listen to where the fugues begin; the change from free toccata-gestures into strict imitative-counterpoint is unmissable. The first fugue (around the middle of the work) is built on a relatively quick subject; the second (later in the work) is built on a more sustained subject.

  3. **The pedal line.** The organ has three keyboards, two manual keyboards (played by the hands) and a pedalboard (played by the feet). The pedalboard provides the bass line of the texture. In a Buxtehude or Bach organ work the pedal line is not merely accompanimental; it is an independent voice as important as any of the hand-played voices. Listen for the pedal line specifically, it is the deepest sound in the texture, often a slow-moving bass line under the more active hand-played figures, occasionally taking a turn at the foreground with its own running passage. The pedal is what gives organ music its weight; learning to hear it as a voice (rather than as background) is part of the listening discipline this chapter is building.

#### One-sentence background

Composed by Dietrich Buxtehude in the early 1700s (precise date uncertain, his organ works survive mostly in manuscripts copied by his pupils and later associates; the chronology is not settled), a five-section organ praeludium in G minor in the North German _stylus phantasticus_ tradition, about 10 minutes; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Ton Koopman playing the historic Schnitger organ at the Stadtkirche St. Cosmae in Stade, Germany (Challenge Classics, recorded 2005, released as part of Koopman's complete Buxtehude organ-works cycle), chosen for the period instrument, the well-engineered acoustic, and Koopman's idiomatic stylus phantasticus phrasing.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Buxtehude Praeludium G minor BuxWV 149 Koopman" or "Buxtehude organ G minor BuxWV 149" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Helmut Walcha on Archiv (recorded 1947-1952 mono and 1956-1971 stereo, Walcha recorded the complete Buxtehude organ works as part of his broader project covering the German Baroque organ repertoire), the canonical mid-twentieth-century North German organ reading; the blind Walcha's pedalwork was famous for its precision.
* Hans Davidsson on BIS (recorded 1990s-2000s, period instruments at various North German Baroque organs), the contemporary HIP standard for Buxtehude; Davidsson is the leading current scholar-organist of the North German tradition.
* Christopher Herrick on Hyperion (recorded 1990s-2000s, various organs across Europe), a well-recorded modern alternative; Herrick is the leading British Bach-and-Buxtehude organist.

### 4.2 The Listening Window

This section is for your second listen, the one where you know what is coming and can attend to specific moments. Timestamps below refer to Koopman's 2005 Challenge Classics recording. The Praeludium in his reading runs about 9 minutes 30 seconds. If you are listening to a different recording, the section-by-section structure still applies but the exact clock times will differ; use the section descriptions rather than the timestamps where necessary.

**0:00-1:15: Section 1 (free toccata opening).** The work opens with a series of dramatic gestures, a rolling-arpeggio figure, a sudden chord, a silence, another running figure, another chord, a brief contrapuntal moment, a return to chordal punctuation. This is the _stylus phantasticus_ in its purest form: deliberately unpredictable in rhythm, with rhetorical pauses, with sudden harmonic shifts. Notice the registration (the choice of organ stops): Koopman uses a bright, clear registration for the opening that lets the rapid figuration register clearly. Notice the pedal: the bass line provides the harmonic foundation but does not dominate; the hands carry the foreground figuration.

**About 1:15-2:30: Transition into Section 2 (the first fugue).** The free toccata gestures gradually settle into a more regular rhythmic figuration. A single voice in the right hand introduces what will become the fugue subject, a moderately quick figure, perhaps eight bars long, that ends in a clear cadence. Listen for the moment of subject statement; once you have heard it, you will recognize it when other voices take it up. The subject's character is moderately quick, somewhat angular, with a clear rhythmic pattern.

**About 2:30-3:45: The first fugue's development.** As the subject is taken up by successive voices, the texture becomes denser. A second voice enters with the subject transposed; a third voice; a fourth (if the fugue is in four voices, Buxtehude's fugues vary in the number of voices). The pedal joins the texture, often taking up the subject in the bass. The fugue develops the subject through a series of entries and episodes (the episodes are the contrapuntal passages between subject-entries, in which the voices play freely-derived material rather than the subject itself). The whole develops over about a minute and a quarter. Listen for the pedal's entries with the subject, they are usually the most dramatic of the fugue's voice-statements because the pedal can give the subject the weight of the bass register.

**About 3:45-5:00: Section 3 (a returning free passage).** After the first fugue closes, the music returns to a free, more sectional character, not as wildly improvisatory as the opening toccata but freer than the strict fugue. The hands play more elaborate figuration; the pedal carries a slower-moving line underneath; the harmony is more adventurous than in the fugue. This middle free passage is the _stylus phantasticus_ tradition's way of separating two fugues, a deliberate change of texture to refresh the listener's ear before the second fugue begins.

**About 5:00-7:00: Section 4 (the second fugue).** A new fugue subject is introduced, distinct from the first fugue's subject, often slower or with a different rhythmic character. The second fugue develops similarly to the first but on its own subject. Listen for the moment of the new subject's statement; you should be able to distinguish the second fugue from the first by ear because the subjects are different musical ideas. Koopman articulates the polyphonic voices clearly enough that you can usually follow at least three of the four voices as the second fugue develops.

**About 7:00-9:30: Section 5 (the closing free passage and the final cadence).** The work closes with another return to the _stylus phantasticus_ , dramatic gestures, chordal punctuation, running figures, perhaps a brief pedal solo, and a final cadence with a long sustained final chord. The pedal often takes a foreground role in this closing section, with long-held bass notes that anchor the harmony as the hands trace elaborate figures above. The final cadence is on G minor (the home key) but with the closing chord usually a G major chord (the _Picardy third_ , the convention of ending a minor-key piece on a major-mode tonic chord, an inheritance from sixteenth-century practice).

If your recording's total time differs from Koopman's 9:30 by more than 25%, you may be hearing a substantially different performance approach, Walcha's reading runs closer to 11 minutes (slower tempos, more dramatic pauses); Davidsson's runs about 9 minutes; some shorter readings tighten the free sections to about 8 minutes. The five-section structure should be clear in every reading; the proportional shape of the work is consistent across performance traditions.

### 4.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The stylus phantasticus

_Stylus phantasticus_ , "fantastical style", is a Latin term for a deliberately free, improvisatory, rhetorical instrumental style developed in seventeenth-century Italian and German keyboard music. The term originates with Athanasius Kircher (1602-1680), the Jesuit polymath whose 1650 _Musurgia Universalis_ , a massive treatise on music theory, codified the term. Kircher described the _stylus phantasticus_ as instrumental music designed to display the musician's ingenuity through unpredictable rhythms, sudden gestures, contrasting sections, and dramatic effects. The style was distinguished from the more strictly-organized stylus ecclesiasticus (the church style, fugue, motet) and stylus cubicularis (the chamber style, sonata, suite).

The North German organ tradition that Buxtehude inherited from his teachers (Franz Tunder, his predecessor at Lübeck; Heinrich Scheidemann; Jan Pieterszoon Sweelinck via his pupils) was the principal home of the _stylus phantasticus_ in late-seventeenth-century practice. The free praeludium with its alternation of toccata-gestures and strict fugues was the standard genre; Buxtehude's roughly twenty surviving praeludia are the genre's canonical examples; the Praeludium BuxWV 149 in G minor is one of the most-played.

The young Bach absorbed the style in person at Lübeck in 1705. He would deploy it across his own organ output, but characteristically transformed. The _Toccata and Fugue in D minor_ BWV 565 (Chapter 5) is in the _stylus phantasticus_ tradition (whether or not it is actually by Bach, the attribution question is open). The great Weimar preludes and fugues (Chapter 5) and the _Passacaglia and Fugue in C minor_ BWV 582 (Chapter 6) are _stylus phantasticus_ descendants. But Bach's mature versions tend to fold the free sections more tightly into the work's overall architecture, the free toccata is shorter, the strict fugue is longer and contrapuntally denser, the whole feels more architecturally planned than Buxtehude's freer alternation. The Buxtehude is more truly improvisatory in feel; the Bach is more strictly constructed even within the free idiom.

For the listener: when you hear a Buxtehude praeludium, the sense of "this music could go anywhere next" is the _stylus phantasticus_ working as designed. When you hear a Bach toccata, the same gestures are present but the music is more clearly heading toward a destination. Compare BuxWV 149 with the BWV 565 opening (which you encountered in _Close Listening: Classical Music_ Chapter 7) and you can hear the difference: same vocabulary, different sense of architectural inevitability.

#### The North German organ-school tradition

The North German organ school is the eighteenth-century musicological term for the tradition of organist-composers active in the Hanseatic and Baltic-Sea cities (Hamburg, Lübeck, Bremen, Stade, Lüneburg, Hamburg again, Stockholm) from the early seventeenth to the early eighteenth centuries. The principal figures are:

* **Jan Pieterszoon Sweelinck** (1562-1621, Amsterdam, technically Dutch but the teacher of most of the North German first generation), the founder figure.
* **Heinrich Scheidemann** (c. 1595-1663, Hamburg), Sweelinck's principal North German pupil; teacher of the next generation.
* **Franz Tunder** (1614-1667, Lübeck), Buxtehude's predecessor at the Marienkirche; founder of the Lübeck Abendmusik tradition.
* **Johann Adam Reincken** (1623-1722, Hamburg, the Catharinenkirche), the senior organist Bach traveled to hear from Lüneburg in 1700-1702 and again in 1720.
* **Dietrich Buxtehude** (c. 1637-1707, Lübeck), the central figure for Bach.
* **Georg Böhm** (1661-1733, Lüneburg, the Johanniskirche), Bach's likely informal teacher in Lüneburg 1700-1702.
* **Nicolaus Bruhns** (1665-1697, Husum), a younger near-contemporary; died young; admired by Bach.
* **Vincent Lübeck** (1654-1740, Hamburg), one of the longer-living members of the tradition.

The school's defining features were: the _stylus phantasticus_ approach to the praeludium genre; the central role of the chorale prelude (chorale settings for organ as preludes to the congregational chorale singing); the use of the elaborate North German Baroque organ instrument (the great organs built by Arp Schnitger and his contemporaries, with multiple keyboards and pedalboards and many ranks of pipes); and the integration of Lutheran chorales as compositional material across the organ output.

Bach absorbed the entire tradition. The chorale-prelude practice he would develop in the Weimar _Orgelbüchlein_ (Chapter 6) is a direct inheritance from the North German tradition. The _stylus phantasticus_ praeludium he would extend into the great Weimar preludes and fugues is a direct inheritance from Buxtehude and Reincken. The pedal-virtuoso tradition (the use of the pedalboard as an independent voice carrying its own subjects and passagework, not merely as bass-line accompaniment) is a North German specialty Bach carried into his own organ writing, the _Passacaglia and Fugue in C minor_ BWV 582 has one of the most pedal-virtuoso passages in the literature.

#### The South German keyboard tradition (Pachelbel)

The South German keyboard school is the parallel tradition active in southern German Catholic and mixed-confessional regions (Nuremberg, Munich, Vienna, Salzburg). The principal figures are Johann Jakob Froberger (1616-1667), Johann Caspar Kerll (1627-1693), Johann Kuhnau (1660-1722, Leipzig, technically central rather than southern but stylistically aligned with the southern tradition), and Johann Pachelbel (1653-1706, Nuremberg).

The South German tradition is generally more melodically simple, more harmonically clear, and contrapuntally more transparent than the North German tradition. Where North German organ music tends toward density and rhapsodic freedom, South German keyboard music tends toward clarity and structural regularity. Pachelbel's Canon and Gigue in D major (the Baroque-warmup anchor in _Close Listening: Classical Music_ Chapter 7) is the most-famous example of the South German tradition's reach into popular consciousness; less well-known but compositionally more important are Pachelbel's chorale preludes and his fugues on the _Magnificat_ tones.

Pachelbel was the teacher of Bach's elder brother Johann Christoph Bach (1671-1721, in Ohrdruf). Bach received his first systematic keyboard instruction in the Pachelbel tradition through this brother (Chapter 3 §3.4). The South German tradition's preference for clarity-of-line and structural regularity is something Bach carried alongside the North German freedom-and-rhapsody; his mature voice combines both inheritances. The _Goldberg Variations_ (Chapter 17) draw more on the South German tradition of clear, transparent texture and strict variation-discipline; the great preludes and fugues at Weimar (Chapter 5) draw more on the North German rhapsodic-fugal practice.

#### The Italian-concerto tradition

The third major tradition Bach absorbed, beyond the German organ school and the German keyboard school, was the Italian instrumental concerto. The principal Italian composers were Arcangelo Corelli (1653-1713, Rome), Giuseppe Torelli (1658-1709, Bologna), and especially Antonio Vivaldi (1678-1741, Venice). Vivaldi's _L'estro armonico_ op. 3 (a set of twelve concertos published 1711 in Amsterdam) was the breakout work that brought the Vivaldian concerto-grosso form (with the strong tutti-soloist alternation, the clear ritornello structure, the major-key brightness) to European attention.

Bach's exposure to Italian instrumental music began in his early studies (Reincken's _Hortus Musicus_ that the young Bach copied at Lüneburg is heavily Italian-influenced) but intensified at Weimar (Chapter 5). Around 1713-14, at Weimar, Bach transcribed about 16 Vivaldi concertos for solo keyboard (six for organ, BWV 593, 594, 596 among them, and ten for harpsichord, BWV 972-987). The transcriptions were not merely arrangements: they were Bach's way of studying Vivaldi's compositional methods in detail by rewriting the works in a different medium. The Italian-concerto practices Bach absorbed through these transcriptions, clear ritornello structures, strong rhythmic drive, brightly-major-key affect, melodic-harmonic clarity, became part of his own subsequent writing, especially in the Brandenburg Concertos (Chapter 8) and the cantata opening choruses.

The Italian influence and the North German influence are not in conflict, they combined in Bach's mature voice. The Brandenburg Concertos are Italianate concertos infused with German contrapuntal density. The Weimar organ works are German _stylus phantasticus_ praeludia infused with Italian clarity-of-structure. The great cantata opening choruses (Chapter 12) are works in which all three traditions, German organ-school grandeur, German chorale-prelude texture, Italian concerto-grosso drive, combine in single movements.

#### The Lutheran chorale tradition (underlying all three)

Underneath the three named traditions (North German organ, South German keyboard, Italian concerto) ran the Lutheran chorale tradition. The Lutheran chorale was the hymn-genre Martin Luther (1483-1546) established in the 1520s and 1530s with his own settings of paraphrased psalms and new-composed sacred songs ("Ein feste Burg ist unser Gott" from 1529 is the most-famous example). By Bach's birth in 1685 the Lutheran chorale repertoire had accreted to several hundred standard tunes, collected and codified in chorale-books published in the seventeenth century by Johann Crüger (1598-1662, the _Praxis pietatis melica_ of 1647 and subsequent editions), Paul Gerhardt (1607-1676, the great German hymn-text poet whose texts Crüger and others set), and others.

The chorale tradition was central to North German and South German keyboard music alike. The chorale prelude, an organ work that takes a chorale tune as its compositional material, settings it in various polyphonic styles, was a standard genre Buxtehude wrote extensively, Pachelbel wrote extensively, and Bach himself would develop further in the Weimar _Orgelbüchlein_ (Chapter 6). The chorale cantata, a vocal work that takes a chorale as its material, was the genre Bach would develop into one of his major lifelong outputs (Chapter 11, BWV 4 as the early example; Chapter 12, BWV 140 as the mature example).

Underneath everything, the chorale tradition was the German-Lutheran shared musical language. A Lutheran congregant in 1705 Lübeck would have known by heart the dozen or two dozen most-common chorales (Luther's "Ein feste Burg" and "Vom Himmel hoch" and "Christ lag in Todesbanden"; Gerhardt's "O Haupt voll Blut und Wunden"; and so on). When the congregation heard a chorale prelude before the chorale was sung, they would have recognized the chorale immediately and oriented to the prelude's elaborate setting from a base of familiarity. When they heard a chorale-cantata movement that began with the chorale's opening line, they would have been ready to recognize the chorale's identity from the first phrase.

This base of shared chorale-knowledge is one of the things modern listeners do not bring to Bach by default. The Lutheran chorales that were the shared cultural vocabulary of Bach's congregants in Mühlhausen, Weimar, and Leipzig are not the shared cultural vocabulary of most modern Bach listeners. Chapter 16 will provide an orientation; for now, register that when Bach uses a chorale tune in a chorale prelude or in a cantata movement, he is using a musical idea his original audience knew by heart, and the work's meaning is partly built on that recognition.

### 4.4 The Stories

#### Buxtehude at Lübeck: a portrait

Dietrich Buxtehude was born around 1637, the exact date is uncertain, probably in Helsingør in Denmark or Helsingborg in Sweden (both towns sit across the Øresund strait; Buxtehude's family moved between them). He was the son of a German-Danish organist; he received his musical training in the family and in nearby church-music establishments. By his late twenties he had taken organist posts in Helsingør and Helsingborg; in 1668 he succeeded his future father-in-law Franz Tunder as organist at the Marienkirche in Lübeck and married Tunder's daughter Anna Margaretha. (The succession-by-daughter-marriage was a common arrangement in German Lutheran organist posts; Buxtehude's later attempt to arrange the same kind of succession for himself, through his eldest unmarried daughter, is the same convention in operation.)

Lübeck in 1668 was one of the most prosperous Hanseatic-League cities, a north-German trading metropolis on the Baltic with a wealthy merchant class and substantial civic-and-religious patronage. The Marienkirche was the city's principal church, built in the fourteenth century, with a celebrated Romantic-Gothic interior, and home to a large multi-manual organ (the so-called "Großorgel", Great Organ, and a separate smaller organ for the choir). Buxtehude as organist had two principal duties: providing organ accompaniment and chorale-prelude playing for the Sunday services, and organizing the annual Abendmusik concert series.

The Abendmusiken (which Tunder had begun and Buxtehude expanded) were unusual in early-modern German music: they were free public concerts in a church, not connected to a regular service, drawing audiences from outside the church's regular congregation. The repertoire included Buxtehude's own large-scale extended cantatas and oratorios, instrumental music, and the organ works for which Buxtehude is now best-remembered. The series attracted attention across Germany; visiting musicians (including Handel in 1703 and Bach in 1705) made the pilgrimage to Lübeck specifically to attend.

Buxtehude's compositional output is substantial: about 120 vocal cantatas (most surviving in manuscript collections compiled by his pupils and copyists, including the so-called Düben Collection in Uppsala that preserves many works otherwise lost); about 90 organ works (praeludia, chorale preludes, ciaconas and passacaglias, free fantasias); a smaller body of instrumental ensemble works (trio sonatas, larger ensemble pieces); and the two large-scale Abendmusik works of 1705 (_Castrum Doloris_ and _Templum Honoris_ for the imperial-succession commemoration) that Bach almost certainly heard in person. Buxtehude died in May 1707 at about age seventy.

#### What the young Bach learned at Lübeck

What Bach specifically took from his Lübeck stay is hard to document directly, we have no diary, no letters, no contemporary record of what he heard or what he discussed with Buxtehude. The Wolff biography pieces the picture together from indirect evidence: the changes in Bach's organ-playing style on his return (the Arnstadt consistory's February 1706 complaints); the works Bach composed in the years after (the early-Weimar organ works show traces of Buxtehude's influence in figuration patterns and harmonic-rhetorical gestures); the later testimony of Bach's pupils (collected in C. P. E. Bach's account of his father's music for Forkel's 1802 biography).

The picture that emerges is: Bach absorbed the _stylus phantasticus_ praeludium genre as a model for his own free-improvisatory writing; he absorbed the pedal-virtuoso practice of the North German tradition; he absorbed Buxtehude's specific approach to chorale-prelude settings (the various polyphonic textures available, cantus firmus in the soprano, cantus firmus in the pedal, ornamental cantus firmus, fughetta on chorale phrases); and he absorbed the broader cultural-professional model of the organist-composer-impresario as a major figure in civic musical life (Buxtehude's running of the Abendmusiken was an institutional model Bach would later draw on at Leipzig with the _Collegium Musicum_ he directed from 1729). The specific compositional debts are difficult to itemize; the cultural-professional inheritance is unmistakable.

The young Bach in 1705 was not yet a great composer. His surviving works from before 1708, the Capriccio BWV 992 (Chapter 3), the cantata BWV 4, the cantata BWV 71, are very competent but not yet at the level his Weimar years would produce. The Lübeck trip was part of the formation process that took him from "very competent young organist" to "the unmistakable major figure of early-eighteenth-century German keyboard music." Buxtehude was the principal teacher in that formation, even though the teaching was informal and short and possibly entirely by example rather than by explicit instruction.

#### Recording history briefly

For BuxWV 149 specifically: Koopman 2005 (the Primary Recording, period instrument on the historic Schnitger at Stade); Davidsson BIS late 1990s-2000s (the contemporary scholarly-standard HIP cycle); Walcha Archiv 1947-71 (the mid-twentieth-century modern-instrument touchstone). The work is short enough that a curious listener can hear two or three readings in the time it takes to read this chapter; the comparison across recordings shows period-instrument practice against modern-instrument practice on the same piece.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The Buxtehude Praeludium connects forward to many later works in this volume.

To the _Toccata and Fugue in D minor_ BWV 565 (Chapter 5). Whether or not BWV 565 is by Bach, it is squarely in the Buxtehude-Reincken North German _stylus phantasticus_ tradition. The opening gestures (the cascading chord-and-flourish pattern), the dramatic pauses, the alternation of free passages and fugal sections, all directly inherited from the Buxtehude praeludium tradition. The BuxWV 149 you have just heard is essentially the direct ancestor of the BWV 565 of _Close Listening: Classical Music_ Chapter 7. Compare them side by side; the family resemblance is unmissable.

To the _Passacaglia and Fugue in C minor_ BWV 582 (Chapter 6). Buxtehude's ciacona in E minor (BuxWV 160) and passacaglia in D minor (BuxWV 161) are direct compositional ancestors of Bach's BWV 582 — the ground-bass-plus-variations-plus-fugue is a North German organ genre Bach inherited and elevated.

To the chorale tradition in both organ (Orgelbüchlein, Chapter 6) and cantata (BWV 4, Chapter 11). The cantus-firmus chorale-prelude practice and the chorale-cantata genre both descend from the Buxtehude-Pachelbel North German tradition; Bach's mature Weimar preludes-and-fugues (Chapter 5: BWV 543-548, BWV 552 "St. Anne") sit in the same genre with the proportions shifted toward tighter architecture.

Chapter 20 §20.2 will collect the chorale-prelude texture as a unifying thread; Chapter 20 §20.1 will collect the fugue-subject-and-answer device. The Buxtehude Praeludium is the volume's deepest historical anchor for both threads, the North German tradition that Bach inherited and transformed.

#### Brief notes on Pachelbel and Reincken

Two more figures bear brief mention as anchor reference points.

**Johann Pachelbel** (1653-1706). The Canon and Gigue in D major is the most-famous work (cross-reference to _Close Listening: Classical Music_ Chapter 7); but Pachelbel's compositional output beyond the Canon is substantial and historically more important. He composed about 200 chorale preludes (many are short fughettas on chorale phrases: the genre Bach would develop into the _Orgelbüchlein_), about 30 organ fugues on Magnificat tones, several fugue collections (the _Magnificat Fugues_ especially), and other keyboard and ensemble works. Pachelbel taught Johann Christoph Bach of Ohrdruf, who taught J. S. Bach. The South German keyboard tradition Bach absorbed in his Ohrdruf years was Pachelbel's tradition.

**Johann Adam Reincken** (1623-1722). Reincken was the elder Hamburg organist Bach traveled from Lüneburg to hear in 1700-1702. His major surviving organ work is the _Hortus Musicus_ (1687), a collection of instrumental sonatas that Bach copied as a young man (Bach's copy survives in part). Reincken outlived Buxtehude by fifteen years and was 97 years old when Bach visited him in Hamburg in 1720 (Bach was 35; Reincken almost a century). Reincken's reported remark on Bach's playing: "I thought this art was dead, but I see it survives in you", is one of the most-cited recognitions of Bach's mastery by a senior contemporary. The remark is from C. P. E. Bach's account in the 1750 Obituary; treated cautiously, like other primary-source family-pride statements, but consistent with what other evidence suggests.

A reader who wants to explore Pachelbel beyond the Canon should start with the Magnificat Fugues (any of John Butt's Pachelbel recordings will introduce the genre); a reader who wants to explore Reincken should start with the _Hortus Musicus_ (recordings by various Baroque ensemble groups including Musica Antiqua Köln are available).

#### The biographical context: 1705 to 1708

Bach's three-month stay at Lübeck in the autumn of 1705 was a decisive professional formation moment. He returned to Arnstadt in February 1706, served out the remaining year-and-a-half of his Arnstadt tenure (with the consistory's irritation continuing), accepted the Mühlhausen organist post in June 1707 and moved there in July, married Maria Barbara in October 1707, served the Mühlhausen post for less than a year, and accepted the Weimar court-organist appointment in July 1708.
