The Lübeck-to-Weimar period is three years that took Bach from "young Arnstadt organist with North German pretensions" to "Weimar court organist with the resources to develop a major output."

The Weimar appointment in 1708 marks the transition from the apprenticeship of Chapters 3-4 into the first mature compositional period, which Chapter 5 will enter. The biographical thread (Thread A) picks up there.

#### Was Bach a German composer or an international one?

A judgment about Bach's compositional identity: the standard nineteenth-century framing of Bach as the foundational German composer (the Forkel 1802 narrative, the Mendelssohn 1829 revival, the BG edition's nationalist project — see Chapter 22) is misleading about what Bach actually was. By his Weimar period in his late twenties, Bach was working within a deliberately-international synthesis: the North German organ school of Buxtehude and Reincken, the South German keyboard tradition of Pachelbel and Froberger, the Italian instrumental concerto of Vivaldi and Corelli, the French dance-suite tradition of Couperin. His mature voice combines all four traditions in works that no specifically-German composer of his generation could have written. To call this "German" music is to confuse Bach's nationality with his compositional identity. The mature Bach is an international synthesizer working in the lingua franca of early-eighteenth-century European art music; the German-foundational reading is reception history, not biographical fact. The reader who treats the catalogue this way sees a different Bach than the one nineteenth-century cultural nationalism constructed.

### Conversation Seeds

If you have heard a Vivaldi concerto (any of L'estro armonico op. 3 or The Four Seasons would work), a Buxtehude organ praeludium, and a Pachelbel chorale prelude, can you identify which of the three traditions an unfamiliar Bach work is drawing on most heavily by ear? Try this with the Brandenburg Concerto No. 3 (Italian-influenced) and the Toccata and Fugue D minor BWV 565 (Buxtehude-influenced) and notice the difference."_

* _"Buxtehude's praeludium has five sections that alternate between free toccata-gestures and strict fugues. The deliberate alternation between freedom and strictness is the structural principle of the genre. Why might a composer want to alternate these contrasting modes within a single work? What does the free section gain from being surrounded by strict fugue, and what does the strict fugue gain from being surrounded by free toccata? Compare the experience to a piece you know in which one mode dominates throughout, say a Vivaldi concerto first movement (mostly strict ritornello structure) or a free Romantic-era fantasia (mostly free)."_

* _"Bach walked roughly 250 miles each way to Lübeck in 1705 specifically to hear Buxtehude play the organ. The trip lasted about sixteen weeks total, four times the leave he had been granted, and earned him a written reprimand from the Arnstadt consistory on his return. What would have been the equivalent professional pilgrimage in any field today, what kind of trip would you make for sixteen weeks if you were a young professional in your early twenties trying to learn from a master? What does it tell you about the structure of musical knowledge in 1705 that such pilgrimages were necessary, and how does it compare with the modern situation where any major performance is available within minutes via recording or streaming?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Ton Koopman 2005 Challenge Classics recording of the Praeludium in G minor BuxWV 149 (or one of the alternatives listed in §4.1). Listen to the Praeludium a second time and attend to these four concrete moments.

  1. **The opening free toccata (0:00-1:15).** Confirm the _stylus phantasticus_ character: dramatic gestures, sudden pauses, sudden running figures, sudden chordal punctuations. Notice that the music seems to be thinking aloud, each new gesture arrives without obvious preparation from what came before. This is the deliberate rhetorical effect; the music is meant to sound improvisatory even where it is written down.

  2. **The first fugue's subject (about 1:15-1:30).** Listen for the moment of the subject's first statement in a single voice; then listen for the second voice's entrance with the same subject transposed; then the third (and possibly fourth) voice entrance. Try to count the voices that take up the subject; the count tells you whether you are listening to a three-voice or a four-voice fugue.

  3. **The pedal line in the second fugue (around 5:00-7:00).** Listen specifically to the pedal, the lowest sound, played by the organist's feet. When the pedal takes up the fugue subject (it will, at some point during the second fugue's development), the entrance is dramatic because the subject is now in the bass register. Notice the moment of the pedal subject-entry; it is usually one of the most-marked moments in the work.

  4. **The closing pedal point (around 8:30-9:30).** Many Buxtehude praeludia close with a long-held pedal note (a _pedal point_) on the dominant or tonic, over which the hands play closing figuration. The held pedal note creates a sense of arrival and stability. Listen for the moment when the pedal settles onto a sustained note; the hands continue active above; the final cadence approaches. This is the _stylus phantasticus_ tradition's standard closing gesture; you will hear it in Bach's BWV 565 closing and in many of the Weimar preludes and fugues.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : another Buxtehude praeludium: for example the Praeludium in D minor BuxWV 140, or the Praeludium in F♯ minor BuxWV 146, or the Praeludium in E minor BuxWV 142. Same composer, same genre, different keys and slightly different proportions. After hearing two or three Buxtehude praeludia you will have a clearer sense of the genre's range and its conventions. The Koopman or Davidsson cycles will give you the complete output to choose from.

**Listen** (comparison): a Pachelbel chorale prelude: for example any of the chorale preludes Pachelbel composed on the chorale "Wachet auf, ruft uns die Stimme" (which Bach would later use in his Cantata BWV 140, Chapter 12) or on "Ein feste Burg." John Butt's Pachelbel recordings (Harmonia Mundi 1990s) are well-engineered. The Pachelbel chorale prelude is a much smaller-scale work than the Buxtehude praeludium (usually 2-3 minutes per piece); the South German tradition Bach absorbed through his elder brother was made of these compact, transparent pieces.

**Read** : Peter Williams, _The Organ Music of J. S. Bach_ (Cambridge University Press, 2003 second edition). Williams's 800-page reference work is the standard treatment of Bach's organ output and includes substantial discussion of the German Baroque organ tradition Bach inherited. The introductory chapters (covering Buxtehude, Pachelbel, Reincken, and the broader North-and-South German tradition) are the most relevant to the present chapter. The book is dense; read selectively. A reader who wants a shorter alternative can use Williams's _J. S. Bach: A Life in Music_ (Cambridge University Press, 2007): about 400 pages, a biographical-and-musical narrative that covers similar ground in less detail.

**Read** (alternative): Christoph Wolff, _Johann Sebastian Bach: The Learned Musician_ (Norton 2000), chapters 1-3. The Wolff treatment of Bach's early years and the German Baroque inheritance is the contemporary scholarly standard; the chapters are about 100 pages combined.

---

## Chapter 5: The Weimar Period 1708-1717, Organist and Concertmaster

The nine years Bach spent at the ducal court of Weimar, July 1708 to December 1717, produced more of the great Bach organ literature than any other period of his life. Most of the celebrated free preludes and fugues; most of the _Orgelbüchlein_ chorale-preludes; the great Passacaglia and Fugue in C minor BWV 582 that Chapter 6 will anchor on; the Vivaldi-concerto transcriptions for solo keyboard and solo organ that brought Italian-concerto idioms into Bach's writing; and most likely (though the attribution is disputed) the _Toccata and Fugue in D minor_ BWV 565 that this chapter anchors on, all date from this period.

This chapter does two things. First, it walks through the Weimar years as a biographical and professional period: the court appointment, the duties, the move from court organist (1708) to concertmaster (1714), the family life across the period, the difficult end (Bach's month-long imprisonment in November-December 1717 before his dismissal to take the Köthen post). Second, it takes the _Toccata and Fugue in D minor_ BWV 565, perhaps the most-famous Bach organ work in the larger culture, the work most people identify as "the Bach organ piece", and gives it the full close-listening treatment that _Close Listening: Classical Music_ Chapter 7 only briefly introduced. The piece's modern attribution dispute, Peter Williams's argument from 1981 onward that BWV 565 may not be by Bach at all, gets honest treatment.

### 5.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

December 6, 1717. Bach has been confined for nearly four weeks in a cell in the Weimar court's judicial offices, on the order of Duke Wilhelm Ernst, the senior of the two co-ruling dukes who employ him. The cell is austere but not punitive in the modern sense; Bach is not under physical restraint; he is permitted writing materials; he can presumably compose. The confinement is a disciplinary measure: Bach has been "too stubbornly demanding his release", _zu hartnäckigen Bezeigung_ , from his Weimar court obligations to take up the Köthen Kapellmeister appointment he was offered in August. Wilhelm Ernst, who can be vindictive, has decided to make a point about the limits of court patronage and the obligations of court servants. After almost four weeks of detention, Wilhelm Ernst relents; on December 2, 1717 the court records show Bach released "_mit angezeigter Ungnade_ ", "with notification of disgrace." Bach leaves Weimar within days. He arrives in Köthen by mid-December to take up the Kapellmeister post that will reshape the next six years of his life.

The four weeks in the Weimar cell are the closing punctuation of a nine-year period that had begun with Bach's enthusiastic acceptance of the Weimar court organist position in July 1708 at age twenty-three. The Weimar years between 1708 and 1717 had been the most compositionally productive period of his life to date. They were also a period of slowly-developing professional frustration: the Weimar court arrangement involved two co-ruling dukes whose mutual hostility made Bach's position increasingly difficult; the senior duke (Wilhelm Ernst, the older uncle) tended to be supportive of Bach but the junior duke (Ernst August, the nephew) developed a closer relationship with Bach and his Köthen prospects through the family connection; the Weimar Kapellmeister position (the senior court music position, distinct from Bach's organist and concertmaster roles) opened up in 1716 and Bach was passed over for it in favor of an outside candidate; Bach's frustration culminated in the resignation that triggered Wilhelm Ernst's punitive imprisonment.

The Weimar years produced the bulk of Bach's organ output. The trip to Lübeck in 1705 had given the young Bach the model; the nine years at Weimar gave him the time, the instrument (the court chapel had a substantial organ; Bach was responsible for its maintenance and for examining other organs in nearby towns), and the obligation (the concertmaster duties from 1714 included composing one new cantata per month) to develop his own organ voice. By the end of the Weimar years he was the unmistakable major figure of German organ music; the _Toccata and Fugue in D minor_ BWV 565, if it is indeed his, almost certainly dates from this period.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

  1. **The opening gesture (the cascade-and-flourish).** The piece opens with one of the most recognized gestures in Western music: a downward-rushing scalar flourish in the right hand, ending on a chord; a brief silence; a rising-then-falling figure; another chord; a held diminished-seventh chord; another silence; a longer descent. This opening is in unison (both hands and pedal play the same line) at the very beginning, then breaks into chordal accompaniment underneath the right-hand figures. The opening lasts about 30 seconds. Listen to it twice if necessary on your first pass; this is the gesture that has been used in horror films and Halloween-music compilations and as the cultural shorthand for "Baroque organ music." The piece's modern fame rests primarily on these 30 seconds.

  2. **The fugue (the middle section).** After the opening toccata gestures conclude (around the 2-minute mark in most recordings), a fugue begins. The fugue subject is a quick descending-then-rising figure announced first by one voice; then a second voice enters with the subject; then a third; then a fourth. The fugue develops the subject across several minutes. By this point in the piece you should be using the fugue-listening skills introduced in Chapter 3's Capriccio fugue. Listen for the voice-entries; try to track the subject as it passes from voice to voice; notice that the fugue is, by Bach's later standards, not particularly contrapuntally dense, the texture is more transparent than the WTC fugues you will encounter in Chapter 10.

  3. **The closing free passage.** After the fugue concludes, the piece returns to a free, rhapsodic character, toccata-gestures, dramatic pauses, chordal punctuations, for a closing passage that returns to the opening's improvisatory feel. This is the _stylus phantasticus_ genre structure you learned in Chapter 4: free → fugue → free, with the alternation between free and strict providing the work's overall shape. Listen for the moment when the fugue's strict imitative texture dissolves back into free toccata-gestures.

#### One-sentence background

Composed sometime in the early-eighteenth century (likely Bach's Weimar period c. 1708-1717 if it is by Bach), surviving in a single mid-eighteenth-century manuscript copy by Johannes Ringk (1717-1778) made probably between the 1730s and 1750s; a three-section organ work (toccata + fugue + closing free passage) in D minor, about 9 minutes; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Helmut Walcha playing the Schnitger organ at the Jakobikirche in Hamburg (Archiv, recorded 1947 mono, re-recorded 1956 stereo, the stereo version is the standard reference; Walcha was a blind organist whose precision and clarity made him the canonical mid-twentieth-century Bach organ interpreter), chosen for the historic instrument, the canonical-modern-instrument status, and Walcha's clear articulation of the contrapuntal voices.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Bach Toccata Fugue D minor BWV 565 Walcha" or "Bach Toccata BWV 565 Schnitger organ" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** (Ch 21 surveys the broader organ recording history):

E. Power Biggs (CBS 1960s, Flentrop organ, Harvard; American mid-twentieth-century reading); Marie-Claire Alain (Erato 1959-67 + 1985-93, French tradition, two cycles); Ton Koopman (Challenge Classics 1990s, period-instrument HIP); Olivier Latry (DG 2002, Notre-Dame de Paris, Cavaillé-Coll cathedral organ — very different acoustic).

### 5.2 The Listening Window

This section is for your second listen. Timestamps below refer to Walcha's 1956 stereo recording. The piece in his reading runs about 8 minutes 50 seconds. If you are listening to a different recording, the section structure still applies but exact clock times will differ.

**0:00-0:30: The opening toccata gestures.** The piece opens with the famous unison cascade in D minor: a four-note descending figure (A-G-F-E in the upper octave), a turn, a chord; another descending figure; a held chord; another descending figure; another held chord. The gestures are deliberately dramatic, deliberately rhetorical, deliberately like an opening speech. Walcha's tempo is steady and his articulation crisp; you can hear each gesture distinctly. The hands and feet (the pedalboard) often play in unison during these opening gestures, which gives the texture its bare, exposed quality, no harmonization underneath, just the single line stated boldly.

**About 0:30-1:00: The chordal-arpeggio section.** After the opening unison gestures, the texture fills out: the right hand plays arpeggiated figures while the left hand and pedal sustain chords underneath. The harmony moves through a series of dramatic shifts. The mood is rhetorical, declamatory; the chord-and-arpeggio pattern resembles operatic recitative more than melodic-musical development.

**About 1:00-2:00: The buildup to the fugue.** The toccata gestures continue, with increasing harmonic complexity and increasing density of figuration. The hands work elaborate figures while the pedal moves through a slow chromatic descent. The harmony eventually settles on a long held chord on the dominant of D minor (an A-major or A-dominant chord); the pause is the longest of the opening section; the listener is being prepared for the fugue that follows.

**About 2:00: The fugue's first subject statement.** A single voice, typically the soprano (the highest-played manual line), states the fugue's subject. The subject is a quick figure: an octave descending leap, then a turn, then a rising-falling pattern, lasting about four seconds. After the soprano voice states the subject, there is a brief moment of clear single-voice texture before the second voice enters with the subject transposed to the dominant (a fifth above the original). This is the fugue exposition beginning.

**About 2:10-2:30: The second and third voice entries.** The alto voice (the second-highest) enters with the subject transposed to A major (the dominant key); the soprano continues with a free contrapuntal line above. Then the tenor voice enters with the subject back in D minor; the soprano and alto continue with free lines around it. This is the standard fugue exposition: each voice in turn states the subject, while the voices that already stated the subject continue with free counterpoint.

**About 2:30-3:00: The pedal entry and the completion of the exposition.** The pedal voice enters with the subject, usually transposed back to A major (the dominant). This is the lowest voice in the texture; the pedal subject-entry is usually the most dramatic of the fugue's voice-entries because it brings the subject into the bass register where it grounds the entire texture. With the pedal-entry, the four-voice fugue exposition is complete: all four voices have stated the subject.

**About 3:00-5:00: The fugue's development.** After the exposition, the fugue develops through a series of subject re-statements alternating with episodes (the contrapuntal passages between subject-entries). The subject returns in various keys (the relative major, F major; the subdominant, G minor; back to the tonic D minor); the episodes use figures derived from the subject but treat them more freely. The Walcha reading articulates the voice-entries clearly; you can usually hear when a new subject-statement begins.

**About 5:00-6:30: The closing fugue passages.** The fugue gradually intensifies through its closing passages, the subject-entries become closer in time (the technique called _stretto_ , the entries overlap rather than being separated by episodes); the harmony moves toward the home key D minor; the dynamics build. The fugue's final cadence arrives around 6:30 with a clear arrival on the D-minor tonic chord, followed by a long-held chord.

**About 6:30-8:50: The closing free passage.** After the fugue's cadence, the music returns to a free toccata character: dramatic chordal punctuations, running figures in the hands, sustained pedal points underneath. The closing passage is shorter than the opening but in the same rhetorical mode. The piece ends with a series of dramatic gestures resolving onto a final D minor chord (with the Picardy third, the major-mode closing third, that you encountered in Chapter 4's Buxtehude praeludium). The final chord is held; the piece ends.

If your recording's total time differs from Walcha's 8:50 by more than 25%, you may be hearing a substantially different performance approach. The Biggs Flentrop reading runs about 9:30 (more dramatic phrasing, more pauses); the Alain readings run closer to 9:30 (slower tempos); the Koopman runs about 8:30 (tighter); the Latry Notre-Dame runs over 10 minutes (massive cathedral acoustic, slower to accommodate the long reverberation). The three-section structure (free toccata → fugue → free closing) should be clear in every reading.

### 5.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The attribution dispute

The Toccata and Fugue in D minor BWV 565, perhaps the single best-known organ work in the Western repertoire, the piece that for many listeners is "the Bach organ piece", has been the subject of a serious modern attribution dispute. The scholarly position can be summarized in three points.

First, the autograph is lost. Bach's own manuscript of the piece does not survive. We do not know whether one ever existed.

Second, the piece survives in a single mid-eighteenth-century manuscript copy made by Johannes Ringk (1717-1778), a Berlin organist who copied many keyboard pieces from various sources between approximately the 1730s and 1750s. Ringk's copy is the _only_ surviving source for BWV 565 from anywhere near Bach's lifetime; all subsequent copies derive from Ringk's. The Ringk manuscript is undated; scholars estimate it was made sometime between the 1730s and the 1750s.

Third, beginning with an article by Peter Williams in _Early Music_ in 1981 ("BWV 565: A toccata in D minor for organ by J. S. Bach?"), modern scholars have raised a series of stylistic concerns about whether the piece is by Bach. The principal arguments against Bach authorship are: the piece's organ-writing has unusual features (the parallel-octave doublings between hands and pedal in the opening; the relative lack of true polyphonic-voice independence; the structure that is closer to the Buxtehude praeludium model than to Bach's more architectural mature practice); the piece's pedal writing is unusual for Bach (the pedal sometimes simply doubles a hand-played line rather than carrying independent material, atypical of Bach's mature pedal writing); the piece's harmonic-rhetorical gestures are closer to mid-century German organ-school practice than to Bach's distinctive idiom; and the surviving source-chain has no documented connection to anyone in Bach's immediate circle (Ringk had no documented connection to Bach himself). Some scholars have proposed alternative attributions, to one of Bach's students or contemporaries, to a young Bach (a juvenile work he disowned), to an arrangement of an originally-violin piece, or to outright unknown authorship. None of these alternative theories has won broad scholarly acceptance; the original attribution to Bach remains the working hypothesis. But the scholarly consensus, after forty years of discussion, is that the attribution to Bach is not as firm as the piece's popular fame would suggest.

The cautious modern position is the following: the piece is in the catalogue under BWV 565 (the BWV numbering was established before the attribution dispute arose, and BWV 565 is the catalogue number that has been used for centuries); the piece's stylistic features are not incompatible with the young Bach of the Weimar period; the modern scholarly conversation has not produced a credible alternative attribution; the piece will continue to be performed and recorded as Bach. But scholars and informed listeners now treat the attribution as open rather than settled, and serious modern Bach scholarship typically presents the BWV 565 with a note acknowledging the dispute.

This volume's treatment: the piece is in the volume because (a) it is in the catalogue, (b) it is the work most listeners arrive at Bach via, and (c) whatever its authorship, it is a representative North German organ work that lets the listener exercise the listening skills the volume builds. The Williams 1981 article is the principal scholarly reference; the Williams _The Organ Music of J. S. Bach_ (Cambridge 2003 second edition) has the fuller treatment.

For the listener: do not let the attribution dispute spoil your hearing of the piece. The music's craftsmanship and rhetorical power are not changed by uncertainty about who wrote it. But hold the attribution as open. When you read elsewhere that "Bach wrote BWV 565," recognize that the claim is plausible but not as firmly evidenced as a non-disputed Bach work (the Brandenburg Concertos, for example, where Bach's own autograph survives intact). The principle generalizes: in Western art-music attribution, single-source works without composer's-autograph are always somewhat vulnerable to reattribution as scholarship advances.

#### The piece's relationship to the stylus phantasticus tradition

Whether or not BWV 565 is by Bach, it is squarely in the Buxtehude-Reincken-Böhm North German _stylus phantasticus_ tradition you encountered in Chapter 4. The three-section structure (free → fugue → free) is the standard Buxtehude praeludium structure; the opening gestures are stylistically continuous with Buxtehude's praeludia; the alternation between free toccata and strict fugue is the genre's defining feature.

What distinguishes BWV 565 (or what would distinguish it as a Bach work) from a Buxtehude praeludium is: the opening gestures are more compactly stated and more dramatically deployed than Buxtehude's freer alternations of similar materials; the fugue subject is more melodically distinctive (Buxtehude's fugue subjects tend to be more anonymous figures); and the work as a whole is more architecturally compressed (about 9 minutes total versus Buxtehude's praeludia at 10-12 minutes). These differences are consistent with the work being by a younger composer working in the Buxtehude tradition but tightening the genre into a more compact form. They are also consistent with the work being by a non-Bach composer working in the same tradition with similar instincts. The stylistic comparison does not by itself resolve the attribution question.

#### The opening: what gives it its distinctive sound

The opening 30 seconds of BWV 565 are the most-imitated 30 seconds of Bach (or near-Bach) in the popular imagination. The opening's distinctive features are:

**Unison texture.** The hands and pedal play the same line in different octaves at the very beginning. Most North German praeludia open with chordal texture or with single-voice melodic statement; the BWV 565 opening's unison-across-the-octaves is a more dramatic choice. The unison gives the opening its bare, exposed, "naked" quality: no harmonization, just the single line stated by multiple voices in unison.

**The Phrygian-mode coloring.** The first three notes (A-G-F) imply a Phrygian-mode opening rather than a strict D-minor opening; the Phrygian mode is the medieval church mode with a half-step between the first and second scale degrees, giving the opening a slightly Eastern or archaic flavor. The Phrygian flavor lasts only seconds before the harmony settles into a clearer D-minor center, but the slightly-modal opening gives the piece part of its distinctive sound.

**The diminished-seventh chord at the third gesture.** After the first two descending-flourish gestures, the third gesture lands on a held diminished-seventh chord: one of the most-dissonant chord types in Baroque harmony. The dissonance is held; the silence afterward is deliberate; the listener is left waiting for resolution. The held-diminished-seventh moment is one of the piece's most-quoted gestures.

**The rhetorical-pause discipline.** The opening uses silence as a structural element. Each dramatic gesture is followed by a brief silence (one or two seconds) before the next gesture begins. The silences are not arbitrary; they are part of the piece's rhetorical effect. A reading that does not respect the silences (a performance that pushes through them, treating them as breath-points rather than as structural pauses) loses much of the opening's force.

For the listener: the four features above are interconnected. The unison texture exposes the line; the Phrygian coloring marks it as archaic; the diminished-seventh creates the moment of suspense; the silences punctuate the suspense. The combination is what makes the opening immediately recognizable.

What does it feel like, this 30 seconds, when a good organist plays it. The first gesture is not a chord but a single line — three fingers and three toes hitting the same notes — and the cathedral acoustic catches the line and holds it for a second after the keys release. The pause. Then a turn. Another chord — and now the dissonance, the diminished seventh, which in technical terms is a chord built of stacked minor thirds and in experiential terms is a chord that wants to go somewhere. It doesn't go. Walcha holds it. The silence after the held chord is the work's central rhetorical device — you are waiting for resolution that the music withholds. The next cascade, when it comes, both relieves the suspense and reactivates it. The opening is dramatic not because it is loud (it isn't, particularly) but because each gesture is followed by a hesitation that asks the listener to anticipate what comes next. A reading that pushes through the silences turns the opening into surface display; a reading like Walcha's that gives each silence its full weight turns it into rhetoric. The fugue that follows is the resolution of all this suspense into ongoing musical argument; the work's overall shape — opening rhetoric → fugal argument → closing rhetoric — is set up entirely by these 30 seconds.

#### The fugue, in context

The fugue (the central section, roughly 2:00 to 6:30 in Walcha's reading) is, by Bach's mature standards, a relatively transparent fugue. The subject is melodically distinctive (the descending octave leap is the most-immediately-recognizable feature); the four-voice exposition is clear; the subject-entries throughout the development are usually easy to follow.

Compared to the fugues of the _Well-Tempered Clavier_ Book 1 (Chapter 10), the BWV 565 fugue is less contrapuntally dense. The WTC fugues often feature inversion, augmentation, stretto, double fugue, and other contrapuntal devices in close proximity; the BWV 565 fugue mostly uses straightforward subject-restatement throughout, with episodes between. The BWV 565 fugue is also more pictorial than analytical, the subject itself sounds like a tossed-off rhetorical idea rather than a compositionally-developed proposition. This is consistent with the work being either a young-Bach work or a non-Bach work; mature Bach fugues are denser and more analytically rigorous.

For the listener: if the BWV 565 fugue is "your first Bach fugue" or "your most familiar Bach fugue," recognize that it is not characteristic of Bach's later fugal practice. The WTC fugues, the Art of Fugue fugues, the chorus fugues in the cantatas and Passions, these are denser, longer, more contrapuntally elaborate. The BWV 565 fugue is the friendliest fugue in the volume's first eight chapters; the later fugues will require more attention.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The BWV 565 connects forward to many works in this volume.

To the Buxtehude Praeludium BuxWV 149 (Chapter 4). The BWV 565 is essentially the cousin of the BuxWV 149, same genre, same three-section structure, similar opening rhetoric. Listening to the two side by side gives you the strongest possible sense of the North German _stylus phantasticus_ tradition Bach absorbed.

To the _Passacaglia and Fugue in C minor_ BWV 582 (Chapter 6). The BWV 582 is the Bach mature work that builds on the same Buxtehude-genre roots as BWV 565, but with a much-tighter architectural plan (a 20-variation passacaglia followed by a double fugue, both on a single bass theme, see Chapter 6 for detail). Comparing BWV 565 (looser, more rhetorical) with BWV 582 (tighter, more architectural) gives you a sense of what Bach's mature voice does within the same tradition.

To Bach's fugal trajectory across the catalogue. The progression from BWV 565 (loose, rhetorical) through the WTC C-major fugue (Chapter 10: tight, four-voice transparent) to _Art of Fugue_ Contrapunctus 1 (Chapter 19: maximally rigorous, abstractly architectural) traces Bach's fugal voice across his life. _Toccata, Adagio, and Fugue in C major_ BWV 564 is the Weimar-period three-section organ work most worth seeking out as a same-period comparison.

The BWV 565 fugue is the volume's most familiar fugue; the later fugues will be denser and require more attention.

### 5.4 The Stories

#### The Weimar court appointment, 1708

Bach took the Weimar court-organist position in July 1708, having left the Mühlhausen organist post in June after barely a year there. The Weimar offer came through Bach's existing connections, Bach had played at the Weimar court briefly in 1703 (during a six-month period between his Lüneburg years and his Arnstadt appointment), and his musical reputation by 1708 was strong enough that the Weimar court was actively recruiting him.

Weimar in 1708 was a small ducal seat, population around 5,000, in the Thuringian region. The court was unusual in being co-ruled by two dukes simultaneously: Wilhelm Ernst (1662-1728, the senior duke, ruling since 1683) and his nephew Ernst August (1688-1748, the junior duke, who would rule jointly from 1707). The two dukes did not get along; their court was effectively divided, with each maintaining his own household and patronage operation despite their formal joint rule. Bach was employed by the senior duke Wilhelm Ernst but had increasingly cordial relations with the junior duke Ernst August across the period.

The Weimar court was strongly Lutheran (Wilhelm Ernst was personally devout and rigorous about religious observance) but also musically rich (the court maintained a significant musical establishment for its size). Bach's initial position as court organist involved playing for the daily court chapel services, accompanying the small court ensemble in occasional cantata performances, and maintaining the chapel organ. The salary was 150 gulden plus accommodation, substantially more than the 50 gulden of Arnstadt or the 85 gulden of Mühlhausen.

In March 1714 Bach was promoted to _Konzertmeister_ , concertmaster, of the court chapel music. The promotion came with a salary increase and with a new obligation: to compose one new cantata per month for the court chapel's monthly Sunday services. The Weimar cantata cycle (1714-1717) produced some of Bach's earliest mature cantatas, works that would be retained and revised across his later Leipzig years. The cantatas of this period include BWV 12 "Weinen, Klagen, Sorgen, Zagen" (1714, the work whose opening chorus would later be parodied as the Crucifixus of the B Minor Mass, Chapter 14), BWV 21 "Ich hatte viel Bekümmernis," BWV 61 "Nun komm, der Heiden Heiland," and roughly twenty others.

The cantata composition obligation gave Bach a regular outlet for vocal-and-instrumental writing that the organist position alone would not have provided. The compositional output of the Weimar years is roughly half organ (the great preludes-and-fugues; the Orgelbüchlein chorale-preludes; the Vivaldi transcriptions) and half cantata (the monthly Konzertmeister cycle). The combination across nine years built Bach into the composer who would arrive at Köthen in late 1717 already in command of essentially all the genres he would work in across the rest of his career.

#### The Vivaldi transcriptions

One of the more consequential events of the Weimar years was Bach's encounter with Antonio Vivaldi's music. Vivaldi's _L'estro armonico_ op. 3, a set of 12 concertos for solo violin, two violins, four violins, and strings, was published in Amsterdam in 1711 by the publisher Estienne Roger. The publication reached central Germany within a few years; copies were available at the Weimar court probably by 1713 or 1714.

Between roughly 1713 and 1715, Bach transcribed about 16 Vivaldi concertos for solo keyboard (six for organ, BWV 593, 594, 596, and ten for harpsichord, BWV 972-987). Several of the transcriptions are based on works from _L'estro armonico_ ; others are based on Vivaldi concertos that Bach knew through manuscript sources or through other Vivaldi publications.

The transcriptions were not commercial, they were not published in Bach's lifetime; they survived in manuscript among his personal papers. The transcriptions appear to have been Bach's way of studying Vivaldi's compositional methods in detail. By rewriting a Vivaldi concerto for a different instrument, Bach was forced to confront every detail of the original, the ritornello structure, the tonal plan, the melodic-harmonic relations, the rhythmic drive. The transcriptions are essentially compositional study notes in the form of arranged works.

The Vivaldi study showed in Bach's subsequent writing. The Brandenburg Concertos (1721, Chapter 8) draw heavily on the Vivaldi concerto-grosso model. The cantata opening choruses (Chapter 12) often use Vivaldian ritornello structures with German contrapuntal elaboration. The Italian-concerto idiom became a permanent part of Bach's vocabulary; the Vivaldi transcriptions are where the absorption happened.

For the listener: the Vivaldi transcriptions themselves are pleasant but not central works in the catalogue. A reader who wants to hear one can find them in any of the complete-keyboard-or-organ-works cycles. The historical importance is greater than the musical-stand-alone importance, the transcriptions matter because of what they did for Bach's compositional voice, not because they are great works in their own right.

#### The Orgelbüchlein

The other major Weimar-period project was the _Orgelbüchlein_ , "Little Organ Book", a planned collection of 164 short chorale preludes covering the entire Lutheran liturgical year. The collection was begun probably around 1713-14 at Weimar; Bach completed 46 of the planned 164 by the end of the Weimar period; he added a handful more later (the collection contains 46 surviving chorale preludes; the autograph manuscript has blank pages with chorale-titles written at the top, awaiting the music that was never written for them).

The _Orgelbüchlein_ is a major Bach output, Chapter 6 will treat it in detail. For now, register that the collection was begun at Weimar, that most of its 46 completed pieces were written during the Weimar years, and that the collection's principle, short chorale-preludes designed to set up the congregational chorale-singing at Lutheran services, exemplifies the kind of work the Weimar court organist position was designed to produce. The Orgelbüchlein chorale-preludes were composed for daily use, not for concert performance; their compositional density and lyrical depth are remarkable given their utilitarian purpose.

#### The end of the Weimar period

The Weimar years ended badly. The trouble began in 1716 when the senior Weimar Kapellmeister position, the position above Bach's concertmaster post, became vacant. Bach hoped for promotion; instead the position went to an outside candidate. The decision rankled. Bach's relationship with the senior duke Wilhelm Ernst, which had been initially supportive, deteriorated over 1716-17.

In August 1717 Bach received the Köthen Kapellmeister offer through his connection with Prince Leopold of Anhalt-Köthen, the Köthen prince was a cousin of the Weimar junior duke Ernst August, who had championed Bach's interests against Wilhelm Ernst's resistance. The Köthen position offered Bach the senior music post (Kapellmeister, equivalent to the position he had been passed over for at Weimar), a significant salary increase, and (importantly) a court patron, Prince Leopold, who was himself a serious musician and intended to give the music establishment substantial resources. Bach accepted in August 1717.

Wilhelm Ernst initially blocked Bach's release from the Weimar position. The court records preserve the September-November 1717 negotiations: Bach formally requested release; Wilhelm Ernst refused; Bach pressed his request; Wilhelm Ernst ordered him imprisoned (November 6, 1717) for "stubbornly demanding" his release. The imprisonment lasted from November 6 to December 2, 1717, about four weeks, in a cell at the court's judicial offices. The cell was austere; Bach was permitted writing materials and could compose. (Tradition has it that he composed parts of the Orgelbüchlein during the imprisonment; the dating is not solidly established but the tradition is plausible.) On December 2 Wilhelm Ernst released Bach "with notification of disgrace." Bach left Weimar by mid-December for Köthen.

The Köthen years that follow are the subject of Chapters 7-10. The Weimar period closed under unhappy circumstances but had been compositionally the most productive period of Bach's life to date.

#### Recording history briefly

BWV 565 has been recorded hundreds of times — Ch 21 surveys the broader organ-recording lineages. For first listening the four §5.1 readings (Walcha, Biggs, Alain, Koopman) plus Latry's Notre-Dame Cavaillé-Coll (DG 2002, before the 2019 fire) sample the principal performance traditions: German Baroque-organ HIP, American mid-century modern, French Romantic-organ. Earlier historical curiosities include Albert Schweitzer's own 1930s recording.

#### BWV 565's twentieth-century reception arc

Chapter 22 will revisit the piece's reception history; the brief note here is that BWV 565's modern fame is largely a twentieth-century construction, particularly through its use in the 1931 film _Dr. Jekyll and Mr. Hyde_ and its inclusion in the 1940 Disney _Fantasia_ (with Leopold Stokowski's symphonic-orchestra arrangement). The arc from obscure mid-eighteenth-century manuscript to twentieth-century universal recognition through film and recording is one of the most-pronounced reception trajectories in the catalogue.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The chapter discusses the modern attribution dispute around BWV 565, Peter Williams's argument from 1981 onward that the piece may not be by Bach at all. The piece is in a single mid-eighteenth-century manuscript copy by Johannes Ringk; the autograph is lost; some stylistic features are unusual for Bach. How does the attribution dispute change how you hear the piece, if at all? If it turns out that BWV 565 is by an unknown North German composer rather than by Bach, does the piece itself become a different work, or is the work the same and only its label changes?"_

* _"The Weimar years produced about half of Bach's organ output, the great preludes and fugues, the Orgelbüchlein, the BWV 582 Passacaglia, and (if it is by Bach) BWV 565 itself. The output is bound up with a specific institutional position, court organist plus concertmaster at the Weimar ducal chapel. What does this tell you about the relationship between artistic output and institutional setting? Could Bach have written the same organ works without the Weimar court-organist appointment? What did the institution provide that the music required?"_

* **Listening exercise.** Pull up the Stokowski orchestral arrangement (1940 Disney _Fantasia_) and the Walcha 1956 organ recording. Play the first 60 seconds of each, back to back. Write down — in one sentence each — what each version is asking the listener to feel. Then ask: which of the two is closer to the work Peter Williams thinks may not be by Bach at all?

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Helmut Walcha 1956 Archiv stereo recording of BWV 565 (or one of the alternatives listed in §5.1). Listen a second time and attend to these four concrete moments.

  1. **The opening 30 seconds (0:00-0:30).** The famous opening cascade. Listen for: the unison texture of the very first gesture (hands and pedal playing the same line); the Phrygian-mode flavor of the opening three notes; the diminished-seventh chord of the third gesture; the rhetorical pauses between gestures. Notice how Walcha respects the silences, they are not breath-points but structural pauses; the music returns from each silence with the next gesture deliberately.

  2. **The fugue's exposition (about 2:00-3:00).** Track the four voices' entries with the subject. The first voice (typically soprano, the highest manual) states the subject in D minor; the second voice (alto) enters with the subject transposed to A major (the dominant); the third voice (tenor) enters with the subject back in D minor; the pedal enters last with the subject typically in A major. Try to follow the subject as it passes from voice to voice; this is the same listening exercise from Chapter 3's Capriccio fugue, now at slightly greater scale.

  3. **The transition out of the fugue back into the closing free passage (around 6:30).** The fugue's final cadence is followed by a return to free toccata-gestures. Listen for the moment of transition: the fugue's regular imitative texture dissolves into chordal punctuations and running figures. The shift is the structural complement to the opening's free-toccata-to-fugue transition; the piece is symmetrical in this respect.

  4. **The closing pedal-point and final cadence (about 8:00-8:50).** The piece ends with a long-held pedal note (the dominant or tonic) under closing figuration in the hands, leading to a final chord with the Picardy-third major-mode closing. Listen for the moment when the pedal settles onto its sustained note; the hands continue active above; the final cadence arrives. The pedal-point closing is the _stylus phantasticus_ tradition's standard ending; you heard a similar gesture in Chapter 4's Buxtehude BuxWV 149.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : a Bach Weimar-period prelude-and-fugue that is unambiguously Bach: for example the _Prelude and Fugue in A minor_ BWV 543 (about 9 minutes) or the _Prelude and Fugue in C major_ BWV 545 (about 7 minutes), or the _Toccata, Adagio, and Fugue in C major_ BWV 564 (about 14 minutes, the last has a particularly beautiful slow middle movement). Walcha's complete-organ-works cycle on Archiv is the easiest single source for the Weimar organ output. Comparing one of these definitely-Bach preludes-and-fugues with BWV 565 gives you a sense of how Bach's own writing in the same genre compares with what may or may not be his.

**Listen** (comparison): the Stokowski orchestral arrangement of BWV 565, used in the 1940 Disney _Fantasia_. The Stokowski arrangement is the version most people who say "I love the Bach Toccata and Fugue" actually have in mind; hearing it side by side with a real organ recording (Walcha or Koopman) shows you how dramatically transformed the piece became under Stokowski's orchestration. The Stokowski reading takes about 9 minutes; it is a thoroughly enjoyable arrangement that bears only an indirect relationship to the original.

**Read** : Peter Williams, "BWV 565: A toccata in D minor for organ by J. S. Bach?" _Early Music_ 9.3 (July 1981), 330-337. The article that launched the modern attribution dispute. Available in academic libraries; about 8 pages. Williams's 2003 _The Organ Music of J. S. Bach_ expands the argument over multiple chapters.

**Read** (alternative): Christoph Wolff, _Johann Sebastian Bach: The Learned Musician_ (Norton 2000), the chapters on the Weimar period. About 50 pages on the 1708-1717 years.

---

## Chapter 6: The Organ Works, Preludes, Fugues, Chorale Preludes, Trio Sonatas, Passacaglia

Bach worked at the organ across his entire career. From the Arnstadt and Mühlhausen years of his late teens and early twenties, through the Weimar peak of his thirties, into the Leipzig years when the organ was still part of his weekly church-music responsibilities, the organ was Bach's principal public-performance instrument and the genre in which he produced his largest single-instrument output. The Bach-Werke-Verzeichnis catalogue's organ-works section, BWV 525-771, runs to about 240 works, the largest single-instrument body in the catalogue.

This chapter does two things. First, it sketches the categories of Bach's organ output: the free preludes-and-fugues; the chorale preludes (settings of Lutheran chorale tunes for organ); the trio sonatas (BWV 525-530, six sonatas for two manuals and pedal); the _Orgelbüchlein_ (the planned chorale-prelude collection of which 46 of 164 are completed); the _Clavier-Übung_ Part III (the 1739 "German Organ Mass", a printed collection of chorale settings on the Lutheran liturgy's principal hymns); the late _Eighteen Chorales_ (BWV 651-668, a collection of large-scale chorale-preludes compiled in Bach's late years from earlier and revised material); and the _Canonic Variations on Vom Himmel hoch_ (BWV 769, 1747). Second, it anchors deeply on one work, the _Passacaglia and Fugue in C minor_ BWV 582, as the representative summit of Bach's organ output, with shorter treatment of the Orgelbüchlein chorale-prelude "Ich ruf zu dir, Herr Jesu Christ" BWV 639 to introduce the chorale-prelude genre.

The chapter is one of the longer chapters in the volume. The organ output is large enough that no shorter treatment would do it justice.

### 6.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

Weimar, c. 1714. Bach is twenty-nine years old, four years into the position of court organist that he took in 1708, and one month into the promotion to concertmaster that has come in March of 1714. The concertmaster appointment formalizes a creative duty (one new cantata per month for the court chapel) that will produce the early-mature cantata cycle from which the volume will return to many works in Chapter 12. The organ duty is unchanged: Bach plays the chapel organ for daily services, examines the organs of nearby town churches when asked, maintains the Weimar instrument, and (in his own time, with the resources the position affords) composes for the instrument.

He is at work on what will become some of the largest single-movement organ works of his career. The _Passacaglia and Fugue in C minor_ BWV 582 is in progress; the dating is uncertain (some scholars place it in the late Arnstadt or Mühlhausen years c. 1706-1708; the more common dating puts it in the early Weimar years c. 1708-1714); the work is unusually large for its genre, combining a 20-variation passacaglia over an 8-bar ground-bass with a double fugue closing in a continuous architecture of perhaps fourteen minutes' playing time. The Buxtehude ciacona in E minor and Buxtehude passacaglia in D minor that Bach has known since his Lübeck stay are the genre ancestors; Bach is now producing the form's largest-scale work in the literature, and one that will stand without serious competition for the next century.

The _Orgelbüchlein_ is also under way. The autograph manuscript, which still survives in Bach's hand, bears the title-page Bach himself wrote: _Orgel-Büchlein_ (Little Organ Book), with a subtitle in German that translates roughly: "wherein a beginning organist is given instruction in performing a chorale prelude in various ways, while at the same time becoming versed in the use of the pedal." The collection is planned at 164 chorale preludes covering the entire Lutheran liturgical year, organized by feast (Advent, Christmas, Epiphany, Passion-tide, Easter, Pentecost, Trinity, then by topic, catechism, morning, evening, penance, communion, and so on). Bach has written titles at the top of all 164 pages; he has composed music for 46 of them. The remaining 118 pages he will leave blank for the rest of his life.

Why he did not complete the planned 164 is not known. Possibly the Weimar duties shifted his time toward cantatas after the 1714 concertmaster appointment; possibly the project simply outlasted his interest; possibly he intended to return to it and never did. What he did complete is one of the most-concentrated chorale-prelude collections in the literature, 46 settings, each typically two to three minutes long, each demonstrating a different polyphonic-textural approach to setting a Lutheran chorale tune.

The chorale preludes are not concert pieces in their primary purpose. They are functional liturgical music, designed to be played before the congregation sings the chorale, helping the listener bring the chorale tune to mind and meditate on its text. A Lutheran service in 1714 Weimar would have included one or two chorales sung by the congregation; the organist would have played a chorale prelude on each chorale before the congregational singing, giving the worshippers a meditative musical preface. The Orgelbüchlein chorale preludes are designed for exactly this use, short enough to function as a service-interlude, lyrical enough to focus the listener's mind, polyphonically interesting enough to reward attention.

The work-anchor for the chapter is the Passacaglia BWV 582; the secondary anchor is the Orgelbüchlein chorale prelude "Ich ruf zu dir, Herr Jesu Christ" BWV 639, which Andrei Tarkovsky used as the principal musical motif in his 1972 film _Solaris_ , a piece that has had an unusual second life in cinema and that exemplifies the _Orgelbüchlein_ 's lyrical depth at small scale.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (Passacaglia BWV 582)

  1. **The 8-bar ground-bass theme.** The Passacaglia opens with the pedal alone playing an 8-bar bass theme in C minor, a slow, austere descending-and-rising figure that establishes the variation pattern for everything that follows. The theme is stated unaccompanied (no chords, no melody above) for its first 8 bars. Listen for the theme carefully on the first hearing; it will return literally or in close variant 20 times across the next 12 minutes. Knowing the theme is the foundation of hearing the variations.

  2. **The 20 variations.** After the bass theme's unaccompanied statement, Bach begins building textures above the repeating bass. Variation 1 adds a single line above the bass; variation 2 adds another line; the textures become progressively richer through about variation 10; then they thin out somewhat in the middle of the cycle; then they build back up to a climactic peak around variation 20. The bass theme continues underneath throughout, repeating its 8-bar shape with each variation. The variations are continuous (no breaks between them); the listener experiences the work as a single continuous arc rather than as 20 discrete pieces.

  3. **The transition into the double fugue.** After variation 20 reaches its peak, the Passacaglia transitions directly into a closing double fugue. The fugue takes the Passacaglia's bass theme as one of its subjects; a second contrasting subject is introduced; the two subjects are developed together. The fugue lasts about 4-5 minutes; the work as a whole closes with a dramatic final cadence on the home key C minor. The transition from passacaglia variations to fugue is one of the most distinctive moments in the work; listen for the moment when the variation-pattern dissolves into the fugue's clear subject-and-answer texture.

#### One-sentence background

Composed c. 1708-1714 at Weimar (or possibly the late Arnstadt-Mühlhausen years c. 1706-1708; the dating is uncertain); a continuous two-section work, 20-variation passacaglia over an 8-bar pedal bass theme + double fugue on the passacaglia theme and a contrasting subject, for organ, in C minor, about 14 minutes; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Helmut Walcha playing the Schnitger organ at the Jakobikirche in Hamburg (Archiv, recorded as part of the 1956-71 stereo complete Bach organ works cycle), chosen for the historic instrument, the canonical-modern-instrument status, and Walcha's clarity in articulating the bass theme as it repeats through 20 variations.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Bach Passacaglia Fugue C minor BWV 582 Walcha" or "Bach Passacaglia BWV 582 organ" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** within the recording-hallucination safeguard (≥10 years old OR ≥3 reviews):

* Ton Koopman on the historic Hinsz organ at the Bovenkerk in Kampen (Challenge Classics, recorded as part of his complete Bach organ works), the contemporary HIP standard; period-instrument approach with clear articulation of the contrapuntal voices.
* Marie-Claire Alain on Erato (recorded twice across her career, the 1959-67 cycle and the 1985-93 re-recording on the Marcussen organ in Sweden), the French-tradition reading; slower tempos and more legato.
* Daniel Chorzempa on Philips (recorded in the 1980s, period instruments), a well-recorded alternative HIP version.
* Olivier Latry at Notre-Dame de Paris (DG, recorded 2002), the great Cavaillé-Coll organ reading; massive resonant acoustics very different from the German Baroque sound.

For the Orgelbüchlein chorale-prelude "Ich ruf zu dir, Herr Jesu Christ" BWV 639, recommended recordings include Walcha (Archiv, part of the complete cycle), Koopman (Challenge Classics), and Helmut Rilling's choir-and-organ accompaniments. The piece is short (about 3 minutes) and appears in many Bach-organ-selection albums.

### 6.2 The Listening Window

This section is for your second listen to the Passacaglia BWV 582. Timestamps below refer to Walcha's 1956 Archiv stereo recording, which runs about 13 minutes 30 seconds.

**0:00-0:25: The bass theme stated unaccompanied.** The pedal alone plays the 8-bar passacaglia theme in C minor. The theme has a specific shape: it begins on the tonic C, moves down through G and F to a low E♭, returns up through F and G to C, then takes a final downward step. The shape is austere; the rhythm is slow and steady; there are no embellishments. Listen to the theme carefully, its shape will be the foundation of everything that follows.

**About 0:25-0:50: Variation 1.** A single line is added above the bass theme in the right hand. The bass theme repeats (still in the pedal); the right-hand voice traces a flowing melodic figure above. The texture is now in two voices (bass and treble); the harmony is implied by the relationship between them. The variation lasts the same 8 bars as the theme statement; at the end of variation 1, the bass theme arrives at its starting C again, ready for variation 2.

**About 0:50-1:15: Variation 2.** A second voice is added above the bass, now there are three voices in the texture. The right-hand figure becomes more elaborate; the new middle voice provides harmonic filling. The 8-bar bass continues underneath. Listen for the bass: it has not changed, even as the texture above it grows richer. This is the principle of variation form: the bass remains constant; what changes is what is built on top of it.

**About 1:15-3:00: Variations 3 through 7.** The texture continues to develop. Different variations use different figuration patterns above the bass: some are flowing arpeggios; some are imitative counterpoint; some are chordal accompaniment; some are running scales. The bass theme repeats with each variation (sometimes with small ornamentations but recognizably the same 8-bar shape). Walcha's clear articulation lets you track the bass as it persists underneath the changing textures.

**About 3:00-5:00: Variations 8 through 12 (the middle development).** The middle of the cycle shows particular textural variety. Variation 12 (around 4:30 in Walcha's reading) shifts the bass theme into a higher voice for the only time in the work, the bass theme appears in a middle voice while the pedal plays a free counterpoint underneath. This is the only departure from the "bass theme always in the pedal" pattern across the entire cycle, and a moment of structural interest.

**About 5:00-7:00: Variations 13 through 16 (the textural diminuendo).** The middle of the cycle shows a deliberate thinning of texture. Some of the variations here are unusually quiet, perhaps two-voice writing only, with the pedal taking the bass theme softly. The deliberate quiet creates a sense of internal-balance within the work; the listener is given a moment of repose before the climactic buildup of variations 17-20.

**About 7:00-9:00: Variations 17 through 20 (the climactic buildup).** The cycle's closing variations build toward maximum textural density. Variation 17 introduces a particularly dense figuration; variations 18 and 19 push the texture toward the most-elaborate writing of the cycle; variation 20 (the final variation) typically uses a "full organ" registration that brings out the maximum dynamic range of the instrument. The bass theme continues underneath but its statement becomes increasingly overwhelmed by the textural richness above.

**About 9:00-13:30: The double fugue.** After variation 20 reaches its peak, the music transitions directly into the closing fugue. The fugue takes the passacaglia bass theme as its first subject, the same 8-bar shape that has served as the variation foundation now becomes a fugue subject. The first voice states the subject; the second voice answers in the dominant; the third and fourth voices follow. The fugue exposition is about 1 minute; the development is about 3 minutes; the final cadence and closing 30 seconds bring the work to its conclusion. Walcha's articulation makes the subject's entries clear; you can follow the fugue's development through the voice-entries.

If your recording's total time differs from Walcha's 13:30 by more than 15%, you may be hearing a substantially different performance approach. The Koopman period-instrument reading runs slightly faster (about 12:30); the Alain readings run slightly longer (closer to 14 minutes); the Latry Notre-Dame reading is longer still (the cathedral acoustic requires slower tempos for clarity). The variation structure and fugue conclusion should be clear in every reading.

### 6.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The passacaglia genre

A _passacaglia_ (Italian; the term derives from a Spanish dance, _pasacalle_) is a variation form built on a slow repeating bass theme. The bass theme is usually 4 or 8 bars long, in slow tempo, in minor mode (most often), with a stately stepwise character. The composer builds successive variations above the repeating bass; each variation occupies one statement of the bass theme. The form has a long ancestry: Italian Renaissance composers used it; French Baroque composers (Lully, Couperin) used it; German Baroque organists (Buxtehude, Pachelbel, Bach) elevated it into a major keyboard genre.

The closely-related _chaconne_ uses a similar variation-over-repeating-bass principle. The terminological distinction between passacaglia and chaconne is unstable across the Baroque period, some authorities distinguish them (passacaglia minor mode, chaconne major mode; passacaglia in 3/4 with weak first beat, chaconne in 3/4 with strong first beat); other authorities treat them as essentially interchangeable. Bach himself wrote one notable example of each: the Passacaglia BWV 582 (this chapter's anchor) and the Chaconne from the D-minor violin Partita BWV 1004 (Chapter 9's anchor for the violin literature). The two works share the variation-over-repeating-bass principle but differ in their instruments (organ vs unaccompanied violin) and in the specific shapes of their bass themes.

The passacaglia/chaconne family is a Bach genre. The compositional principle, a fixed pattern repeated underneath while the texture above varies, gave Bach a vehicle for exploring textural variety within a strict architectural frame. The principle recurs across his catalogue: the Goldberg Variations (Chapter 17) use a related but distinct variation principle (variations over a bass and harmonic pattern rather than over a single bass line); the Crucifixus of the B Minor Mass (Chapter 14) is built on a descending-chromatic ground-bass like a passacaglia; the Art of Fugue Contrapunctus 1 (Chapter 19) is a fugue but its monothematic constraint is in the same family.

The Passacaglia BWV 582 is, in its size and architectural complexity, unique in Bach's output. No other Bach passacaglia exists; the form gets one definitive Bach exemplar and no further attempts. The contemporary scholarly view is that Bach saw the BWV 582 as the form's culmination, there was no need to revisit it.

#### The 20-variation structure

A 20-variation cycle is unusual at this scale. Most Baroque passacaglias are shorter, Buxtehude's BuxWV 161 passacaglia is about 8 minutes; many smaller passacaglias are 4-5 minutes. Bach's BWV 582 at 14 minutes is roughly double the genre's typical scale. The size requires Bach to engineer the variation cycle so that it does not become monotonous, 20 successive variations on the same 8-bar bass could become tedious if not carefully managed.

Bach manages the cycle through a deliberate textural arc. The first several variations build textural density (variation 1 has two voices; variation 2 has three; subsequent variations layer increasing numbers of independent lines above the bass). The middle variations (roughly 8-15) show textural variety without continuous buildup, some variations are denser, some are thinner. The closing variations (16-20) build toward the climactic peak. The arc is roughly: build up → vary the texture → build up again to climax. The architecture is more like a symphonic movement than like a simple succession of variations.

Several variations have distinctive features worth special attention. Variation 12 (the only variation in which the bass theme appears outside the pedal) is the structural midpoint and represents the only departure from the "bass in pedal" principle. Variation 16 is sometimes singled out by analysts as a textural turning point, the buildup toward the climactic close begins around here. Variation 20 is the climactic close to the variation cycle, leading directly into the fugue.

For the listener: the 20-variation count can be tracked by following the bass theme. Each statement of the 8-bar bass theme constitutes one variation. If you count the bass-theme statements from variation 1 through the work, you should reach 20 by the moment of the fugue's entry. This is a useful structural exercise on a second or third listen.

#### The double fugue

The work's closing fugue is a _double fugue_ , a fugue with two contrasting subjects, both of which are developed simultaneously in the same fugue. The principle is more complex than a single-subject fugue: instead of one melodic-rhythmic idea being passed around the voices, two different ideas are alternated, combined, and developed together.

The first subject of BWV 582's fugue is the passacaglia's bass theme, the same 8-bar shape that has served as the variation foundation. The second subject is a contrasting figure introduced after the first subject is established; the second subject is more active, with quicker note-values and a different melodic shape. Bach develops both subjects together: at times one is foregrounded with the other in counterpoint; at times they are combined as simultaneous contrapuntal lines.

The double-fugue principle is harder to follow than a single-subject fugue. On a first hearing, focus on the first subject (the passacaglia bass theme) as it appears in the fugue's voices; on a second hearing, try to identify the second subject when it enters. The double-fugue principle will recur in this volume, Chapter 19's Art of Fugue Contrapunctus 9 is a double fugue; the Art of Fugue Contrapunctus 14 (the unfinished triple fugue) extends the principle to three subjects. The BWV 582 double fugue is the first major double fugue in the volume; the listening skill it builds is one you will use in Chapter 19.

#### Chorale prelude: "Ich ruf zu dir, Herr Jesu Christ" BWV 639

The Orgelbüchlein chorale prelude "Ich ruf zu dir, Herr Jesu Christ", "I call to you, Lord Jesus Christ", sets a Lutheran chorale by Johannes Agricola (1494-1566), one of Luther's contemporaries. The chorale's text is a penitential prayer; the tune is a relatively simple modal melody in F minor. Bach's Orgelbüchlein setting takes the chorale tune as the cantus firmus (the principal melody) in the soprano voice, with two accompanying voices in the middle register and a slow descending bass in the pedal. The setting is about 3 minutes long.

The Orgelbüchlein setting of BWV 639 is one of Bach's most-lyrical chorale preludes. The chorale melody (in the soprano) is heard as the principal voice; the two middle voices weave a flowing accompaniment around it; the pedal carries a slow, sustained bass that moves underneath. The harmonic effect is plaintive, the F minor key, the descending bass, the soft texture, and at the same time consoling, the melodic line moves with a quiet certainty rather than with agitation. The piece is a model of the genre: short, complete, polyphonically rich at small scale, deeply meditative.

Andrei Tarkovsky used BWV 639 as the central musical motif of his 1972 film _Solaris_ (the Soviet science-fiction adaptation of Stanisław Lem's novel) — in the form of an electronic processing by the film's composer Eduard Artemyev, who treated the Bach original through a then-novel synthesizer adaptation. The chorale prelude (Artemyev version, with the Bach original audible underneath) recurs throughout the film at moments of emotional depth. The Tarkovsky-Artemyev use brought BWV 639 to many listeners who would not otherwise have encountered it; the piece has had a substantial second life in cinema and as an extracted Bach miniature, both in the original organ version and in the electronic adaptation.

For the listener: BWV 639 is the volume's most-accessible chorale prelude; if the prospect of working through the Passacaglia's 14-minute architecture is daunting, BWV 639 is a 3-minute alternative entry into the Orgelbüchlein and into Bach's chorale-prelude practice generally.

#### The cantus firmus principle

The Orgelbüchlein's compositional principle is the _cantus firmus_ , the "fixed song." A cantus firmus is a pre-existing melody (a chorale tune, a Gregorian chant, a popular song) taken as the foundation of a polyphonic composition; the composer adds new contrapuntal voices around the cantus firmus while leaving the cantus firmus's melody intact. The principle dates back to medieval polyphony and was the standard organizing technique of Renaissance and early Baroque sacred music.

In an Orgelbüchlein chorale prelude, the cantus firmus is the Lutheran chorale tune. Bach places the cantus firmus in one of the voices (most often the soprano, sometimes the pedal, occasionally an inner voice or an ornamental upper voice) and adds two or three contrapuntal voices around it. The cantus firmus is heard against its contrapuntal frame; the listener who knows the chorale tune recognizes it immediately and hears the contrapuntal frame as commentary on the tune.

For a modern listener who does not know the chorale tunes, the cantus firmus principle is harder to hear. The chorale tune is one of the polyphonic voices; without prior recognition, it can be hard to distinguish from the other voices. The remedy is to learn (or to look up) the chorale tune separately, ideally hearing it sung as a hymn first, before listening to Bach's chorale-prelude setting. The recognition of the chorale's first phrase often suffices; once you know what the chorale-tune sounds like, you can follow it as the cantus firmus through the Bach setting.

The Lutheran chorale knowledge is one of the genuine entry-barriers to Bach's organ chorale-prelude output for a non-Lutheran modern listener. Chapter 16 will provide more orientation on the Lutheran chorale tradition; the present chapter notes the issue and recommends that you listen to "Ich ruf zu dir, Herr Jesu Christ" first as a hymn (any hymnal recording will do, the chorale is included in Lutheran hymnals worldwide) and then as a chorale prelude in Bach's setting. The 5-minute investment makes the chorale-prelude listening much more rewarding.

### 6.4 The Stories

#### The Bach organ output: scope and categories

Bach's organ output is conventionally divided into the following categories:

**Free preludes and fugues, toccatas, fantasias** (about 35 works). The large-scale free works: the Toccata and Fugue in D minor BWV 565 of Chapter 5; the Prelude and Fugue in E♭ major BWV 552 (the "St. Anne," because the fugue subject resembles the English hymn-tune St. Anne); the Prelude and Fugue in A minor BWV 543; the Prelude and Fugue in B minor BWV 544; the Prelude and Fugue in E minor "Wedge" BWV 548; the Prelude and Fugue in C minor BWV 546; and many more, including the Passacaglia BWV 582 (this chapter's anchor) in its hybrid passacaglia-fugue category. These are the largest single-movement organ pieces in the catalogue, ranging from about 7 to 15 minutes per work.

**Chorale preludes** (about 160 works). The chorale preludes are scattered across multiple collections: the Orgelbüchlein (46 surviving completed pieces from the planned 164); the Clavier-Übung Part III (the so-called "German Organ Mass," 1739, containing about 21 chorale preludes on the principal Lutheran liturgical chorales: Kyrie, Gloria, the Lord's Prayer, the Commandments, baptism, communion, Magnificat); the so-called Eighteen Chorales (BWV 651-668, a late-life collection compiled in Bach's last decade from earlier and revised material); the Schübler Chorales (BWV 645-650, a set of six chorale preludes Bach himself published in 1748-49, transcribing some of his cantata chorale movements for organ); the Neumeister Collection (BWV 1090-1120, a set of 31 early chorale preludes discovered in a Yale University library in 1985 and attributed to the young Bach); and miscellaneous chorale preludes from various periods.

**Trio sonatas** (BWV 525-530, six sonatas). The six trio sonatas for organ: composed at Leipzig in the 1730s, possibly for Bach's eldest son Wilhelm Friedemann's pedagogical use, are an unusual organ genre: each sonata is in three independent voices, one on each of two manuals plus the pedal. The voices are equally weighted; the texture is closer to a chamber-music trio (two melodic instruments plus continuo) than to typical organ writing. The trio sonatas are some of the most-technically-demanding works in the organ repertoire because the organist's two hands and feet must each carry an independent melodic-contrapuntal line simultaneously.

**Canonic and contrapuntal works**. The Canonic Variations on "Vom Himmel hoch" BWV 769 (1747): a set of five canon-variations on the Christmas chorale, composed in the late period alongside the Musical Offering and the Art of Fugue. The Variations are a contrapuntal display piece on the smaller scale of the Musical Offering.

**Concerto transcriptions**. The Vivaldi-and-other-concerto transcriptions for organ: BWV 593 (Vivaldi A minor concerto from L'estro armonico Op. 3 No. 8), BWV 594 (Vivaldi D major concerto from Op. 7), BWV 596 (Vivaldi D minor concerto from L'estro armonico Op. 3 No. 11), and several smaller transcriptions of other composers' works (Johann Ernst, Telemann). These were Bach's Weimar-period study exercises (Chapter 5 §5.4); they exist as completed organ works in their own right.

The chapter's anchor (BWV 582) and secondary anchor (BWV 639) are representative samples from the first two categories. A reader who wants to explore further can use Peter Williams's _The Organ Music of J. S. Bach_ (Cambridge 2003 second edition) as the work-by-work reference; the Walcha or Koopman complete cycles as the listening reference; and any of the standard Bach biographies for the historical context.

#### The Orgelbüchlein in detail

The Orgelbüchlein is one of Bach's most-deliberate compositional projects. The autograph manuscript, which Bach wrote out himself, bears a careful organizational structure: the planned 164 chorales are organized by liturgical purpose, with chorales for the various seasons of the church year first (Advent, Christmas, New Year's, Epiphany, Lent, Passion-tide, Easter, Ascension, Pentecost, Trinity), followed by chorales for topical purposes (catechism, morning, evening, communion, confession, death-and-eternal-life). Bach wrote the chorale titles at the top of each planned page and the music below; when he abandoned the project (probably around 1717 at the end of the Weimar period, possibly returning briefly to it during the imprisonment), 46 of the 164 pages had music and 118 had only the title.

The 46 completed chorale preludes are diverse in compositional approach. They demonstrate, between them, most of the standard polyphonic techniques for setting a chorale tune:

* **Cantus firmus in the soprano** (the most-common setting; the chorale melody is in the highest voice, with contrapuntal accompaniment in the lower voices). Examples: "Wachet auf, ruft uns die Stimme" BWV 645 (not in the Orgelbüchlein but representative of the type); many of the simpler Orgelbüchlein settings.

* **Cantus firmus in the pedal** (the chorale melody is in the bass voice, played by the pedal, with contrapuntal accompaniment above). Examples: "Christ lag in Todesbanden" BWV 625 (Orgelbüchlein setting); "Ach Gott, vom Himmel sieh darein" BWV 741.

* **Cantus firmus ornamented in the soprano** (the chorale melody is in the soprano voice but heavily ornamented with melismas, turns, and grace notes, partly disguising the original tune). Examples: "Ich ruf zu dir, Herr Jesu Christ" BWV 639 (this chapter's secondary anchor); "O Mensch, bewein dein Sünde groß" BWV 622 (one of the most-extraordinary Orgelbüchlein settings, a long, ornamented soprano line in F major over a flowing accompaniment that has been called the most-beautiful chorale prelude in the collection).

* **Chorale as fughetta** (the chorale tune's first phrase becomes a fugue subject; the entire setting is a small fugue based on the chorale). Examples: "Liebster Jesu, wir sind hier" BWV 633/634 (Orgelbüchlein has two settings of the same chorale, one a simpler cantus-firmus setting and one a fughetta).

* **Canon on the chorale** (two voices carry the chorale tune in canon, the second voice imitates the first at a fixed interval and time-delay). Examples: "Erschienen ist der herrliche Tag" BWV 629.

* **Imitative settings** (no strict cantus firmus; all voices treat the chorale-derived material in imitation). Examples: several Orgelbüchlein pieces.

The Orgelbüchlein's compositional density is striking. Each piece is short (2-3 minutes) but each demonstrates a different polyphonic approach. The collection functions as a compositional treatise on chorale-prelude writing as much as it functions as a liturgical resource. A serious organ student in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries (and into the twentieth) would have studied the Orgelbüchlein as a compositional model; many later composers' chorale-prelude writing derives from it.

#### The Eighteen Chorales (BWV 651-668)

The so-called Eighteen Chorales, a collection compiled in Bach's last decade, are large-scale chorale-preludes, each typically 5-8 minutes long, that represent the late Bach's most-ambitious organ-chorale settings.
