The collection includes some of the most-celebrated single chorale preludes: "Komm, Heiliger Geist, Herre Gott" BWV 651 (an extensive Pentecost setting with the chorale tune in long notes); "Schmücke dich, o liebe Seele" BWV 654 (a serene, lyrical setting that Robert Schumann famously called one of the most-beautiful pieces in the entire Bach catalogue); "Nun komm, der Heiden Heiland" BWV 659 (a contemplative Advent setting); and "Vor deinen Thron tret ich hiermit" BWV 668, the so-called "Deathbed Chorale", a setting that Bach (according to family tradition) dictated to his son-in-law Johann Christoph Altnickol from his deathbed in late July 1750. The Deathbed Chorale story is treated cautiously by modern scholarship; the autograph evidence suggests the piece was composed earlier and revised in Bach's late years, but the family-tradition story has been so persistently transmitted that it remains in many performances and recordings.

The Eighteen Chorales are not in the Orgelbüchlein and were not part of the Orgelbüchlein project; they form a separate late-compositional cluster, possibly assembled by Bach in the late 1740s as a kind of summing-up of his chorale-prelude practice. A reader who wants to hear the late chorale-prelude voice should explore the Eighteen Chorales as a complement to the Orgelbüchlein. Recordings by Walcha (Archiv), Koopman (Challenge Classics), or specialist organists are widely available.

#### Notable organists, briefly

Ch 21 surveys the full organ-recording history. For first orientation: Walcha (Archiv 1956-71 stereo, mod-inst — the canonical mid-twentieth-century cycle, played from memory despite blindness from age 19) plus Koopman (Challenge Classics 1990s-2000s, HIP on historic Schnitger and Silbermann instruments). These two together cover the principal performance traditions. Marie-Claire Alain's three Erato cycles (1959-67, 1978-79, 1985-93) document the French Bach-organ tradition; Daniel Chorzempa and Masaaki Suzuki offer further HIP alternatives.

#### Cross-piece links you will hear later

The Passacaglia BWV 582 connects forward to the broader variation-over-fixed-bass family. The Buxtehude passacaglia BuxWV 161 (Chapter 4) is the direct ancestor; the violin Chaconne BWV 1004 (Chapter 9) is the unaccompanied-instrument twin peak on a 4-bar bass; the Goldberg Variations (Chapter 17) are the late-period harmonically-elaborated cousin; the _Art of Fugue_ Contrapunctus 9 (Chapter 19) extends the BWV 582 closing double-fugue's principle further into triple fugue; the Crucifixus of the _B Minor Mass_ (Chapter 14) brings the descending-chromatic-bass version to its most-grieving emotional pole.

The Orgelbüchlein BWV 639's cantus-firmus chorale-prelude texture grows into the chorale-cantata genre (BWV 4 in Chapter 11; the 1724-25 cycle in Chapter 12) at vocal-and-instrumental scale, and continues into the late Eighteen Chorales (§6.4 above).

Chapter 20 §20.2 will collect the chorale-prelude texture as a unifying thread; §20.3 the double-fugue principle; §20.4 the canon. The Passacaglia and the Orgelbüchlein chorale-preludes are the volume's deepest organ-music anchors for these threads.

#### The biographical context: 1714 to 1717

The BWV 582 was composed sometime in the 1708-1714 Weimar period; the Orgelbüchlein chorale-preludes were composed across the same period, with 46 of the planned 164 completed by the end of the Weimar years. The biographical thread (Thread A) is set out in Chapter 5; Chapter 7 will pick it up at Köthen.

#### How much chorale knowledge does the modern listener need?

A judgment about Lutheran chorale recognition: most contemporary engagement with Bach's chorale-prelude output proceeds without chorale-tune recognition, and most listeners do find the works rewarding without it. The cantus-firmus listening discipline works at a purely structural level — you can hear that one voice is sustained against contrapuntal weaving without needing to know what tune the sustained voice is. So chorale-knowledge is not strictly required. But the works carry an additional layer of meaning when the chorale tune is recognized, because each chorale tune brings its own theological-textual associations that Bach's contrapuntal commentary is engaging. A reader who learns even ten to fifteen of the principal chorales (Ein feste Burg, Wachet auf, O Haupt voll Blut und Wunden, Vom Himmel hoch, etc.) substantially enriches her listening across the whole sacred output. The investment is modest (a few hours with any Lutheran hymnal recording); the payoff persists across hundreds of subsequent listening hours. The volume's recommendation: not required, strongly worthwhile.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The Passacaglia BWV 582 is built on the principle of a fixed bass theme repeating underneath while the texture above varies. The principle is unusual, most music we hear works the other way (the harmony changes; the melody develops; the bass is in service to the harmonic change). What does the inverted principle (fixed bass; varying texture above) make possible that the standard arrangement does not? What kind of attention does it require from the listener?"_

* _"The 20-variation cycle of BWV 582 occupies about 9 minutes before transitioning into the closing double fugue. 20 successive statements of the same 8-bar bass theme could become monotonous; Bach engineers the cycle as a textural arc (build up → vary → build to climax). Listen to the cycle again and see if you can identify the arc's shape, at what point does the texture reach its first peak, where is the middle of the cycle, where does the climactic buildup begin? Compare the architectural shape you hear with the variation-count structure (20 successive 8-bar variations), are they aligned, or does the architectural arc cut across the variation-numbering?"_

* _"The Orgelbüchlein chorale prelude 'Ich ruf zu dir, Herr Jesu Christ' BWV 639 was used by Andrei Tarkovsky as the central musical motif of his 1972 film Solaris. The piece originally functioned as a 3-minute liturgical interlude before the chorale was sung at a Weimar Sunday service. Two and a half centuries later it became a film-score touchstone for an emotional-philosophical science-fiction film. What does it tell you about Bach's compositional voice that a small liturgical piece could function so powerfully in a context so distant from its original purpose? Are there other liturgical-functional Bach pieces you can think of that have had similar second lives in secular contexts?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Helmut Walcha 1956 Archiv stereo recording of the Passacaglia BWV 582 (or one of the alternatives listed in §6.1). Listen a second time and attend to these four concrete moments.

  1. **The opening bass theme (0:00-0:25).** Listen to the unaccompanied pedal statement of the 8-bar bass theme. Try to memorize the theme's shape, the descending and rising contour, the rhythm, the slow steady pace. The theme will return 20 times across the next 12 minutes; recognition of it is the foundation of hearing the variations.

  2. **The textural buildup through variations 1-7 (0:25 to about 3:00).** Listen for the progressive addition of voices above the bass. Variation 1 adds one voice; variation 2 adds another; subsequent variations layer in additional contrapuntal lines. Track the bass theme as it repeats underneath the changing textures, try to count the 8-bar bass-statements as they recur.

  3. **Variation 12 (around 4:30).** This is the only variation in the work where the bass theme leaves the pedal. The pedal plays a free counterpoint; the bass theme appears in a middle voice. Listen for the moment of the departure, it is structurally distinctive and recognizable once you know to look for it.

  4. **The transition from variation 20 to the fugue (around 9:00).** Listen for the moment when the variation-pattern dissolves into the fugue's strict imitative texture. The bass theme that has been the foundation of the variations now becomes the fugue subject; the first voice of the fugue states the subject; the second voice answers in the dominant. The transition is one of the most distinctive structural moments in the work.

Then find a recording of the Orgelbüchlein chorale prelude "Ich ruf zu dir, Herr Jesu Christ" BWV 639 (Walcha, Koopman, or any of the standard recordings). Listen once. The piece is about 3 minutes. Notice the chorale tune in the soprano voice (ornamented); the two middle voices weaving an accompaniment; the slow descending pedal line underneath.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : a Bach trio sonata for organ: BWV 525-530, six sonatas. The Trio Sonata in E♭ major BWV 525 is the standard starting point. About 12 minutes total across three movements (Allegro moderato, Adagio, Allegro). The trio-sonata genre is unique in Bach's organ output (no other composer wrote organ trio sonatas at this level); the three-voice texture (two manuals plus pedal, each carrying an independent line) is the most-transparent form of contrapuntal listening the organ literature offers. Walcha or Koopman cycles include the trio sonatas.

**Listen** (comparison): the Buxtehude Passacaglia in D minor BuxWV 161: the direct compositional ancestor of Bach's BWV 582. About 8 minutes. Koopman's complete Buxtehude organ-works cycle on Challenge Classics is the easiest source. Hearing the Buxtehude immediately before or after the BWV 582 shows you what Bach inherited and what he added.

**Read** : Peter Williams, _The Organ Music of J. S. Bach_ (Cambridge University Press, 2003, second edition). About 800 pages, work-by-work treatment of the entire organ output. The chapters on the Passacaglia and on the Orgelbüchlein are the most-relevant to the present chapter. Williams's voice is analytical and rigorous; the book is a reference more than a reading; consult selectively.

**Read** (alternative): Christoph Wolff, _Johann Sebastian Bach: The Learned Musician_ (Norton 2000), the chapters on the Weimar period and on the late organ output. About 80 pages combined.

---

## Chapter 7: The Köthen Court 1717-1723, Bach's Secular Period

The six years Bach spent at the small Calvinist court of Anhalt-Köthen between December 1717 and April 1723 are a strange and luminous exception within the catalogue. They are the only extended period of his career in which he was not principally a church musician. He composed almost no sacred music during these years (a handful of cantatas for civic-ceremonial purposes; none of the great Lutheran sacred works); the daily round of cantata composition that defined Weimar before 1717 and Leipzig after 1723 simply did not exist at Köthen. What Bach composed instead was secular instrumental music for the court ensemble he directed.

The Köthen catalogue is remarkable. The Brandenburg Concertos (dedicated March 24, 1721; the subject of Chapter 8). The six Cello Suites BWV 1007-1012 (probably c. 1720; Chapter 9). The six Violin Sonatas and Partitas BWV 1001-1006 (probably c. 1717-1720; also Chapter 9). The _Well-Tempered Clavier_ Book 1 BWV 846-869 (completed at Köthen 1722; Chapter 10). The four Orchestral Suites BWV 1066-1069 (probably begun at Köthen and completed at Leipzig). The Double Concerto for Two Violins in D minor BWV 1043 (composition place uncertain but plausibly Köthen). The Inventions and Sinfonias BWV 772-801 (1723, the last year at Köthen). The French Suites BWV 812-817 (Köthen and early Leipzig). Roughly half the secular instrumental music in Bach's catalogue was composed at Köthen across six years.

This chapter sets up the Köthen context for the chapters that follow. It is one of the shorter chapters in the volume, there is no single anchor work; the next four chapters (8 through 10) take the Köthen output piece by piece. Chapter 7 covers the biographical and institutional setting: the move from Weimar in December 1717; the structure of the small Köthen court and Prince Leopold's patronage; the daily life of the court Kapellmeister; Maria Barbara's death in July 1720 and the marriage to Anna Magdalena in December 1721; the gradual deterioration of conditions at Köthen and the move to Leipzig in April 1723.

The chapter has no Listening Window section, the listening is reserved for the anchor works of Chapters 8 through 10. The section structure is adapted: §7.1 covers the Köthen context; §7.2 covers Prince Leopold and the court establishment; §7.3 covers the family life of the Köthen years; §7.4 covers the close of the Köthen period and the Leipzig transition.

### 7.1 The Move from Weimar to Köthen

When Bach was released from his Weimar imprisonment on December 2, 1717 "with notification of disgrace," he traveled the roughly forty miles north from Weimar to Köthen by horse-coach within the week. He arrived in Köthen by mid-December to take up the position of Kapellmeister to Prince Leopold of Anhalt-Köthen. He was thirty-two years old.

Köthen in 1717 was a small Saxon-Anhalt princely seat with a population of about 2,500. The town was in the territory of the principality of Anhalt-Köthen, one of the four Anhalt principalities (Anhalt-Bernburg, Anhalt-Dessau, Anhalt-Köthen, Anhalt-Zerbst) into which the broader Anhalt region had been divided in 1606. The principality was small; Prince Leopold's domain comprised perhaps thirty villages around Köthen and a few outlying possessions. The court establishment was correspondingly modest by central-European standards, a few dozen courtiers, a small civic-administrative apparatus, a court chapel, a small standing musical establishment.

What made Köthen unusual for Bach was its Calvinist religious orientation. Most of central Germany, including all of Bach's previous workplaces (Eisenach, Ohrdruf, Lüneburg, Arnstadt, Mühlhausen, Weimar), was Lutheran. The Anhalt principalities had been Calvinist since the early seventeenth century, when the dukes of Anhalt converted from Lutheranism to the Reformed (Calvinist) tradition following political and theological influences from the Netherlands. The Calvinist tradition was austere in its liturgical practice; elaborate church music was discouraged or forbidden; the central religious activity was preaching and unaccompanied congregational psalm-singing. A Calvinist court did not require, and did not want, the kind of weekly cantata composition that had been Bach's principal duty at Weimar.

This had two consequences for Bach. First, the position of Kapellmeister at Köthen was not principally a church-music position. The Kapellmeister directed the court's secular musical establishment, the court orchestra of about 16-18 players, the chamber-music activities for the prince and his guests, the festive music for court occasions. Sacred music was performed at the small court chapel for the prince and a few attendant courtiers but was modest in scale; the court chapel was Calvinist, not Lutheran, and avoided elaborate musical setting.

Second, because the prince and his immediate court were Calvinist while many of his subjects (and Bach's family) remained Lutheran, the religious arrangements were complex. Bach himself was Lutheran (he had been raised in the Lutheran tradition; he would die in it); Maria Barbara was Lutheran; their children were baptized as Lutherans (they attended the Lutheran Agnuskirche in Köthen, the town's surviving Lutheran congregation alongside the prince's Calvinist court chapel). Bach's official duties were to the Calvinist court; his personal religious life remained Lutheran. The arrangement worked, but it required some compartmentalization.

The Köthen Kapellmeister post paid 400 thaler per year (about three times the Weimar salary), came with apartment in the Schloss itself, ranked second only to the court chamberlain among court appointments, and gave Bach substantial compositional freedom — the prince expected secular instrumental music for court entertainment but did not dictate specifics. The principal sacrifice was the church-music focus: for a composer whose deepest inheritance was Lutheran, six years writing for the court chamber rather than for the church was unusual, and the Köthen output reflects it.

### 7.2 Prince Leopold and the Court Establishment

Prince Leopold of Anhalt-Köthen (1694-1728) was twenty-three years old at Bach's arrival in December 1717, nine years younger than the new Kapellmeister. Leopold had succeeded to the throne in 1715 at age twenty-one after the regency that had followed his father's death; he was a serious-minded young ruler with substantial education and substantial personal interests in music.

Leopold had received a rigorous musical education as part of his princely formation. He had studied violin, viola da gamba, and harpsichord; he was a competent performer on all three; he could read and discuss compositional matters at a level of detail unusual for a princely patron. His Grand Tour of 1710-13 (a standard piece of aristocratic education at the time) had included an extended stay in Italy, where he had absorbed the Italian instrumental concerto tradition firsthand and had purchased instruments and scores for his eventual court ensemble. By the time of his accession in 1715, Leopold was preparing to establish a serious music establishment at his small court.

The Köthen court orchestra under Leopold consisted of about 16-18 players: a small string section (typically 3 first violins, 3 second violins, 1 viola, 1 cello, 1 violone, the violone being the late-seventeenth-century predecessor of the modern double bass), a wind section (1-2 oboes, 1-2 flutes or recorders, 1 bassoon, 2 horns), and a continuo group (harpsichord, organ if available, cello). The players were both court servants and traveling musicians on contract; many were leading German instrumentalists of the period (the violinist Joseph Spiess; the gambist Christian Bernhard Linigke; the trumpet player Johann Ludwig Schreiber; among others). The orchestra was small by German-court standards (Dresden's electoral court had more than seventy musicians) but substantial for a small principality.

The prince himself participated in the orchestra. He played viola da gamba, usually in the chamber-music sessions rather than the full orchestra, and (according to family tradition) sometimes violin. The prince's participation gave the music-making at Köthen a quality unusual at most German courts: the patron was not merely listening but joining. This shaped the Kapellmeister's compositional choices: Bach knew that pieces he composed might be performed with the prince participating, and the chamber-music output of the Köthen years often includes prominent viola da gamba parts (the three Viola da Gamba Sonatas BWV 1027-1029 most likely date from Köthen and were probably composed for Leopold's playing).

The court's musical activities included:

* **Weekly chamber-music sessions** , small ensemble performances in the prince's private apartments, with Leopold often participating. These were the principal vehicle for new chamber-music compositions.
* **Festive ceremonial music** , for the prince's birthday, for visiting dignitaries, for the New Year, for court anniversaries. These were the principal vehicle for the larger ensemble works.
* **Travel music** , when the prince traveled (to Carlsbad for the summer cure, to Berlin for diplomatic visits, to other courts), he often took a portion of the orchestra along. Bach traveled with the prince several times; the Carlsbad trip of summer 1720 was the trip during which Maria Barbara died.
* **Sacred music for the court chapel** , modest, in the Calvinist tradition. Bach's contribution here was small.
* **Public concerts** , rare; the court chapel and chamber music were the principal venues, not public concerts.

Bach's relationship with Prince Leopold was unusually close. The Wolff biography (Norton 2000) characterizes it as a friendship rather than a court-servant relationship. Leopold respected Bach's musical mastery; Bach respected Leopold's musical ability and his patronage commitment. The two appear to have spent substantial time together in chamber-music sessions and in informal musical discussion. Bach's later writings, what survives of his correspondence, the few personal references in his other documents, speak warmly of the Köthen years and of Prince Leopold specifically.

The closeness of the relationship shaped what Bach wrote: the Brandenburgs, the Cello Suites, the Violin Sonatas and Partitas, and the WTC Book 1 were all composed within this specific institutional context, a small court with a serious musical patron who could appreciate complex contrapuntal craft. The specific dedicatees and compositional histories are treated where the works themselves are anchored in Chapters 8-10.

### 7.3 Family Life at Köthen

The personal events of the Köthen years are dramatic. Bach arrived in December 1717 with Maria Barbara (his wife of ten years) and the four surviving children from their marriage: Catharina Dorothea (born 1708, age 9 at the move); Wilhelm Friedemann (born 1710, age 7); Carl Philipp Emanuel (born 1714, age 3); Johann Gottfried Bernhard (born 1715, age 2). The family settled into the Schloss Köthen apartment that came with the Kapellmeister position.

The first thirty months at Köthen were stable. Maria Barbara managed the household; Bach attended to his Kapellmeister duties; the children grew. There is no indication of any unusual stress or disruption during this period. Bach traveled occasionally, to Hamburg in November 1720 to perform for the elderly Reincken (Chapter 4); to Carlsbad in the summers of 1718, 1719, and 1720 with Prince Leopold's traveling court, but the absences were brief.

In May 1720 Bach traveled with Leopold's court to Carlsbad for the prince's annual summer cure. While Bach was away, Maria Barbara fell ill at Köthen, the illness progressed rapidly, and she died on July 7, 1720 at age 35; the cause was not documented. The news did not reach Bach in time to return. He came back to Köthen in mid-July to discover Maria Barbara already buried — C. P. E. Bach's 1750 Obituary preserves the family tradition that he had left her well and returned to find her dead. He was left with four young children, ages eleven, ten, six, and five, and a household needing a new manager; the cultural expectation was prompt remarriage.

The answer came quickly. Anna Magdalena Wilcke (1701-1760) was a soprano at the Köthen court, recently appointed to the small group of court singers. She was twenty years old in 1720, sixteen years younger than Bach; she came from a court-musician family (her father was a trumpeter at the Zeitz court). Bach almost certainly first encountered her in the court music establishment; the documentation does not specify exactly when their relationship began.

Bach and Anna Magdalena married on December 3, 1721, eighteen months after Maria Barbara's death. The wedding was at the Schloss Köthen rather than at a town church, possibly because of the religious-confessional complications (a Lutheran groom and bride being married in a Calvinist court). The marriage was followed by a court banquet that Prince Leopold himself hosted; the closeness of the prince's personal involvement is one of the indications of the warmth of his relationship with Bach.

Anna Magdalena would become one of the most-important figures in Bach's life. She was a serious musician in her own right (her singing career at Köthen continued for a time after the marriage; she would later copy many of Bach's manuscripts, the primary surviving source for the Cello Suites is a fair copy in her hand; the _Notebook for Anna Magdalena Bach_ of 1722 was the family's domestic-music collection). She bore Bach thirteen children over the next twenty years, of whom seven survived past infancy. She managed the Bach household at Köthen and then at Leipzig until Bach's death in 1750, after which she lived as a widow for ten years (dying in 1760 in increasingly straitened circumstances after most of Bach's estate passed to the surviving sons of his first marriage).

Bach's compositional output did not visibly suffer from these personal disruptions. The Brandenburg Concertos, the Cello Suites, the Violin Sonatas and Partitas, the WTC Book 1 were all composed across this period; several have dates falling in the months immediately around Maria Barbara's death. The Cello Suites' slow movements are sometimes argued to carry the imprint of grief, but the dating does not securely support the reading — the later chapters treat these works on their own musical terms rather than as biographical documents.

### 7.4 The Close of the Köthen Period and the Move to Leipzig

The Köthen position began to deteriorate around 1721-22, partly through events beyond Bach's control and partly through changes in the prince's circumstances.

In December 1721, just a few days after Bach's own wedding to Anna Magdalena, Prince Leopold married Princess Friederike Henriette of Anhalt-Bernburg. The princess was young (about 18 at marriage); she was not musically inclined. The court chronicler later reported that she was an "_amusa_ ", literally "without the muses," meaning without artistic or musical inclination. Her presence at court began to shift the prince's attention away from music. The chamber-music sessions Leopold had previously hosted personally became less frequent; the prince's musical participation in the orchestra dwindled; the court establishment's compositional commissions slowed.

Whether Friederike Henriette was personally responsible for these shifts is uncertain. Some scholars (including the Wolff biography) read Bach's later remarks about the Köthen-period decline as evidence that Friederike Henriette played a substantial role in turning Leopold away from his music establishment; other scholars treat the attribution more cautiously, noting that Leopold's musical engagement may have shifted for other reasons (the responsibilities of marriage, the political pressures on a small Saxon-Anhalt principality, the financial constraints on the court budget). Whatever the cause, the practical effect for Bach was that the most-engaged years of the Köthen patronage were over by early 1722.

In addition, the prince's small treasury was under increasing strain. The Anhalt principalities were vulnerable in the broader political shifts of central-European politics; the cost of maintaining a court ensemble was substantial; the prince's budget was probably tightening. Bach's compositional output continued (the WTC Book 1 was completed in 1722; the Inventions and Sinfonias were being assembled across 1722-23) but the broader compositional environment was changing.

In mid-1722 the Leipzig Thomaskantor Johann Kuhnau died. The Leipzig city council began the recruiting process that would, ten months later, end with Bach being offered the post — third after Telemann (who used the Leipzig offer to extract a raise from Frankfurt) and Graupner (whose Darmstadt patron refused to release him). The Leipzig appointment narrative belongs to Chapter 11; here the relevant fact is only that Bach accepted, that Leopold released him without resistance, and that the Bach household moved from Köthen to Leipzig in May 1723.

The Köthen years close at the moment of departure. Five and a half years; the entire secular-instrumental core of the catalogue (Brandenburgs, Cello Suites, Violin Sonatas and Partitas, WTC Book 1) composed within them; personally happy except for the Maria Barbara loss; closing with a move to Leipzig that would set the rest of Bach's life.

#### Köthen as institutional accident

A judgment worth making explicit: the secular-instrumental masterworks of the Köthen years — the Brandenburg Concertos, the Cello Suites, the Violin Sonatas and Partitas, the WTC Book 1 — exist as they do because of the specific institutional accident of a Calvinist court with a serious musical patron who wanted secular chamber music and did not require weekly cantatas. Bach would not have written these works in this form at Weimar (where the concertmaster duties required monthly cantatas) or at Leipzig (where the Thomaskantor duties required weekly cantatas). The Köthen Bach is a different Bach than the Bach who preceded and followed him — focused on what a small court patron's chamber-music establishment could perform, freed from liturgical scheduling, given the time to develop instrumental forms in depth. If Leopold had remained engaged with music past 1721, Bach might have stayed at Köthen; the Leipzig sacred output — the cantatas, the Passions, the B Minor Mass — would not exist. The catalogue we know is the result of two specific institutional turns: the Calvinist Köthen patronage that produced the secular instrumental peak, and the Friederike-Henriette-induced decline of that patronage that pushed Bach to Leipzig. Neither was inevitable.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The Köthen years are the only extended period in Bach's career when he was not principally a church musician. The Calvinist court did not require elaborate church music; Bach composed almost no sacred work during these six years. What does the secular focus tell us about Bach's compositional voice, was the secular Bach materially different from the sacred Bach, or is the music recognizably the same composer working in a different generic frame? Reading the music alone, would you be able to tell that the Brandenburg Concertos are by the same person who later wrote the St. Matthew Passion?"_

* _"Prince Leopold of Köthen was 23 years old when Bach arrived as Kapellmeister; he was himself a serious musician who played viola da gamba and harpsichord and who often participated in the court chamber-music sessions. The Wolff biography characterizes the Bach-Leopold relationship as a friendship rather than as a standard court-servant relationship. What kind of compositional environment does it create when the patron is also a participating musician? How is it different from a patronage relationship in which the patron is a passive listener-and-payer?"_

* _"Maria Barbara Bach died suddenly in July 1720 while Bach was traveling with the Köthen court at Carlsbad. The news did not reach Bach in time to return for her death or burial. The biographical disruption was significant, four children needing a mother, a household needing a manager, a personal grief. Yet Bach's compositional output continued and may even have intensified across the years that followed (the Brandenburg Concertos, the Cello Suites, the WTC Book 1 all date from the 1720-22 period). How do you understand the relationship between personal-life disruption and compositional output? Does the music absorb the disruption, or is it separable from it?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

This is a setup chapter without an anchor work. Use the time you would otherwise spend on a re-listen to do one of the following.

  1. **Listen orientation-style to a brief Köthen-period work.** Pick one short work from the Köthen output: the Brandenburg Concerto No. 4 in G major BWV 1049 (Chapter 8 will treat in detail; about 16 minutes), the Cello Suite No. 1 in G major BWV 1007 Prelude (Chapter 9; about 3 minutes), the WTC Book 1 Prelude and Fugue in C major BWV 846 (Chapter 10; about 4 minutes), or the Two-Part Invention No. 1 in C major BWV 772 (Chapter 10; about 1 minute). Listen once without close attention. Just orient yourself to the secular Bach voice, the Bach who is working in the chamber-music genre rather than in the church-cantata genre. Notice what is recognizable as Bach (the contrapuntal density, the harmonic invention, the rhythmic drive) and what is different from the sacred Bach you have not yet encountered in detail in this volume.

  2. **Find a recording of a Bach Viola da Gamba Sonata (BWV 1027-1029).** These three sonatas were probably composed at Köthen for Prince Leopold's own playing. Each runs about 12-15 minutes. Recordings by Jordi Savall (with Ton Koopman as harpsichord, Astrée 1995), Wieland Kuijken with Gustav Leonhardt (Sony 1980s), or Vittorio Ghielmi with Lorenzo Ghielmi (Passacaille 2005) are available. The gamba sonatas give you the chamber-music sound the Köthen prince himself would have participated in.

  3. **Read a biographical chapter on the Köthen period.** Christoph Wolff, _Johann Sebastian Bach: The Learned Musician_ (Norton 2000), chapter on the Köthen years (roughly chapter 8 in Wolff's numbering, about 40 pages). Or Malcolm Boyd, _Bach_ (Master Musicians, Oxford 2000), chapter on Köthen (about 25 pages). The biographical detail will set up the four chapters that follow.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : Bach, Viola da Gamba Sonata in G major BWV 1027 (about 13 minutes, four movements: Adagio: Allegro ma non tanto, Andante, Allegro moderato). This was probably composed at Köthen for Prince Leopold's playing. Savall and Koopman (Astrée 1995) is the standard reference.

**Listen** (alternative): Bach, French Suite No. 5 in G major BWV 816 (about 18 minutes, seven dance movements). Most likely composed at Köthen for Anna Magdalena's playing (the French Suites appear in the _Notebook for Anna Magdalena_ of 1722). Recordings by András Schiff (Decca 1990s) or Murray Perahia (Sony 2010s) for piano; Pierre Hantaï or Trevor Pinnock for harpsichord.

**Read** : Christoph Wolff, _Johann Sebastian Bach: The Learned Musician_ (Norton 2000), the chapter on Köthen 1717-1723. About 40 pages. The most-comprehensive single treatment of the period in current scholarship.

**Read** (alternative, shorter): Malcolm Boyd, _Bach_ (Master Musicians, OUP 2000), chapter on Köthen. About 25 pages.

**Read** (specialized): the autobiographical and biographical references in Bach's surviving correspondence: what little survives from the Köthen years. The most-relevant single document is Bach's October 1730 letter to his school friend Georg Erdmann, written from Leipzig, in which Bach reflects on the Köthen years and mentions the conflict with Friederike Henriette that turned Leopold away from music. The Erdmann letter is in _The New Bach Reader_ (W. W. Norton, 1998, edited by Hans T. David, Arthur Mendel, and Christoph Wolff), pp. 151-152.

## Chapter 8: The Brandenburg Concertos BWV 1046-1051 (1721)

The six concertos that Bach assembled in early 1721 as a presentation copy for Christian Ludwig, Margrave of Brandenburg, are the most-famous orchestral works of his career and probably the most-famous Baroque concertos in the standard repertoire. They are not symphonies, Bach did not compose symphonies; the symphony as a genre was a generation after his time, but they occupy roughly the same place in his catalogue that the symphonies do for Haydn, Mozart, and Beethoven: the major works for orchestral forces, the works in which the composer engaged most directly with the public-concert tradition rather than with chamber music or church music.

The set is unusual in several respects. No two of the six concertos have the same instrumentation. The first (in F major BWV 1046) calls for two horns, three oboes, bassoon, violino piccolo (a small high-tuned violin), and strings, a relatively large ensemble. The second (in F major BWV 1047) calls for trumpet, recorder, oboe, violin, and strings, a smaller chamber group with the trumpet given a virtuoso role. The third (in G major BWV 1048) calls for three violins, three violas, three cellos, and continuo, a string-only ensemble with no separate soloist group. The fourth (in G major BWV 1049) calls for violin and two recorders as soloists with string ensemble. The fifth (in D major BWV 1050) calls for flute, violin, and harpsichord as soloists with string ensemble, and gives the harpsichord a virtuoso solo cadenza in the first movement, effectively creating a proto-harpsichord-concerto. The sixth (in B♭ major BWV 1051) calls for two violas, two violas da gamba, cello, violone, and continuo, no violins, only middle-and-lower strings. The variety is deliberate: Bach is offering the Margrave six different demonstrations of what the concerto-grosso form can do.

This chapter does three things. First, it sets up the concertos' historical and institutional context, the March 1721 dedication, the Margrave of Brandenburg, the Köthen ensemble Bach had available, the place of the concertos in the broader Köthen instrumental output. Second, it anchors deeply on the Brandenburg Concerto No. 4 in G major BWV 1049 as the principal work, with shorter treatment of the No. 2 in F major BWV 1047 and the No. 5 in D major BWV 1050 to introduce the set's range. Third, it cross-references back to _Close Listening: Classical Music_ Chapter 7, which anchored the Brandenburg No. 3 in G major.

### 8.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote

March 24, 1721. Bach is thirty-six years old, in his fourth year as Kapellmeister at the Köthen court. He is at his desk in his Schloss Köthen apartment, completing the dedicatory letter that will accompany a presentation manuscript of six concertos to Christian Ludwig, Margrave of Brandenburg. The Margrave (1677-1734) is the youngest son of Frederick William, the "Great Elector" of Brandenburg-Prussia; he is the half-uncle of the current Brandenburg-Prussian king Frederick William I; he maintains a small private musical establishment at his Berlin residence and is a recognized patron of music in the German-Prussian aristocratic circle. Bach had played for the Margrave during one of his Berlin visits (probably in March 1719 when Bach traveled to Berlin to receive a new harpsichord ordered for the Köthen court); the Margrave had at that time invited Bach to send him some of his compositions. The 1721 dedication is the response to that invitation, made about two years after the invitation itself.

The dedicatory letter (preserved with the manuscript) is in French, the standard language of aristocratic correspondence in early-eighteenth-century Germany. The letter opens with elaborate formal courtesies and offers the Margrave the six concertos "having had a few years ago the good fortune to be heard by Your Royal Highness, by virtue of his commands, and having noticed then that he took some pleasure in the small talents which Heaven has given me for music, and having received His Royal Highness's order to send him a few pieces of my composition; I have then, in accordance with this most-gracious order, taken the liberty of rendering my very humble duty to Your Royal Highness by the present concertos, which I have arranged for several instruments." The letter is conventional in form, the elaborate self-deprecation and patron-flattery are standard aristocratic-dedication rhetoric of the period, but it preserves the basic facts: the concertos are being sent in fulfillment of the Margrave's earlier invitation; they are concertos for several instruments; Bach is offering them as a humble servant's gift.

The six concertos are sent. What happens next is interesting in its silence. There is no surviving record of any Margrave response to the dedication; no surviving record of any payment or honorarium; no surviving record of any performance at the Margrave's court. The manuscript was placed in the Margrave's library (a substantial private music collection); when the Margrave died in 1734, the library was inventoried and the six concertos were valued at a relatively small sum. The manuscript was preserved through the eighteenth century in the Brandenburg-Prussian royal library; it was rediscovered in the 1840s by Siegfried Wilhelm Dehn, librarian of the Königliche Bibliothek in Berlin, and was published in 1850 by Dehn as part of the early Bach revival. The first modern performances of the complete set as we now know it date from the late nineteenth century; the broader concert-repertoire establishment of the set dates from the early twentieth century.

The bibliographic-and-reception story is striking. The most-famous orchestral set in the Bach catalogue, performed thousands of times now annually around the world, was probably never performed by the Margrave or his musicians; was placed in a library and forgotten for over a century; was rediscovered as part of the broader nineteenth-century Bach revival; and entered the standard concert repertoire only in the twentieth century. The Brandenburg Concertos are an instance of the broader Bach-reception pattern (Chapter 22): works that we now consider central to Western music were peripheral in their own time and became central only through the long nineteenth-and-twentieth-century reception process.

For Bach's own purposes in March 1721, the six concertos were probably already in his pocket. They were not composed for the Margrave; they were assembled from works already in Bach's portfolio and possibly already performed at Köthen. Scholarship suggests that several of the six were composed in the Köthen years (the BWV 1049, the BWV 1050, the BWV 1048 likely originated in this period) while one or two may have origins earlier, possibly in the Weimar years (the BWV 1046 contains material that may go back to Weimar). The 1721 dedication is the moment of formal assembly and presentation, not the moment of compositional origin.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen (Brandenburg Concerto No. 4 BWV 1049)

  1. **The three soloists in the opening (the concertino group).** The Brandenburg 4 is scored for a solo violin and two recorders (the _concertino_ , the soloist group), plus a string ensemble (the _ripieno_ , the "filling" or "tutti") with harpsichord continuo. The opening movement begins with the full ensemble (concertino plus ripieno) stating the principal theme, the _ritornello_ , in unison. About 30 seconds into the movement, the texture changes: the ripieno strings step back; the three soloists (violin and two recorders) emerge. Listen for the texture-change. From this point onward through the first movement, the music alternates between full-ensemble ritornello statements (when the principal theme returns in the tutti) and soloist passages (when the three soloists take the foreground with new or transformed material). This is the concerto-grosso principle: alternation between concertino and ripieno across the movement.

  2. **The recorders' duet.** Within the concertino group, the two recorders are paired, they often play parallel figures, sometimes in unison, sometimes in thirds, sometimes in echo. Listen to the two recorders specifically as a pair; they have an extensive duet-role across the first movement. The violin sometimes joins them, sometimes leads them, sometimes plays against them.

  3. **The second movement's contrasting character.** After the bright G major first movement, the second movement shifts into E minor (the relative minor) at a much slower tempo. The character is contemplative, almost mournful; the texture is more chamber-music-like (the soloists more foregrounded, the ripieno more restrained). This is the standard three-movement concerto-grosso pattern: fast outer movements in the home key, slow middle movement in a contrasting key. The contrast between the bright opening and the somber slow movement is one of the principal expressive resources of the concerto-grosso form.

#### One-sentence background

Composed at Köthen c. 1719-1721 (one of the six concertos assembled in March 1721 for the dedication to Christian Ludwig, Margrave of Brandenburg); a three-movement concerto for violin and two recorders (concertino) with strings and harpsichord continuo (ripieno) in G major, the three movements being Allegro, Andante, Presto, totaling about 16 minutes; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is Trevor Pinnock with the English Concert (Archiv, recorded 1982, the canonical period-instrument complete-Brandenburg recording for the 1980s-90s generation, on the Archiv label), chosen for the period instruments, the well-balanced concertino-ripieno relationships, and the canonical-HIP status.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Bach Brandenburg Concerto 4 BWV 1049 Pinnock English Concert" or "Bach Brandenburg 4 G major" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** (Ch 21 surveys the broader Brandenburg recording history):

Richter (Archiv 1967, mod-inst, warm and slower); Harnoncourt (Teldec 1964 mono + 1981-82 stereo, first HIP); Goebel (Archiv 1986-87, aggressive HIP); Gardiner (Erato 1982, dramatic HIP); Savall (Alia Vox 2014, late HIP).

For BWV 1047 specifically, the trumpet soloist matters — Crispian Steele-Perkins on Pinnock, David Blackadder on Gardiner. For BWV 1050, the harpsichord cadenza in mvt 1 is the structural climax — Pinnock himself takes it on his own set; Andreas Staier on Goebel takes it more aggressively.

### 8.2 The Listening Window

This section is for your second listen to the Brandenburg Concerto No. 4 BWV 1049. Timestamps refer to Pinnock's 1982 Archiv recording, which runs about 16 minutes 30 seconds across three movements.

#### First movement (Allegro, G major, about 8 minutes)

**0:00-0:30: The opening ritornello.** The full ensemble, three soloists (violin, two recorders) plus ripieno strings and harpsichord, states the principal theme in G major. The theme has a characteristic bright, dancing quality: a quick figure in the violins that ascends and then descends in a singing arc, with the recorders doubling the upper line in some moments and playing independently in others. The ritornello establishes G major firmly; the rhythmic feel is unmistakably concerto-grosso (a strong sense of forward motion, with regular harmonic-rhythm).

**About 0:30-1:30: The first soloist passage.** The ripieno steps back; the three soloists emerge. The solo violin takes the lead; the two recorders provide commentary in alternation. Listen for the moment of texture-change at about 0:30, the orchestra thins; the soloists become the focus. The material here is partly derived from the ritornello and partly free; the soloists develop figures that lead the music away from G major into related keys.

**About 1:30-2:00: A short ritornello return in a related key.** The full ensemble returns briefly with the principal theme, now in a related key (probably D major, the dominant). The ritornello-return is one of the structural pillars of the concerto-grosso form: the principal theme reappears periodically across the movement, marking the structural milestones, while the soloist passages between provide the developmental material.

**About 2:00-3:30: Extended soloist development.** The longest soloist passage of the movement. The two recorders take an extended duet-role here; the solo violin sometimes joins them, sometimes plays against them. The music moves through several keys (G major → D major → E minor → A minor → and back). The texture is chamber-music-like; the harpsichord continuo can be heard providing harmonic support throughout.

**About 3:30-4:00: Another ritornello return.** The full ensemble returns with the principal theme. The pattern is becoming clear: ritornello → soloist passage → ritornello → soloist passage, across the movement.

**About 4:00-7:30: Further soloist passages and ritornello returns.** The pattern continues, with the music moving through additional keys and with the soloists developing the material in various directions. The soloist passages here are longer and more contrapuntally elaborate than the early ones; Bach is using the movement's overall length to allow extended development.

**About 7:30-8:00: The final ritornello.** The principal theme returns in G major (the home key); the movement closes with a firm cadence on G major.

#### Second movement (Andante, E minor, about 4 minutes)

**0:00: The slow movement opens.** The character changes immediately: the key shifts from G major to E minor (the relative minor); the tempo slows; the texture becomes more transparent (the ripieno is more restrained; the soloists more foregrounded). The opening melody is in the recorders, with the violin and continuo providing accompaniment. The mood is contemplative, slightly mournful.

**About 0:30-2:00: The principal theme's development.** The melody passes between the recorders and the violin; the recorders sometimes play in canon (one entering and the other following at a short time-delay); the harmony moves through several minor-mode keys. Listen for the dialogue between the three soloists; the second movement is essentially a chamber-music piece embedded in the concerto framework.

**About 2:00-3:30: A more elaborated section.** The soloist textures become more chromatic; the harmonic motion more adventurous. The mournful character is sustained throughout; the movement does not brighten significantly until very near the end.

**About 3:30-4:00: The closing cadence.** The movement closes with a gradual harmonic preparation for the third movement's return to the home key; the final chord of the second movement is the dominant of G major (the home key), preparing for the immediate entry of the third movement.

#### Third movement (Presto, G major, about 5 minutes)

**0:00: The third movement opens.** The character changes again: the key returns to G major; the tempo accelerates dramatically (Presto, "very fast"); the texture becomes contrapuntally dense. The opening is, unusually for a concerto-grosso, a fugue. A single voice (typically the solo violin) states the fugue subject; subsequent voices enter with the subject at staggered times. The fugue subject is long and angular, a moderately quick figure that traces a distinctive melodic shape.

**About 0:30-2:00: The fugue's exposition and early development.** All five principal voices (the two recorders, the solo violin, and the two ripieno violin sections, first and second violins) state the subject in turn. The fugue is more contrapuntally complex than the BWV 565 fugue (Chapter 5) or the Capriccio BWV 992 fugue (Chapter 3); the entries are closer in time and the texture builds more quickly. Listen for the fugue-entries; track the subject as it passes from voice to voice.

**About 2:00-4:00: The fugue's middle and later development.** The fugue continues to develop through the movement; the soloists alternate the subject-statements with the ripieno; the harmony moves through a wider range of keys. The movement is a fast fugal finale in the concerto-grosso tradition (the _fugued finale_ is a Baroque convention; both Handel and Vivaldi used it; Bach pushes the fugal density further than either).

**About 4:00-5:00: The closing passages.** The fugue's final section returns to the home key G major; the texture becomes denser and faster as the closing cadence approaches. The final cadence is on G major with the full ensemble in tutti; the work ends decisively.

If your recording's total time differs from Pinnock's 16:30 by more than 15%, you may be hearing a substantially different performance tempo. Karl Richter (1967 Archiv) takes the Brandenburg 4 at about 18 minutes; Reinhard Goebel (1986-87 Archiv) takes it at about 15 minutes; Gardiner (1982 Erato) is close to Pinnock at about 16:30. The three-movement structure should be clear in every recording; the contrasts between the bright outer movements and the mournful inner movement are the form's principal expressive resource.

### 8.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The concerto-grosso form

The Brandenburg Concertos are _concerti grossi_ , concertos of the "large" kind, in which a small soloist group (the _concertino_) is set in alternation with a larger ensemble (the _ripieno_ , also called the _tutti_). The form developed in Italy in the late seventeenth century; Arcangelo Corelli (1653-1713) is generally regarded as the form's founder, with his Op. 6 set of twelve concerti grossi (published posthumously in 1714) the canonical model. Vivaldi developed the form further in the early eighteenth century, often with a single soloist (the _concerto solo_ form, a violin concerto, a flute concerto, a bassoon concerto) but sometimes with multi-soloist concertino groups.

Bach absorbed the Italian-concerto tradition through his Weimar-period Vivaldi transcriptions (Chapter 5 §5.4). His own concertos, the Brandenburg set, the Concerto for Two Violins BWV 1043, the keyboard concertos (mostly Leipzig-period transcriptions), draw on both the Corelli concertino-grosso tradition and the Vivaldi solo-concerto tradition. The Brandenburg set itself shows the range: some concertos have multi-instrument concertini (BWV 1049, BWV 1050, BWV 1047), some have single principal soloists (none in this set really, though the BWV 1050 effectively gives the harpsichord the leading-soloist role), some are all-ensemble works without a clear concertino-ripieno distinction (BWV 1048, BWV 1051).

The form's principal expressive resources are: the alternation between concertino and ripieno; the contrast between the smaller-textured soloist passages and the larger-textured tutti passages; the use of ritornello-form first movements (where the principal theme returns periodically across the movement in different keys); and the use of three-movement fast-slow-fast structures (the standard Vivaldian shape). The Brandenburg 4 uses all four of these resources straightforwardly; other Brandenburg concertos modify or extend the pattern in distinctive ways (the BWV 1048 has only two movements; the BWV 1051 has an unusual slow movement structure).

#### The Brandenburg 4's distinctive features

The Brandenburg 4 is notable within the set for several features.

**The two-recorders pairing in the concertino.** The recorders are paired throughout the work, often playing as a duet or in echo. The recorder pair functions as a single composite voice almost as much as it functions as two separate voices. This is unusual: most concerto-grosso concertini distinguish each soloist clearly; Bach's pairing of the recorders gives the concertino a different internal structure than typical.

**The solo violin's prominence.** The solo violin is the most-foregrounded individual voice in the concertino. The violin has long passages where it plays alone with continuo accompaniment; the recorders provide commentary; the ripieno is mostly silent. In some passages the concerto effectively becomes a violin concerto with recorder doubling. This makes the Brandenburg 4 the most violin-virtuoso of the set after the Brandenburg 2 (where the high trumpet is the principal soloist).

**The fugal finale.** The third movement is a fugue: unusual in concerto-grosso practice. Most concerto-grosso finales are dance-form movements (gigue, bourrée) or freer ritornello-form movements. Bach's choice to use a fugue as the finale gives the work a contrapuntally-tighter close than the typical concerto-grosso. The fugue is in five voices (the two recorders, the solo violin, the first ripieno violins, and the second ripieno violins) and is the most-elaborate fugue in the Brandenburg set.

**The E-minor slow movement.** The Andante in E minor is the most-contemplative slow movement in the Brandenburg set. The mood is mournful; the contrast with the bright G major outer movements is sharp. The slow movement is essentially a chamber-music piece for three soloists with light string accompaniment; the recorders carry the principal melodic material; the violin provides counter-melody and ornamentation.

#### The Brandenburg 2 BWV 1047 (briefly)

The Brandenburg Concerto No. 2 in F major is the most-famous individual concerto of the set, principally for the spectacular high-trumpet writing in the first and third movements. The concertino group is trumpet, recorder, oboe, and violin (four soloists of contrasting timbres); the ripieno is strings and harpsichord. The first movement (Allegro, F major, about 5 minutes) features the trumpet in the highest register, the so-called _clarino_ trumpet writing, with notes extending into the trumpet's most-difficult range. The slow second movement (Andante, D minor, about 4 minutes) drops the trumpet entirely; the recorder, oboe, and violin carry the contemplative slow movement alone. The fast third movement (Allegro assai, F major, about 3 minutes) brings back the trumpet for a brilliant fugal close.

The Brandenburg 2 first movement is one of three Bach selections on the Voyager Golden Record (1977); Ch 22 §22.9 treats the selection's significance for Bach reception.

For the listener: the trumpet writing in the Brandenburg 2 is what most listeners notice first. The trumpet plays in the _clarino_ register, the very high range of the natural trumpet (the trumpet without valves, used in Bach's time), and the writing is one of the most-difficult passages in the trumpet repertoire. Modern trumpet players use a smaller, higher-pitched trumpet (the _piccolo trumpet_) for this work; period-instrument players use a reproduction natural trumpet (a difficult instrument to play in tune at all, much less at this technical demand). The Crispian Steele-Perkins recording with Pinnock and the Wynton Marsalis recording with various ensembles (CBS 1980s) are well-regarded examples of the trumpet writing in performance.

#### The Brandenburg 5 BWV 1050 (briefly)

The Brandenburg Concerto No. 5 in D major is the longest of the set (about 21 minutes) and is structurally remarkable for the harpsichord cadenza in the first movement, a cadenza of about 3-4 minutes in which the harpsichord plays as a virtuoso soloist with the ripieno silent. The cadenza is the harpsichord's most-extensive solo passage in any pre-Classical work; it effectively transforms the harpsichord from a continuo instrument (which it normally is in concerto-grosso practice) into a leading concertante soloist. The Brandenburg 5 is sometimes called the first harpsichord concerto in the modern sense, the first work in which the harpsichord is treated as a leading soloist rather than as accompaniment.

The concertino of the Brandenburg 5 is flute, violin, and harpsichord; the ripieno is strings (without harpsichord, since the harpsichord is in the concertino). The three soloists alternate the principal material in the first and third movements; the harpsichord cadenza is the first movement's structural climax. The slow second movement is a contemplative trio (flute + violin + harpsichord) without ripieno, essentially a chamber-music movement within the concerto framework.

The Brandenburg 5 is the most-recorded individual concerto of the set after the No. 2. The harpsichord cadenza is the structural feature most often discussed in concert program notes; recordings vary in how aggressively the harpsichordist plays the cadenza (Pinnock, on his own instrument, takes the cadenza at a relatively restrained tempo; Andreas Staier with Goebel takes it more aggressively; Trevor Pinnock and Andreas Staier together are perhaps the two leading harpsichord-soloist readings).

#### Cross-piece links

The Brandenburg 4 (and the broader set) connects forward and backward to many works in this volume.

To the Brandenburg 3 in G major BWV 1048 (_Close Listening: Classical Music_ Chapter 7). The Brandenburg 3 is the most-recorded concerto of the set; _Close Listening: Classical Music_ Chapter 7 used it as the second anchor for the Baroque-warmup chapter alongside BWV 565. The two G-major Brandenburgs (No. 3 and No. 4) make an interesting pair: both in the same key, both bright in character, but with very different concertino structures (the No. 3 is all-strings with no separate concertino; the No. 4 has the violin-and-two-recorders concertino).

To the Cello Suites and Violin Partitas (Chapter 9). The Brandenburgs and the unaccompanied-string works represent the two principal sides of Bach's Köthen instrumental output: orchestral concertos on one side, unaccompanied solo-string works on the other. Both bodies of work are from approximately the same Köthen period (the late 1710s through about 1721).

To the cantata opening choruses and Passion choruses (Chapters 12, 13) and to the Leipzig harpsichord concertos (BWV 1052-1057, mostly Köthen-period transcriptions). Bach's later large-scale concerted vocal movements use the Vivaldian ritornello principle inherited from the Italian concerto-grosso tradition; the keyboard-as-concerto-soloist innovation of the Brandenburg 5 anticipated the Leipzig keyboard-concerto practice by more than a decade.

Chapter 20 §20.7 will collect the Italianate-concertante writing as a unifying thread; the Brandenburg Concertos are the volume's principal anchor for that thread.

### 8.4 The Stories

#### The Margrave Christian Ludwig of Brandenburg and the dedication

Christian Ludwig (1677-1734) was the seventh son of Frederick William, the Great Elector of Brandenburg-Prussia (1620-1688), and the youngest from his second marriage to Sophie Dorothea of Schleswig-Holstein. By 1721 Christian Ludwig was a senior member of the Brandenburg-Prussian royal family, half-uncle of the reigning King Frederick William I (the "Soldier King," ruling 1713-40), great-uncle of the future Frederick the Great. He held no political office; he was a courtly figure who maintained a substantial private establishment in Berlin including a private music collection and a small musical ensemble.

The Margrave was a known music-patron and a known collector of scores. His music library included works by Telemann, Heinichen, Pisendel, Reinhard Keiser, Antonio Caldara, and others, a substantial collection of contemporary Baroque music. The Bach dedication of 1721 added six Bach concertos to this collection; the manuscript was placed in the library and apparently used (or not used) as one collection-item among many.

What happened to the six concertos in the Margrave's hands is conjectural. There is no surviving record of any performance at the Margrave's establishment. Some scholars have argued that the Margrave's ensemble was probably too small to perform several of the concertos (the Brandenburg 1, in particular, requires three oboes, a bassoon, and two horns, plus strings, more wind players than the Margrave's typical ensemble would have included). Other scholars have argued that the Margrave's ensemble was perfectly adequate but that the concertos were simply not performed. The silence in the documentary record cannot be definitively interpreted.

When Christian Ludwig died in 1734, thirteen years after receiving the dedication, his estate was inventoried; the Brandenburg Concertos were valued at the modest sum of about 24 groschen (a few thaler total), suggesting that the concertos had not appreciated significantly in perceived value during the Margrave's possession. The manuscript passed into the Brandenburg-Prussian royal library where it remained for the next century.

The manuscript's preservation through this period is itself the contingent good fortune that allowed the Brandenburg Concertos to survive at all. If the Margrave's library had been dispersed at his death (as many private libraries were); if the Brandenburg-Prussian royal library had been damaged or destroyed in the eighteenth-century wars (the Seven Years War 1756-63 was particularly destructive of Brandenburg-Prussian institutional records); or if Siegfried Dehn had not catalogued the manuscript in the 1840s, any of these would have lost us the Brandenburg Concertos as we know them.

#### The Köthen ensemble and the performance question

A separate question is whether the Brandenburg Concertos were performed at Köthen during Bach's tenure. The Köthen ensemble (about 16-18 players, as described in Chapter 7) could have performed most of the six concertos but not all: the Brandenburg 1 requires three oboes, which may have stretched the Köthen wind section; some of the works require multiple violinists in the ripieno, which the Köthen string section could have provided.

The most-likely scenario, consistent with the available documentation, is that several of the Brandenburg concertos were performed at Köthen before the 1721 dedication, they had been composed for the Köthen ensemble's resources and would have been the natural repertoire for the court's chamber-music sessions and ceremonial occasions. The 1721 dedication was the moment of formal assembly into a presentation set for the Margrave; the individual concertos had compositional origins distributed across the Köthen years (and possibly earlier, for the Brandenburg 1 which has material that may go back to Weimar).

The Köthen performances would have featured Joseph Spiess as the principal violinist (he was the leading violinist in the Köthen ensemble; he probably played the violin solo in the Brandenburg 4 and Brandenburg 5); various recorder players for the Brandenburg 4 and 2; the court's trumpet player for the Brandenburg 2; Bach himself at the harpsichord (he played the harpsichord cadenza in the Brandenburg 5 personally, the harpsichord part is distinctively in Bach's idiomatic keyboard-writing). The Köthen ensemble was small enough that the Bach household members may have participated: Maria Barbara's death in 1720 limits her possible participation, but her younger sister Friedlena, who lived with the Bach family from before the move from Weimar, may have been involved as a vocalist for some of the secular cantatas Bach composed at Köthen.

#### Recording history briefly

The Brandenburgs have hundreds of recordings — Ch 21 surveys the principal lineages. For first orientation: Pinnock (Archiv 1982, HIP) plus Richter (Archiv 1967, mod-inst) — comparing the two on Brandenburg 4 specifically shows the range of plausible interpretive choices.

#### As a set or as individuals?

A judgment about how to engage with the Brandenburgs: they are routinely performed as individual concertos in standard concert programs (a single Brandenburg between other works) and as a complete set in dedicated Brandenburg programs. Both approaches are defensible; the volume's position is that the set is the work and the individual concertos are excerpts. Bach assembled six concertos with deliberately different instrumentations specifically to demonstrate the range of the concerto-grosso form; each concerto answers the others, with the variety as the structural argument. A single Brandenburg in isolation is excellent music; the set is something more — a compositional demonstration that lets each individual work be heard against the others' contrasting approaches. The recommendation: hear all six in sequence at least once (about two hours total), then return to individual ones as interest develops. The Pinnock 1982 set is the most-natural single-album entry point.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The Brandenburg Concertos were dedicated to Christian Ludwig, Margrave of Brandenburg, in March 1721. The dedication does not appear to have been performed at the Margrave's court; the manuscript was filed in his library and apparently forgotten; rediscovery came in the 1840s. The set's modern fame is entirely a product of the nineteenth-and-twentieth-century reception. What does this kind of reception history change about how we hear the works? Should we think of the Brandenburg Concertos as the music of the Margrave's library (deposited there in 1721 and forgotten for a century), or as the music of the twentieth-century concert repertoire (re-energized through performance traditions that Bach could not have anticipated)?"_

* _"No two of the six Brandenburg Concertos have the same instrumentation. The variety is deliberate, Bach is offering the Margrave six different demonstrations of what the concerto-grosso form can do. What kind of compositional thinking does this require? How is composing six concertos with deliberately-different instrumentations different from composing six string quartets (where the instrumental forces are fixed) or six symphonies (where the orchestral forces are largely standardized)? What does the variety give the listener that uniformity would not?"_

* _"The Brandenburg 4's third movement is a five-voice fugue, unusual as the finale of a concerto-grosso (most are dance-form or freer ritornello movements). Listen to it specifically against a Vivaldi concerto-grosso finale (any of L'estro armonico op. 3 works). What does Bach's choice of a fugal finale do that a dance-form finale does not, and what does it cost? Does the fugal density change the concerto's emotional arc?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Trevor Pinnock 1982 Archiv recording of the Brandenburg Concerto No. 4 BWV 1049 (or one of the alternatives listed in §8.1). Listen a second time and attend to these four concrete moments.

  1. **The concertino-ripieno alternation in the first movement (throughout the first 8 minutes).** Listen for the texture-changes between the full-ensemble ritornello statements and the three-soloist passages. Try to identify each return of the ripieno; the ritornello-pattern is the structural pillar of the movement. The principal theme returns four or five times across the first movement; can you count the entrances?

  2. **The two recorders as a paired duet (especially in the first and second movements).** Listen to the two recorders specifically as a pair; they often play parallel figures, sometimes in unison, sometimes in thirds, sometimes in echo. Try to follow one of the two recorders specifically for an extended passage (perhaps in the soloist sections of the first movement, or the recorder duet at the start of the second movement). Notice the harmonic relationships between the two recorders.

  3. **The slow movement's mournful character (the second movement, about 4 minutes).** Listen for the contrast between the bright G major outer movements and the somber E minor middle movement. Notice the chamber-music-like texture (the ripieno is restrained throughout); the recorders carry the principal melodic material; the violin and harpsichord provide commentary. The slow movement is the work's emotional center.

  4. **The fugal third movement (about 5 minutes).** Listen for the fugue's opening exposition, track the subject as it passes from one voice to the next. The five-voice exposition takes about a minute; once all five voices have entered with the subject, the fugue develops through episodes and additional subject-statements. The fugue is contrapuntally denser than the BWV 565 fugue (Chapter 5) and the Capriccio fugue (Chapter 3); your fugue-listening skill is being extended.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : a Vivaldi concerto from the _L'estro armonico_ op. 3 set (1711). The Concerto No. 8 in A minor for two violins and strings (RV 522): the concerto Bach transcribed for organ as BWV 593, is a natural pairing with the Brandenburg 4 because of the multi-soloist concertino structure. Recordings by Trevor Pinnock with the English Concert (Archiv 1980s) or by Fabio Biondi with Europa Galante (Virgin Classics 2000s) are standard.

**Listen** (comparison): another Brandenburg concerto, ideally one with very different instrumentation. The Brandenburg 6 BWV 1051 (in B♭ major, scored for two violas, two violas da gamba, cello, and continuo: no violins) has the most-unusual instrumentation of the set; the all-lower-strings texture gives the work a distinctive dark-warm sound very different from the bright violin-led Brandenburgs. About 17 minutes.

**Listen** (cross-volume): the Brandenburg 3 in G major BWV 1048: already encountered in _Close Listening: Classical Music_ Chapter 7. About 11 minutes. The Brandenburg 3 has only two movements (Allegro and Allegro, with the middle movement being a single sustained chord or a short improvised passage by the conductor, the score has only two written chord-tones for the middle movement). Compare the all-strings, no-soloists Brandenburg 3 with the violin-and-two-recorders Brandenburg 4; the contrast between the two concertos in the same key is one of the set's defining variety-demonstrations.

**Read** : Christoph Wolff, _Johann Sebastian Bach: The Learned Musician_ (Norton 2000), the chapter on the Köthen instrumental music. About 30 pages.

**Read** (specialized): Malcolm Boyd, _Bach: The Brandenburg Concertos_ (Cambridge Music Handbooks, Cambridge University Press, 1993). A short book (about 100 pages) devoted entirely to the Brandenburg set; the standard introductory monograph on the set in English. Worth reading if you want to spend more time with the Brandenburgs after the chapter.

---

## Chapter 9: The Cello Suites BWV 1007-1012 and the Violin Sonatas and Partitas BWV 1001-1006

The unaccompanied-string works of Bach's Köthen years are the central works of Western art music for a single melodic string instrument. The six Cello Suites BWV 1007-1012 (composed probably c. 1717-1723, with the Anna Magdalena fair copy made c. 1727-1731 at Leipzig as the primary surviving source) are the only major Baroque-period set of solo-cello compositions in the standard repertoire. The six Violin Sonatas and Partitas BWV 1001-1006 (autograph dated 1720, composed probably 1717-1720 at Köthen) are the central solo-violin works of the Western repertoire alongside the Beethoven and Brahms violin concertos and the Paganini Caprices. Both bodies of work were composed during Bach's six-year Köthen period; both bodies represent compositional and instrumental achievements that have stood without serious competition for three centuries.

This chapter does two things. First, it sets up the unaccompanied-string genre and the compositional principle that distinguishes Bach's approach: a single melodic instrument carrying what sounds like multiple voices through arpeggiation, chordal writing, and the polyphonic illusion. Second, it anchors deeply on four specific works: the Cello Suite No. 1 in G major BWV 1007 Prelude (the most universally recognized opening in cello literature); the Cello Suite No. 5 in C minor BWV 1011 (the scordatura suite with the A-string tuned down); the Cello Suite No. 6 in D major BWV 1012 (probably written for a five-string instrument); and the Chaconne from the Violin Partita No. 2 in D minor BWV 1004 fifth movement (the great 15-minute variations-on-a-ground-bass work that is the single largest movement for unaccompanied violin in the repertoire).

The chapter is long because the unaccompanied-string output is large and because the listening discipline involved is technically substantial. By the end you will have heard the Cello Suite No. 1 Prelude, the Chaconne, and excerpts from the other suites and partitas, and you will have developed the listening habit for single-instrument polyphony that the volume's contrapuntal-listening thread requires.

### 9.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote, the Anna Magdalena manuscript

Köthen, c. 1727-1731. Anna Magdalena Bach is at her desk in the Bach household, by this time the family has moved to Leipzig (April 1723) and Anna Magdalena is in her mid-to-late twenties, mother of several children of her own and stepmother to the four surviving children from Maria Barbara's first marriage. She is preparing a fair copy of a set of works by her husband: the six Cello Suites BWV 1007-1012, in a manuscript that will become the only surviving complete source for the suites from anywhere near Bach's lifetime. Bach's own autograph manuscript of the Cello Suites has been lost, when, how, and why is not known; perhaps it was lost in the eighteenth century; perhaps it remained in the family but was never inherited along with the major Leipzig estate. Whatever the circumstances, Anna Magdalena's fair copy is what we have.

The manuscript is in Anna Magdalena's careful hand. The title page reads, in German: _6\. Suites a Violoncello Solo senza Basso composées par Sr J. S. Bach Maître de Chapelle_ , "Six Suites for Violoncello Solo without Bass, composed by Mr. J. S. Bach Master of the Chapel." The "Maître de Chapelle" title refers to Bach's Köthen Kapellmeister position; the fair copy was probably made before Bach's Leipzig appointment fully replaced the Köthen title in his public self-identification. The manuscript runs about 100 pages of close-notated music; Anna Magdalena's hand is clear and steady throughout, with relatively few corrections; she was a serious copyist who knew the music intimately.

The Cello Suites were not published in Bach's lifetime. They were not part of the regular concert repertoire in the eighteenth or nineteenth centuries. They survived in the Anna Magdalena fair copy and in a few derivative manuscript copies; one early printed edition (Janet et Cotelle, Paris, c. 1825) made them nominally available to professional cellists but did not establish them in the active repertoire. The man who rediscovered the suites for the modern repertoire was Pablo Casals (1876-1973), the great Catalan cellist, who came across an old copy of the Janet et Cotelle edition at a music shop in Barcelona around 1890 when he was thirteen years old. Casals spent the next twelve years studying the suites privately; he first performed one of the suites in public in 1901; he gradually introduced them into his concert repertoire over the following decades; he recorded the complete set for EMI between 1936 and 1939, in the recording-sessions that have served as the suites' principal twentieth-century document.

The Casals story is a particularly clear example of the suites' twentieth-century revival pattern. Before Casals, the Cello Suites were known to specialist cellists but were not standard repertoire; cellists considered them too austere, too difficult, too lacking in virtuoso display to function as solo concert pieces. After Casals's 1930s recordings and his sustained advocacy across the decades that followed, the Cello Suites became the central solo-cello repertoire of the twentieth and twenty-first centuries; every professional cellist now learns them as foundational works; multiple complete-cycle recordings have been made by virtually every major cellist of the past sixty years.

The Violin Sonatas and Partitas have a different reception history. Bach's autograph manuscript of these works survives intact (it is dated 1720 in Bach's hand and bears the title _Sei Solo a Violino senza Basso accompagnato_ , "Six Solos for Violin without Bass Accompaniment"). The works were known throughout the nineteenth century, Mendelssohn included the Chaconne in his Berlin concert programs, and Brahms made an arrangement of the Chaconne for piano left hand in 1879, but became fully standard repertoire only with Joseph Joachim's late-nineteenth-century performances and the early-twentieth-century recordings by Yehudi Menuhin (a 1934 recording at age 18) and Adolf Busch (1929-30).

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen, Cello Suite No. 1 Prelude

  1. **The arpeggiated single line.** The Prelude is in a single melodic line, the cello plays one note at a time throughout (no chords, no double-stops). The single line moves rapidly through a series of broken chords (arpeggios, the notes of a chord played one after the other rather than simultaneously). The arpeggiated single-line writing creates an _implied polyphony_ : the listener's ear assembles the arpeggiated notes into chords, even though the cello is technically playing one note at a time.

  2. **The pedal-point sections.** Several passages in the Prelude have the cello holding a single sustained low note (the "pedal point") while the higher notes move above. Listen for these moments, they appear several times across the 2-3-minute Prelude. The pedal-point effect strengthens the implied polyphony: the sustained bass note clearly anchors the harmony while the upper notes move.

  3. **The G-major framework.** The Prelude is in G major throughout, with brief excursions through related keys (D major, E minor) before returning home.
