The music's character is bright and dancing, with a sense of forward motion; the implied polyphony creates harmonic richness despite the technically-monophonic surface.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen, Chaconne from Partita No. 2 BWV 1004

  1. **The 4-bar bass theme stated at the opening.** The Chaconne opens with a 4-bar statement of its bass theme, a slow descending-and-rising figure in D minor that will serve as the harmonic foundation for the entire 15-minute work. The bass theme is stated with chordal accompaniment in the violin (the violin plays full chords by stopping multiple strings simultaneously, a technique that requires considerable bow-arm control on the violin). Listen for the bass theme; memorize its shape; it will return 64 times across the next 15 minutes in variations.

  2. **The 64 variations.** After the opening bass theme statement, the variations begin. Each variation is 4 bars long (occupying one statement of the bass theme); the variations are continuous without breaks. The first variation is texturally simple; subsequent variations add increasing rhythmic and contrapuntal complexity. About two-thirds of the way through the work (around variation 35 or so), the key shifts from D minor to D major for a section of variations in the brighter mode; the variations then return to D minor for the closing third of the work.

  3. **The chordal writing on the violin.** The Chaconne uses the violin's chordal capabilities extensively. The violin plays double-stops (two notes simultaneously), triple-stops (three notes simultaneously, requiring the bow to cross three strings), and quadruple-stops (four notes simultaneously, played as broken chords because the violin cannot bow all four strings at once). The chordal writing creates a full harmonic texture that approaches what a multi-voice keyboard piece would have. The 15-minute Chaconne is essentially a polyphonic-keyboard piece written for unaccompanied violin.

#### One-sentence backgrounds

**Cello Suite No. 1 in G major BWV 1007** : composed at Köthen probably c. 1717-1720, surviving primarily in the Anna Magdalena fair copy made at Leipzig c. 1727-31 (the dating is estimated from paper-and-watermark analysis and is not definitively established), six dance movements (Prelude, Allemande, Courante, Sarabande, two Minuets, Gigue) totaling about 18-20 minutes; the **Primary Recording** for the suites is Pablo Casals (EMI, 1936-39, the recording cycle that established the suites in the modern repertoire), chosen for its canonical status and for Casals's sustained advocacy of the suites.

**Cello Suite No. 5 in C minor BWV 1011** : composed at Köthen probably c. 1720, with the unusual scordatura tuning (the A-string is tuned down a tone to G), six dance movements totaling about 26-28 minutes; for this suite the Yo-Yo Ma 1998 Sony recording is a useful alternative (the second of Ma's three complete-cycle recordings, in which Ma takes a more interpretively-engaged approach than in his earlier 1983 cycle).

**Cello Suite No. 6 in D major BWV 1012** : composed at Köthen probably c. 1720, probably written for a five-string instrument (most likely a viola pomposa or related instrument), six dance movements totaling about 32-35 minutes; for this suite Anner Bylsma's 1992 Sony recording on a five-string instrument is a useful alternative for hearing what the suite sounds like on the original-style instrument.

**Chaconne from Violin Partita No. 2 in D minor BWV 1004** : composed at Köthen probably 1717-1720 (autograph dated 1720), the fifth movement of the Partita No. 2 (the partita's other four movements are Allemande, Courante, Sarabande, Gigue, with the Chaconne added as a fifth movement uniquely in the violin set), about 13-16 minutes; the **Primary Recording** for the violin works is Henryk Szeryng (DG, 1967 complete cycle, modern instrument), chosen for the canonical-modern-violin tradition and the clear articulation of the polyphonic voices.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Bach Cello Suite 1 G major BWV 1007 Casals" or "Bach Cello Suites Casals 1936" EN: "Bach Chaconne Partita 2 D minor BWV 1004 Szeryng" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** (Ch 21 surveys the broader solo-string recording traditions):

Cello Suites: Yo-Yo Ma (CBS 1983 + Sony 1998 + Sony 2018, three cycles); Anner Bylsma (Sony 1979 + 1992, period-style); Pierre Fournier (Archiv 1960-61, French school); Janos Starker (BIS 1992); Mstislav Rostropovich (EMI 1991); Steven Isserlis (Hyperion 2007); Mischa Maisky (DG 1985 + 1999).

Violin Sonatas and Partitas: Itzhak Perlman (EMI 1986-88, big-tone soloist); Hilary Hahn (Sony 1997 partial + Sony 2018 partial); Sergiu Luca (Nonesuch 1977, first complete HIP); Rachel Podger (Channel Classics 1999, contemporary HIP standard); Christian Tetzlaff (Virgin 1993 + Hänssler 2005 + Ondine 2017); Yehudi Menuhin (HMV 1934-35, recorded at age 18); Joseph Szigeti (Vanguard 1955-56).

### 9.2 The Listening Window

This section offers shorter Listening Window treatments for the two principal anchor works (the Cello Suite No. 1 Prelude and the D-minor Chaconne); the other anchored pieces (Cello Suite No. 5, Cello Suite No. 6) get briefer mentions in §9.3.

#### Cello Suite No. 1 Prelude, Listening Window (timings refer to Casals 1936-39, ~2 minutes 30 seconds)

**0:00-0:30: The opening arpeggios in G major.** Casals enters with the famous opening figure, an ascending broken-chord arpeggiation in G major. The first note is the low G; the second the D above; the third the B; the fourth the high G. The four notes outline the G major chord. The figure then descends and the pattern continues for several bars, the cello tracing broken chords throughout. Listen for the implied harmony: even though the cello plays one note at a time, your ear assembles the broken chords into perceived chords.

**About 0:30-1:00: Modulating arpeggios.** The arpeggios continue but the implied harmony begins to shift through related keys. The music briefly touches D major (the dominant), then moves through other key areas, with the arpeggio pattern as the constant texture and the implied harmony as the moving element.

**About 1:00-1:30: The pedal-point section.** Listen for a moment when the cello holds a sustained low note (the pedal point) while higher notes move above. The pedal-point is a structural moment in the Prelude, it grounds the harmony firmly while the surface continues to move. The effect strengthens the implied-polyphony illusion: the listener clearly hears a sustained bass-line and a moving upper voice.

**About 1:30-2:30: The buildup to the cadence.** The arpeggios continue to develop; the harmony moves through additional keys; the implied counterpoint becomes denser. The closing passages build harmonic tension; the final cadence on G major arrives around 2:30. Casals takes the Prelude at a moderate tempo; many modern cellists take it slightly faster.

#### Chaconne from Partita No. 2, Listening Window (timings refer to Szeryng 1967, ~13 minutes 30 seconds)

**0:00-0:20: The opening bass-theme statement.** The Chaconne opens with the 4-bar bass theme stated by the violin with chordal accompaniment. The bass theme descends from D through C# down to A, then ascends through B to D. The chords above outline the harmonic framework. Listen for the bass theme; you should be able to hear its descending-then-rising shape clearly. Memorize the theme; it will return 64 times across the next 13 minutes.

**About 0:20-3:00: The first major group of variations (variations 1-15 or so).** The variations begin. Each variation occupies one 4-bar statement of the bass theme; the variations are continuous without breaks. The early variations are texturally relatively simple, single melodic lines, double-stops in places. Listen for the underlying bass theme as it repeats; try to track it across the variations.

**About 3:00-6:00: The variations grow more elaborate.** The variations become more rhythmically and texturally complex. Some variations use rapid running passages (sixteenth notes or thirty-second notes); some use chordal writing; some use a mix. The harmonic motion remains anchored on the repeating bass theme.

**About 6:00-9:00: The middle major section, including the shift to D major.** Around the middle of the work (the exact moment varies between recordings; in Szeryng around 6:30), the key shifts from D minor to D major. The character changes immediately, brighter, more open, with a different emotional weight. The D-major section runs for about 20 variations (about 3 minutes); the harmonic-textural variety continues but in the brighter mode.

**About 9:00-12:00: The return to D minor and the late variations.** The key returns to D minor for the closing section of the work. The variations grow more contrapuntally dense and more emotionally weighty in the closing section; the harmonic motion stays anchored on the bass theme but the variations push closer to the limits of what the unaccompanied violin can sustain.

**About 12:00-13:30: The closing variations and final cadence.** The variations reach their textural climax in the closing passages; the cadence arrives with a return to the work's opening bass-theme statement (a structural symmetry, the work ends where it began, with the bass theme stated plainly with chordal accompaniment). The final cadence is on D minor (with the Picardy-third major-mode closing, the work ends on a D major chord rather than D minor).

If your recording's total time differs from Szeryng's 13:30 by more than 15%, you may be hearing a substantially different performance. Hilary Hahn's 1997 Sony reading runs closer to 14:30; Rachel Podger's 1999 HIP reading runs about 13 minutes; Perlman's 1986-88 reading runs about 14 minutes; Joseph Szigeti's 1955-56 reading runs about 15 minutes (slow and weighty). The variation structure should be clear across recordings.

### 9.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The polyphonic illusion on a single melodic instrument

The central compositional principle of the unaccompanied-string works is the _polyphonic illusion_ , the technique by which a single melodic instrument can imply multiple voices through arpeggiation, chordal writing, and rhetorical organization. The cello and the violin are each technically monophonic instruments (they can play one note at a time, or chord notes in close succession), but careful compositional writing can give the impression of two, three, or four independent voices moving simultaneously.

The principal techniques are:

**Arpeggiation.** The notes of a chord played one after the other rather than simultaneously. The listener's ear assembles the arpeggiated notes into a perceived chord. The Cello Suite No. 1 Prelude is built almost entirely on arpeggiation; the cello plays one note at a time but the listener hears chord-progressions.

**Chordal writing (double-stops, triple-stops, quadruple-stops).** The violin and cello can play multiple notes simultaneously by stopping multiple strings and bowing them together. A double-stop is two notes simultaneously; a triple-stop is three notes simultaneously; a quadruple-stop is four notes simultaneously (played as a quickly-broken chord, since the bow cannot easily sustain four-note chords). The Chaconne uses chordal writing extensively to create full harmonic textures.

**Implied polyphony through rhetorical organization.** A single melodic line can imply multiple voices through phrasing: short rapid figures alternating with longer sustained notes; the rapid figures register as one voice (an active "upper voice") while the sustained notes register as another voice (a slower-moving "bass" or "alto" line). The fugues in the violin Sonatas BWV 1001, 1003, 1005 use this technique extensively; the fugue subject appears in different "voices" (different registers and rhythmic patterns) and the listener perceives multiple contrapuntal lines even though the violin plays one note at a time at most moments.

**Implied bass-line through pedal points.** A sustained low note (the pedal point) creates a clear "bass voice"; faster notes above the pedal create the "upper voice." The Cello Suite No. 1 Prelude has several pedal-point sections that strengthen the implied two-voice texture.

Combined, these techniques let Bach write what amount to polyphonic keyboard pieces for unaccompanied string instruments. The Chaconne, in particular, is essentially a multi-voice keyboard piece transcribed for solo violin, the harmonic richness and contrapuntal density rival a keyboard fugue, even though the technical surface is a single bowed instrument.

For the listener: the polyphonic illusion is hard to hear without prior attention. A casual listener often hears the unaccompanied-string works as monophonic-with-occasional-chords; the implied polyphony does not register. The listening discipline involves trying to hear the implied voices specifically, the upper voice, the middle voice, the bass voice, and following each as a separate compositional line. The technique is a more demanding extension of the four-voice listening you developed on "Sheep May Safely Graze" in Chapter 2; there the four voices were sounded literally; here they are implied. The skill transfers but requires more concentration.

#### The Cello Suite as dance suite

Each of the six Cello Suites follows the standard Baroque dance-suite structure: a Prelude followed by five or six dance movements (Allemande, Courante, Sarabande, two optional gallantries, Minuets, Bourrées, or Gavottes, and Gigue). The form is inherited from the French clavecin tradition (Chambonnières, Louis Couperin); Bach uses it across his keyboard suites (French Suites, English Suites, Partitas) and across the Cello Suites and Violin Partitas.

The standard dance movements have characteristic tempos and meters:

* **Prelude** : free preluding; no fixed meter or character; functions as an introduction.
* **Allemande** : moderate tempo, 4/4 meter, often beginning with an upbeat figure; the allemande was originally a slow processional dance in German tradition.
* **Courante** : faster tempo, 3/4 or 6/4 meter; "courante" means "running"; the dance has a characteristic running rhythmic pattern.
* **Sarabande** : slow tempo, 3/4 meter with a strong second beat; the sarabande was originally a Spanish-Latin-American dance (sometimes spelled "zarabanda"); by Bach's time it had become a deeply expressive slow movement, often the emotional center of the suite.
* **Optional gallantries** (Minuets, Bourrées, Gavottes): moderate tempos, often in pairs (Minuet I and Minuet II, played as ABA with the second minuet as the trio).
* **Gigue** : fast tempo, often in 6/8 or 12/8 meter; the gigue is the closing dance, typically with a buoyant character; originally derived from the English jig.

The six Cello Suites are in six different keys, organized in a deliberate progression: No. 1 G major, No. 2 D minor, No. 3 C major, No. 4 E♭ major, No. 5 C minor, No. 6 D major. The keys move through the principal scale-degrees of the C-major scale; the affective range moves from the bright pastoral G major of No. 1 to the dark minor-mode of No. 5 (the only suite in a flat minor key) to the brilliant D major of No. 6.

Each suite has its own character. The No. 1 in G major is the simplest texturally and serves as the entry-point for most listeners. The No. 3 in C major has perhaps the most-celebrated Bourrée (a dance movement that has become a standalone concert piece in the broader repertoire). The No. 5 in C minor uses scordatura, the A-string is tuned down to G, which gives the cello a darker, more resonant sound and allows certain chordal voicings that the standard tuning does not permit. The No. 6 in D major is the longest and most-virtuoso, with extensive multiple-stop writing; the work was probably composed for a five-string instrument (a viola pomposa or similar), which would have made the high register easier than on the standard four-string cello.

#### The Cello Suite No. 5 (the scordatura suite, briefly)

The Cello Suite No. 5 in C minor BWV 1011 is the only Cello Suite that uses _scordatura_ , non-standard tuning. The cello's A-string is tuned down a tone to G (the other three strings are tuned normally, G, D, G low to high becomes G, D, G, G with the top string moved down). The scordatura tuning has two effects: the cello's overall sound becomes darker and more resonant (the lowered top string lets the lower strings ring sympathetically); and certain chordal voicings become possible (the cello can now play chord-voicings that involve the lowered top string in specific harmonic relationships not available in standard tuning).

The C-minor suite has a distinctive character. The Prelude is a long French-overture-style movement (a slow majestic opening followed by a faster fugal section); the work has an extensive Allemande and Courante; the Sarabande is the emotional center, one of the most-extraordinary Sarabandes in the cycle, a slow contemplative movement with deeply expressive chordal writing. Bach himself transcribed the Cello Suite No. 5 for solo lute (BWV 995), making the suite the only one to survive in two of his own versions; the lute transcription is sometimes performed and recorded.

For the listener: the No. 5 Sarabande is one of the most-celebrated single movements in the Cello Suites. About 4-5 minutes; slow, contemplative, with sustained chords throughout; very different from the dancing brightness of the No. 1. A recording by Yo-Yo Ma (any of his three cycles), Anner Bylsma, or Steven Isserlis will give you the work; the Casals 1939 recording (one of the latest of his complete cycle's sessions) is the canonical document.

#### The Cello Suite No. 6 (the five-string instrument question, briefly)

The Cello Suite No. 6 in D major BWV 1012 was probably composed for an instrument with five strings rather than the standard four. The work uses the cello's highest register extensively, passages that go above the standard cello's comfortable range and that fit naturally on a five-string instrument with the additional high string. The specific instrument Bach had in mind has been debated for two centuries; the most-common scholarly identification is the _viola pomposa_ (a five-string instrument played on the arm like a viola but with the deeper range of a small cello). Other identifications include the _violoncello piccolo_ (a five-string small cello, attested in some Bach cantatas) and a now-lost five-string cello variant specific to Bach's circle.

On a modern four-string cello the No. 6 is among the most-technically-demanding works in the cellist's repertoire, the high passages require difficult thumb-position playing that the original five-string instrument would have made simpler. Some modern recordings use a five-string instrument (Anner Bylsma's 1992 Sony recording is the canonical period-instrument document; Pieter Wispelwey's recordings have used five-string instruments for the No. 6); other recordings use the standard four-string cello and let the difficulties show.

For the listener: the No. 6 is the most-virtuoso of the Cello Suites; about 32-35 minutes total; the longest of the six. The Allemande and the Gigue are the principal display pieces; the Sarabande is contemplative but on a different scale from the No. 5 Sarabande (the No. 6 Sarabande is more flowing, less brooding). A recording by Bylsma or Wispelwey on five-string instrument is worth seeking out for the original-style sound.

#### The Violin Partitas, and the Chaconne, in deeper context

The six Violin Sonatas and Partitas BWV 1001-1006 follow an alternating pattern: BWV 1001 is a Sonata in G minor; BWV 1002 is a Partita in B minor; BWV 1003 is a Sonata in A minor; BWV 1004 is a Partita in D minor; BWV 1005 is a Sonata in C major; BWV 1006 is a Partita in E major. The Sonatas follow the _sonata da chiesa_ (church-sonata) pattern: four movements alternating slow-fast-slow-fast, with the second movement being a long fugue. The Partitas follow the dance-suite pattern (similar to the Cello Suites).

The Partita No. 2 in D minor BWV 1004 is the most-celebrated of the violin works because of its fifth movement, the Chaconne. The first four movements of the Partita (Allemande, Courante, Sarabande, Gigue) are conventional dance-suite movements totaling about 16 minutes. The Chaconne added as the fifth movement is about 13-15 minutes by itself, almost as long as all the other movements combined. The Chaconne has no counterpart in any of the other violin Partitas; it is unique to the D-minor Partita and stands as the largest single movement in the unaccompanied-string output.

The Chaconne's compositional principle is the same as the Passacaglia BWV 582 (Chapter 6), variations over a fixed bass-and-harmonic pattern. The Chaconne's bass theme is shorter (4 bars vs the Passacaglia's 8 bars); the number of variations is larger (about 64 vs the Passacaglia's 20); the overall length is roughly comparable (the Chaconne's 13-15 minutes vs the Passacaglia's 14). The Chaconne is the violin counterpart of the Passacaglia: same compositional family, different instrument, similar architectural scale.

The Chaconne's place in the violin literature is unique. It has been arranged for piano (Brahms made a famous left-hand-only arrangement in 1879; Busoni made a two-hand arrangement that is the more-commonly-performed piano version); it has been arranged for orchestra (several Romantic-era orchestrators); it has been arranged for guitar, lute, and various ensemble combinations. The arrangements multiply because the Chaconne's compositional depth is felt to transcend any specific instrumental medium. The original violin version remains the central document.

A particular interpretive tradition has read the Chaconne as a memorial work for Maria Barbara Bach (who died in July 1720, the year of the Partita's autograph dating). The interpretation rests on the alignment of dates (the autograph is dated 1720, Maria Barbara died July 1720) and on the work's emotional weight (the Chaconne's gravity has been read as personal grief). The interpretation has been popularized in books, essays, and program notes for decades. Modern scholarship treats the interpretation cautiously: the autograph's 1720 date does not specify which months of 1720; the Chaconne's emotional weight does not require a specific biographical anchor; the broader pattern of attributing major works to specific personal events in a composer's life is generally suspect. The Chaconne may be a memorial work for Maria Barbara, or it may not be; the available evidence does not settle the question. The listener can entertain the biographical reading as one possibility among others without treating it as established fact.

#### Notable cellists and violinists

The cello and violin works have a long and distinguished recording history; a complete summary is impossible in chapter scope. Chapter 21 will give the recording history more extensively. Briefly here:

**Cellists** : Pablo Casals (1876-1973, the Catalan cellist whose 1936-39 EMI recordings established the Cello Suites in the modern repertoire); Yo-Yo Ma (born 1955, three complete cycles: CBS 1983, Sony 1998, Sony 2018); Anner Bylsma (1934-2019, the Dutch period-instrument cellist; Sony 1979 + 1992); Pierre Fournier (1906-1986, the French school; Archiv 1960-61); Mstislav Rostropovich (1927-2007, the Russian school; EMI 1991); Janos Starker (1924-2013, multiple recordings including BIS 1992); Mischa Maisky (born 1948, DG 1985 + 1999); Steven Isserlis (born 1958, Hyperion 2007); Pieter Wispelwey (born 1962, Channel Classics 2012 third cycle).

**Violinists** : Yehudi Menuhin (1916-1999, HMV 1934-35 the early-twentieth-century document); Joseph Szigeti (1892-1973, Vanguard 1955-56); Nathan Milstein (1903-1992, multiple recordings); Henryk Szeryng (1918-1988, DG 1967); Arthur Grumiaux (1921-1986, Philips 1960-61); Itzhak Perlman (born 1945, EMI 1986-88); Hilary Hahn (born 1979, Sony 1997 + 2018); Sergiu Luca (1943-2010, Nonesuch 1977, first complete HIP cycle); Rachel Podger (born 1968, Channel Classics 1999, the contemporary HIP standard); Christian Tetzlaff (born 1966, multiple cycles); Gidon Kremer (born 1947, ECM 2005); Isabelle Faust (born 1972, Harmonia Mundi 2009-12).

The volume's recommended first orientation is Casals 1936-39 for the cello suites and Szeryng 1967 for the violin works; once these are heard, Bylsma 1992 (cello, HIP) and Podger 1999 (violin, HIP) are the recommended period-instrument alternatives.

#### Cross-piece links

The Cello Suites and Violin Partitas connect forward to many works in this volume.

To the Brandenburg Concertos (Chapter 8). Both bodies of work are from the same Köthen period; the unaccompanied-string works and the orchestral concertos together represent the two principal sides of Bach's Köthen instrumental output.

To the WTC Book 1 (Chapter 10). The fugues in the violin Sonatas BWV 1001, 1003, 1005 are unaccompanied-instrument fugues; the WTC keyboard fugues are the parallel keyboard-instrument fugues. Comparing the two, the violin Sonata G-minor fugue with a WTC G-minor fugue, for instance, shows how Bach's fugal voice operates across different instrumental media.

To the variation-over-fixed-bass family across the catalogue. The Chaconne is the violin counterpart of the Passacaglia BWV 582 (Chapter 6, same scale on organ); the Goldberg Variations (Chapter 17) are the keyboard late-period harmonically-elaborated sibling; the Crucifixus of the B Minor Mass (Chapter 14) is the vocal version at maximum grief, with descending chromatic bass.

Chapter 20 will collect the variation-over-repeating-bass family as a unifying thread (in §20.4 the canon and §20.7 the Italianate-concertante writing are nearby threads). The Cello Suites and Violin Partitas are the volume's solo-string anchors for the multi-voice-on-a-single-instrument compositional principle.

### 9.4 The Stories

#### The Casals rediscovery (briefly)

Pablo Casals (1876-1973) was the central figure in the modern revival of the Cello Suites. Born in Catalonia, Casals had begun studying cello as a child; his discovery of the Cello Suites at a Barcelona music shop around 1890 (the standard date; some accounts give 1889 or 1891) at age thirteen is the founding story of the modern Cello Suites tradition. Casals himself recorded an account of the discovery in his late-life conversations with Albert Kahn (published as _Joys and Sorrows_ in 1970): he was walking with his father, looking for repertoire for a cello recital, when he came across the old printed edition of the Bach suites at a music shop. He took the book home and began studying it; he sustained the study for twelve years before performing one of the suites publicly in 1901. The recordings (1936-39, EMI, at Casals's chosen studio in Paris and London) were made when Casals was in his late fifties; they document a mature performance practice developed across forty years of private study.

The Casals recordings have several characteristic features. The tempos are deliberate, often slower than modern HIP readings; the phrasing is long-breathed; the dynamic range is restrained (within the limits of 1930s recording technology, which compresses dynamics by modern standards); the chordal passages are full and resonant. Casals's reading establishes a particular kind of nobility for the suites, austere, dignified, deeply serious. The reading is not the only valid approach; many modern cellists (Yo-Yo Ma, Bylsma, Isserlis) take very different approaches. But Casals's reading remains the central twentieth-century document.

#### The Anna Magdalena manuscript (in more detail)

The Anna Magdalena manuscript fair-copy of the Cello Suites is the primary surviving source; Bach's own autograph is lost. The manuscript was made probably between 1727 and 1731 at Leipzig, as part of Anna Magdalena's broader copying activity in the Bach household. Anna Magdalena copied many Bach works across the Köthen and Leipzig years, the Violin Sonatas and Partitas (her copy is the secondary source after Bach's autograph); various cantata parts; many keyboard pieces. The Cello Suites copy is one of her major contributions to the surviving Bach corpus.

The manuscript has some scholarly issues. Anna Magdalena's copying is generally accurate but contains occasional errors; she sometimes used a slightly different ornamentation notation than Bach's autographs do (where they survive); there are passages where her bowing-and-articulation marks may not reflect Bach's intentions precisely. Modern editorial scholarship has worked to reconstruct the original Bach text behind Anna Magdalena's copy by comparing it with derivative manuscripts (other eighteenth-century copies, also derived from Bach's lost autograph but through different chains of transmission) and with broader Bach practice. The current critical editions (Bärenreiter and others) provide a scholarly text that incorporates the Anna Magdalena source as the primary reference plus corrections derived from comparative scholarship.

The manuscript is now in the Staatsbibliothek zu Berlin (the Berlin State Library) along with most of the other surviving Bach manuscripts. It is accessible to scholars by appointment; the music is widely available in modern editions for any cellist who wants to play the suites.

#### Modern vs period instrument: a position on the Cello Suites

The Cello Suites are routinely played on both the modern four-string cello (the descendant of the eighteenth-century instrument with substantial design changes since) and on period-style Baroque cellos. The polyphonic-illusion compositional principle works on both; the actual recordings (Casals on modern, Bylsma on period, Yo-Yo Ma on modern, Wispelwey on period) demonstrate that both can produce major Bach performances.

A judgment worth stating: the volume's position is that period-instrument recordings give the listener access to the specific sonic conditions Bach would have known, and the period gut strings produce overtone behavior the modern steel strings cannot, but the modern cello reading is not historically wrong — it is a contemporary continuation of a centuries-old tradition of playing the suites on whatever cello the cellist actually has. Casals's 1936-39 modern-cello recordings did not need to be on a period instrument to function as the foundational document of the suites' modern reception. The HIP argument that period instruments are the only correct vehicle is partial; the period instrument is one historically-grounded choice among several, not the uniquely-right one. For first listening, the volume recommends Casals 1936-39 (modern, historical foundation) plus Bylsma 1992 (period, scholarly standard); hear both before deciding which approach speaks to you.

#### Cross-volume connection: the Gavotte en Rondeaux

The Partita No. 3 in E major BWV 1006 Gavotte en Rondeaux (in Arthur Grumiaux's Philips recording) is one of three Bach selections on the Voyager Golden Record — Ch 22 §22.9 treats the selection's significance. For this chapter the practical point: the Gavotte is worth seeking out as a complement to the D-minor Chaconne. The two pieces represent opposite poles of the violin Partita output — the bright, dancing 3-minute Gavotte vs the dark, contemplative 15-minute Chaconne — and hearing them together shows the affective range of the unaccompanied-violin set. Recordings: the Grumiaux is the Voyager-selected reading; Szeryng, Hahn, Podger, and Tetzlaff are equally good alternatives.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The chapter argues that the unaccompanied-string works create a 'polyphonic illusion', a single melodic instrument implies multiple voices through arpeggiation, chordal writing, and rhetorical organization. Casual listeners often hear these works as monophonic-with-occasional-chords; the implied polyphony does not register without attention. Listen to the Cello Suite No. 1 Prelude again with the polyphonic-illusion specifically in mind. Can you hear an 'upper voice' (the rapid surface notes) and a 'bass voice' (the implied harmonic foundation)? Or does the listening collapse into a single line?"_

* _"Pablo Casals discovered the Cello Suites at a Barcelona music shop around 1890 at age thirteen and spent the next twelve years studying them privately before performing one in public in 1901. The Cello Suites had been known to specialist cellists but were not standard repertoire before Casals's twentieth-century advocacy. What does it mean for a major body of work by one of the central Western composers to have remained essentially unknown to the broader cello repertoire for nearly two centuries after its composition? What kind of compositional features make a work 'discoverable' or 'undiscoverable' to subsequent musical generations?"_

* **Counter-claim prompt.** The chapter treats the polyphonic illusion as Bach's central compositional achievement on these instruments. Read against it: maybe what makes the Cello Suite No. 1 Prelude work is not the implied counterpoint at all but something simpler — the cello's grain, the room's acoustic, the player's bow. Listen once with the chapter's polyphonic-illusion frame in mind; once without it. Which hearing was richer? What does your answer tell you about how much of "the work" lives in the score versus in the sound?

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Pablo Casals 1936-39 EMI recording of the Cello Suite No. 1 in G major (or one of the alternative cello recordings listed in §9.1). Listen to the Prelude a second time and attend to these three concrete moments.

  1. **The opening arpeggios (0:00-0:30).** Listen for the ascending broken-chord pattern of the opening. Notice the implied harmony: the cello plays single notes but your ear assembles them into chords. Try to register the chord-progression of the first 30 seconds (G major, D major, G major, C major, and so on, varying by recording).

  2. **The pedal-point section (variable timing, usually about 1:00-1:30).** Listen for the moment when the cello holds a sustained low note while higher notes move above. The pedal-point clarifies the implied two-voice texture; you can hear the "bass" clearly (the sustained note) and the "upper voice" clearly (the moving notes above).

  3. **The closing buildup and cadence (last 30-45 seconds).** Listen for the harmonic intensification as the closing cadence approaches. Casals takes the closing passages with deliberate weight; the arrival on the final G major chord is the structural climax.

Then find the Henryk Szeryng 1967 DG recording of the Chaconne from the D-minor Partita (or one of the alternative violin recordings listed in §9.1). Listen to the Chaconne a second time and attend to these three concrete moments.

  1. **The opening bass theme statement (0:00-0:20).** Listen for the 4-bar bass theme, the descending-then-rising figure in D minor. Memorize the theme; you should be able to hum it after one or two careful listens. The theme will return 64 times across the next 13 minutes.

  2. **The shift to D major (around 6:00-6:30 in Szeryng).** Listen for the moment when the key shifts from D minor to D major. The character changes immediately, brighter, more open. The D-major section runs for about 20 variations.

  3. **The return to D minor and the closing variations (about 9:00-13:30).** Listen for the moment when the key returns to D minor. The closing variations grow more emotionally weighty and more contrapuntally dense as the work approaches its conclusion.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : a fugue from one of the violin Sonatas: for example the second movement of the Sonata No. 1 in G minor BWV 1001 (about 5 minutes) or the second movement of the Sonata No. 2 in A minor BWV 1003 (about 8 minutes) or the second movement of the Sonata No. 3 in C major BWV 1005 (about 11 minutes, the largest of the three violin fugues). The violin fugues are remarkable examples of the polyphonic-illusion technique applied to extended fugal writing; the C-major fugue in particular is one of the most-extraordinary unaccompanied-instrument works in the literature.

**Listen** (comparison): the Cello Suite No. 5 Sarabande (about 4-5 minutes). The single most-contemplative movement in the Cello Suites; a slow, deeply expressive piece in C minor with sustained chordal writing throughout. The contrast with the dancing brightness of the Cello Suite No. 1 Prelude is sharp; the two pieces together show the affective range of the cycle.

**Listen** (cross-volume): the Partita No. 3 in E major BWV 1006 Gavotte en Rondeaux (about 3 minutes: the Voyager Golden Record selection). Bright, dancing, with a recurring rondo theme. The contrast with the D-minor Chaconne shows the violin Partitas' affective range.

**Read** : Eric Siblin, _The Cello Suites: J. S. Bach, Pablo Casals, and the Search for a Baroque Masterpiece_ (Atlantic Books, 2009). A journalist's narrative of the Cello Suites' history, the Casals rediscovery, and the modern reception. About 320 pages; accessible non-specialist treatment. The book is the standard popular-narrative introduction to the suites.

**Read** (alternative, scholarly): Allen Winold, _Bach's Cello Suites: Analyses and Explorations_ (Indiana University Press, 2007). A scholarly two-volume treatment of the suites with detailed analytical commentary; for readers who want the music-analytical depth.

**Read** (specialized): Joel Lester, _Bach's Works for Solo Violin: Style, Structure, Performance_ (Oxford University Press, 1999). The standard scholarly treatment of the Violin Sonatas and Partitas; about 350 pages.

## Chapter 10: The Well-Tempered Clavier Book 1 BWV 846-869 (1722) + Inventions and Sinfonias BWV 772-801

The _Well-Tempered Clavier_ Book 1, twenty-four preludes and fugues in all 24 major and minor keys, completed at Köthen in 1722, is the central keyboard work of Bach's mature career and the foundational document of Western keyboard pedagogy and composition for the three centuries that have followed. Every serious classical pianist of the past 200 years has studied the WTC; many have made it a lifelong project (Glenn Gould recorded it twice; András Schiff recorded it twice; Angela Hewitt recorded it twice). The work is to keyboard music what the Brandenburg Concertos are to orchestral music: the central canonical document, the work against which other works in the genre are measured, the work that has shaped subsequent compositional practice across centuries.

The companion Inventions and Sinfonias BWV 772-801, completed at Köthen in 1723 (Bach's last year there), are the pedagogical companion to the WTC. The Inventions are fifteen short two-voice pieces; the Sinfonias are fifteen short three-voice pieces; together they form a thirty-piece collection composed primarily for Bach's eldest son Wilhelm Friedemann's keyboard instruction. The Inventions and Sinfonias are not "minor works", they are some of the most-distilled compositional exercises in Bach's catalogue, each demonstrating a specific contrapuntal-melodic principle within a small frame.

This chapter does three things. First, it sets up what "well-tempered" meant in early-eighteenth-century keyboard tuning, why the WTC organized itself as 24 prelude-and-fugue pairs in all keys, and what the work's pedagogical and compositional purposes were. Second, it anchors deeply on the WTC Book 1 Prelude and Fugue No. 1 in C major BWV 846 (the most-famous opening in keyboard literature), with treatment of the Prelude and Fugue No. 2 in C minor BWV 847 and the Prelude and Fugue No. 22 in B♭ minor BWV 867 to show the cycle's range. Third, it introduces the Two-Part Invention No. 1 in C major BWV 772 as the simplest example of the Inventions pedagogical principle.

### 10.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote, the autograph title page

Köthen, 1722. Bach is thirty-seven years old, in his fifth year as Kapellmeister at the Köthen court, eighteen months into his second marriage (to Anna Magdalena, married December 1721), and four years into the personal-and-professional rhythm of the Köthen years that will close with his departure for Leipzig the following year. He is preparing the title page of a manuscript collection he has been working on for some time, perhaps for several years, since some of the pieces in the collection date from the Weimar period or earlier, and that he is now formally assembling into a single document.

The title page (which survives in Bach's hand) reads, in German: _Das Wohltemperierte Clavier oder Praeludia und Fugen durch alle Tone und Semitonia so wohl tertiam majorem oder Ut Re Mi anlangend, als auch tertiam minorem oder Re Mi Fa betreffend. Zum Nutzen und Gebrauch der Lehr-begierigen Musicalischen Jugend, als auch derer in diesem studio schon habil seyenden besonderem Zeit Vertreib aufgesetzet und verfertigt_ , "The Well-Tempered Clavier or Preludes and Fugues through all the tones and semitones, both with the major third or Ut Re Mi, and the minor third or Re Mi Fa. For the benefit and use of the musical youth desirous of learning, as well as for the special pastime of those already skilled in this study, composed and prepared." The title page is signed _Anno 1722_ and identifies Bach as _Joh. Seb. Bach, p.t. Capellmeister Sr. Königl. Maytt. zu Cöthen_ , "Master of the Chapel to His Royal Highness the Prince of Köthen."

The title page is unusual for the careful articulation of the work's purpose. Most Bach autograph title pages are brief, composer, work title, date, sometimes a dedication. The WTC title page is more like a small foreword: the work's musical principle (preludes and fugues through all 24 keys), the work's pedagogical purpose (for musical youth and for skilled players' use), the formal compositional framing. Bach is announcing what the WTC is and what it is for; the announcement is itself a piece of compositional self-statement.

What "well-tempered" meant. The phrase has been the subject of three centuries of scholarly debate. The technical question concerns the tuning systems used for early-eighteenth-century keyboard instruments. Through most of the seventeenth century, keyboards were tuned in _meantone_ temperament, a tuning system that made certain keys (the keys close to C major) sound very good and other keys (the keys far from C major, in the sharp or flat directions) sound unpleasantly out of tune. Composers in meantone temperament avoided the distant keys; almost no Renaissance or early-Baroque music exists in keys like F♯ major or G♯ minor because these keys sound terrible in meantone.

By the late seventeenth century, organists and tuners had begun developing alternative temperaments, tuning systems that distributed the intonation problems more evenly across the 24 keys, so that all keys would sound acceptable (though not necessarily equally good). The various alternative systems were collectively called _wohltemperierte_ , "well-tempered." Specific systems were developed by Andreas Werckmeister (the most-discussed candidate for Bach's preferred tuning), Johann Georg Neidhardt, Johann Philipp Kirnberger, and others; each had slightly different mathematical-acoustical properties. By the time Bach assembled the WTC in 1722, well-tempered tunings were the standard for serious keyboard instruments in German Baroque practice.

The WTC's deliberate organization through all 24 keys was a demonstration project: a work to display the well-tempered tuning's capabilities, showing that every key could now be used for serious composition. The work also functioned as a compositional teaching project: 24 prelude-and-fugue pairs, one in each key, demonstrating the range of textures, characters, and contrapuntal techniques available within the keyboard medium. And the work functioned as a pedagogical resource for Bach's own teaching, the same musical-youth-instruction purpose explicitly noted on the title page.

The WTC's modern reception treats it as the foundational keyboard work of the Western tradition. Mozart studied it at Baron van Swieten's Vienna circle in the 1780s (the Mozart volume covered this). Beethoven studied it under his teacher Christian Gottlob Neefe in Bonn in the 1780s (the Beethoven volume covered this). Chopin's Op. 28 Preludes are explicitly modeled on the WTC's 24-key organization (the Chopin volume, Chapter 9, covered this). Schumann, Brahms, Reger, Hindemith, Shostakovich (his 24 Preludes and Fugues Op. 87 of 1950-51), and many others followed the WTC's structural model. Every major composer in the Western tradition since 1750 has engaged with the WTC at some level.

For Bach's purposes in 1722, the WTC was probably first a private and pedagogical project. He may have performed selected preludes and fugues at the Köthen court chamber-music sessions; he certainly used the collection in his keyboard teaching (Wilhelm Friedemann would have been twelve in 1722, old enough to begin serious WTC study; Carl Philipp Emanuel was eight, perhaps too young for the WTC but ready for the Inventions). The work would be expanded later: Bach would compose Book 2 of the WTC (BWV 870-893) at Leipzig in the 1740s, completing the cycle as a 48-piece corpus (Chapter 19 will return to Book 2).

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen, C-major Prelude and Fugue BWV 846

**Prelude:**

  1. **The arpeggio pattern throughout.** The Prelude is built almost entirely on a single arpeggio pattern: a five-note ascending broken-chord figure played by the right hand, with the left hand doubling the bass. The pattern repeats with almost no variation across the entire Prelude, the only thing that changes is the harmony underneath. Listen for the harmonic motion specifically: the pattern stays the same; the chords change. This is one of the most-distilled compositional structures in the keyboard literature, a single rhythmic-melodic figure providing the surface, with the harmonic motion providing all the variety.

  2. **The slow harmonic-rhythm.** The chords change about once every two beats, slow enough that you can hear each chord clearly, slow enough that the Prelude has a serene, contemplative character. The slow harmonic-rhythm is what makes the Prelude work as an introduction, it sets up a meditative atmosphere that the fugue will then take in a more contrapuntally-active direction.

  3. **The closing pedal-point.** The Prelude closes with a long-held bass note (the pedal-point on G, the dominant of C major) under the arpeggio pattern continuing in the upper voices. The pedal-point lasts for several bars and prepares the cadence on C major. The structure is the same pedal-point cadence pattern you have encountered in Buxtehude (Chapter 4) and Bach (Chapter 5): the bass anchors; the upper voices move; the cadence arrives.

**Fugue:**

  1. **The four-voice exposition.** The fugue is in four voices. The first voice (the soprano, the highest) states the subject; the second voice (alto) answers in the dominant; the third (tenor) and fourth (bass) follow in turn. Listen for the four entries; track the subject as it passes from voice to voice. The fugue's exposition is the moment when the contrapuntal architecture is being built.

  2. **The subject's character.** The subject is short and gentle, a four-note descending figure followed by a brief continuation, total length about 1.5 seconds at typical tempo. The subject is not melodically distinctive in the way some fugue subjects are (the BWV 565 fugue subject is more angular and memorable). The C-major fugue's subject is deliberately understated; the compositional interest is in how the four-voice texture develops from the simple subject.

  3. **The contrapuntal density.** The four-voice fugue, when all four voices are sounding, is denser than the BWV 565 fugue (Chapter 5) and the Capriccio fugue (Chapter 3) you have encountered. The C-major fugue is comparatively transparent for a WTC fugue (some WTC fugues are denser); but the four-voice texture is consistently fuller than the previous fugues in the volume. Listen for the moments when all four voices are sounding simultaneously; try to hear the individual lines within the texture.

#### One-sentence background

The WTC Book 1 was assembled at Köthen in 1722, comprising 24 prelude-and-fugue pairs in all 24 major and minor keys (the title-page autograph survives); the Prelude and Fugue No. 1 in C major BWV 846 is the opening pair, with the Prelude about 2 minutes and the Fugue about 2 minutes, totaling about 4 minutes; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is András Schiff (Decca, 1984-85, the first of his two complete-cycle recordings, on piano), chosen for the clear voice-leading, the lyrical phrasing, and the canonical-pianist status.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Bach WTC Book 1 Prelude Fugue C major BWV 846 Schiff" or "Bach Well-Tempered Clavier C major BWV 846" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** (Ch 21 surveys the broader WTC recording history):

Piano: Glenn Gould (CBS 1962-63 Book 1 + 1969-71 Book 2, modernist-contrarian); Sviatoslav Richter (Melodiya 1970-73 + RCA 1972-73 live, spacious); Edwin Fischer (HMV 1933-1936, the first complete WTC); Angela Hewitt (Hyperion 1997-99 + 2008 re-recording, analytic).

Harpsichord: Wanda Landowska (RCA 1949-53, twentieth-century touchstone); Trevor Pinnock (Hänssler 2003-05, contemporary HIP standard); Masaaki Suzuki (BIS 2019-2022, most-recent major HIP cycle).

### 10.2 The Listening Window

This section is for your second listen to the C-major Prelude and Fugue BWV 846. Timings refer to the Schiff 1984 Decca recording, which has the Prelude at about 2:00 and the Fugue at about 2:00, total about 4 minutes.

#### Prelude (about 2 minutes)

**0:00-0:30: The opening arpeggios.** The Prelude opens with the right hand playing the characteristic five-note arpeggio pattern over the left-hand's sustained bass. The first chord outlined by the arpeggio is C major (the home key); the second chord is also C major (slightly different voicing); subsequent chords move through related keys. Listen for the slow harmonic motion, the chords change about once every two beats, slow enough that you can hear each chord distinctly. The harmonic-rhythm is one of the Prelude's most-distinctive features.

**About 0:30-1:00: The first harmonic departures.** The harmony begins to move further from C major. The arpeggio pattern stays the same; the implied chords shift through D minor, F major, G major, and back. The motion is gentle but persistent.

**About 1:00-1:30: The middle harmonic adventure.** The Prelude reaches its most-distant harmonic territory in the middle, with brief touches of more-remote keys before returning home. The arpeggio pattern remains constant; the harmony provides all the interest.

**About 1:30-2:00: The closing pedal-point and cadence.** The bass settles onto a long-held G (the dominant of C major); the arpeggio pattern continues above. The pedal-point lasts for several bars; the closing cadence on C major arrives at the end. The Prelude's serene contemplative character is sustained to the final chord.

The Charles Gounod 1853 _Ave Maria_ uses this Prelude as its accompaniment, with Gounod's added soprano melody on the Latin Marian text "Ave Maria, gratia plena" above the Bach arpeggios. The Gounod/Bach hybrid is one of the most-recorded vocal works in the Western catalogue and has given the C-major Prelude a parallel life as the basis for a sacred vocal work. (A reader who wants to hear the Gounod can find versions by virtually every major soprano of the past century; the Maria Callas, Renée Fleming, and Kathleen Battle recordings are among the standard alternatives.)

#### Fugue (about 2 minutes)

**0:00-0:15: The fugue's exposition begins.** A single voice (the soprano, the highest) states the subject in C major. The subject is a four-note descending figure followed by a brief continuation; it lasts about 2 seconds. After the soprano states the subject, the alto voice enters with the subject transposed to G major (the dominant); the two voices now sound together, the alto carrying the subject, the soprano continuing with free counterpoint above.

**About 0:15-0:30: The third and fourth voice entries.** The tenor voice enters with the subject back in C major; then the bass voice enters with the subject in G major. Now all four voices are sounding: the four-voice texture is fully built. Listen for the moment of the bass-entry; the subject in the bass register is one of the most-distinctive moments of the fugue.

**About 0:30-1:30: The fugue's development.** With the four-voice texture built, the fugue develops through additional subject-statements and episodes. The subject returns in various voices and various keys; the episodes (the contrapuntal passages between subject-entries) develop figures derived from the subject. The harmony moves through related keys (D minor, A minor, F major, back to C major). Schiff's clear articulation lets you track the subject as it moves through the texture.

**About 1:30-2:00: The closing passages and final cadence.** The fugue's closing section returns to C major with greater contrapuntal density. The final cadence is on C major, with all four voices arriving on the tonic chord. The Fugue closes the prelude-fugue pair on a contemplative-resolved note.

If your recording's total time for the BWV 846 pair differs from Schiff's 4:00 by more than 25%, you may be hearing a substantially different performance approach. Glenn Gould's 1963 reading runs about 3:30 (faster tempos, more articulated); Sviatoslav Richter's reading runs about 5 minutes (slower, weightier); Wanda Landowska's harpsichord reading runs about 4:30. The structural shape, gentle prelude + four-voice fugue with clear exposition, should be evident in every recording.

### 10.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The prelude-and-fugue pair as a Bach unit

The prelude-and-fugue pair is a Bach-developed compositional unit. The two pieces are designed as a complementary pair: the prelude sets up the key's affect through (typically) a freer, more rhetorical character; the fugue then takes the same key and uses it in a strict imitative-contrapuntal structure. The pair's combined effect is to demonstrate two different compositional approaches to the same key in immediate succession.

The WTC Book 1 contains 24 such pairs, one in each key. The pairs are not uniform in length or character: some preludes are short, dance-like, almost throwaway (the C-major Prelude is one of these, a 2-minute miniature); others are extended works in their own right (the E♭ major Prelude of BWV 852 is a French overture nearly 5 minutes long; the D minor Prelude of BWV 875 from Book 2 has a similar scale). Similarly, some fugues are short and transparent (the C-major fugue is one); others are large, elaborate, and contrapuntally dense (the C♯ minor fugue of BWV 849 is a 5-minute five-voice fugue with three subjects, one of the most-complex contrapuntal works in the keyboard literature).

The pair's compositional logic depends on the prelude setting up the key's character and the fugue developing it. The pair's compositional flexibility lets Bach demonstrate the keyboard medium's range across 48 pieces (24 in Book 1, 24 in Book 2): every key, every kind of prelude, every kind of fugue, every contrapuntal device.

#### The C-major Prelude's affective character

The C-major Prelude is famously simple in its surface. The arpeggio pattern is unchanged throughout; the harmonic motion is gentle; the contrapuntal-melodic interest is minimal in the conventional sense. The Prelude's affective character is meditative, serene, almost devotional, qualities that have made it irresistible to subsequent generations of arrangers (Gounod's _Ave Maria_ is the most-famous example; there are dozens of other arrangements for various ensemble combinations).

The Prelude's simplicity is deceptive. The single arpeggio pattern requires careful harmonic-rhythmic planning to sustain interest for 2 minutes; the slow harmonic-rhythm requires the harmonies to be carefully calibrated to provide enough motion without becoming too active; the closing pedal-point cadence requires precise voicing to function as an effective close. Bach's compositional craft here is in the engineering of a simple surface that supports two minutes of attentive listening without monotony, a problem that many composers face and few solve as cleanly.

For the listener: the C-major Prelude is the easiest WTC piece to recognize on a first hearing and to remember after. It is also, perhaps deceptively, one of the WTC's most-distilled compositional achievements: a single arpeggio pattern supporting a complete musical statement.

#### The fugue subject and the four-voice exposition (refresher)

The fugue subject-and-answer pattern has been introduced in Chapters 3 (Capriccio fugue), 5 (BWV 565 fugue), and 8 (Brandenburg 4 fugal finale); the WTC Book 1 fugues are where the device gets its mature compositional treatment. The C-major fugue's specific features: the subject is short and gently shaped (no dramatic leaps, no angular figures); the exposition unfolds in four voices in the order soprano-alto-tenor-bass; the exposition takes about 30 seconds total; the development takes about 1:30; the work closes on the C-major tonic chord.

The C-major fugue is a relatively transparent fugue. Subsequent WTC fugues are denser (the C♯ minor fugue BWV 849 has three subjects; the F♯ minor fugue BWV 859 is in five voices). The C-major fugue is the gentlest possible introduction to Bach's mature fugal practice; the more-demanding fugues come later in the volume's listening (Chapter 19's Art of Fugue is the most-demanding fugue-encounter in the volume).

For the listener: practice the four-voice listening on the C-major fugue. Try to follow each individual voice through the work, soprano in one hearing, alto in another, tenor in another, bass in another. The skill builds on the four-voice listening from "Sheep May Safely Graze" (Chapter 2); here the voices are more independent and more contrapuntally active.

#### The C-minor Prelude and Fugue BWV 847 (briefly)

The Prelude and Fugue No. 2 in C minor BWV 847, the second pair of the WTC Book 1, has a very different character from the No. 1. The C-minor Prelude is a toccata-like piece: rapid, urgent, with running figures in both hands. The C-minor Fugue is in three voices (one fewer than the C-major Fugue), with a subject that has a more-distinctive melodic shape, a rising figure followed by a turn and a descending tail.

The C-minor pair is the standard contrasting example to the C-major pair. Where the C-major is gentle and serene, the C-minor is urgent and dramatic. The pair's juxtaposition (which Bach placed deliberately as the cycle's first two pairs) demonstrates the WTC's affective range immediately: a single composer working in adjacent keys can produce works of completely different characters.

#### The B♭-minor Prelude and Fugue BWV 867 (briefly)

The Prelude and Fugue No. 22 in B♭ minor BWV 867, late in the WTC Book 1, represents the cycle's contemplative-meditative pole. The Prelude is slow and deeply expressive (about 4 minutes); the Fugue is in five voices and is one of the longest in Book 1. The B♭-minor pair has been singled out by many performers (Schiff, Hewitt, Pollini have all spoken about the pair) as a particular high-water mark of the WTC. The pair's emotional weight and contrapuntal density together represent what the WTC can achieve at its fullest scale.

For the listener: after working through the C-major pair, the B♭-minor pair is the recommended next anchor for hearing how the WTC's range extends from the gentle opening to the deeply expressive late preludes-and-fugues. Listening time about 8 minutes for the pair.

#### The Inventions and Sinfonias BWV 772-801

The Inventions and Sinfonias are the WTC's pedagogical companion. Bach composed them at Köthen in 1723 (the last year before the Leipzig move) primarily for Wilhelm Friedemann's keyboard instruction. The collection has thirty pieces, fifteen Two-Part Inventions (BWV 772-786) and fifteen Three-Part Sinfonias (BWV 787-801), organized by key (each piece in a different key, covering most of the principal major and minor keys).

The Inventions are two-voice pieces: the right hand and left hand each carry an independent melodic line; the two lines weave together in continuous counterpoint. The pieces are short, typically 1 to 2 minutes each, and demonstrate the principle of two-voice keyboard polyphony at the simplest possible scale. The Two-Part Invention No. 1 in C major BWV 772 is the most-frequently-encountered piece (about 1 minute long, used as the first piece in most piano-pedagogy curricula). The principle: a short melodic idea (often called the "motive" or "theme") is stated by one voice; the other voice imitates the motive; the two voices develop the motive together through a brief contrapuntal exchange; the piece closes.

The Sinfonias are three-voice pieces: the same principle as the Inventions but with three independent voices instead of two. The pieces are correspondingly more demanding for the player (three independent lines on a keyboard require all ten fingers to be doing something specific) and for the listener (three independent lines are harder to follow than two). The Sinfonias are typically 1-2 minutes each.

The Inventions and Sinfonias as a thirty-piece collection. They function pedagogically (a serious keyboard student today still studies them as the entry-point to Bach's contrapuntal keyboard practice); compositionally (they demonstrate at small scale the contrapuntal principles that drive the larger fugues and works); and as compact musical achievements in their own right (some of the pieces, the Two-Part Invention No. 4 in D minor, the Three-Part Sinfonia No. 11 in G minor, others, are surprisingly affecting for their small scale).

For the listener: a few of the Inventions and Sinfonias are worth hearing as complement to the WTC. The Two-Part Invention No. 1 in C major BWV 772 (about 1 minute) demonstrates the genre at its simplest; the Two-Part Invention No. 4 in D minor BWV 775 (about 1.5 minutes) demonstrates the genre at a more-expressive scale; the Three-Part Sinfonia No. 11 in G minor BWV 797 (about 3 minutes) demonstrates the three-voice principle at its most-developed.

#### Notable pianists and harpsichordists (briefly)

The WTC has more recordings than perhaps any other body of keyboard music. The principal recording traditions can be sketched as follows.

**The early-twentieth-century pioneer generation.** Wanda Landowska (1879-1959) recorded the complete WTC on harpsichord for RCA in 1949-53; the recording is the early-twentieth-century touchstone for the WTC on its original instrument. Edwin Fischer (1886-1960) recorded the complete WTC on piano for HMV in 1933-1936: the first complete WTC recording; Fischer's lyrical, dignified reading is the canonical early-twentieth-century piano document.

**The mid-twentieth-century definitive generation.** Glenn Gould recorded Book 1 on CBS in 1962-63 and Book 2 in 1969-71; the Gould recordings are the modernist-contrarian readings (fast tempos, extreme articulation, distinctive interpretive choices) that have polarized listeners for sixty years. Sviatoslav Richter recorded the WTC on Melodiya in 1970-73 and on RCA in 1972-73 (live); the Richter readings are spacious, weighty, with full piano tone. Friedrich Gulda recorded the WTC several times; his Decca recordings are the Austrian-school reference.

**The late-twentieth-century pianist generation.** András Schiff recorded Book 1 on Decca in 1984-85 and Book 2 in 1985-87: the Primary Recording for this chapter; his readings are lyrical, balanced, with clear voice-leading. Angela Hewitt recorded the complete WTC on Hyperion in 1997-99 and re-recorded it in 2008; her readings are analytical, balanced, with careful voice-leading. Maurizio Pollini recorded Book 1 on DG in the 1980s, the high-modernist piano reading. Murray Perahia recorded Book 1 on Sony in 2013.

**The HIP-revolution generation.** Trevor Pinnock recorded the complete WTC on harpsichord for Hänssler in 2003-05; Bob van Asperen recorded the complete WTC on Aeolus in 1999-2003; Masaaki Suzuki recorded the complete WTC on BIS in 2019-2022. The harpsichord readings give the WTC its original instrument's sound; the difference from piano readings is substantial (the harpsichord's plucked-string tone is sharper, more transient, less sustained than the piano's hammered-string tone).

**The contemporary generation.** Igor Levit recorded selections from the WTC on Sony; Daniel Barenboim recorded the complete WTC on Erato in 2003 and re-recorded portions on Warner in 2020; Beatrice Rana, Kit Armstrong, and many others have recorded the WTC in the past decade.

For the listener: the recommended starting orientation is Schiff 1984-85 for piano and Pinnock 2003-05 for harpsichord; the two together give the WTC in both of its plausible instrumental media. After hearing the two, Gould's CBS recordings are worth hearing for the modernist contrast; Richter and Landowska are worth hearing for the historical-canonical readings.

#### Cross-piece links

The WTC Book 1 is the central fugal-practice reference in this volume. The earliest fugue (Capriccio BWV 992 closing fugue, Chapter 3) and the most-rhetorical (BWV 565, Chapter 5) bracket it from the early side; Brandenburg 4's fugal finale (Chapter 8) and the unaccompanied-string fugues (Chapter 9, polyphonic-illusion technique on a single melodic instrument) work the same device in different media; the late _Art of Fugue_ (Chapter 19) and WTC Book 2 (Chapter 19) are the mature late-period fugal output. The WTC Book 1 sits at the center of this network.

Chapter 20 §20.1 (fugue subject and answer), §20.4 (canon), §20.6 (permutation fugue) will collect the contrapuntal devices the WTC demonstrates.

### 10.4 The Stories

#### The WTC Book 1 within Bach's keyboard project

The WTC Book 1 is part of a broader keyboard-project Bach pursued across his career. The major keyboard-collection publications include:

* _Clavier-Übung_ (Keyboard Practice) I (1731): six Partitas for solo keyboard.
* _Clavier-Übung_ II (1735): the Italian Concerto BWV 971 + the French Overture BWV 831, a pair of contrasting national-style demonstrations.
* _Clavier-Übung_ III (1739): the "German Organ Mass", chorale preludes + the great Prelude and Fugue in E♭ major BWV 552 ("St. Anne").
* _Clavier-Übung_ IV (1741): the Goldberg Variations BWV 988 (Chapter 17).

Plus the manuscript collections that Bach did not publish:

* _Inventions and Sinfonias_ BWV 772-801 (1723, Köthen).
* _Well-Tempered Clavier_ Book 1 BWV 846-869 (1722, Köthen) and Book 2 BWV 870-893 (c. 1742, Leipzig, Chapter 19).
* English Suites BWV 806-811 (c. 1715-1720, Weimar-Köthen transition).
* French Suites BWV 812-817 (c. 1722, Köthen).

The aggregate keyboard output is substantial, about 220 keyboard works including the major collections, the Clavier-Übung volumes, and many smaller pieces. The output represents Bach's lifelong engagement with the keyboard as a compositional medium; the WTC's place at the center of this output reflects its position as the central work of the keyboard project.

#### The Anna Magdalena Notebook (briefly)

The _Notebook for Anna Magdalena Bach_ of 1722, assembled by Bach for his second wife shortly after their marriage in December 1721, is the household-music collection of the Bach household at Köthen and (continuing) at Leipzig. The 1722 Notebook is the first of two such notebooks (a second was assembled in 1725 at Leipzig); both contain a mix of pieces by Bach himself, by his various children (Wilhelm Friedemann's keyboard exercises; Carl Philipp Emanuel's early pieces), and by other composers (a few pieces by Hasse, Couperin, and others).

The 1722 Notebook contains several of the French Suites in early versions (the suites would be revised in subsequent years); various minuets, marches, and short pieces for keyboard; the _Aria_ that would later become the basis of the Goldberg Variations (in its 1722 Notebook version it is a simple sarabande, not the elaborate Goldberg Aria of 1741); various vocal-keyboard pieces; and Anna Magdalena's own copying of various Bach works. The Notebook is a window into the domestic music life of the Bach household, the daily music-making that surrounded the formal compositional projects.

The Anna Magdalena Notebook is not anchored in this volume but is worth a brief mention for context. Recordings of the Notebook (or selections from it) are available, Janina Fialkowska, András Schiff, and others have recorded selected pieces; specialized period-instrument groups have recorded the complete Notebook.

#### The biographical context: 1722 to 1723

The WTC Book 1 was completed at Köthen in 1722. The Inventions and Sinfonias were completed at Köthen in 1723 (the last year there). The biographical thread (Thread A) continues from Chapter 7's Köthen-court setup into Chapter 11's Leipzig appointment.

#### Concert music or pedagogy?

A judgment about what the WTC actually is: the title page explicitly identifies the work as "for the benefit and use of the musical youth desirous of learning, as well as for the special pastime of those already skilled in this study" — both pedagogy and concert music, in that order. Modern practice has substantially tipped toward the concert-music reading; the WTC appears as repertoire in major piano recitals, recorded in dozens of complete cycles by leading pianists, treated as the central keyboard work of Western music. The pedagogical use continues (every serious classical pianist studies the WTC), but the cultural framing emphasizes concert performance over teaching. The volume's view is that both functions are real and that the over-emphasis on concert performance has costs: it tends to favor the more-virtuosic preludes-and-fugues over the more-modest ones, and it tends to obscure the WTC's character as a working compositional textbook that Bach used in his teaching. A reader who plays piano even at intermediate level should consider working through a few WTC pairs at the keyboard — not for performance but for the listening discipline that playing creates — as a complement to listening to recordings.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The C-major Prelude of the WTC is built almost entirely on a single arpeggio pattern; the harmonic motion provides all the variety. Charles Gounod's 1853 Ave Maria uses the same prelude as accompaniment for a Latin Marian text. Many other arrangers have built on the same Bach foundation. What kind of compositional structure is particularly hospitable to subsequent arrangement and reuse? Are there modern equivalents, popular songs that have served as the harmonic skeleton for innumerable subsequent versions, jazz standards that get reharmonized in every direction?"_

* _"The Well-Tempered Clavier organizes itself as 24 prelude-and-fugue pairs in all 24 major and minor keys, a deliberate demonstration of the well-tempered tuning system's range. Bach's organization influenced subsequent composers: Chopin's Op. 28 Preludes (the Chopin volume, Chapter 9) use the same 24-key organization; Shostakovich's 24 Preludes and Fugues Op. 87 (1950-51) use it explicitly modeled on Bach. What does the comprehensive-organization-by-key project give a composer that a less-structured collection would not? What is the listener gained or lost by the structural organization?"_

* _"Every serious classical pianist of the past 200 years has studied the WTC; many have made it a lifelong project. Glenn Gould recorded both books; András Schiff recorded both books twice; Angela Hewitt recorded both books twice. What does it mean for a piece of music to be 'foundational' for a tradition, a work that every serious practitioner is expected to study and many to perform? What kind of relationship does a pianist develop with a work she returns to across decades?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the András Schiff 1984 Decca recording of the WTC Book 1 (or one of the alternative recordings listed in §10.1). Listen to the Prelude and Fugue No. 1 in C major BWV 846 a second time and attend to these four concrete moments.

  1. **The Prelude's harmonic motion (throughout, with attention to the first 30 seconds).** Listen for the harmonic changes underneath the constant arpeggio pattern. The chords change about once every two beats; try to follow the harmonic motion through the Prelude. The slow harmonic-rhythm is the Prelude's distinctive feature.

  2. **The Prelude's closing pedal-point (about 1:30-2:00).** Listen for the moment when the bass settles onto a long-held G (the dominant of C major); the arpeggio pattern continues above; the closing cadence on C major arrives. The pedal-point structure is the same Buxtehude-tradition cadence pattern you have encountered before.

  3. **The Fugue's four-voice exposition (about 0:00-0:30).** Listen for the four voice-entries with the subject. Soprano first; then alto; then tenor; then bass. Once all four voices have entered, the fugue's four-voice texture is fully built. Try to track each voice through the exposition.

  4. **The Fugue's contrapuntal density at full texture (anywhere after about 0:30).** When all four voices are sounding simultaneously, the texture is denser than any previous fugue in the volume. Try to hear the individual lines within the texture; try to identify when each subject-entry occurs. This is the fugue-listening discipline being built; the C-major fugue is a relatively transparent example, but the discipline transfers to denser fugues you will encounter in Chapter 19.

Then, if time allows, find the Two-Part Invention No. 1 in C major BWV 772 (the Schiff recording includes the Inventions on a separate disc; many of the WTC recording cycles include the Inventions and Sinfonias as a companion volume). Listen once. The Invention is about 1 minute; the two-voice texture is the simplest possible example of Bach's contrapuntal keyboard writing.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : the Prelude and Fugue No. 2 in C minor BWV 847 (about 4 minutes total). The contrast with the C-major pair is sharp; the C-minor Prelude is rapid and urgent, the C-minor Fugue is in three voices with a distinctive subject. Together with the C-major pair, the first two pairs of the WTC demonstrate the affective range available in adjacent keys.

**Listen** (later WTC anchor): the Prelude and Fugue No. 22 in B♭ minor BWV 867 (about 8 minutes total). The contemplative-meditative pole of the cycle; the Prelude is slow and deeply expressive; the Fugue is in five voices. Singled out by many performers as a particular high-water mark of the WTC.

**Listen** (comparison): a fugue from one of the violin Sonatas (Chapter 9 Going Deeper recommended this). The Sonata No. 3 in C major BWV 1005 fugue (about 11 minutes) is the most-extended violin fugue. Comparing it with a WTC fugue shows how the same compositional voice operates across very different instrumental media.

**Read** : David Ledbetter, _Bach's Well-tempered Clavier: The 48 Preludes and Fugues_ (Yale University Press, 2002). The standard scholarly treatment of the WTC. About 450 pages of work-by-work analysis with substantial historical context.

**Read** (alternative, shorter): Eric Lewin Altschuler, _Bachanalia: The Essential Listener's Guide to Bach's Well-Tempered Clavier_ (Little, Brown, 1994). A more-accessible non-specialist guide; about 300 pages.

**Read** (specialized): Joseph Kerman, _The Art of Fugue: Bach Fugues for Keyboard, 1715-1750_ (University of California Press, 2005). A short scholarly book devoted to the keyboard fugues across Bach's career, including the WTC and the Art of Fugue. About 200 pages.

---

## Chapter 11: The Move to Leipzig 1723, Thomaskantor

The April 1723 appointment of Johann Sebastian Bach as Thomaskantor at Leipzig is the most-consequential institutional decision in Western music history. It placed Bach in the position he would hold for the next twenty-seven years, until his death on July 28, 1750, and it gave him the institutional context (a major Saxon-Lutheran city, a long-standing church-music establishment, an annual cycle of liturgical requirements) within which the sacred output that would constitute the central body of his life's work would be produced. Without the Leipzig appointment there would be no cantata cycles, no Passions, no B Minor Mass, no Christmas Oratorio, none of the great Lutheran-sacred works that have defined Bach's reception across three centuries.

This chapter does three things. First, it walks through the 1722-23 Leipzig appointment process: Telemann was the first choice and declined; Christoph Graupner was the second choice and was refused release by his Darmstadt patron; Bach was offered the post in April 1723 as the third choice; he accepted and moved to Leipzig in May. Second, it describes the institutional context of the Thomaskantor position, the four Leipzig churches, the contractual duties, the Latin-and-music teaching at the Thomasschule, the relationship with the Leipzig city council. Third, it anchors on Cantata BWV 4 "Christ lag in Todesbanden", the early chorale cantata composed at Mühlhausen c. 1707-1708 and revised at Leipzig for the 1724 and 1725 Easter performances, as the work that bridges the early and the Leipzig-mature cantata practice and as the listener's introduction to the chorale-cantata genre.

### 11.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote, the 1723 appointment

Leipzig, late April 1723. The Leipzig city council has been deliberating since the previous summer about the appointment of a successor to Johann Kuhnau, the Thomaskantor who died in June 1722. The position is one of the most-prestigious musical appointments in Saxon Germany: music director for the four main Leipzig churches (Thomaskirche, Nikolaikirche, Neue Kirche, Peterskirche); Latin-and-music teacher at the Thomasschule (the boys' school attached to the Thomaskirche); composer-in-residence for the liturgical year's musical requirements; civic music figure for major Leipzig occasions. The salary is generous; the accommodation (an apartment in the Thomasschule itself) is comfortable; the cultural prestige is significant.

The council has been working through candidates for nearly a year. The first choice was Georg Philipp Telemann (1681-1767), then Kapellmeister at Frankfurt am Main and one of the most-famous composers in Germany. Telemann was offered the post in August 1722 and indicated his willingness; he then negotiated an improved offer at Frankfurt (the Frankfurt authorities, learning of the Leipzig offer, raised Telemann's salary substantially to keep him) and declined Leipzig in November 1722. The council moved to its second choice: Christoph Graupner (1683-1760), Kapellmeister at the Darmstadt court. Graupner was offered the post in December 1722 and accepted. He then sought formal release from his Darmstadt patron, Landgrave Ernst Ludwig of Hesse-Darmstadt. The Landgrave refused to release Graupner; Graupner had to decline Leipzig in April 1723.

By April 1723 the council had been negotiating for eight months without securing a Thomaskantor. The third candidate was Bach.
