Bach had been on the candidate list from the beginning of the process but had been considered the lesser candidate, Telemann and Graupner were both more-famous, both held more-prominent court appointments, both had more-extensive Italian-style cantata catalogues that the Leipzig council considered the modern style. Bach was respected in central German musical circles but was less-broadly known; he was associated more with the German contrapuntal tradition than with the Italian-influenced concerted style that Telemann and Graupner represented.

The famous remark from one council member is recorded in the surviving minutes of the April 22, 1723 council meeting: _Da man nun die besten nicht bekommen könne, müsse man mittlere nehmen_ , "Since the best could not be obtained, we must accept the mediocre." The remark, by Councilor Abraham Christoph Platz, is often cited as evidence of how unrecognized Bach was in his lifetime. It deserves more careful reading. Platz was speaking in the institutional context of an eight-month failed recruitment; "the best" referred specifically to Telemann (who had declined) and Graupner (whose patron had refused release); "the mediocre" was a relative term within a candidate pool that had been narrowed by external factors more than by absolute judgment about quality. Platz's remark records the council's frustration at not securing its first choices, not a considered judgment that Bach was musically mediocre.

Bach was formally offered the Thomaskantor post in April 1723. The negotiations took several weeks: the council required Bach to take a Latin examination (the Thomaskantor was responsible for teaching Latin at the Thomasschule alongside music; the council needed assurance that Bach could conduct Latin lessons competently); Bach had to demonstrate his composition through audition (he had submitted Cantatas BWV 22 "Jesus nahm zu sich die Zwölfe" and BWV 23 "Du wahrer Gott und Davids Sohn" as audition pieces in February 1723, performed at the Thomaskirche); Bach had to formally request his release from the Köthen Kapellmeister position (which Prince Leopold granted without difficulty, see Chapter 7). The contractual terms were negotiated through April and into early May; Bach signed the contract on May 5, 1723; he moved his household from Köthen to Leipzig later in May.

The first Sunday of Bach's Leipzig tenure was June 6, 1723, the first Sunday after Trinity, for which he conducted his Cantata BWV 75 "Die Elenden sollen essen" ("The wretched shall eat") at the Nikolaikirche in the morning and a repeat at the Thomaskirche in the afternoon. The cantata was performed as part of the regular Sunday liturgy; Bach conducted from the keyboard with the Thomanerchor (the boys' choir attached to the Thomasschule) and the city's adult instrumentalists. The first Sunday's cantata launched what would become a continuous 27-year project: weekly cantata composition for the Leipzig liturgical year.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen, Cantata BWV 4 "Christ lag in Todesbanden"

  1. **The opening Sinfonia.** The cantata opens with a short instrumental introduction (about 1 minute) for strings and continuo. The Sinfonia establishes E minor (the home key of the cantata) and the chorale-melody material that will dominate the rest of the work. Listen for the chorale tune, "Christ lag in Todesbanden", in the strings; the tune is presented as a slow, contemplative line.

  2. **The opening chorus (Versus 1).** After the Sinfonia, the first vocal movement enters, a four-voice choral setting of the first verse of the Lutheran Easter chorale "Christ lag in Todesbanden" (text by Martin Luther, 1524). The chorale tune appears as a _cantus firmus_ in the soprano voice (slow long notes), while the lower voices weave contrapuntal lines around it. The text is the chorale's first verse: "Christ lay in death's bonds, given for our sin; he is risen again and has brought us life." Listen for the chorale tune in the soprano specifically, its slow, sustained shape against the lower voices' more active counterpoint.

  3. **The seven verses' varying scoring.** The cantata is structured as a setting of the seven verses of the Lutheran chorale, with each verse scored for a different combination of voices and instruments (the opening Sinfonia + 7 verses = 8 movements total). The principle: an opening chorus + alternating verse-settings (for soprano alto duet, tenor solo, four-voice choir, bass solo, soprano-tenor duet, choir again, four-part chorale). The variety of scoring across the seven verses keeps the cantata's relatively-uniform melodic material (always derived from the same chorale tune) fresh across the work's 22 minutes.

#### One-sentence background

Composed in early form at Mühlhausen c. 1707-1708 (one of Bach's earliest surviving cantatas; the version performed at the Mühlhausen Easter Sunday 1708 service); revised at Leipzig for the Easter Sunday 1724 performance and again revised for Easter 1725; the surviving manuscript represents the Leipzig version; a chorale cantata for SATB choir, soloists (SATB), strings, and continuo in E minor, 8 movements (Sinfonia + 7 verses of the chorale), about 22 minutes; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is John Eliot Gardiner with the Monteverdi Choir and English Baroque Soloists (Soli Deo Gloria, recorded 2000 as part of the Bach Cantata Pilgrimage, on period instruments), chosen for the project's scholarly authority and the well-engineered sound.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Bach Cantata BWV 4 Christ lag in Todesbanden Gardiner" or "Bach Cantata 4 Easter" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** (one-line; Ch 21 surveys the broader cantata recording traditions):

* Karl Richter (Archiv 1968, modern instruments)
* Nikolaus Harnoncourt (Teldec 1980, period instruments)
* Philippe Herreweghe (Harmonia Mundi 1996, period instruments, one-voice-per-part)
* Masaaki Suzuki (BIS 1995, period instruments)
* Ton Koopman (Erato 1995, period instruments)

### 11.2 The Listening Window

This section is for your second listen to Cantata BWV 4. Timings refer to Gardiner's 2000 Soli Deo Gloria recording, which runs about 22 minutes across 8 movements.

**0:00-1:00: Sinfonia.** Instrumental introduction for strings and continuo. The chorale tune appears in the strings (in long notes); the bass moves slowly in the continuo. The mood is contemplative, slightly somber, the E minor key and slow tempo establish the cantata's reflective character. The Sinfonia closes with a held chord that prepares the first vocal entry.

**1:00-4:30: Versus 1: "Christ lag in Todesbanden."** Four-voice choral movement with the chorale tune as cantus firmus in the soprano voice. The alto, tenor, and bass voices weave contrapuntal lines around the cantus firmus; the strings double the choir; the continuo provides bass and harmonic support. The text is the chorale's first verse celebrating the Resurrection: "Christ lay in death's bonds, given for our sin; he is risen again and has brought us life, for which we shall be joyful, praise God and be thankful, and sing Hallelujah! Hallelujah!" The "Hallelujah" closing builds to a bright cadence in E major (the Picardy-third closing).

**4:30-7:00: Versus 2: "Den Tod niemand zwingen kunnt."** Duet for soprano and alto with continuo. The text addresses the human inability to overcome death: "No one could overpower death among all the children of men; this came all from our sins, no innocence was to be found." The setting uses the chorale tune throughout (in the two voices, with the bass continuo accompanying); the chorale-tune material is shared between the soprano and the alto. The mood is contemplative, in keeping with the text's gravity.

**7:00-9:00: Versus 3: "Jesus Christus, Gottes Sohn."** Tenor solo with violin obbligato and continuo. The text celebrates the redemption: "Jesus Christ, Son of God, has come in our place, and has put away sin, thus depriving death of its power and harm." The violin obbligato has an active, dancing figuration that contrasts with the tenor's more-sustained line, the figuration suggests the joyful release the text describes.

**9:00-11:30: Versus 4: "Es war ein wunderlicher Krieg."** Four-voice choral movement, the cantata's structural center. The text is the famous Easter-Sunday image of the wonderful war between death and life: "It was a wonderful war when death and life struggled together; life won the victory, it swallowed up death." The setting is contrapuntally dense, all four voices in active counterpoint, with the chorale tune in the alto voice (not the soprano this time). The combat-of-life-and-death image is depicted musically through the intricate weaving of the four voices.

**11:30-13:30: Versus 5: "Hier ist das rechte Osterlamm."** Bass solo with strings and continuo. The text celebrates the Easter Lamb (Christ as the Paschal sacrifice): "Here is the true Paschal Lamb, of which God has commanded, that was roasted on the high cross-stem in fervent love; his blood marks our door, faith holds it before death; the strangler may no longer harm us." The strings provide a quiet accompanying texture; the bass voice is foregrounded.

**13:30-16:00: Versus 6: "So feiern wir das hohe Fest."** Duet for soprano and tenor with continuo. The text invites the worshipping community to the Easter feast: "So we celebrate the high feast with heartfelt joy and delight, which the Lord lets shine upon us; he is himself the sun, who through the brightness of his grace illuminates our hearts completely; the night of sin has vanished." The duet has a more-flowing, dance-like character than the earlier verses, appropriate to the festive Easter text.

**16:00-19:00: Versus 7: "Wir essen und leben wohl."** Four-voice choral movement. The text describes the Easter communion: "We eat and live well in true Easter cakes; the old leaven shall not be with the Word of grace; Christ wants to be our food and alone to feed the soul; faith will not live by anything else. Hallelujah!" The setting is the cantata's compositional climax; the four-voice texture is densely contrapuntal; the closing "Hallelujah" builds to a celebratory cadence.

**19:00-22:00: Versus 8 (or Versus 7 in some numbering): "Wir essen und leben wohl" (closing chorale).** The closing four-part chorale is the cantata's simplest movement: a straightforward four-voice harmonization of the chorale tune, in the standard Lutheran chorale-harmonization style. The text is repeated from the previous movement (or, in some performances, advances to the closing verse of the chorale); the music is the chorale tune harmonized at the customary four-voice level. The closing chorale is the moment when, in the Bach-era Leipzig service, the congregation would have joined in singing along with the choir.

If your recording's total time differs from Gardiner's 22:00 by more than 15%, you may be hearing a substantially different performance approach. Richter (1968) runs about 24 minutes (slower, weightier); Harnoncourt (1980) runs about 21 minutes; Suzuki (1995) runs about 22 minutes; Herreweghe (1996) runs about 23 minutes. The 8-movement structure and the cantus-firmus-based design should be evident in every recording.

### 11.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The chorale cantata genre

A _chorale cantata_ is a multi-movement vocal-instrumental work in which every movement is based on a single Lutheran chorale, the chorale's tune appears in each movement (sometimes as a clear cantus firmus, sometimes as the basis for the contrapuntal-melodic material); the chorale's text either appears directly (set verse by verse) or in textually-related adaptations. The chorale-cantata genre is characteristically Lutheran-German and characteristically focused on a specific chorale's liturgical-theological identity.

Bach worked in the chorale-cantata genre across his career but most-intensively in his second annual Leipzig cycle (the so-called "chorale cantata cycle" of 1724-1725). The 1724-25 cycle was designed as a complete-year liturgical cycle in which each Sunday's cantata would be a chorale cantata on the chorale-tune appropriate to that Sunday's liturgical theme. The cycle produced about 40 chorale cantatas (the surviving group is incomplete, some are lost; some were composed in subsequent years to fill gaps); the cycle includes some of the most-celebrated Bach cantatas (BWV 140 "Wachet auf", Chapter 12; BWV 80 "Ein feste Burg", Chapter 12; and many others).

Cantata BWV 4 "Christ lag in Todesbanden" is a chorale cantata, but composed before the 1724-25 cycle proper, in its early Mühlhausen form (c. 1707-1708) and revised for Leipzig Easter 1724 and 1725 performances. It is sometimes called Bach's "first chorale cantata", though the genre Bach inherited from earlier composers (Buxtehude, the Pachelbel tradition) was already in place; Bach's contribution was to push the form's compositional depth and architectural ambition further than his predecessors had.

The chorale tune in BWV 4 is the Easter chorale "Christ lag in Todesbanden" by Martin Luther (text and tune both by Luther, published 1524 in the Erfurt Enchiridion). The chorale's text is a paraphrase of the early-medieval Easter sequence "Victimae paschali laudes"; the tune is in the Phrygian-mode tradition with a characteristic melodic shape that has been singled out by scholars as one of the most-archaic-feeling Lutheran chorales (the modal coloring gives it a distinctive ancient character compared to later chorales).

Bach's cantata setting puts the chorale tune through systematic variation across the seven verses: cantus firmus in the soprano (V.1), shared between soprano and alto (V.2), in the tenor with violin obbligato (V.3), in the alto (V.4, the structural center), in the bass (V.5), shared between soprano and tenor (V.6), four-voice setting (V.7), simple chorale harmonization (V.8 or the closing chorale). The systematic variation is one of the cantata's compositional features, every movement uses the same chorale-tune material, but the texture and the voice carrying the tune vary continuously. The result is a cantata that is unified by its chorale source but varied in its musical surface.

For the listener: hearing BWV 4 with the chorale tune specifically in mind is the recommended listening discipline. Try to identify the chorale tune in each movement, in some movements it is foregrounded (V.1 in the soprano; V.5 in the bass); in others it is more disguised (V.4 in the alto, against dense counterpoint). The exercise builds the chorale-recognition skill that subsequent Leipzig-cantata listening will rely on.

#### The cantus firmus, extended from Chapter 6

The cantus firmus principle introduced in Chapter 6 (the Orgelbüchlein chorale preludes) extends straightforwardly to the chorale cantata: same compositional foundation, same listening discipline, larger scale. Where the chorale prelude is a 2-3 minute organ piece with the chorale tune sustained against contrapuntal voices, the cantata movement is a 3-5 minute vocal-and-instrumental piece doing the same compositional work with full ensemble. The cantus-firmus listening habit built on BWV 639 transfers directly.

#### The Leipzig liturgical year (briefly)

The Lutheran liturgical year at Leipzig in Bach's time was organized around the standard Christian liturgical calendar: Advent (four Sundays before Christmas); Christmas (December 25); the Sundays after Christmas; Epiphany (January 6); the Sundays after Epiphany; Septuagesima, Sexagesima, and Quinquagesima (the three Sundays before Lent); the six Sundays of Lent; Holy Week (Palm Sunday through Easter); Easter Sunday; the Sundays after Easter; Ascension; Pentecost; Trinity Sunday; and the long stretch of about 25-27 Sundays after Trinity, leading back to Advent and the start of a new year. Each Sunday in the year had specific scripture readings, sermon themes, and (in the Lutheran tradition) chorale-singing.

The Thomaskantor was responsible for the music at the principal Sunday services at the Thomaskirche and the Nikolaikirche (which alternated weekly as the city's principal morning-service location). The standard music for a Sunday morning service included: (a) introit motet; (b) Kyrie and Gloria (in Latin, in the Lutheran practice that retained Latin for these parts of the liturgy); (c) the cantata (performed between the Gospel reading and the sermon); (d) the chorale (the congregational hymn-singing); (e) the closing musical pieces. The cantata was the principal substantial musical contribution; the cantata typically lasted 20-30 minutes and was the work that the Thomaskantor would have composed (or selected from previous compositions) for that Sunday.

The cycle's compositional demands were significant. In Bach's first three Leipzig years (1723-1726) he averaged about one new cantata per week; the total output across those three years was probably about 150 cantatas. The pace then slowed somewhat, Bach turned to occasional pieces, to the Passions, and to other large-scale works, but cantata composition continued at varying intensity across his entire 27-year Leipzig tenure.

#### The role of the chorale in Lutheran worship

The Lutheran chorale was a central feature of the Lutheran Sunday service. The congregation sang the chorales together; the chorale-singing was both an act of worship (the congregation joining in the praise of God) and a doctrinal teaching (the chorale texts often expressed Lutheran theological positions; singing them reinforced the doctrinal content). A typical Sunday service would include two to four chorales sung by the congregation, with the chorale tunes accompanied by the organ.

The chorale-cantata genre was specifically designed to engage with this congregational chorale-singing. The cantata took the day's principal chorale as its compositional foundation; the cantata's musical treatment of the chorale would prepare the congregation for the singing that would follow (or, in some structural arrangements, the cantata would conclude with the congregation joining in the closing chorale). The chorale-cantata was, in this sense, a music-theological commentary on the day's chorale, using the chorale's melody and (often) its text as the basis for an extended musical-theological exposition.

For the listener: the role of the chorale in worship explains why Bach's cantatas so often have a closing four-voice chorale movement as the final piece. The closing chorale is the moment when the congregational singing would have joined the choir; the simple four-voice harmonization is at the level the congregation could follow; the chorale-text would have been printed in the service bulletin or hymnal. The closing chorale of BWV 4 (the "Wir essen und leben wohl" setting) is in this tradition, a moment of participatory congregational worship, after the more-elaborate previous movements have been presented by the choir.

A modern non-Lutheran listener does not bring this participatory frame to the cantata listening. The closing chorale registers as another musical movement rather than as a transition from listening to singing. Chapter 16 will provide more context on the Lutheran-liturgical frame; for now, register that the closing chorale's relative simplicity is part of its participatory design, not an aesthetic limitation.

#### Cross-piece links

Cantata BWV 4 connects forward to many later works in this volume.

To the Orgelbüchlein chorale preludes (Chapter 6). Both bodies use the cantus-firmus principle to set Lutheran chorales; the cantata extends the principle to vocal-and-instrumental scale.

To the chorale cantata cycle of 1724-25 (Chapter 12). BWV 140 "Wachet auf" is the great mature chorale cantata; BWV 80 "Ein feste Burg" another. BWV 4 here is the early Mühlhausen-version specimen that the cycle's mature voice would later refine.

To the broader Leipzig sacred output (Chapters 13-14). Passion chorales function similarly to the cantata's chorale settings (Chapter 13); B Minor Mass movements often parody cantata material (Chapter 14, e.g. Crucifixus from BWV 12).

Chapter 16 will collect the Lutheran-chorale tradition; Chapter 20 will return to the chorale-prelude / chorale-cantata texture as part of the contrapuntal synthesis.

### 11.4 The Stories

#### The Leipzig institutional setting

Leipzig in 1723 was the principal city of Saxony, population about 30,000, a major commercial center (the Leipzig trade fair was one of the largest in central Europe), and a major university town (the University of Leipzig, founded 1409, was one of the oldest universities in Germany). The city was Lutheran but had a substantial Reformed (Calvinist) minority and a small Catholic community; the political authority was the Leipzig city council, which appointed the Thomaskantor and supervised the city's church-music establishment.

The Thomaskirche (St. Thomas Church) was the principal Lutheran church of Leipzig, a Gothic building from the thirteenth century, renovated in the sixteenth century, with substantial music establishment. The Nikolaikirche (St. Nicholas Church) was the other principal church, of comparable size and importance. The Neue Kirche (New Church) and Peterskirche (St. Peter's Church) were smaller and had less-elaborate musical establishments.

The Thomaskantor was responsible for music at all four churches, with the principal services at the Thomaskirche and Nikolaikirche alternating week by week. The Thomaskantor directed the Thomanerchor (the boys' choir attached to the Thomasschule, with about 50-55 boys aged 8-22); the choir was divided into four parts (soprano, alto, tenor, bass) with the youngest boys singing soprano, the older boys singing alto, and the oldest boys (with broken voices) singing tenor and bass. The choir was supplemented for major services by adult professional instrumentalists (the Stadtpfeifer, the town musicians, and additional contracted players).

The Thomasschule was a boarding school for the choir-boys, attached to the Thomaskirche. The Thomaskantor lived in the Thomasschule itself (in the apartment block reserved for senior school staff) and was responsible for teaching the boys both music and Latin. Bach's accommodation at the Thomasschule housed the Bach family across the 27 Leipzig years; the apartment was substantial (multiple rooms; a music-rehearsal space; accommodations for the various boarders and apprentices who lived with the family at various times).

The Thomaskantor's salary was a complex package: a base salary (about 100 thaler per year, modest); plus various supplementary payments for specific services (cantatas at weddings, funerals, civic occasions); plus the accommodation; plus the meal allowance for the boy-singers (who ate at the Bach household); plus other benefits. The total annual income was substantially higher than the base salary suggests, Bach himself estimated his total compensation in the October 1730 letter to his old school friend Georg Erdmann at about 700 thaler per year (about 100 thaler base + about 600 thaler in supplementary payments). The income supported a substantial household with many dependents.

#### The 1722-23 appointment process (in more detail)

The Leipzig council's deliberations during 1722-23 are recorded in the surviving city-council minutes; the records have been studied extensively by Bach biographers. The principal documents are:

* July 11, 1722: Telemann formally offered the Thomaskantor position; he indicates willingness but begins negotiating with his Frankfurt patron.
* November 22, 1722: Telemann formally declines the Leipzig offer (his Frankfurt salary was raised).
* December 9, 1722: Graupner formally offered the Leipzig position; he accepts and seeks release from Darmstadt.
* February 7, 1723: Bach performs his audition cantatas at the Thomaskirche (BWV 22 and BWV 23, composed for the audition) — done while Graupner's release was still pending, in case the council needed a third option.
* April 21, 1723: Graupner formally declines, the Darmstadt Landgrave having refused his release.
* April 22, 1723: The council deliberates on the third candidate; Platz's "we must accept the mediocre" remark is recorded; the council votes to offer the position to Bach.
* May 5, 1723: Bach signs the Leipzig contract.
* May 22, 1723: Bach moves his household from Köthen to Leipzig.
* May 31, 1723: Bach formally takes up the Thomaskantor position.
* June 6, 1723: Bach conducts his first Leipzig cantata (BWV 75) at the Nikolaikirche.

The audition process is worth noting separately. The council required two cantatas as audition pieces, BWV 22 "Jesus nahm zu sich die Zwölfe" (for the seventh Sunday after Epiphany, Quinquagesima) and BWV 23 "Du wahrer Gott und Davids Sohn" (for the same Sunday or a related liturgical occasion). Both cantatas were composed specifically for the audition and were performed at the Thomaskirche on February 7, 1723 (Quinquagesima Sunday). The council heard the cantatas alongside the regular Sunday service; Bach conducted; the cantatas were apparently well-received. Both cantatas remained in Bach's portfolio and would be performed again at Leipzig in subsequent years.

The contractual terms Bach accepted in May 1723 are preserved. They include: (a) the requirement to compose cantatas for each Sunday of the year and for major liturgical feast days; (b) the requirement to direct the Thomanerchor and the city's adult instrumentalists; (c) the requirement to teach Latin and music at the Thomasschule (the Latin teaching was eventually mostly delegated to assistants, but Bach formally remained responsible); (d) the requirement to administer the music at all four main Leipzig churches; (e) various subsidiary duties (examining choirboys, providing music for weddings and funerals, conducting the city's funeral-music establishment). The contract was a substantial commitment; Bach would honor it for the next twenty-seven years.

#### Cantata BWV 4 in compositional context

The cantata BWV 4 "Christ lag in Todesbanden" has an unusually complex compositional history. The early version was composed at Mühlhausen probably around 1707-1708, Bach was 22-23 years old, just married to Maria Barbara, in his only year as Mühlhausen organist. The Mühlhausen version was likely performed at the Easter Sunday 1708 service at the Blasiuskirche; the manuscript of the Mühlhausen version does not survive.

Bach kept the cantata in his portfolio. When he arrived at Leipzig in 1723, he had this Mühlhausen-era work available as part of his existing repertoire. He revised it for the Leipzig Easter Sunday 1724 service (April 9, 1724) and again for Easter Sunday 1725 (April 1, 1725). The Leipzig versions were performed at the Thomaskirche or Nikolaikirche (the two churches alternated weekly; the specific Easter-Sunday location depended on the year). The Leipzig manuscript is the surviving primary source; it represents the work as Bach himself performed it at Leipzig.

The cantata's compositional features show the early Bach voice: the chorale-cantata genre handled with assurance; the seven-verse structure with systematic variation across verses; the four-voice contrapuntal writing in the choral movements; the obbligato-instrument writing in the solo movements. The Leipzig revisions probably tightened some of the orchestration and adjusted the work for the Leipzig forces (the Thomanerchor was larger than the Mühlhausen choir Bach had used in 1708; the Leipzig instrumental forces were also more substantial). The musical core of the work is from the Mühlhausen period; the Leipzig version is a refined presentation of the early-Bach voice.

The cantata's modern reception treats BWV 4 as the early-cantata masterwork and the work that bridges the early and mature cantata practice. It is the most-recorded of the early cantatas; it appears in all the complete-cantata cycles (Harnoncourt/Leonhardt, Richter, Rilling, Gardiner, Koopman, Suzuki); it is performed frequently as a stand-alone Easter cantata in concert settings.

#### Recording history briefly

Ch 21 surveys the major cantata recording cycles (Richter / Harnoncourt+Leonhardt / Rilling / Gardiner / Herreweghe / Koopman / Suzuki). For first orientation on BWV 4 specifically: Gardiner 2000 (period instruments, from the Cantata Pilgrimage) plus Richter 1968 (modern instruments, from the canonical Archiv cycle). The contrast between them is substantial.

#### Was Bach the right choice?

A judgment about the famous "third-choice" narrative: the Leipzig city council was right to appoint Bach in 1723 and would have been wrong to appoint either Telemann or Graupner, regardless of the council's own perception of the relative ranking. Telemann was a more-famous composer in 1723 and would have produced cantatas of high competence (Telemann composed roughly 1,700 cantatas across his career — far more than Bach), but the cantatas Telemann actually wrote are technically polished without being depth-producing in the way Bach's cantatas are. Graupner's Darmstadt court output is even more competent and even less individually compelling. The Leipzig council selected Bach because their first two choices were unavailable; if the council had been able to choose freely, they likely would have chosen Telemann and lost — Telemann at Leipzig would have produced something competent and forgettable. The accident of Telemann's Frankfurt raise and the Darmstadt Landgrave's refusal to release Graupner produced one of the great institutional accidents in cultural history: Bach got the post that gave him the institutional context to compose the 200 cantatas, the Passions, the B Minor Mass, the Christmas Oratorio, and the late contrapuntal works. The Platz "we must accept the mediocre" remark is funny in retrospect; it is also a reminder that institutional judgments about artistic significance are frequently wrong in their own moment.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"Bach was the third choice for the Leipzig Thomaskantor position in 1722-23. The first choice (Telemann) declined for a better Frankfurt offer; the second choice (Graupner) was refused release by his Darmstadt patron; Bach was offered the post by default. The famous council remark, 'since the best could not be obtained, we must accept the mediocre', has been cited for centuries as evidence of how unrecognized Bach was. The chapter argues for a more nuanced reading. What does the third-choice fact actually tell us about Bach's contemporary standing? Are there modern equivalents, a director who took on a position only after the first two choices declined, who then proceeded to do work that was recognized as foundational decades later?"_

* _"Cantata BWV 4 was composed at Mühlhausen c. 1707-1708 (when Bach was 22-23) and revised at Leipzig for the Easter Sunday 1724 and 1725 performances (when Bach was 39-40). The same work survived from the early to the mature period of Bach's career. Listen to BWV 4 with the question: what about this music suggests the early voice (the 22-year-old Bach in 1708), and what suggests the mature voice (the 40-year-old Bach who revised it for Leipzig)? Are there features you can identify that point in one direction or the other?"_

* _"The chorale-cantata genre takes a single Lutheran chorale as the foundation for an extended multi-movement work. The seven verses of BWV 4 are all built on the chorale tune 'Christ lag in Todesbanden,' but the texture and scoring vary continuously across the verses. What kind of compositional skill does this require? Is it harder or easier to compose a work in which all movements share the same melodic material, versus a work in which each movement has its own thematic material?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the John Eliot Gardiner 2000 Soli Deo Gloria recording of Cantata BWV 4 (or one of the alternatives listed in §11.1). Listen to the cantata a second time and attend to these four concrete moments.

  1. **The chorale tune in the opening Sinfonia (0:00-1:00).** Listen for the chorale tune "Christ lag in Todesbanden" in the strings. The tune appears in long notes; the bass moves slowly underneath; the harmony is somber. Try to memorize the tune's shape; you will hear it many times across the next 22 minutes.

  2. **The cantus firmus in Versus 1 (1:00-4:30).** Listen for the chorale tune in the soprano voice, in long sustained notes, against the more-active lower voices. The cantus firmus is the central feature of the opening chorus; the lower voices weave contrapuntal lines around the cantus firmus.

  3. **The chorale tune in Versus 4 (around 9:00-11:30), the structural center of the cantata.** Now the chorale tune appears in the alto voice rather than the soprano. The four-voice texture is contrapuntally dense; the chorale tune is sometimes hard to identify against the lower voices' activity. Try to follow the alto specifically; the cantus firmus is in the alto's sustained line.

  4. **The closing chorale (Versus 8, around 19:00-22:00).** The simplest movement of the cantata, a four-voice chorale harmonization, in the standard Lutheran chorale-singing style. The chorale tune is in the soprano (where it would normally be in congregational singing); the lower voices provide harmonic accompaniment. The simplicity is intentional; this is the moment when the congregation would have joined the choir in singing.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : another early Bach cantata: for example BWV 71 "Gott ist mein König" (the Mühlhausen civic-council cantata of 1708, the one of Bach's few cantatas to be printed during his lifetime; about 17 minutes). Recordings by Harnoncourt, Suzuki, or Gardiner are available. The contrast with BWV 4, both early cantatas, different genres, shows the range of Bach's early cantata practice.

**Listen** (mature chorale cantata): a chorale cantata from the 1724-25 chorale cantata cycle: for example BWV 78 "Jesu, der du meine Seele" (the famous duet "Wir eilen mit schwachen, doch emsigen Schritten" is the most-performed Bach soprano-alto duet; the cantata is about 22 minutes total). Hearing a mature chorale cantata after BWV 4 shows how Bach extended the genre over the seventeen years between the works.

**Read** : Alfred Dürr, _The Cantatas of J. S. Bach_ (Oxford University Press, 2005 English edition, originally German 1971 with revisions). The cantata-by-cantata commentary; about 2-3 pages per cantata. The entry on BWV 4 gives the compositional history and detailed structural analysis.

**Read** (alternative): John Eliot Gardiner, _Bach: Music in the Castle of Heaven_ (Knopf 2013), chapter on the Lutheran chorale cantatas. About 50 pages. Gardiner (cf. Ch 1 §1.2) as a working conductor of all of Bach's cantatas gives a different perspective than Dürr's scholarly-cataloguing approach.

---

## Chapter 12: The Leipzig Cantatas, Overview and Selected Anchors

The cantatas Bach composed at Leipzig between 1723 and his late years constitute the central body of his life's work. About 200 cantatas survive from a presumed total of roughly 300 (Christoph Wolff's estimate, based on the documentary evidence that Bach composed at least five complete annual cycles plus additional pieces for specific occasions). The surviving cantatas represent approximately two-thirds of Bach's Leipzig sacred-vocal output; the lost cantatas (about 100) are known by title from the Leipzig service-bulletin records and other archival sources but the music itself has not survived.

The cantata cycle is the most-extensive single body of sacred music in the Western catalogue. No other composer has produced an equivalent. The cantatas range from short 10-minute pieces (typically for ordinary Sundays with limited time in the service) to substantial 30-35-minute pieces (typically for major feast days like Easter, Christmas, Pentecost, the Marian feasts); from simple solo-with-continuo arias to elaborate large-chorus-with-orchestra movements with trumpets, timpani, and obbligato instruments. The cantatas span Bach's compositional voice in its full range: pastoral, contemplative, jubilant, mournful, mystical, dramatic.

This chapter does three things. First, it sets up the cantata cycle's structural and chronological organization: Bach's five annual cycles, the chorale-cantata cycle of 1724-25, the parody technique, the lost cantatas, the modern Dürr cantata-chronology that organizes the catalogue. Second, it anchors deeply on four representative cantatas: BWV 140 "Wachet auf, ruft uns die Stimme" (the great chorale cantata of 1731, perhaps Bach's most-celebrated single cantata); BWV 147 "Herz und Mund und Tat und Leben" (1723, the cantata containing the chorale "Jesu, Joy of Man's Desiring" in two of its movements); BWV 80 "Ein feste Burg ist unser Gott" (the Reformation Day chorale cantata on Luther's 1529 chorale); and BWV 82 "Ich habe genug" (1727, the bass-and-oboe solo cantata, one of the most-celebrated Bach solo cantatas). Third, it surveys the principal cantata recording cycles and discusses how to navigate the 200-cantata catalogue.

This is one of the longest chapters in the volume. The cantata output is large enough to require substantial treatment; even at this length the treatment is selective.

### 12.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote, the Leipzig cantata cycle

Leipzig, late November 1731. Bach is forty-six years old, in his ninth year as Thomaskantor. The Sunday morning of November 25, 1731 is the twenty-seventh Sunday after Trinity, the last Sunday of the liturgical year before the next year's Advent cycle begins. The Gospel reading for the day is from Matthew 25:1-13: the parable of the wise and foolish virgins, in which ten virgins are waiting for the bridegroom to arrive at midnight; five had brought oil for their lamps and were ready when he came, five had not and missed the moment. The parable is among the more-dramatic of Jesus's eschatological teachings; it is also (in the Lutheran liturgical tradition) the principal Gospel reading for the last Sunday before Advent, with the bridegroom's arrival serving as a figure for the Second Coming of Christ.

The day's chorale is "Wachet auf, ruft uns die Stimme" by Philipp Nicolai (1556-1608), the Lutheran pastor and hymn-writer who composed both the text and the tune in 1599 during the plague that struck his Westphalian congregation that year. The chorale's text is a paraphrase of the Matthew parable, expanding the dramatic situation across three verses: Verse 1, the watchman's call from the city's high tower at midnight, summoning the wise virgins to wake up because the bridegroom is coming; Verse 2, the virgins' response and the procession to meet the bridegroom; Verse 3, the heavenly Jerusalem and the eternal feast. The tune is one of the most-famous Lutheran chorales, a Phrygian-mode melody with a distinctive descending-and-rising shape that has been called among the most-perfect chorale tunes in the Lutheran repertoire.

Bach has composed BWV 140 "Wachet auf, ruft uns die Stimme" for this specific Sunday. The cantata is in seven movements totaling about 30 minutes. The structural plan is the standard chorale-cantata structure of the 1724-25 cycle (though BWV 140 was composed in 1731, several years after the main chorale-cantata cycle had ended): an opening chorus on the chorale's first verse (the watchman's call); a tenor recitative; a soprano-bass duet (the soul as bride and Christ as bridegroom, the famous duet "Wann kömmst du, mein Heil?", "When will you come, my Salvation?"); a tenor chorale (the chorale tune in the tenor voice with violin obbligato above, the most-famous single movement of the cantata, often extracted as a stand-alone piece); a bass recitative; another soprano-bass duet; and the closing four-voice chorale.

The cantata's central image, the bridegroom's arrival at midnight, is dramatized through the music with extraordinary specificity. The opening chorus depicts the watchman's call from the city tower with rising trumpet-like figures in the upper voices and the chorale tune in the soprano as the watchman's voice itself. The famous tenor chorale (Movement 4, the "Zion hört die Wächter singen", "Zion hears the watchmen sing") sets the chorale tune in the tenor voice with a flowing obbligato in the violins that has been called among the most-beautiful obbligato lines in the Bach catalogue. The soprano-bass duets dramatize the bride-bridegroom encounter using the elaborate Italian-opera-derived love-duet idiom Bach had absorbed and transformed.

The cantata is performed at the Nikolaikirche (it was the Nikolaikirche's week to host the principal Sunday service; the Thomaskirche would have the principal service the following week). The Thomanerchor sings; the Stadtpfeifer and contracted players accompany; the soloists are drawn from the choir and from contracted singers. The congregation hears the cantata between the Gospel reading and the sermon. The sermon would have expounded the Matthew parable; the cantata's musical-theological commentary would have prepared the congregation for the sermon and for the chorale-singing that would follow.

The cantata is performed at the Nikolaikirche on November 25, 1731. It is performed again at subsequent November-last-Sunday services in the years to follow (the standard Bach practice was to revive cantatas at the corresponding Sunday in subsequent years, often with minor revisions). The cantata's surviving manuscript is in Bach's autograph; the work survives in performance into the twentieth and twenty-first centuries as one of the most-recorded individual Bach cantatas. The "Zion hört die Wächter singen" tenor chorale is among the most-famous individual movements in the Bach catalogue; the cantata as a whole is, alongside BWV 4 "Christ lag in Todesbanden" and BWV 147 "Herz und Mund," the cantata most listeners encounter as their introduction to Bach's Leipzig cantata cycle.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen, BWV 140 "Wachet auf, ruft uns die Stimme"

  1. **The opening chorus's monumental scale.** The first movement is one of Bach's largest single cantata movements, about 6-7 minutes long, with the chorale tune in the soprano against an elaborate orchestral framework. The orchestra includes a French-horn signal-figure (the "watchman's horn") that announces the cantata's opening; the chorale tune enters in the soprano with the entire ensemble in full texture; the lower voices weave contrapuntal lines around the cantus firmus. This is the chorale-cantata opening at maximum scale.

  2. **The "Zion hört die Wächter singen" tenor chorale (Movement 4).** The famous middle movement. The chorale tune is in the tenor voice; a beautiful flowing obbligato is in the unison violins and violas above; the bass continuo is underneath. The texture is unusually transparent, three voices in clear contrapuntal relationship. The movement is about 4-5 minutes long; it has been extracted as a stand-alone piece for many performance contexts. Listen for the obbligato specifically, the flowing line above the tenor's cantus firmus is one of Bach's most-celebrated melodic inventions.

  3. **The two soprano-bass duets (Movements 3 and 6).** The bride-bridegroom duets are the cantata's emotional core. The soprano represents the Soul (the human worshipper); the bass represents Christ (the heavenly bridegroom). The duets are scored with obbligato instruments (violin in the third movement, oboe in the sixth) and have the dialogue-aria character of Italian-opera love duets transformed into Lutheran-theological setting. Listen for the dialogue between soprano and bass, the soul's questioning, the bridegroom's response, and the obbligato instrument's commentary above.

#### One-sentence backgrounds

**BWV 140 "Wachet auf, ruft uns die Stimme"** : composed at Leipzig 1731 for the Twenty-Seventh Sunday after Trinity (November 25, 1731); a chorale cantata on Philipp Nicolai's 1599 chorale, 7 movements totaling about 30 minutes; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is John Eliot Gardiner with the Monteverdi Choir and English Baroque Soloists (Soli Deo Gloria, 2000 Cantata Pilgrimage), chosen for the project's scholarly authority and the dramatic vitality of the readings.

**BWV 147 "Herz und Mund und Tat und Leben"** : composed at Weimar c. 1716 in earlier form, revised at Leipzig 1723 for the feast of the Visitation of Mary (July 2, 1723); ten movements totaling about 30 minutes; contains "Jesu, Joy of Man's Desiring" as the chorale-melody of two movements (Movements 6 and 10, the latter being the cantata's closing).

**BWV 80 "Ein feste Burg ist unser Gott"** : composed at Leipzig for Reformation Day (October 31), Bach's chorale-cantata setting of Luther's 1529 chorale on Psalm 46; eight movements totaling about 30 minutes; the dating of the work's various versions is complex (earlier Weimar version BWV 80a; main Leipzig version dating disputed between 1727-30 and 1735-40).

**BWV 82 "Ich habe genug"** : composed at Leipzig 1727 for the feast of the Purification of Mary (Candlemas, February 2); a solo cantata for bass voice with oboe obbligato, strings, and continuo; 5 movements totaling about 22 minutes.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Bach Cantata 140 Wachet auf Gardiner" or "Bach BWV 140 Sleepers Awake" EN: "Bach Cantata 82 Ich habe genug" EN: "Bach Cantata 147 Jesu Joy of Man's Desiring" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** (Ch 21 surveys the broader cantata recording traditions):

For BWV 140: Richter (Archiv 1978, mod-inst), Harnoncourt (Teldec 1976, HIP), Herreweghe (Harmonia Mundi 1995, HIP), Suzuki (BIS 2002, HIP).

For BWV 82, the bass soloist matters as much as the conductor — recommended: Lorraine Hunt Lieberson with Craig Smith (Nonesuch 2003, mezzo with the work transposed; she died of breast cancer in 2006 at 52, three years after the session); Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau with Richter (Archiv 1960s-80s, multiple sessions); Hans Hotter (EMI 1950s); Klaus Mertens with Koopman (Erato); Andreas Scholl with Herreweghe (Harmonia Mundi 1990s, countertenor — rare for this work).

For BWV 147 and BWV 80: the major cycles (Gardiner, Richter, Harnoncourt, Suzuki, Koopman, Herreweghe) all include both.

### 12.2 The Listening Window

This section is for your second listen to BWV 140 "Wachet auf, ruft uns die Stimme." Timings refer to the Gardiner 2000 Soli Deo Gloria recording, which runs about 30 minutes.

**0:00-7:00: Movement 1: Coro. "Wachet auf, ruft uns die Stimme."** The opening chorus, the cantata's largest single movement. The orchestra enters with a horn-and-string figure that serves as the watchman's call; the chorale tune is in the soprano voice as a slow cantus firmus; the alto, tenor, and bass voices weave contrapuntal lines around it. The text is the chorale's first verse: "Wake up, calls the voice of the watchmen, high in the tower; wake up, you city of Jerusalem! Midnight is this hour; they call us with bright voice: where are you, wise virgins? Look up, the Bridegroom comes; arise, take up your lamps! Hallelujah! Make yourselves ready for the wedding; you must go to meet him." The movement closes with a triumphant "Alleluia" buildup.

**About 7:00-7:30: Movement 2: Tenor recitative.** "He comes, he comes, the Bridegroom comes! Daughters of Zion, come forth; his procession hurries from on high to your mother's house. The Bridegroom comes, who like a roe and a young hart leaps upon the hills..." A short tenor recitative (about 30 seconds), establishing the dramatic situation between the opening chorus and the soprano-bass duet that follows.

**About 7:30-13:00: Movement 3: Soprano-bass duet "Wann kömmst du, mein Heil?" with violin obbligato.** The first bride-bridegroom duet. The soprano (the Soul) asks: "When will you come, my Salvation?" The bass (Christ) responds: "I come, your portion." The two voices alternate questions and answers; the violin obbligato weaves a flowing line above; the continuo provides bass support. The duet has the Italian-opera-love-duet character that Bach had absorbed and transformed into the Lutheran-theological setting; the dialogue is between human soul and divine bridegroom rather than between operatic lovers.

**About 13:00-17:30: Movement 4: Chorale "Zion hört die Wächter singen."** The famous movement. The tenor voices of the choir (sometimes performed by a tenor soloist) carry the chorale tune as cantus firmus; the unison violins and violas play a beautiful flowing obbligato line above; the continuo bass moves underneath. The text is the chorale's second verse: "Zion hears the watchmen sing; her heart leaps for joy; she wakes and rises in haste. Her friend comes from heaven, glorious; strong in mercy, mighty in truth; her star rises, her sun shines forth..." The texture is unusually transparent, only three voices (the chorale tune in tenor; the obbligato in violins/violas; the bass), and the obbligato is one of Bach's most-celebrated melodic inventions. Listen specifically for the obbligato line; track it as a separate voice across the entire movement.

**About 17:30-18:30: Movement 5: Bass recitative.** "Step in to me, you chosen Bride; do not delay any longer; I wait for you with longing..." A short recitative (about 1 minute) preparing the second bride-bridegroom duet.

**About 18:30-24:30: Movement 6: Soprano-bass duet "Mein Freund ist mein" with oboe obbligato.** The second bride-bridegroom duet. The Soul and Christ now sing as united bride and bridegroom: "My friend is mine, and I am his." The duet is more flowing and less dialogue-like than the first duet (the bride and bridegroom are no longer questioning each other but joined together). The oboe obbligato weaves above the two voices; the texture is rich and lyrical.

**About 24:30-30:00: Movement 7: Chorale "Gloria sei dir gesungen."** The closing chorale. A simple four-voice harmonization of the chorale tune (the third verse of Nicolai's chorale): "Glory be sung to you, with the tongues of men and angels, with harps and beautiful cymbals. Of twelve pearls are the gates, in your city we are companions to the angels high around your throne." The simple chorale-singing texture brings the cantata to a peaceful close; the congregation in the Bach-era service would have joined the choir in singing.

If your recording's total time differs from Gardiner's 30:00 by more than 15%, you may be hearing a different performance approach. Richter (1978) runs about 33 minutes (slower, weightier); Harnoncourt (1976) runs about 29 minutes; Herreweghe (1995) runs about 30 minutes; Suzuki (2002) runs about 28 minutes.

### 12.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The cantata cycle's chronological organization

Bach's Leipzig cantata composition can be organized into five annual cycles plus various occasional pieces.

**First annual cycle (1723-24)** : Bach's first year at Leipzig. He composed cantatas at a rate of roughly one per week through the year, with the cycle starting at his arrival in late May 1723 and running through the Trinity season of 1724. The cycle contains many of Bach's early Leipzig works (BWV 75, BWV 76, BWV 21, BWV 24, BWV 64, BWV 67, and many others); the style varies but tends toward the standard cantata structure of opening chorus + recitatives + arias + closing chorale.

**Second annual cycle (1724-25): the chorale cantata cycle.** Bach's second Leipzig year was dedicated to chorale cantatas: every Sunday's cantata was a chorale-based work using the Sunday's appropriate chorale as foundation. The cycle is incomplete (some cantatas are lost; some were composed in subsequent years to fill gaps) but the surviving works form the most-deliberate body of chorale cantatas in the Bach catalogue. BWV 4 (Chapter 11) was revived for this cycle; BWV 80 (in its principal form), BWV 140 (composed later in 1731 but in the chorale-cantata style of this cycle), BWV 62, BWV 78, BWV 99, BWV 96, BWV 38, and many others belong to this cycle.

**Third annual cycle (1725-27)** : a more diverse cycle, including Christian Friedrich Henrici (Picander)-libretto cantatas and other works. The cycle was apparently less unified in plan than the second cycle; Bach was working on the St. John Passion (composed 1724) and the St. Matthew Passion (composed 1727) alongside the cantata cycle. BWV 82 (1727) belongs to this cycle.

**Fourth annual cycle (1728-29)** : also less unified; the Picander cantatas of this period include works like BWV 174, BWV 159, and others. The cycle is partially lost; documentary records suggest cantatas existed but the music has not survived.

**Fifth annual cycle (early 1730s)** : even less complete in the surviving record. Many of the later cantatas may belong to this cycle or to subsequent years; the dating is uncertain.

**Occasional cantatas** : throughout the Leipzig years Bach composed cantatas for specific occasions outside the regular liturgical cycle: weddings, funerals, civic events, secular celebrations. The _Coffee Cantata_ BWV 211 (c. 1734-35), the _Peasant Cantata_ BWV 212 (1742), and various secular cantatas belong to this group.

The Alfred Dürr cantata-chronology (developed in the 1950s-60s and codified in his _Bach: The Cantatas_ of 1971) is the modern standard reference for dating the cantatas. Dürr's chronology is based on paper-and-watermark analysis of Bach's autograph manuscripts plus liturgical-occasion analysis plus textual references; the chronology is widely accepted and forms the basis of the cantata-numbering and cycle-assignment used in most contemporary recordings and scholarly editions.

#### The standard cantata structure

A standard Leipzig cantata has the following structural pattern:

* **Opening chorus** (4-7 minutes): a large-scale movement for choir and orchestra, often setting a key Biblical verse or chorale verse. The opening chorus is typically the cantata's musical centerpiece and is usually the most-elaborate single movement.
* **Recitative** (1-2 minutes): a setting of prose text in declamatory style, with continuo accompaniment. The recitative develops the cantata's textual-theological argument between the more-substantial musical movements.
* **Aria** (5-7 minutes): a setting of lyric text for a solo voice with one or more obbligato instruments and continuo. The aria is typically the cantata's emotional center; multiple arias may appear in a single cantata.
* **Recitative + Aria pairs** : the standard pattern is alternation of recitatives and arias, with the recitatives preparing the arias dramatically.
* **Closing chorale** (1-2 minutes): a simple four-voice harmonization of a chorale tune, sung by the choir and (typically) joined by the congregation. The closing chorale brings the cantata to a participatory worship-conclusion.

The chorale cantata adds a structural constraint: the chorale tune appears throughout the cantata as compositional material; the opening chorus uses the chorale as a cantus firmus; the closing chorale is a four-voice harmonization of the same tune. The chorale cantata's middle movements (recitatives and arias) often use the chorale tune more freely, sometimes as melodic-thematic material, sometimes only in implied harmonic relationships.

The BWV 140 fits this structure with some specific features: the opening chorus is unusually large; the central tenor chorale (Movement 4) is a chorale-prelude-like setting embedded within the cantata; the two soprano-bass duets are the cantata's dramatic centers.

#### BWV 147 and "Jesu, Joy of Man's Desiring" (briefly)

Cantata BWV 147 "Herz und Mund und Tat und Leben", "Heart and mouth and deed and life", is one of Bach's most-recorded cantatas because of the chorale movements at positions 6 and 10 of the cantata's structure. Both movements set the chorale "Jesu, meiner Seelen Wonne" (text by Martin Janus 1665, tune by Johann Schop 1641); the chorale's English-language adaptation as "Jesu, Joy of Man's Desiring" has made it among the most-famous chorale melodies in the Western catalogue, often performed at weddings, in choral concerts, and in arrangements for various ensemble combinations.

The cantata as a whole is in 10 movements covering about 30 minutes. It is structured in two parts, Movements 1-6 are Part I; Movements 7-10 are Part II. (The Lutheran tradition divided substantial cantatas into two parts, with the first part performed before the sermon and the second part after; the structural division is preserved in BWV 147 and in many other Bach cantatas.) The two chorale movements ("Jesu, Joy of Man's Desiring") sit at the end of each part, Movement 6 closes Part I; Movement 10 closes Part II. The two settings use the same chorale tune with the same obbligato instrumental framework (a flowing line in the upper strings); the two movements are essentially identical musically, distinguished only by their text-verse content.

The "Jesu, Joy of Man's Desiring" movements have had a particular separate life outside the cantata. Myra Hess's 1926 piano arrangement (made for her wartime National Gallery concerts in London during World War II) brought the music to massive popular recognition; arrangements for chorus, organ, string ensemble, and various combinations are now standard in the Western performance tradition. The music's broader cultural placement (in films, at weddings, on television soundtracks) means many listeners encounter "Jesu, Joy of Man's Desiring" without realizing it is from a Bach cantata; the rediscovery of the cantata context is one of the rewards of returning to the source.

The cantata as a whole is composed for the feast of the Visitation of Mary (July 2, the celebration of the meeting between the pregnant Mary and the pregnant Elizabeth, mother of John the Baptist). The text engages with the spiritual theme of Mary's interior song of praise (the Magnificat, which Bach also set as a Latin Magnificat, Chapter 15) and the broader theme of the soul's joyful encounter with Christ. The chorale "Jesu, meiner Seelen Wonne", "Jesus, joy of my soul", sits at the center of the textual-theological argument.

#### BWV 80 "Ein feste Burg ist unser Gott" (briefly)

Cantata BWV 80 "Ein feste Burg ist unser Gott", "A mighty fortress is our God", is Bach's chorale cantata on Martin Luther's 1529 chorale (text and tune both by Luther). The chorale is the most-iconic Lutheran chorale; Luther wrote it as a paraphrase of Psalm 46 ("God is our refuge and strength, a very present help in trouble"); the chorale has been the unofficial Lutheran anthem from the Reformation onward. Heinrich Heine called it the "Marseillaise of the Reformation."

The cantata's compositional history is unusually complex. The work exists in multiple versions: an early Weimar version (BWV 80a, c. 1714-1717, in different scoring); a probable Leipzig version of c. 1727-1730; a probable later Leipzig revision; and a later interpolation by Bach's son Wilhelm Friedemann that adds trumpets and timpani in the opening chorus (the timpani-and-trumpets version is one that many modern recordings present, but the Bach-Werke-Verzeichnis catalogue has been clarified about which version represents Bach's own intentions versus Wilhelm Friedemann's). The cantata's modern reception treats the scoring question as somewhat fluid; different recordings make different choices.

The cantata is in eight movements totaling about 30 minutes. The opening chorus is a contrapuntal masterpiece, a long, contrapuntally-dense setting of Luther's first verse, with the chorale tune as cantus firmus in the soprano and four lower voices in dense contrapuntal weaving. The cantata's central movements develop the chorale text through arias and chorale-based settings; the closing four-voice chorale brings the work to its participatory conclusion.

The cantata's annual occasion is Reformation Day (October 31, the anniversary of Luther's 1517 posting of the Ninety-Five Theses on the Wittenberg church door). The Reformation Day service in Bach's Leipzig would have been a major occasion, the celebration of the Lutheran tradition's founding moment, and BWV 80 is the cantata Bach composed for it.

#### BWV 82 "Ich habe genug" (briefly)

Cantata BWV 82 "Ich habe genug", "I have enough", is among Bach's most-celebrated solo cantatas. Composed at Leipzig for the feast of the Purification of Mary (Candlemas, February 2), the cantata uses the Old Testament figure of Simeon (Luke 2:25-32, the elderly man in the Temple who receives the infant Jesus and sings the Nunc dimittis: "Now let your servant depart in peace, for my eyes have seen your salvation") as the textual-spiritual frame. The cantata's central theme is the human peace that comes from spiritual fulfillment, Simeon has "enough" because he has seen Christ; the believer can be "ready to die in peace" because of faith in Christ.

The cantata is scored for solo bass voice, oboe obbligato, strings, and continuo, a small ensemble compared to the choral cantatas. The five movements are: (1) opening bass aria "Ich habe genug" with oboe obbligato, the cantata's most-famous movement, about 8 minutes long; (2) bass recitative "Ich habe genug, mein Trost ist nur allein"; (3) bass aria "Schlummert ein, ihr matten Augen" with strings, a deeply-restful aria about closing the weary eyes in peaceful sleep, often performed as a stand-alone piece; (4) bass recitative "Mein Gott! Wann kommt das schöne: Nun!"; (5) closing bass aria "Ich freue mich auf meinen Tod", the joyful affirmation of welcoming death.

The cantata is one of Bach's most-recorded solo cantatas. The text's theme, the believer's peaceful readiness for death, has resonated with audiences across the centuries; the music's combination of contemplative depth and quiet intensity has made the cantata a favorite of bass and (in transposed versions) other voice ranges. Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau recorded BWV 82 multiple times in his career; the Lorraine Hunt Lieberson 2003 Nonesuch recording (transposed for mezzo-soprano, with Hunt Lieberson dying of breast cancer at age 52 in 2006, three years after the recording) has the additional poignancy of the singer's later death; many other bass and mezzo singers have recorded the work in canonical performances.

For the listener: BWV 82 is the recommended next-listening anchor after BWV 140. The solo-cantata scale is more intimate; the deeply-personal text is uniquely powerful; the music's combination of acceptance, peace, and joyful expectation gives the cantata an emotional unity that the multi-movement choral cantatas have at greater scale. About 22 minutes; rewarding for repeated listening.

#### Cross-piece links

The Leipzig cantatas connect forward to many later works in this volume.

To the Passions (Chapter 13) and the Christmas Oratorio (Chapter 15). The Passions are large-scale developments of cantata structure with the Gospel-narrative thread woven through; the Christmas Oratorio is structured as six related cantatas across the 1734-35 liturgical Christmas season. Same compositional voice, expanded scale.

To the B Minor Mass (Chapter 14). Many Mass movements are parodied from earlier cantatas (Crucifixus from BWV 12; Gratias/Dona from BWV 29; others).

To the chorale-prelude / chorale-cantata traditions (Chapters 6 and 11). The Leipzig cantatas use the same cantus-firmus principle Bach inherited and developed across his career.

Chapter 20 §20.2 (the chorale-prelude texture as a unifying thread) and §20.7 (the Italianate-concertante writing) will collect themes the Leipzig cantatas exemplify at full scale.

### 12.4 The Stories

#### The Leipzig cantata performance practice

A Bach cantata in Leipzig was performed by the Thomanerchor (boys' choir, about 50-55 voices though typically only about 16-20 sang at any given service) plus adult instrumentalists (the Stadtpfeifer and contracted players, totaling about 12-18 musicians). The performance forces were modest by modern symphonic standards but standard for a major Lutheran city in the eighteenth century. The choir was divided into four parts (soprano, alto, tenor, bass) with the youngest boys singing soprano, slightly older boys singing alto, and the oldest boys (with broken voices) singing tenor and bass. Adult soloists were typically drawn from the choir's older members; occasionally professional adult soloists were contracted for major works.

The instrumentalists included a continuo group (harpsichord or organ + cello + violone/bass) plus the variable melodic and harmonic instruments the cantata required (strings, oboes, flutes/recorders, horns, trumpets, timpani, varying by cantata). The Stadtpfeifer (the city's official town musicians) provided the core instrumental forces; additional contracted players were brought in for major occasions.

The cantata's preparation time was typically one week. Bach would compose a cantata across Monday through Wednesday or Thursday; he would copy out the performance parts (often with help from his older sons and from his copyists, including Anna Magdalena); the choir and instrumentalists would rehearse on Thursday and Friday; the cantata would be performed at the Sunday service. The pace was demanding but feasible for a composer of Bach's compositional fluency; the surviving cantatas show the work was done at speed but with high craft throughout.

The cantata's performance context was the regular Sunday morning service at the Thomaskirche or Nikolaikirche (the two main Leipzig churches alternated). The service ran about 3-4 hours from early morning; the cantata was performed between the Gospel reading and the sermon; the cantata's text and music were designed to engage with the sermon's theme. After the sermon, the congregation would sing the day's chorales (with the organ accompanying); the service would close with the standard liturgical pieces. The cantata was one element of a complex liturgical sequence; it functioned within the broader service rather than as a stand-alone concert piece.

#### The parody technique

Bach frequently reused his own earlier compositions in subsequent works, adapting movements to new texts, sometimes to entirely new theological contexts, by changing the words while keeping the music. The practice is called _parody_ in the eighteenth-century musicological sense (not the modern sense of "comic imitation", eighteenth-century _parody_ simply meant the practice of setting new text to existing music).

Bach's parody technique was extensive. Many of his cantatas reuse music from secular cantatas (the Christmas Oratorio is the most-famous example, Chapter 15, with about half its movements parodied from earlier secular cantatas). Many of the B Minor Mass movements are parodied from earlier cantata movements (Chapter 14). Several Christmas-season cantatas reuse Easter or Pentecost music from earlier years. The practice was conventional in eighteenth-century church music; Bach was not unusual in reusing music, but he was unusual in the systematic and large-scale nature of his reuse.

The parody technique has scholarly and theological implications. The Lutheran tradition treated the text as theologically primary; the music's affective character could legitimately serve multiple contexts as long as the affective fit was appropriate. Bach himself defended the practice in correspondence (the available documentation supports the position that he saw parody as a legitimate compositional resource). Modern scholarship has worked extensively on parody-tracing, identifying which works are parodied from which sources, which versions are the original and which the reuse, and the parody relationships are well-documented in the modern critical editions.

For the listener: when you encounter a Bach cantata movement and recognize the same music from another work, the parody relationship is the explanation. The Crucifixus of the B Minor Mass, the same music as the opening chorus of Cantata BWV 12 "Weinen, Klagen, Sorgen, Zagen", is one of the most-celebrated examples. The Christmas Oratorio's first chorus "Jauchzet, frohlocket" reuses music from the secular cantata BWV 214 "Tönet, ihr Pauken!" for a princely-birthday celebration. The reuse is not laziness; it is a deliberate compositional strategy that lets Bach apply his best musical inventions to multiple textual contexts.

#### Recording history briefly

The major cantata cycles (Richter / Harnoncourt+Leonhardt / Rilling / Gardiner / Herreweghe / Koopman / Suzuki) all include the cantatas anchored here; Ch 21 surveys the cycles in detail. A few work-specific notes: Richter's BWV 140 with Edith Mathis and Theo Adam is the canonical modern-instrument version; Herreweghe 1995 is the celebrated BWV 140 HIP recording. BWV 82 has additional bass-soloist non-cycle recordings worth seeking — Hans Hotter (EMI 1950s), Bryn Terfel (DG 2000s), plus the Lorraine Hunt Lieberson Nonesuch 2003 transposition already mentioned. For "Jesu, Joy of Man's Desiring" (the famous BWV 147 movements), the cantata-context recordings are the recommended starting point; the many ensemble arrangements lose the cantata's structural integrity.

#### Is the standard cantata structure formulaic?

A judgment about the cantata genre's standard structure (opening chorus + recitatives + arias + closing chorale): the structure is formal but not formulaic. Across the 200 surviving cantatas, Bach uses the same skeleton to produce works of dramatically different affective content (the celebratory BWV 80; the contemplative BWV 82; the dialogue-dramatic BWV 140; the chorale-cantata cycle 1724-25 with its unified chorale-cantus-firmus principle). The formal skeleton lets Bach work efficiently — a new cantata each week could not be built from compositional scratch — while the affective content within the skeleton remains genuinely various. Composers who used similar formal skeletons (Telemann, Graupner) often did produce work that reads as formulaic; Bach's cantatas, working within the same structural conventions, do not. The difference is what we mean by saying that the structure is form rather than formula. A reader who works through twenty or thirty cantatas will register the variety; a reader who hears only the most-famous half-dozen may not.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The chapter discusses Bach's five annual cantata cycles and the practice of weekly cantata composition during the first three Leipzig years. The total output across those three years was about 150 cantatas, close to one per week sustained for three years. What kind of artistic-economic-institutional setting makes this kind of sustained production possible? Can you imagine a modern equivalent, an artist or writer producing a substantial work every week for three years as a regular professional commitment?"_

* _"The chorale-cantata genre takes a single Lutheran chorale and builds an entire 30-minute work around it, the chorale tune appears throughout the cantata as compositional material; the chorale's text often drives the cantata's theological argument. The genre is fundamentally Lutheran and fundamentally focused on a specific chorale's identity. What does it mean for a piece of art to be built so explicitly around an existing text-and-music inheritance? Are there other kinds of art that work this way (sonnets built on existing sonnet conventions; paintings explicitly engaging earlier paintings; films that explicitly remake earlier films), and what do they share with Bach's chorale-cantata practice?"_

* _"BWV 140 'Wachet auf' was composed for a specific Sunday, the Twenty-Seventh Sunday after Trinity, November 25, 1731, with a specific Gospel reading (Matthew 25, the parable of the wise virgins) and a specific chorale (Philipp Nicolai 1599). The cantata is functional liturgical music designed for one specific liturgical moment. It is also one of the most-recorded individual Bach cantatas in the modern repertoire. What does the trajectory from 'one Sunday's service music' to 'central work in the modern Bach repertoire' tell us about how religious-liturgical music transfers into broader artistic reception? Is the music the same in both contexts, or does it become a different work?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the John Eliot Gardiner 2000 Soli Deo Gloria recording of BWV 140 "Wachet auf" (or one of the alternatives listed in §12.1). Listen to the cantata a second time and attend to these four concrete moments.

  1. **The opening chorus's horn signal and chorale entry (0:00-2:00).** Listen for the horn-and-strings figure that opens the cantata, the watchman's call from the city tower. Then listen for the chorale tune entering in the soprano as a slow cantus firmus while the lower voices weave contrapuntal lines around it. The opening minute or so establishes the cantata's monumental scale.

  2. **The "Zion hört die Wächter singen" tenor chorale (around 13:00-17:30).** This is the most-famous single movement of the cantata. Listen specifically for the obbligato line in the unison violins and violas above the tenor's chorale-tune. The texture is unusually transparent, only three voices. The obbligato is one of Bach's most-celebrated melodic inventions; track it as a separate voice throughout the movement.

  3. **The two soprano-bass duets (around 7:30 and around 18:30).** Listen for the dialogue character of the duets, the soul's questioning, the bridegroom's response. The duets are the cantata's most-operatic moments, with the Italian-opera-love-duet idiom transformed into Lutheran-theological setting.

  4. **The closing chorale (about 24:30-30:00 onwards in some recordings).** The simple four-voice chorale harmonization that closes the cantata. Notice the difference in texture between this and the elaborate previous movements; the closing chorale is the participatory-singing moment of the cantata's liturgical context.

Then, if time allows, listen to BWV 82 "Ich habe genug", the bass-and-oboe solo cantata. The Lorraine Hunt Lieberson 2003 Nonesuch recording is the recommended version. About 22 minutes. The opening aria "Ich habe genug" (about 8 minutes) is the cantata's emotional anchor; the central aria "Schlummert ein, ihr matten Augen" (about 11 minutes including its surrounding recitatives) is the cantata's contemplative center.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : another chorale cantata from the 1724-25 cycle: for example BWV 78 "Jesu, der du meine Seele" (about 22 minutes), or BWV 62 "Nun komm, der Heiden Heiland" (about 20 minutes, the Advent cantata), or BWV 99 "Was Gott tut, das ist wohlgetan" (about 22 minutes). Each shows the chorale-cantata genre at the cycle's compositional peak.

**Listen** (a different cantata-type): a non-chorale cantata: for example BWV 21 "Ich hatte viel Bekümmernis" (about 35 minutes, one of Bach's longest cantatas; composed at Weimar 1714 and revived at Leipzig). Hearing a non-chorale cantata after the chorale cantatas shows the broader range of the cantata genre beyond the chorale-cantata specifically.

**Listen** (a secular cantata): BWV 211 "Schweigt stille, plaudert nicht": the _Coffee Cantata_ (about 25 minutes). A comic-secular cantata from c. 1734-35; the daughter Lieschen refuses to give up coffee despite her father's threats. The contrast with the sacred cantatas is dramatic and shows the breadth of Bach's compositional voice.

**Read** : Alfred Dürr, _The Cantatas of J. S. Bach_ (Oxford University Press, 2005 English edition). The cantata-by-cantata reference; about 1,000 pages total. The entry on BWV 140 is about 4 pages; BWV 147 is about 4 pages; BWV 80 is about 4 pages; BWV 82 is about 3 pages. Read selectively.

**Read** (alternative): John Eliot Gardiner, _Bach: Music in the Castle of Heaven_ (Knopf 2013), the chapters on the chorale cantatas and the cantata cycles. Gardiner (cf. Ch 1 §1.2) brings extensive cultural-and-musical context from his conductor's experience with the Bach Cantata Pilgrimage.

**Read** (specialized): Eric Chafe, _Tonal Allegory in the Vocal Music of J. S. Bach_ (University of California Press, 1991). A scholarly treatment of how Bach used keys and harmonies to represent theological themes in the cantatas; about 400 pages. For readers who want the deep theological-analytical layer.
