## Chapter 13: St. John Passion BWV 245 (1724) and St. Matthew Passion BWV 244 (1727 / 1736)

The two surviving mature Passion settings of Bach's Leipzig career are among the largest works in his catalogue and among the central works of Western sacred music. The _St. John Passion_ BWV 245, first performed Good Friday April 7, 1724 (Bach's first Leipzig Passion, in his first Leipzig year), runs about two hours and uses the Gospel of John's compressed and dramatic trial-and-crucifixion narrative as its scriptural foundation. The _St. Matthew Passion_ BWV 244, first performed Good Friday April 11, 1727 and revised for Good Friday March 30, 1736 (the now-canonical double-chorus version), runs about three hours and uses the Gospel of Matthew's longer and more contemplative trial-and-crucifixion narrative.

The Passions are not cantatas at greater scale. They are something different, Passion oratorios in the German Lutheran tradition that Bach inherited from earlier composers (Heinrich Schütz's three Passion settings of the 1660s; the broader German-Lutheran Passion tradition going back to the sixteenth century) and developed into the form's modern canonical exemplars. The Passion form combines: a Gospel narrative (sung by the Evangelist as a solo tenor with continuo accompaniment, in recitative-style settings of the Gospel text); character solos (the words of Jesus, Peter, Pilate, and other Gospel-narrative figures sung by individual soloists); chorale settings (Lutheran chorales interspersed throughout, functioning as the worshipping community's contemplative voice on the narrative); and arias (lyrical reflections on specific narrative moments, providing emotional commentary on the Gospel events). The combination produces a multi-hour musical-dramatic-theological work that has no exact precedent before Bach and no exact successor.

This chapter does two things. First, it sets up the German Lutheran Passion tradition and the structural-theological principles common to both Passions. Second, it anchors deeply on three specific movements: the opening chorus of the St. Matthew Passion "Kommt, ihr Töchter, helft mir klagen" (the most-famous Bach choral opening, with its double-chorus-plus-ripieno-boys'-chorale structure); the bass aria "Mache dich, mein Herze, rein" from the St. Matthew Passion (a late-Passion contemplative aria after Christ's death); and the bass arioso "Mein teurer Heiland" from the St. John Passion (a late-Passion arioso with the chorale "Jesu, der du warest tot" interleaved with the bass solo). Brief treatment of the historical 1829 Mendelssohn revival of the St. Matthew Passion, the event that began the modern Bach reception, closes the chapter's contextual treatment.

The chapter is one of the longest in the volume. The Passions are large enough that no shorter treatment would do justice to even three movements.

### 13.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote, the 1829 Mendelssohn revival

Berlin, March 11, 1829. The Berlin Singakademie, a large amateur choral society of about 300-400 singers, directed by Carl Friedrich Zelter (1758-1832), the senior figure of Berlin musical life, assembles in its hall on the Friedrichstrasse for what will become the most-consequential single concert in nineteenth-century music history. The twenty-year-old Felix Mendelssohn (1809-1847) is conducting the rehearsal-and-performance preparation of Bach's _St. Matthew Passion_ , a work that has not been performed publicly anywhere since Bach's own death in 1750. The score, in the hand of one of Bach's students (Christian Friedrich Penzel), has been preserved in the Singakademie's library since the late eighteenth century but has not been performed. Felix Mendelssohn, who has been studying the score since 1825 (he was sixteen when his grandmother Bella Salomon presented him with a copy of the Passion as a Christmas gift), has spent four years working to persuade Zelter to allow a public performance.

The performance is heavily edited. Mendelssohn has cut substantial portions of the Passion to fit a single-evening concert format (roughly two-thirds of the original three-hour Passion remained, with about a third of the movements cut); he has rearranged some movements; he has modified the orchestration to fit the available Berlin instrumental forces (the eighteenth-century instruments Bach had used were no longer the standard performance instruments in 1829 Berlin; modern instruments were substituted, with the resulting timbral differences from the Bach original). The performance is in many respects not the Bach Passion as Bach himself would have heard it, but it is recognizably the Passion, and it brings the work to public attention for the first time in seventy-nine years.

The audience is substantial, about 1,000 people, including most of the Berlin musical, intellectual, and aristocratic establishment. The reception is intense. Reports from the audience speak of weeping (a not-uncommon nineteenth-century response to powerful religious music, but more than usually marked at this performance); of recognition that the work was something extraordinary; of a sense that German musical history had recovered a major figure who had been forgotten. The performance is repeated on March 21 to comparable acclaim; a third performance follows in April. The Passion enters the early-Romantic musical consciousness of Berlin.

The 1829 performance does several things. First, it brings the St. Matthew Passion to a public audience for the first time since 1750. Second, it brings Bach's name to the broader German musical-intellectual public, many of the 1829 audience members had heard of Bach (Forkel's 1802 biography had given Bach a name; specialists had played his keyboard works since the 1780s) but had not heard a substantial work performed. Third, it begins the broader Bach revival, the gradual rediscovery, performance, and publication of Bach's works that would occupy German musical scholarship for the rest of the nineteenth century and the founding of the Bach-Gesellschaft (Bach Society) in 1850 with the project of publishing the complete works.

Felix Mendelssohn himself called the event "the greatest of all my projects." (The remark is attributed to him in various sources, with some variation; the spirit if not the exact words is consistent.) He would conduct the Passion again at subsequent performances across his career; he would conduct other Bach works (the _Magnificat_ , various cantatas) at the Leipzig Gewandhaus from 1835 onward; he would be a central figure in the broader nineteenth-century Bach revival until his early death in 1847 at age 38.

The 1829 performance is the founding moment of the modern Bach reception. The Passion that Bach composed in 1727 and revised in 1736 for the small Leipzig Lutheran congregation became, through the 1829 Berlin performance and the subsequent nineteenth-century reception, one of the central works of Western art music. The trajectory from "a Good Friday service piece for a Lutheran church in Saxony" to "central work in the European concert repertoire" took about a century. The 1829 moment is the structural turning point.

Chapter 22 will return to the broader Bach reception history; for the present chapter, the 1829 performance frames what the Passion has become, a work that was performed for one Leipzig Good Friday in 1727, restaged with revisions in 1736, performed sporadically in Leipzig in subsequent years during Bach's lifetime, then not performed publicly for seventy-nine years, then revived in 1829 in Berlin, and has been performed continuously since.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen, the St. Matthew Passion opening chorus "Kommt, ihr Töchter, helft mir klagen"

The opening chorus is the most-famous Bach choral opening and one of the most-celebrated single movements in the Western catalogue. About 10 minutes long; the largest opening chorus Bach ever composed.

  1. **The double-chorus + ripieno-boys structure.** The opening is scored for two complete four-voice choirs (Chorus I and Chorus II), each with its own orchestra, plus a ripieno soprano line (a third, simpler treble line sung by the boys of the choir-school). The first chorus represents the "Daughters of Zion" (the city of Jerusalem, figured as a grieving woman); the second chorus represents "the Believers" (the Christian congregation of all subsequent ages). The ripieno boys carry the chorale "O Lamm Gottes unschuldig" ("O innocent Lamb of God") in slow cantus-firmus notes above the dialogue between the two main choirs. The texture is unusually dense, three independent vocal lines (Chorus I, Chorus II, ripieno boys' chorale) plus two orchestras.

  2. **The dialogue between Chorus I and Chorus II.** The two main choirs address each other across the movement. Chorus I calls: "Come, daughters, help me lament. See!" Chorus II asks: "Whom?" Chorus I: "The Bridegroom." Chorus II asks: "How?" Chorus I: "Like a lamb." The text is from Christian Friedrich Henrici (Picander)'s 1729 libretto for the Passion. The dialogue structure positions Chorus I as the leader and Chorus II as the responder, the leader-and-followers relationship between a chorus and an answering chorus is a Baroque convention but at this scale unprecedented in Bach's output.

  3. **The chorale "O Lamm Gottes unschuldig" above the dialogue.** While Chorus I and Chorus II carry on their dialogue, the ripieno boys sing the Lutheran chorale "O Lamm Gottes unschuldig" (text and tune by Nikolaus Decius 1531, a Lutheran chorale paraphrase of the Agnus Dei). The chorale tune is in long sustained notes; the boys' high register places the chorale audibly above the two choirs' dialogue. The structural effect is theological: the human dialogue (the two choirs) takes place "under" the heavenly chorale (the boys). Listen for the chorale specifically, its slow shape is unmissable once you know to look for it.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen, the St. Matthew Passion bass aria "Mache dich, mein Herze, rein"

The bass aria sits in the late Passion, after the death of Christ and immediately before the closing chorus "Wir setzen uns mit Tränen nieder" ("We sit down with tears"). About 6-7 minutes long; one of the most-contemplative arias in the Passion.

  1. **The text of the aria.** "Mache dich, mein Herze, rein, ich will Jesum selbst begraben. Denn er soll nunmehr in mir für und für seine süße Ruhe haben. Welt, geh aus, lass Jesum ein!", "Make yourself pure, my heart, I will bury Jesus myself. For he should henceforth have his sweet rest in me forevermore. World, get out, let Jesus in!" The text dramatizes the believer's spiritual response to Christ's death, the heart cleansed to become Christ's resting-place.

  2. **The two-violin obbligato.** The aria is scored for bass voice with two solo violins (in unison or in close two-voice writing), plus two oboes da caccia (a darker-toned member of the oboe family) and strings, plus continuo. The two violins weave a slow, flowing line above the bass voice; the obbligato has the same kind of long-breathed-singing character as the obbligato in the BWV 140 "Zion hört die Wächter singen" tenor chorale (Chapter 12), but at a slower tempo and more contemplative character.

  3. **The B-flat major key and the slow tempo.** The aria is in B-flat major (a warm, settled key) at a slow tempo. The combination, major key plus slow tempo plus the contemplative text, gives the aria a serene contemplative character that contrasts with the surrounding Gospel-narrative dramatic movements. The aria functions as a moment of stillness within the larger Passion's flow.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen, the St. John Passion bass arioso "Mein teurer Heiland"

The bass arioso sits late in the St. John Passion, immediately after Christ's death. About 4-5 minutes long; structurally unusual in that the arioso interleaves the bass solo with a chorale sung by the choir.

  1. **The chorale "Jesu, der du warest tot" interleaved with the bass solo.** The arioso has an unusual structure. The bass voice sings a free arioso text (a contemplative meditation on Christ's death) while the choir interjects, between the bass's phrases, the chorale "Jesu, der du warest tot, lebest nun ohn Ende" ("Jesus, who was dead, now lives without end"). The bass and the chorale alternate; the texture is bass solo → chorale phrase → bass solo → chorale phrase. The structure creates a dialogue between the individual believer (the bass voice) and the community (the chorale-singing choir).

  2. **The text of the arioso.** "Mein teurer Heiland, lass dich fragen, da du nunmehr ans Kreuz geschlagen und selbst gesagt: Es ist vollbracht, bin ich vom Sterben freigemacht?", "My precious Savior, let me ask you, now that you are nailed to the cross and have said yourself 'It is finished,' am I freed from death?" The text is the believer's personal question to the crucified Christ; the chorale provides the community's answer through its repeated assertion of Christ's resurrection.

  3. **The viola da gamba obbligato.** The arioso is scored for bass voice with obbligato viola da gamba (a six-string bowed instrument, the predecessor of the modern cello in some genealogies; played between the knees rather than on the shoulder), strings, and continuo. The gamba provides a sustained, melodically-rich line beneath the bass voice, the gamba's particular timbre (slightly darker, more sustaining than the cello, with subtle resonance differences) gives the arioso a distinctive sound that the more-common-bass-aria scoring would not have. Period-instrument recordings preserve the gamba sound; modern-instrument recordings often substitute a cello.

#### One-sentence backgrounds

**St. Matthew Passion BWV 244** : composed at Leipzig in 1727 for Good Friday (April 11, 1727); revised for Good Friday 1736 (the version with the double-chorus structure became canonical); the libretto by Christian Friedrich Henrici (Picander) sets the Gospel of Matthew chapters 26-27 (the Last Supper through the burial of Christ); the work is in two parts totaling about 3 hours with 68 movements; scored for two complete choirs, ripieno boys' chorale (in the opening chorus), four soloists per chorus (eight soloists total), two complete orchestras, plus organ; the **Primary Recording** for the St. Matthew Passion is John Eliot Gardiner with the Monteverdi Choir and English Baroque Soloists (Archiv 1988), chosen for the period-instrument authority, the dramatic vitality, and the canonical-recent status.

**St. John Passion BWV 245** : composed at Leipzig in 1723-24 for Good Friday (April 7, 1724), Bach's first Leipzig Passion, performed in his first Leipzig year; revised multiple times in subsequent years; the libretto draws primarily from the Gospel of John chapters 18-19 with interpolations from Matthew; the work is in two parts totaling about 2 hours with 40 movements; scored for four-voice chorus, soloists, single orchestra, continuo; the **Primary Recording** for the St. John Passion is Philippe Herreweghe with the Collegium Vocale Gent (Harmonia Mundi 1989), chosen for the period-instrument authority and the spaciously meditative reading.

Klemperer (EMI 1961, mod-inst, ~3h40m); Richter (Archiv 1958 + 1979, mod-inst, ~3h20m); Harnoncourt (Teldec 1970, the first HIP; 2000 re-recording); Herreweghe (Harmonia Mundi 1998); Koopman (Erato 1992); Suzuki (BIS 1999); Brüggen (Glossa 2010, late HIP).

Richter (Archiv 1964, mod-inst); Harnoncourt (Teldec 1971, first HIP); Gardiner (Archiv 1986 + Soli Deo Gloria 2003 live); Suzuki (BIS 1999); Koopman (Erato 1994).

### 13.2 The Listening Window

This section is for your second listen to the three anchor movements. Listening times are total work running times for context; the specific movements occupy variable positions within the larger works.

#### St. Matthew Passion opening chorus "Kommt, ihr Töchter, helft mir klagen" (about 10 minutes)

Find the Gardiner 1988 Archiv recording (or one of the alternatives). The opening chorus begins the work; the movement itself is about 10 minutes.

**0:00-0:30: The orchestral introduction.** Both orchestras enter together with a slow, sighing figure, a descending tone-pattern in the upper strings, supported by the lower strings and continuo. The descending pattern is the work's opening sigh; it will recur in modified form throughout the movement.

**About 0:30-1:30: Chorus I's first call.** Chorus I enters: "Kommt, ihr Töchter, helft mir klagen, sehet!", "Come, daughters, help me lament, see!" The opening call is in the higher voices first, then taken up by the full Chorus I. The orchestral accompaniment continues the sighing figure underneath.

**About 1:30-3:00: The dialogue and the ripieno chorale entry.** Chorus II enters with the response: "Wen?", "Whom?" Chorus I answers: "Den Bräutigam!", "The Bridegroom!" The dialogue continues: "Wie?", "Sieh' ihn!", "Wie ein Lamm", "Like a lamb!" During this dialogue, the ripieno boys enter with the chorale "O Lamm Gottes unschuldig", the chorale tune in slow notes above the dialogue. The boys' chorale carries the central theological image (Christ as the Lamb of God) above the human dialogue (the question-and-answer between Chorus I and II).

**About 3:00-7:00: The development of the dialogue and the chorale.** The dialogue continues with greater dramatic intensity; the chorale is repeated through its full set of phrases (the chorale "O Lamm Gottes unschuldig" has three phrases that the ripieno boys sing in succession across the movement). The two main choirs gradually combine their forces; the orchestra becomes more elaborate; the harmonic motion intensifies.

**About 7:00-10:00: The closing buildup and final cadence.** The movement builds to a closing climax with all three vocal forces (Chorus I, Chorus II, ripieno boys) and the two orchestras at maximum density. The chorale completes its three phrases just as the dialogue reaches its final statements. The final cadence is in E minor (the home key); the orchestral sighing figure returns at the close.

#### St. Matthew Passion bass aria "Mache dich, mein Herze, rein" (about 6-7 minutes, in Part II late)

Find the aria within the Passion's Part II, it sits near the close of the work, between the burial of Christ and the closing chorus. In the Gardiner recording the aria is on the second of the two discs.

**0:00-1:00: Opening obbligato.** The two solo violins enter with a slow, flowing line; the oboes da caccia add quiet sustained harmony underneath; the continuo provides bass and harmonic support. The opening minute establishes the serene contemplative character.

**About 1:00-3:00: The bass voice's first complete statement of the text.** "Make yourself pure, my heart, I will bury Jesus myself..." The bass enters with the aria's principal melody, weaving with the two violins' obbligato. The text is sung slowly; each phrase is given time to register.

**About 3:00-5:00: The middle section.** The aria's middle section develops the text with greater harmonic motion; the obbligato becomes more elaborate; the bass voice's line traces variations on the principal melody.

**About 5:00-7:00: The return of the opening material and the closing cadence.** The aria returns to its opening material (the standard ABA aria structure with a closing return to A); the closing minute brings the aria to a peaceful close in B-flat major.

#### St. John Passion bass arioso "Mein teurer Heiland" (about 4-5 minutes, in Part II late)

Find the Herreweghe 1989 Harmonia Mundi recording (or one of the alternatives). The arioso sits in the St. John Passion's Part II late, after Christ's death.

**0:00-0:30: Opening.** The viola da gamba enters with a sustained melodic line; the continuo provides bass support; the bass voice prepares to enter.

**About 0:30-1:30: The bass voice's first phrase.** "Mein teurer Heiland, lass dich fragen...", "My precious Savior, let me ask you..." The bass voice presents the believer's personal question; the gamba continues underneath with the slow flowing line.

**About 1:30-2:00: First chorale interjection.** The choir enters with the chorale "Jesu, der du warest tot, lebest nun ohn Ende", "Jesus, who was dead, now lives without end." The chorale phrase is brief; the choir provides the answer to the bass's question.

**About 2:00-3:00: Second exchange.** The bass voice continues with another phrase of arioso text; the choir interjects with the next phrase of the chorale.

**About 3:00-5:00: Subsequent exchanges and closing cadence.** The bass and choir alternate through the remaining phrases of the arioso and chorale; the closing cadence brings the arioso to its end.

If your recording's total times for the Passions differ significantly from the standard, you are hearing different performance approaches. Gardiner's 1988 St. Matthew runs about 2 hours 40 minutes; Klemperer's 1961 St. Matthew runs about 3 hours 40 minutes (much slower; weightier). Herreweghe's 1989 St. John runs about 2 hours 5 minutes; Richter's 1964 St. John runs about 2 hours 30 minutes. The performance traditions diverge substantially on tempo and weight.

### 13.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The German Lutheran Passion tradition

The Passion-oratorio as a German Lutheran genre has its origins in the sixteenth-century practice of singing the Passion narrative on Good Friday in the Lutheran service. The earliest Lutheran Passion settings were chant-based, the Evangelist (the narrator) and the various characters sang the Gospel text in modal Gregorian-chant settings, with the congregation singing chorales at appropriate moments. Across the seventeenth century, the chant-based Passion was gradually elaborated: composers began setting the Gospel text in more-elaborate musical settings; arias and reflective movements were added; the Passion grew from a chant-based service piece into an elaborate multi-hour musical-dramatic work.

Heinrich Schütz (1585-1672) composed three surviving Passion settings (St. Matthew, St. John, St. Luke; an attributed St. Mark setting is now doubted) in the 1660s. Schütz's Passions are still relatively austere, chant-influenced Evangelist passages, simple chorale interpolations, no elaborate arias, but they are the major seventeenth-century anchor of the German Lutheran Passion tradition. Composers like Reinhard Keiser, Christoph Graupner, and Telemann composed Passion settings in the early eighteenth century that brought the form closer to the elaborate Italian-influenced cantata-with-Gospel-narrative form Bach would inherit.

The Bach Passions inherit the genre and push it further than anyone before him. The Gospel narrative is set in elaborate recitative for tenor Evangelist (with continuo accompaniment), with separate soloists for the character roles (Jesus, Peter, Pilate, etc.). Chorales are interspersed throughout in elaborate four-voice harmonizations (sometimes with simple instrumental accompaniment, sometimes more elaborate). Arias provide lyrical-contemplative responses to specific narrative moments. Choral movements expand the narrative's collective voice. The combination produces a multi-hour multi-movement work that is unprecedented in scale.

Bach is documented to have composed four mature Passion settings: St. John, St. Matthew, St. Mark (the music is lost; the libretto survives, BWV 247), and a St. Luke setting (BWV 246) that is now generally considered spurious or by an unknown composer. The two surviving Passions (St. John and St. Matthew) are the central works of the German-Lutheran Passion tradition.

#### The St. John vs the St. Matthew (comparison)

The St. John Passion is the earlier of Bach's two surviving Passions, composed for Good Friday 1724, Bach's first Leipzig Passion year. It is shorter (about 2 hours total), more compressed dramatically, more focused on the trial-and-crucifixion sequence (the Gospel of John has the most direct trial-and-crucifixion narrative of the four Gospels, with less of the broader teaching-and-context that fills the synoptic Gospels). The St. John Passion's opening chorus "Herr, unser Herrscher" is monumental but less elaborately constructed than the St. Matthew opening, a single-chorus work rather than the St. Matthew's double-chorus-plus-ripieno.

The St. Matthew Passion is the later and larger work, composed for Good Friday 1727, revised in 1736 for the canonical double-chorus version. It is longer (about 3 hours), more contemplative, less compressed dramatically. The Gospel of Matthew contains the most-extensive Passion narrative (with the longer trial-Garden-of-Gethsemane sequences, the more-extensive teaching at the Last Supper, the Apostle Peter's denial as a substantial dramatic episode); Bach's setting takes the longer narrative and surrounds it with more arias and choral movements. The double-chorus structure (Chorus I and Chorus II) gives the work a structural duality the St. John does not have; the symbolic Daughter Zion / Believers' Chorus dialogue throughout the work is a feature unique to the St. Matthew.

Many listeners experience the St. Matthew as the deeper and more-emotionally-sustained of the two; many others find the St. John more dramatically focused and intense. Both are major works; both are routinely performed and recorded. A reader who has not heard either should hear the St. Matthew first (it is more-familiar in the broader concert repertoire) and then the St. John for the comparison.

#### The role of the Evangelist

The Evangelist, the tenor soloist who narrates the Gospel text in recitative, is the central narrative voice of both Passions. The Evangelist sings the prose Gospel text in declamatory style, with continuo accompaniment, in long passages between the more-substantial choral and aria movements. The Evangelist sets the dramatic context: he narrates the actions ("And Jesus took the bread..."), describes the dialogue ("And they answered..."), and provides the narrative connective tissue between the more-substantial musical movements.

The Evangelist's role is demanding. The tenor must sing for hours of recitative across the Passion's full length, with relatively few moments of melodic relief; the recitative must be declaimed with precision (since the Gospel text must be intelligible) but also with musical sensitivity (since the recitative is one of Bach's most-distinctive vocal idioms). The great Bach-Passion Evangelists of the modern era, Peter Pears, Peter Schreier, Anthony Rolfe Johnson, Ian Bostridge, Christoph Prégardien, Mark Padmore, Werner Güra, are among the most-celebrated performers in the entire Bach catalogue.

For the listener: the Evangelist's recitative is the structural spine of both Passions. A first hearing typically focuses on the choral movements and the arias (which are more-immediately-arresting musically); subsequent hearings reveal the Evangelist's recitative as the work's narrative center. The Evangelist's musical-rhetorical work is one of the great achievements of the Passion form.

#### The chorales in the Passions

Both Passions interleave Lutheran chorales throughout the work, typically 10-15 chorales in the St. John, 14-15 in the St. Matthew. The chorales function as the worshipping community's contemplative voice on the Gospel narrative, at moments when the narrative reaches an emotional or theological climax, a chorale enters to give the community a moment of reflective response.

The chorales used in the Passions are the standard Lutheran-tradition chorales, texts and tunes that Bach's 1727 Leipzig congregation would have known by heart. The chorale "O Haupt voll Blut und Wunden" ("O sacred head, now wounded") by Paul Gerhardt (1656) is the most-repeated chorale in the St. Matthew Passion, it appears five times across the work in different harmonic settings, each tied to a particular narrative moment. The chorale "Herzliebster Jesu" ("Beloved Jesus, what crime have you committed") by Johann Heermann (1630) appears twice in the St. Matthew. The chorale "Befiehl du deine Wege" ("Commit your way") by Paul Gerhardt is another recurring presence.

The chorale-singing in Bach's Leipzig service would have been participatory, the congregation joined the choir in singing the chorales. The modern concert-hall performance of the Passions does not preserve this participatory element; the audience listens passively while the choir sings. The original liturgical participation is lost in the modern context but worth registering: the chorales were not concert-music in 1727 Leipzig but congregational hymn-singing within a substantial musical-liturgical framework.

#### Cross-piece links

The Passions connect forward to many later works in this volume.

To the B Minor Mass (Chapter 14). The Mass and the Passions are Bach's two great late-period sacred-vocal works; parody relationships connect them at the movement level.

To the Christmas Oratorio (Chapter 15). The festive companion piece — same Gospel-narrative + chorales + arias + choral movements structure, applied to Christmas rather than Good Friday.

To the cantatas (Chapter 12) and the chorale tradition (Chapters 6, 11, 16). The Passions are super-cantatas with Gospel-narrative as structural spine; their chorales draw on the same Lutheran chorale tradition the cantatas use.

Chapter 16 will collect the Lutheran-chorale tradition as a unifying thread; Chapter 22 will return to the 1829 Mendelssohn revival in the context of Bach reception history.

### 13.4 The Stories

#### The 1727 first performance of the St. Matthew Passion (in more detail)

Good Friday April 11, 1727 was the first Leipzig performance of the St. Matthew Passion. The work had been composed across the previous months of late 1726 and early 1727; Bach had assembled the libretto with Christian Friedrich Henrici (Picander), who supplied the non-Gospel poetic-aria texts; the composition had been worked through in stages; the performance was at the Thomaskirche in the Good Friday evening service.

The performance forces: the Thomanerchor (probably about 16-20 boys singing the principal four-voice choir, with 8 additional boys for the ripieno chorale in the opening chorus); adult professional soloists for the bass and (probably) alto solo roles; the Stadtpfeifer and contracted instrumentalists for the two orchestras (each of about 12-15 players); Bach himself probably conducting from the first-choir harpsichord and the second-choir organist providing the second-choir continuo.

The 1727 performance has been called the "first version" of the St. Matthew. The work was apparently performed again in subsequent years with revisions; the 1736 revision (Good Friday March 30, 1736) is the version that became canonical, Bach's autograph manuscript dates from c. 1736 (a fair copy he made of the revised version that he intended to leave to his sons). The 1736 version is what modern performances use; the 1727 version differs in some details and has been reconstructed by scholars (the John Eliot Gardiner / Monteverdi Choir 2016 recording is one of the few performances of the 1727 version specifically).

What modern listeners hear when they listen to a "St. Matthew Passion" recording is, in almost all cases, the 1736 revised version. The 1727 original was Bach's first thinking; the 1736 revision is Bach's mature and considered position on the work.

#### The 1724 first performance of the St. John Passion (briefly)

Good Friday April 7, 1724 was the first Leipzig performance of the St. John Passion. The work had been composed across Bach's first nine months at Leipzig (he had arrived in May 1723; the cantata cycle was running through his first liturgical year; the Passion was the centerpiece of the Good Friday service in the new tenure). The performance was at the Nikolaikirche (the alternation pattern between Thomaskirche and Nikolaikirche meant the principal Good Friday service was at the Nikolaikirche in 1724).

The St. John Passion was revived in subsequent years with revisions: 1725, 1732, 1739, and 1749 are documented performances. The various revisions changed the work substantially, some movements were added, others removed, the order of some sections adjusted. Modern scholarship has identified four versions of the St. John Passion, designated I (1724), II (1725), III (1732), IV (1749). The most-commonly-performed version in modern performances is a hybrid that combines elements from versions I and IV (the Neue Bach-Ausgabe critical edition reconstructs a "canonical" version that has been the basis of most modern performances).

The St. John Passion's reception has been more complicated than the St. Matthew's. The St. John's compressed dramatic intensity, its emphasis on the trial-and-crucifixion sequence, and (in particular) its setting of certain anti-Jewish passages in the Gospel of John have made the work more difficult for modern audiences. Some choirs have edited or rephrased the more-problematic anti-Jewish texts in modern performances; others have maintained the original libretto and provided contextual program-note discussion of the issues.

A position worth stating: the volume's view, following the broader consensus among contemporary Bach scholars including Daniel Melamed, is that the responsible modern performance maintains the original Bach text without modification but provides substantial contextual framing (program notes, pre-performance discussion, sometimes a brief spoken introduction) so that the audience encounters the eighteenth-century Lutheran text within an explicitly twenty-first-century critical frame. Editing the text protects the audience from a confrontation that the work itself stages; rewriting Bach to fit modern sensibilities damages both the historical document and the listener's chance to engage with what the work actually is. The discomfort the text produces in modern listeners is part of what the work means in its current reception; suppressing the discomfort suppresses the engagement. This is a defensible position but not the only one — performers and listeners who prefer the edited approach have their own arguments — and the discussion is ongoing.

#### The Mendelssohn 1829 performance (in fuller context)

The Mendelssohn 1829 St. Matthew Passion performance has been treated extensively in Chapter 22 (the volume's reception-history chapter). The brief account in §13.1 of this chapter is enough to register the event's significance; Chapter 22 develops the broader context, Forkel's 1802 biography, the Bach Society 1850, the Bach-Gesellschaft complete-works edition project 1851-1900, the gradual nineteenth-century building of Bach's modern reputation.

A few additional details for the present chapter: the 1829 performance was substantially edited and adapted to suit Berlin tastes and forces. The original double-chorus structure was preserved (more or less); the orchestration was modified to use modern instruments (the recorders, the oboes da caccia, the violas da gamba of Bach's original instrumentation were replaced or transposed for the modern Berlin ensemble); some movements were cut to fit the single-evening concert format; the Evangelist's recitatives were sometimes performed with piano accompaniment rather than continuo. The performance preserved the basic structure of the Passion but presented it through the lens of 1829 Berlin musical conventions.

The 1829 performance was repeated on March 21, 1829, with additional public performances following in April. The reception was intense and continued. Mendelssohn's role in the broader Bach revival continued across the next two decades; he conducted Bach works at the Leipzig Gewandhaus from 1835 onward; he was one of the founders of the Leipzig Bach Society in 1842 and contributed to the broader publication and performance of Bach's works until his early death in 1847 at age 38.

#### Recording history briefly

Ch 21 surveys the Passion recording history. The principal recordings, already listed in §13.1, are split across the modern-instrument tradition (Klemperer EMI 1961 — slow monumental, ~3h40m; Richter Archiv 1958+1979) and the HIP tradition (Harnoncourt Teldec 1970 — first HIP; Gardiner Archiv 1988 — the Primary Recording for the St. Matthew here; Herreweghe Harmonia Mundi 1989 St. John + 1998 St. Matthew; Suzuki BIS 1999; Koopman Erato 1992/1994; Brüggen Glossa 2010).

#### Coda: "Erbarme dich"

The volume's three anchor movements in this chapter are the opening chorus and two contemplative arioso passages. A fourth movement deserves separate mention even without anchoring: the alto aria "Erbarme dich, mein Gott" from the St. Matthew Passion Part II, sung after Peter's denial of Christ.

The aria opens with a solo violin, alone with the continuo, playing a slow descending melody. The melody is in B minor; the rhythm is a slow 12/8 — the same lilting pastoral meter "Sheep May Safely Graze" used in Chapter 2 to entirely different effect. Here the meter is not pastoral; it is sicilianic, the slow Italian lament tradition. The violin sings before any voice enters. The line descends, hesitates on a long sustained note, descends again, reaches a tentative cadence, begins again. About thirty seconds of solo violin establishing the affect before the alto voice enters with the words _"Erbarme dich, mein Gott, um meiner Zähren willen"_ — Have mercy, my God, for the sake of my tears.

What the violin gives the aria is permission. The vocal line that follows is among the most-grieving in Bach — a soaring lament that traces an arc above the violin's continuing figuration — but the violin has already established what kind of grieving this is. It is not the violent grief of the Crucifixus's chromatic bass; it is the quiet, sustained, almost-tender grief of Peter recognizing that he has denied his teacher. The grief is for a personal failure, not for a cosmic catastrophe. The violin and the alto together carry this distinction across nearly seven minutes. By the close, when the alto's line and the violin's line have intertwined through several long melodic arcs, the listener has been brought through a moment of intimate confession in a way no other movement in the Passion attempts.

The standard recordings — Janet Baker with Klemperer (EMI 1961), Andreas Scholl with Herreweghe (Harmonia Mundi 1998), Bernarda Fink with Concerto Köln (Harmonia Mundi 2008) — all handle the aria with different interpretive temperaments. Hear at least one. If you only listen to one extracted passage from the Passion outside the volume's three anchors, this is the candidate.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The 1829 Mendelssohn performance of the St. Matthew Passion is the founding moment of the modern Bach reception. The Passion that Bach composed in 1727 for a Leipzig Good Friday service was, by 1829, a work that had not been performed publicly anywhere in 79 years; after Mendelssohn's performance, it became one of the central works of Western art music. What does this trajectory tell us about how artistic reputation is constructed? Are there other examples, works that were peripheral in their composer's lifetime but became central decades or centuries later, that you can identify in other fields?"_

* _"The St. Matthew Passion opening chorus uses three independent vocal forces (Chorus I, Chorus II, and the ripieno boys' chorale) plus two orchestras. The texture is among the most complex in any Bach work. What does this kind of compositional complexity require of the listener? Is the multi-layer texture audible as multi-layer on a first hearing, or does it collapse into a single complex sound? What kind of listening discipline does the chapter's earlier work (the four-voice listening from Chapter 2, the polyphonic-illusion listening from Chapter 9) prepare you for here?"_

* _"The bass arioso 'Mein teurer Heiland' from the St. John Passion has an unusual structure, the bass voice's solo phrases alternate with chorale phrases sung by the choir. The structure creates a dialogue between the individual believer (the bass voice asking a personal question to the crucified Christ) and the community (the chorale-singing choir providing the answer of Christ's resurrection). What does this kind of structural dialogue contribute to the work's theological-emotional argument? Are there other Bach moments where the structural pattern itself carries the theological meaning?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the John Eliot Gardiner 1988 Archiv recording of the St. Matthew Passion (or one of the alternatives listed in §13.1). Listen to the opening chorus "Kommt, ihr Töchter, helft mir klagen" a second time and attend to these four concrete moments.

  1. **The orchestral sighing figure (0:00-0:30).** Listen for the descending tone-pattern in the upper strings that opens the movement. The sighing figure is the work's opening musical idea; it will recur throughout the movement and elsewhere in the Passion.

  2. **The dialogue between Chorus I and Chorus II.** Listen for the question-and-answer structure: Chorus I calls; Chorus II responds. The dialogue is in the text ("Come, daughters, help me lament, Whom?, The Bridegroom!, How?, Like a lamb!"); follow the dialogue in the music.

  3. **The ripieno boys' chorale "O Lamm Gottes unschuldig."** Listen for the chorale tune sung by the boys in slow long notes above the two main choirs. The chorale's high register places it audibly above the dialogue; the slow shape is unmissable once you know to look for it.

  4. **The closing climactic buildup (about 7:00-10:00).** Listen for the moment when all three vocal forces and the two orchestras combine at maximum density. The closing buildup is one of the most-dense textures in any Bach work.

Then, if time allows, find the Philippe Herreweghe 1989 St. John Passion recording (or one of the alternatives) and listen to the bass arioso "Mein teurer Heiland" (about 4-5 minutes in Part II late). Listen for the dialogue between the bass voice and the chorale-singing choir. The structural alternation creates the dialogue between believer and community.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : the closing chorus of the St. Matthew Passion "Wir setzen uns mit Tränen nieder": "We sit down with tears", about 7-8 minutes. The Passion's closing movement is one of the most-celebrated extended laments in the Western catalogue; it is the structural counterpart to the opening chorus.

**Listen** (St. John): the opening chorus "Herr, unser Herrscher": "Lord, our ruler", about 9-10 minutes. The St. John's opening chorus is monumental in a different way from the St. Matthew's, single-chorus, more dramatically driven, with a distinctive descending-bass figure underneath that has been compared to the Passacaglia BWV 582's bass theme.

**Read** : Daniel R. Melamed, _Hearing Bach's Passions_ (Oxford University Press, 2005). The source-of-record for the Passions in modern English-language scholarship. About 200 pages; addresses the structural-historical issues, the performance-history questions, and the modern reception issues with particular attention to the St. John Passion's textual problems.

**Read** (alternative): John Eliot Gardiner, _Bach: Music in the Castle of Heaven_ (Knopf 2013), the chapters on the Passions. About 50 pages.

**Read** (specialized): Eric Chafe, _Tonal Allegory in the Vocal Music of J. S. Bach_ (University of California Press, 1991), the chapters on the Passions. About 80 pages combined.

## Chapter 14: B Minor Mass BWV 232 (c. 1733-1749)

The _B Minor Mass_ BWV 232 is the most-complex compositional history of any major Bach work and his largest single sacred composition. The work was assembled across nearly two decades, portions composed at separate moments across Bach's late career, the various movements adapted (often through the parody technique introduced in Chapter 12) from earlier cantata movements, the completed mass never performed in its entirety in Bach's lifetime. The work's complete-missa-tota form (Kyrie, Gloria, Credo, Sanctus, Agnus Dei in the full Latin Mass setting) is unprecedented in Bach's output; he composed no other complete missa tota and rarely worked in Latin liturgical text outside this work. The Mass is, in its assembled-late-period form, Bach's final large-scale statement on his compositional voice, the work in which he gathered together his best music from across decades and reworked it into a unified Mass setting.

This chapter does two things. First, it sets up the Mass's complex compositional history: the 1733 Kyrie-and-Gloria sent to the Dresden Catholic court as part of Bach's petition for a Saxon court title; the 1724 Sanctus reused from a Leipzig service piece; the 1747-49 assembly of the remaining movements; the work as a whole as a late-life sum. Second, it anchors deeply on six movements that represent the Mass's principal achievements: the Kyrie eleison I (the work's monumental choral opening); the Christe eleison (the lyrical soprano duet in D major); the Crucifixus (the work's most-concentrated lament); the Et resurrexit (the resurrection-explosion that immediately follows the Crucifixus); the 1724-reused Sanctus (the six-voice scoring); and the closing Dona nobis pacem (which musically reuses material from the Gloria's "Gratias agimus tibi").

### 14.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote, the 1733 Dresden petition and the Mass's beginning

Leipzig, July 1733. Bach is forty-eight years old, ten years into his Thomaskantor position, and increasingly impatient with the politics of his Leipzig employment. The local city council has been irritating him for years, disputes over choral funding, disputes over his teaching responsibilities at the Thomasschule, disputes over who controlled musical decisions at the four main churches. Bach has been looking for ways to strengthen his hand against the council, and one promising avenue is a Saxon court title. If Bach could be appointed _Hofcompositeur_ , Court Composer, to the Saxon Elector at Dresden, he would have a formal court connection that would override the Leipzig council's authority on many matters. The Saxon court is Catholic (the Elector Friedrich August II of Saxony was also King August III of Poland, having converted to Catholicism in 1697 to secure the Polish throne); the Dresden court music includes elaborate Catholic Mass settings; Bach as a Lutheran composer would need to demonstrate his ability to compose acceptable Catholic-church music.

Bach's strategy is to send the new Elector (who has just succeeded his father Friedrich August I in February 1733) a substantial Mass setting as a petition for the court title. He composes a Kyrie and Gloria, the first two movements of a Catholic Mass, which together form what was called a _missa brevis_ in some eighteenth-century usage (a "short mass", just the opening movements, not the complete five-movement missa tota). The Kyrie and Gloria total about an hour of music; they are scored for full chorus, soloists, and orchestra with trumpets and timpani. The Kyrie has three sub-movements (Kyrie eleison I + Christe eleison + Kyrie eleison II); the Gloria has nine sub-movements covering the full Gloria text in elaborate musical setting.

In July 1733 Bach travels to Dresden personally to present the Mass to the Elector. The petition letter (which survives) is dated July 27, 1733; it asks the Elector to consider granting Bach a court title to "predicate" him with court privileges that would protect him against the Leipzig council's pressures. The Kyrie-Gloria mass is presented as evidence of Bach's compositional capacities.

The petition is not immediately granted. The Elector apparently takes the music but does not appoint Bach to a court title; the appointment is delayed for three years. In November 1736, three years after the petition, the Elector finally grants Bach the title _Compositeur bey Königl. Hoff-Capelle zu Dresden_ (Composer at the Royal Court Chapel at Dresden). The title does what Bach had hoped, it gives him standing against the Leipzig council; the council's irritation with Bach is moderated, though disputes continue across the remaining years of his life.

The Kyrie-Gloria of 1733 is set aside in the Dresden archives after the petition. Bach apparently keeps a copy himself. The work as composed in 1733 is complete in itself (it is a missa brevis, Kyrie + Gloria are a complete sub-genre, not requiring continuation into the Credo, Sanctus, Agnus Dei).

What happens between 1733 and the late 1740s is the more-complex part of the Mass's history. Bach composes a Sanctus separately, in 1724, for a Christmas Day service at the Thomaskirche; the 1724 Sanctus is independent of the 1733 Kyrie-Gloria, and Bach probably did not yet anticipate combining them. Across the 1730s and into the 1740s, Bach composes other Latin Mass movements for various occasions, short Missa brevis settings for the Saxon court (the so-called "Lutheran Masses" BWV 233-236, each consisting of Kyrie and Gloria), and various individual movements that would later be incorporated into the B Minor Mass.

In the late 1740s, probably 1747-1749, Bach assembles the B Minor Mass as a complete work. He takes the 1733 Kyrie-and-Gloria (the missa brevis sent to Dresden) as the foundation; he adapts the 1724 Sanctus and adds the new movement "Pleni sunt coeli" and "Osanna" to make a complete Sanctus section; he composes new music or parodies existing music for the Credo (the longest section in the assembled Mass, about 30 minutes); he composes new music or parodies existing music for the Benedictus and Agnus Dei. The complete Mass, Kyrie + Gloria + Credo + Sanctus + Agnus Dei, is finalized probably in late 1748 or 1749.

The complete B Minor Mass is never performed in Bach's lifetime. There is no documentary record of any performance of the assembled missa tota. Bach may have intended the Mass for a future occasion (perhaps a Mass at the Dresden court that never materialized); he may have intended it as a compositional summing-up of his sacred-music output that did not require live performance. Whatever his intentions, the assembled Mass remains a kind of compositional last will and testament, a substantial work that Bach completed and apparently set aside.

The first complete public performance of the B Minor Mass came in 1859 in Leipzig, more than a century after Bach's death. The work was then gradually established in the concert repertoire across the late nineteenth and twentieth centuries; it is now one of the central works of Western sacred music and one of the most-recorded large choral works in the catalogue.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen

The B Minor Mass is one of the longest works in the Bach catalogue, about 1 hour 50 minutes to 2 hours 5 minutes total, depending on tempo choices. The chapter's anchors are six specific movements; a complete-work listening will take about 2 hours and reward the time. For the first listen, the recommended approach is to listen to the six anchor movements specifically, in their positions within the larger work.

  1. **The Kyrie's monumental scale (the opening Kyrie eleison I, about 9-10 minutes).** The Mass opens with a massive choral movement on the words "Kyrie eleison", "Lord, have mercy." The setting begins with a slow, sustained statement of the chorus on the opening words, followed by a substantial five-voice fugue (the Mass uses five-voice texture extensively, more than Bach's typical four-voice chorus practice). The Kyrie's opening is one of the most-massive choral openings in Western art music; it announces immediately that the Mass is on a scale larger than even the Passion choruses.

  2. **The Crucifixus's lament (about 3 minutes).** Listen for the descending chromatic bass-line that grounds the Crucifixus. The chorus sings the words "Crucifixus etiam pro nobis sub Pontio Pilato; passus et sepultus est", "Crucified for us under Pontius Pilate; he suffered and was buried", over a descending chromatic ground-bass pattern. The bass-line descends step by step across each phrase, repeating throughout the movement. The texture is austere; the harmonic motion is dense; the affect is grief at its most concentrated. The movement is parodied (with the new Latin text) from the opening chorus of Cantata BWV 12 "Weinen, Klagen, Sorgen, Zagen" (1714, the Weimar period).

  3. **The Et resurrexit's explosion (about 4 minutes).** Immediately after the Crucifixus's profound lament, the Et resurrexit explodes into D major with trumpets, timpani, and a fast tempo. The text: "Et resurrexit tertia die secundum scripturas; et ascendit in caelum, sedet ad dexteram Patris", "And he rose on the third day according to the scriptures; and he ascended into heaven, and sits at the right hand of the Father." The dynamic contrast with the preceding Crucifixus is one of the most-dramatic moments in the entire Mass.

#### One-sentence background

The B Minor Mass BWV 232 was assembled by Bach across nearly two decades; the 1733 Kyrie-Gloria was sent to the Dresden Catholic court; the 1724 Sanctus was reused; the remaining movements were composed and assembled probably 1747-49 as Bach's late-period compositional summing-up; the complete Mass was never performed in Bach's lifetime; about 1 hour 50 minutes total, 27 movements across the five Mass sections; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is John Eliot Gardiner with the Monteverdi Choir and English Baroque Soloists (Archiv 1985, the canonical-recent HIP standard recording), chosen for the period-instrument authority, the dramatic vitality, and the canonical status of the Gardiner recording in late-twentieth-century reception.

#### Search Hints

EN: "Bach B Minor Mass BWV 232 Gardiner" or "Bach Mass in B Minor" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings** (Ch 21 surveys the broader Mass recording history):

Klemperer (EMI 1967, mod-inst, ~2h25m, slow monumental); Richter (Archiv 1961, mod-inst); Harnoncourt (Teldec 1986, HIP); Herreweghe (Harmonia Mundi 1988 + 1996, HIP); Suzuki (BIS 2007, HIP); Koopman (Erato 1994, HIP); Parrott (Virgin Veritas 1985, one-voice-per-part, ~1h45m).

### 14.2 The Listening Window

This section takes the six anchor movements in turn. Timings refer to the Gardiner 1985 Archiv recording, which runs about 1 hour 50 minutes total.

#### Kyrie eleison I (about 9-10 minutes)

**0:00-0:30: The opening choral statement.** The Mass opens with a slow, monumental statement of "Kyrie eleison" by the full chorus over a sustained orchestral chord. The opening is in B minor; the chorus's first entry is in homophonic (all voices together) texture; the effect is solemn and weighty. Listen for the slow harmonic motion in the orchestra; the opening takes its time to establish the home key and the work's contemplative weight.

**About 0:30-3:00: The orchestral introduction's continuation and the first fugue exposition.** After the opening choral statement, the orchestra continues with a substantial introduction that develops material that will become the fugue's subject. The orchestra plays the subject in instrumental form; the texture builds; eventually the chorus enters with the subject in five-voice fugal texture.

**About 3:00-7:00: The five-voice fugue's development.** The fugue develops the "Kyrie eleison" text in five-voice contrapuntal writing. The subject passes from voice to voice; the harmony moves through B minor and related keys; the texture sustains substantial density. The five-voice fugue is more elaborate than the typical four-voice fugues of the WTC (Chapter 10); the additional voice adds contrapuntal complexity.

**About 7:00-9:30: The closing buildup and final cadence.** The fugue builds to its closing climax; the closing cadence is in B minor with all five voices and full orchestra at maximum density. The Kyrie I closes with substantial weight; the listener is prepared for the central Christe duet that follows.

#### Christe eleison (about 5 minutes)

**0:00-1:00: The opening duet.** Christe eleison is a duet for two soprano soloists with continuo and obbligato violins. The two voices enter in close imitation; the duet is in D major (the relative major of B minor); the character is more flowing and lyrical than the Kyrie's massive opening. The duet's text is "Christe eleison", "Christ, have mercy", set as a long-breathed lyrical exchange between the two voices.

**About 1:00-4:00: The duet's development.** The two voices interweave through the central section, with the obbligato violins providing decorative figuration above. The harmony moves through related keys before returning to D major. The duet is one of Bach's most-celebrated soprano-duet movements.

**About 4:00-5:00: The closing cadence.** The duet returns to its opening material and closes in D major. The Christe is followed by the second Kyrie (Kyrie eleison II), a brief return to the choral five-voice texture before the Gloria begins.

#### Crucifixus (about 3 minutes)

**0:00: The opening.** The Crucifixus begins with a quiet entry of the descending chromatic bass-line in the continuo and lower strings. The bass-line descends step by step through about 8 bars before repeating. The chorus enters with the words "Crucifixus etiam pro nobis sub Pontio Pilato" over the descending bass.

**About 0:30-2:00: The development.** The chorus continues the text through "passus et sepultus est", "he suffered and was buried." The descending bass continues throughout, repeating with each new phrase of text. The harmony moves through several distant keys (the descending chromatic bass forces harmonic motion through unusual chord progressions); the affect is grief at its most concentrated.

**About 2:00-3:00: The closing fade.** The chorus repeats the closing text "passus et sepultus est"; the harmony settles toward a final cadence on G major (a surprising shift from the work's broader minor-mode setting). The closing is quiet and sustained; the chorus's voices fade toward silence.

#### Et resurrexit (about 4 minutes)

**0:00: The explosion.** Immediately after the Crucifixus's quiet G major close, the Et resurrexit erupts into D major (the bright relative major). The full chorus enters with trumpets, timpani, and full orchestra; the tempo is fast; the dynamic is at maximum. The contrast with the preceding Crucifixus is one of the most-dramatic moments in the Mass; the listener experiences the sudden transformation from grief to triumph.

**About 0:30-3:00: The development.** The chorus sings the resurrection text with substantial elaborateness, the resurrection itself, the ascension, the seating at the right hand of the Father, all set in D major's brilliant key. The bass-line is active; the orchestral writing is dense; the chorus has frequent fast-moving passages.

**About 3:00-4:00: The closing cadence.** The Et resurrexit closes in D major with the trumpets and timpani at full force. The closing is one of the Mass's brightest moments, the triumph of resurrection celebrated at maximum scale.

#### Sanctus (about 5 minutes)

**0:00-1:00: The opening.** The Sanctus opens with a slow, majestic statement of the chorus on the words "Sanctus", "Holy." The scoring is six voices (two soprano lines, two alto lines, plus tenor and bass), Bach's only six-voice chorus in the principal Sacred works. The slow tempo and the rich six-voice texture create a sense of weight and grandeur.

**About 1:00-3:00: The Sanctus's central section.** The chorus continues with the full Sanctus text: "Sanctus, Sanctus, Sanctus Dominus Deus Sabaoth. Pleni sunt coeli et terra gloria eius", "Holy, holy, holy Lord God of Hosts. Heaven and earth are full of your glory." The texture continues at six voices; the harmony moves through related keys; the affect sustains the slow majestic character.

**About 3:00-5:00: The Pleni sunt coeli buildup and closing.** The "Pleni sunt coeli" text gets a more-active musical treatment with fast running passages; the closing returns to the slow majestic character; the final cadence brings the Sanctus to its end. The Sanctus is among the most-celebrated single movements of the Mass.

#### Dona nobis pacem (about 4 minutes, the closing)

**0:00: The opening.** The Dona nobis pacem opens with a slow, sustained chorus on the words "Dona nobis pacem", "Grant us peace." The music is the same as the Gloria's "Gratias agimus tibi", the closing Dona is a parody (reuse) of the earlier Gloria movement.

**About 0:30-3:00: The development.** The Dona develops the "grant us peace" text through several phrases; the harmonic motion is rich; the affect is contemplative-pleading. The closing is the Mass's final word, a prayer for peace in the human community.

**About 3:00-4:00: The closing cadence.** The Mass closes on D major with the chorus and orchestra at moderate dynamic level. The conclusion is solemn rather than triumphant; the Mass ends with a quiet plea for peace rather than a celebratory acclamation.

### 14.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The missa tota and the Lutheran tradition

The _missa tota_ , the complete Latin Mass (Kyrie, Gloria, Credo, Sanctus, Agnus Dei), was the central composed sacred work in the Catholic liturgical tradition. From the medieval period through the late seventeenth century, Catholic composers had produced thousands of complete Mass settings; the form had been developed through Renaissance composers (Josquin, Palestrina, Lassus) into a major genre and continued in elaborate eighteenth-century Catholic-church settings (Caldara, Fux, Hasse, Mozart-with-his-late-eighteenth-century Mass settings).

The Lutheran tradition retained the Kyrie and Gloria as part of its main Sunday service but did not retain the Credo, Sanctus, and Agnus Dei as parts of the regular liturgy. Lutheran composers therefore typically set Kyrie-and-Gloria as a missa brevis (the "short Mass" of just the opening movements); they rarely composed complete missa tota settings. Bach's BWV 233-236 (the four "Lutheran Masses" of the 1730s) are missa brevis settings, Kyrie + Gloria only; no Credo, Sanctus, or Agnus Dei.

The B Minor Mass is the unique exception in Bach's output. The complete missa tota assembly across two decades represents Bach's only complete-Mass composition. The work's potential occasion is unclear: the Dresden Catholic court would have been a possible setting (the elaborate Catholic Mass setting would have been appropriate to the Catholic-rite Saxon court); but no documentary record of any planned performance survives. The most-likely explanation is that the assembled missa tota represented Bach's compositional ambition rather than a specific commission, Bach gathering together his best music into a complete-Mass form as a kind of compositional summing-up.

For the listener: the B Minor Mass is unusual in Bach's output for its Latin liturgical text. Most of Bach's sacred output is in German with Lutheran-chorale settings; the B Minor Mass is in Latin with traditional Mass-text settings. The shift from German to Latin reflects the work's Catholic-rite framework. A Lutheran congregation in Bach's Leipzig would not have heard the complete Mass at a regular service; the Kyrie and Gloria might have been sung at major occasional services, but the full missa tota was not part of the regular Lutheran liturgy.

#### The parody technique in the Mass

The B Minor Mass is the most-extensively-parodied of Bach's major works. About half the Mass's movements (approximately 13 of 27 movements) are derived from earlier Bach compositions, typically cantata movements that have been adapted with new Latin texts. The principal parodies:

* **The Crucifixus** is parodied from the opening chorus of Cantata BWV 12 "Weinen, Klagen, Sorgen, Zagen" (composed at Weimar in 1714). The original was a Weimar-period cantata; Bach took the chorus's compositional structure (descending chromatic bass, lament-voice writing) and adapted it for the Crucifixus.
* **The "Gratias agimus tibi"** (from the Gloria) is parodied from the opening chorus of Cantata BWV 29 "Wir danken dir, Gott" (1731). The Dona nobis pacem at the Mass's close uses the same music as the Gratias, making the same music appear at two different positions in the Mass with different texts.
* **The "Patrem omnipotentem"** (from the Credo) is parodied from the opening chorus of Cantata BWV 171 "Gott, wie dein Name, so ist auch dein Ruhm."
* **The "Et expecto resurrectionem mortuorum"** (from the Credo) is parodied from a cantata movement (BWV 120, the "Jauchzet, ihr erfreuten Stimmen" chorus).
* **Various aria movements** are parodied from cantata arias with new Latin texts.

The parody pattern serves several purposes. It lets Bach apply his best music from across decades to the Mass's structural-theological context; the resulting Mass has the compositional weight of Bach's full mature output. It also reflects the compositional reality that the Mass was assembled across many years from materials Bach had at hand. The parody is not lazy reuse; it is a deliberate compositional strategy.

For the listener: the parody movements still function as integral parts of the Mass. The Crucifixus (parodied from BWV 12) works perfectly in the Mass's context, the descending-chromatic-bass lament is appropriate to the Crucifixion text as it was to the cantata's "weeping, lamenting" text. The Mass coherently presents the parodied material in its new liturgical context.

#### The descending chromatic bass and the lament tradition

The Crucifixus's descending chromatic bass is one of the most-celebrated examples of a long compositional tradition in Western music, the _lamento bass_ , a descending step pattern that signified grief or lament. The tradition can be traced back through Henry Purcell's "Dido's Lament" (1689, the most-famous English-Baroque example), through Claudio Monteverdi's _Lamento della Ninfa_ (1638, the most-famous Italian-early-Baroque example), through the broader sixteenth-and-seventeenth-century Italian-and-English madrigal-and-aria literature, back into the medieval and renaissance use of descending bass-lines as emblems of grief.

The Crucifixus uses a specific variant: the descending _chromatic_ tetrachord (the descending step pattern that passes through every chromatic half-step) rather than the descending diatonic tetrachord (which moves through diatonic scale-degrees only). The chromatic version is more dissonant, more weighted, more grief-laden than the diatonic version. The "extra" half-steps in the descending pattern create momentary harmonic dissonances that are part of the lament's effect.

Bach used the descending chromatic bass extensively across his career. The Crucifixus is the most-celebrated example. The Cantata BWV 12 chorus that became the parody source is another example. The Capriccio BWV 992's lament (Chapter 3) is an early version. The descending chromatic bass appears in arias, chorus movements, and (in the Passacaglia BWV 582, Chapter 6, though that work uses a different bass shape) related variation forms.

#### The Crucifixus → Et resurrexit transition in close-up

The single most-dramatic moment in the B Minor Mass, and arguably in any sacred-music work, is what happens in the silence between the Crucifixus and the Et resurrexit. The Crucifixus closes quietly, the chorus's voices dropping into a final cadence — but the cadence is on G major rather than the home key of the Mass, an unexpected resolution that does not feel resolved. The chorus sings "passus et sepultus est" — he suffered and was buried — and the music does what the text describes: it settles, lies down, almost disappears. For about a second, possibly two seconds in a slow conducting (Klemperer takes the moment longer than Gardiner does), there is nothing. The continuo has gone silent; the chorus has finished; the trumpets and timpani that will dominate what follows have not yet entered. This is the deepest silence in any Bach work.

Then the silence breaks. Not gradually, not with preparation: the full orchestra enters at full dynamic in D major (the bright relative major of the work's B-minor home key), with three trumpets and timpani at the front of the ensemble, with the chorus erupting on the word "Et" — and — followed by "resurrexit" — he rose. The shift from quiet G-major dissolution to full-orchestra D-major triumph is the most-immediate dynamic transformation in the Western sacred-music repertoire. Listeners who have heard the moment dozens of times still report a physical response — a startled inhalation, a shift in attention, a moment of recognition that the music has done what the theology asserts.

The transition works because Bach engineered both the Crucifixus's drop and the Et resurrexit's eruption to require the silence between them. The Crucifixus's G major is not a stable arrival; it is a harmonic suspension that asks for something to follow. The Et resurrexit's D major is not just a fresh start; it is the resolution the Crucifixus's G has been asking for. The silence is the moment in which the listener registers both the asking and the granting. Cut the silence, perform the two movements attached, and the effect collapses — the dramatic impact of the resurrection is in the second of nothing that precedes it.

Chapter 20 §20.9 collects the chromatic-harmony thread as part of the synthesis of Bach's contrapuntal voice. The Crucifixus is the synthesis chapter's central example.

#### The stile antico in the Mass

The Mass contains several movements in the _stile antico_ , Bach's deliberate use of the archaic Renaissance-style polyphony for moments requiring solemnity and gravity. The principal stile antico passages are:

**The Confiteor** (from the Credo). This is the most-elaborate stile antico moment in the Mass: a slow contrapuntal setting of the words "Confiteor unum baptisma in remissionem peccatorum", "I confess one baptism for the remission of sins." The texture is Renaissance-style five-voice counterpoint with a Gregorian-chant-derived cantus firmus in the alto voice; the harmony is modal rather than functionally-tonal; the affect is archaic and solemn. The movement is one of the Mass's most-celebrated single movements and one of Bach's most-extended stile antico explorations.

**Other stile antico moments** include portions of the Credo and selected movements where the archaic style serves the theological context.

Chapter 20 §20.5 will collect the stile antico as a unifying thread. The B Minor Mass's Confiteor is the volume's primary example of the device.

#### Cross-piece links

To the cantatas + Passions + Christmas Oratorio (Chapters 12, 13, 15). The Mass's parody movements come from cantatas (Crucifixus from BWV 12; Gratias/Dona from BWV 29); the Passions and Christmas Oratorio are Bach's other late-period sacred-vocal monumental works using related parody-and-assembly approaches.

To the descending-bass lament family. The Crucifixus belongs to the same compositional family as the Capriccio BWV 992 lament (Chapter 3, the earliest), the Passacaglia BWV 582 (Chapter 6, organ), the violin Chaconne (Chapter 9), and the Goldberg "Black Pearl" Variation 25 (Chapter 17, late keyboard).

To the late-period fugal practice. The Mass's five-voice fugues (in the Kyrie and elsewhere) are in the same fugal-writing tradition as the WTC Book 1 (Chapter 10) and the _Art of Fugue_ (Chapter 19).

### 14.4 The Stories

#### The work's compositional history at a glance

§14.1 gives the narrative; here is the timeline as a quick reference:

* **1714** — Cantata BWV 12 composed at Weimar (later parodied as the Crucifixus).
* **1724** — Sanctus composed at Leipzig for Christmas Day service.
* **1731** — Cantata BWV 29 composed (later parodied as the Gratias and the Dona nobis pacem).
* **1733** — Kyrie + Gloria composed and sent to Dresden with the court-title petition.
* **1736** — Dresden court title (_Compositeur_) finally granted.
* **1747-1749** — The complete missa tota assembled at Leipzig.
* **July 28, 1750** — Bach dies; the assembled Mass is among the works he leaves.
* **1859** — First complete public performance, Leipzig, more than a century after Bach's death.

#### The work's place in Bach's catalogue

The B Minor Mass is, in its assembled form, Bach's largest single work. It is also one of his last completed compositions, possibly the last large-scale work he completed before his death. The Mass alongside the Art of Fugue (Chapter 19) and the late Eighteen Chorales (Chapter 6) and the Goldberg Variations (Chapter 17, completed earlier in the decade) form Bach's late compositional output.

The work's standing has been substantial. Schumann called the Mass "the greatest musical work of all times and peoples." (The remark may be apocryphal in its exact form but is consistent with the spirit of nineteenth-century reception.) Twentieth-century commentary has been similarly enthusiastic. The Mass is routinely included in lists of the greatest works of Western sacred music; it is one of the most-recorded large choral works in the catalogue.

The Mass's reception is complicated by its complex compositional history and by the question of its intended performance context. Some scholars have argued that the Mass should be performed only in religious-liturgical contexts (since it is a Catholic Mass setting); others have argued that the concert-hall performance is now its canonical context (since it was not performed in a Catholic Mass during Bach's lifetime anyway). Modern performance practice is split: some recordings are in concert-hall settings; others are in church-liturgical contexts.

#### Recording history briefly

The principal recordings are already listed in §14.1. Of special note: the Andrew Parrott / Taverner Consort recording (Virgin Veritas 1985) uses the smallest possible vocal forces consistent with the HIP scholarly arguments — one singer per voice part (5 soloists for the five-voice movements, 4 for the four-voice). The resulting chamber-music transparency is the principal alternative to the larger-choir HIP tradition; some listeners find it revelatory, others under-scaled. Worth at least one hearing for comparison with the Gardiner full-chorus reading. Ch 21 covers the broader Mass recording history.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The B Minor Mass was assembled across nearly two decades, portions composed at separate moments and adapted from earlier works. The complete missa tota was never performed in Bach's lifetime; the first complete public performance came in 1859, more than a century after his death. What does this kind of slow assembly tell us about Bach's compositional process? Is the Mass a 'complete work' in the sense that we might use the term for a symphony composed in a single creative burst, or is it something different, a compositional anthology, a late summing-up, a deliberately-non-performable work?"_

* _"The Crucifixus is parodied from the opening chorus of Cantata BWV 12 'Weinen, Klagen, Sorgen, Zagen' (1714, Weimar). The same music was originally set to a text about 'weeping, lamenting, mourning, despairing' and later set to a text about the Crucifixion. The two contexts (the Cantata's broader lament; the Mass's specific Crucifixion) are related but not identical. What does it mean for the same music to be appropriate to both contexts? What does the parody relationship suggest about how Bach thought of musical content as related to specific text?"_

* **Stopwatch exercise.** Time the silence between the Crucifixus's final G-major chord and the Et resurrexit's first downbeat in three recordings (Klemperer 1967, Gardiner 1985, Parrott 1985). The three conductors disagree on how long the silence should hold. Which timing convinces you, and why? The answer reveals what you think the silence is _for_ — relief, suspense, theological breath, or simple structural punctuation.

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the John Eliot Gardiner 1985 Archiv recording of the B Minor Mass (or one of the alternatives listed in §14.1). Listen to the six anchor movements (Kyrie I, Christe, Crucifixus, Et resurrexit, Sanctus, Dona nobis pacem) a second time and attend to these four concrete moments.

  1. **The Kyrie I's five-voice fugue (about 3:00-7:00 in Gardiner).** Listen for the fugue subject's entries in the five voices. The five-voice texture is denser than the typical four-voice fugues you have encountered; the additional voice adds contrapuntal complexity. Try to track the subject as it passes from voice to voice.

  2. **The Crucifixus's descending chromatic bass (throughout the 3 minutes).** Listen for the bass-line specifically. The descending chromatic pattern repeats throughout the movement; the chorus weaves above it. The bass is the structural foundation; the chorus provides the textural commentary.

  3. **The Crucifixus-to-Et-resurrexit transition (between the two movements).** Listen for the moment of dramatic shift. The Crucifixus closes quietly on G major; the Et resurrexit erupts immediately into D major with full orchestra and trumpets. The transition is among the most-effective dramatic moments in Western sacred music.

  4. **The Sanctus's six-voice texture (throughout the 5 minutes).** Listen for the six voices specifically. Two soprano lines, two alto lines, plus tenor and bass, the only six-voice chorus in the Mass and in any of Bach's principal sacred works. The texture is unusually rich; the slow tempo lets you hear the individual voices.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** : the Cantata BWV 12 "Weinen, Klagen, Sorgen, Zagen": the cantata whose opening chorus became the Crucifixus through the parody technique. The cantata is about 22 minutes; the opening chorus is about 5 minutes (slightly longer than the Mass's Crucifixus). Hearing the original cantata version after the Mass's Crucifixus shows you the parody relationship at work.

**Listen** (a stile antico anchor): the Confiteor movement of the Credo. About 4-5 minutes. The Renaissance-style polyphony in five voices with the Gregorian chant cantus firmus is the volume's central stile antico anchor; Chapter 20 §20.5 will return to it.

**Listen** (cross-volume): the Gloria from the B Minor Mass: the second section after the Kyrie. About 30 minutes total in nine sub-movements. The Gloria's "Gloria in excelsis Deo" opening is one of the most-celebrated celebratory choral movements in the catalogue.

**Read** : George B. Stauffer, _Bach: The Mass in B Minor_ (Schirmer 1997; reprinted Yale University Press 2003). The standard scholarly monograph on the Mass in English. About 300 pages; detailed work-history-and-analysis.

**Read** (alternative): John Butt, _Bach: Mass in B Minor_ (Cambridge Music Handbooks, Cambridge University Press, 1991). A shorter book (about 130 pages) covering the work's compositional history and structural-theological analysis.

**Read** (alternative): John Eliot Gardiner, _Bach: Music in the Castle of Heaven_ (Knopf 2013), the chapter on the B Minor Mass. About 50 pages.
