## Chapter 19: The Art of Fugue BWV 1080 (with a note on Well-Tempered Clavier Book 2)

Two late-period keyboard projects shape Bach's last decade: _The Art of Fugue_ BWV 1080 (the chapter's principal subject, composed across approximately 1740-1750 and left unfinished at Bach's death) and the _Well-Tempered Clavier_ Book 2 BWV 870-893 (a brief secondary anchor, compiled c. 1742). The _Art of Fugue_ is the most-systematic exploration of fugal technique in any composer's output, fourteen fugues plus four canons, all built on transformations of a single D-minor theme, demonstrating systematically the principal contrapuntal devices Bach had developed across his career. WTC Book 2 is the parallel-keyboard counterpart to Book 1 (Chapter 10), twenty-four prelude-and-fugue pairs in all 24 major and minor keys, compiled at Leipzig about twenty years after Book 1 was completed at Köthen — the chapter treats it more briefly, since the Book 1 chapter has already established the prelude-and-fugue practice that Book 2 continues.

This chapter does three things. First, it sets up the _Art of Fugue_ 's compositional history and architectural plan, with deep close-listening on three Contrapuncti: Contrapunctus 1 (the opening simple four-voice fugue on the basic D-minor subject); Contrapunctus 9 alla Duodecima in stylo francese (the stretto-fugue display piece); and Contrapunctus 14 (the intended quadruple fugue, broken off mid-phrase after the third subject — the B-A-C-H signature — has been introduced but before the fourth subject's entry). Second, it sets up the _Well-Tempered Clavier_ Book 2 in relation to Book 1 (Chapter 10) and anchors on the B♭-minor Prelude and Fugue BWV 891 as Book 2's contemplative summit. Third, it folds both works back into the volume's broader contrapuntal-voice synthesis (which Chapter 20 will collect).

### 19.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook Anecdote, the unfinished Contrapunctus 14

Leipzig, July 1750. Bach is sixty-five years old; he has been in declining health for several years (his eyesight has been failing since at least the late 1740s; his general health has been deteriorating). In March 1750 he had undergone cataract surgery, performed by John Taylor (1703-1772), the English itinerant ophthalmologist who would later also operate on Handel; both Bach and Handel lost their sight after Taylor's surgeries. After the surgery, Bach's general health rapidly worsened; he became blind and bedridden; in mid-July his strength gave way; on July 28, 1750, he died.

Among the projects Bach left unfinished at his death was the _Art of Fugue_ , a collection of fugues he had been working on for at least a decade. The autograph manuscript (which survives) shows the work in progress at the moment of Bach's death: the principal fourteen fugues are mostly complete; the four canons that were to be inserted at appropriate points in the sequence are mostly complete; the final fugue, Contrapunctus 14, is incomplete.

Contrapunctus 14 was intended (on the available evidence) to be a quadruple fugue, a fugue with four subjects, each first presented separately and then combined together in the final pages. The first subject is the basic D-minor theme that has been the foundation of the entire _Art of Fugue_ ; the second subject is a contrasting theme introduced after the first; the third subject is the famous "B-A-C-H" theme (B-flat-A-C-B in German note-naming, spelling Bach's surname as notes), introduced about halfway through the movement; the fourth subject (the basic theme transformed in some way) was apparently to be introduced near the end and the four subjects combined in the closing pages.

The manuscript breaks off in the middle of the third-subject-and-second-subject section, just as the B-A-C-H signature has been introduced and the fourth subject's entry is approaching. The manuscript stops mid-phrase; the writing simply ends. C. P. E. Bach added a note at the bottom of the manuscript: _Über dieser Fuge, wo der Name BACH im Contrasubject angebracht worden, ist der Verfasser gestorben_ , "Over this fugue, where the name BACH was introduced in the countersubject, the composer died." The note was probably added shortly after Bach's death; it became the source of the romantic tradition that Bach died mid-composition over Contrapunctus 14.

Modern scholarship treats the romantic tradition more cautiously. The Wolff biography and others have argued that the unfinished state of Contrapunctus 14 may reflect that Bach set the work aside (perhaps because of declining health, perhaps because of competing late-life projects, perhaps because the compositional plan reached an impasse) rather than that he died mid-composition. The manuscript's physical state (the paper, the writing-conditions) does not unambiguously establish the death-mid-composition narrative; the alternative (set-aside-and-not-resumed) is equally consistent with the surviving evidence. The traditional story is moving and has become canonical in popular Bach reception; modern scholarship suggests the truth is uncertain.

The _Art of Fugue_ was published posthumously in 1751, the year after Bach's death, by his sons Wilhelm Friedemann and Carl Philipp Emanuel (probably with help from Bach's son-in-law Johann Christoph Altnickol, who had been one of Bach's last students and who had copied substantial Bach manuscripts in the late years). The 1751 publication includes the fourteen fugues and the four canons; it also includes the chorale prelude "Wenn wir in höchsten Nöten sein" BWV 668 ("When we are in highest need") as a closing piece, a Bach chorale prelude that the publishers added at the work's end, possibly to provide a sense of completion for the unfinished Contrapunctus 14. The added chorale prelude is sometimes referred to as the "Deathbed Chorale" (the tradition holds that Bach dictated it to Altnickol from his deathbed, with revised text from "Wenn wir in höchsten Nöten sein" to "Vor deinen Thron tret ich hiermit", "Before your throne I now step"); modern scholarship treats the deathbed tradition cautiously, as with the broader Contrapunctus 14 deathbed story.

The _Art of Fugue_ has had an unusual reception history. The 1751 publication was a financial failure, the work was too specialized for the broader market; copies sat unsold. The work was rediscovered in the early nineteenth century alongside the broader Bach revival; performances began in the nineteenth century and continued into the twentieth. The unusual problem of the _Art of Fugue_ is that Bach did not specify the instrumentation, the work survives in open score with no instrument assignments. Modern performances have used a wide range of instrumentations: solo keyboard (harpsichord or piano), string quartet, viol consort, mixed instruments, orchestra. Different instrumentations produce different works; the _Art of Fugue_ is one of the few works in the standard repertoire that does not have a canonical instrumental version.

#### One to three things to notice on the first listen, Contrapunctus 1

  1. **The D-minor theme.** The basic theme of the entire _Art of Fugue_ is a slow, austere D-minor melody, the kind of theme that Bach develops throughout the work in various transformations. Contrapunctus 1 presents the theme in its simplest fugal setting: a four-voice fugue on the basic theme, with each voice entering in turn with the theme. Listen for the theme's shape carefully; it will return throughout the entire work.

  2. **The four-voice exposition.** The four voices enter in succession: the alto first; the soprano; the tenor; the bass. The four-voice texture is the standard fugue exposition; the listening skill from the WTC Book 1 (Chapter 10) transfers directly.

  3. **The austere character.** Contrapunctus 1 has an austere, almost academic character, the theme is unornamented, the fugue's development is straightforward, the texture is less elaborate than some of Bach's other late-period fugues. This austerity is deliberate: the _Art of Fugue_ is a compositional textbook as much as it is a concert work; the simplest fugue presents the theme cleanly so that subsequent fugues can develop it in various directions.

#### One-sentence backgrounds

**The Art of Fugue BWV 1080** : composed at Leipzig across c. 1740-1750, left unfinished at Bach's death July 28, 1750; published posthumously in 1751 by Bach's sons; fourteen fugues plus four canons, all built on transformations of a single D-minor theme, plus a closing chorale prelude added by the publishers; in open score with no specified instrumentation; total length about 75-90 minutes depending on tempo and instrumentation; the **Primary Recording** for this chapter is the Hesperion XX viol consort directed by Jordi Savall (Astrée 1986, with viol consort instrumentation), chosen for its successful approach to the instrumentation question.

**WTC Book 2 BWV 870-893** : compiled at Leipzig c. 1742, twenty years after WTC Book 1 was completed at Köthen; twenty-four prelude-and-fugue pairs in all 24 major and minor keys (the same key-organization as Book 1); total length about 2 hours 15 minutes; for the WTC Book 2 the standard recording recommendations are extensions of the Book 1 recommendations from Chapter 10 (Gould, Schiff, Hewitt, Richter, Pinnock, etc.).

#### Search Hints

EN: "Bach Art of Fugue BWV 1080 Hesperion Savall" or "Bach Art of Fugue Goebel" or "Bach Kunst der Fuge" EN: "Bach WTC Book 2 BWV 883 F sharp minor" _(Search results as of 2026-05; verify before relying on this.)_

**Alternative recordings: Art of Fugue** (Ch 21 surveys the broader recording history; instrumentation choice produces dramatically different works — sample at least two):

Münchinger (Decca 1965, mod-inst string orchestra); Goebel (Archiv 1984, HIP mixed instruments); Glenn Gould (CBS 1962, partial, organ + piano); Tatiana Nikolayeva (Hyperion 1992, piano); Pierre-Laurent Aimard (DG 2008, piano); Emerson String Quartet (DG 2008); Helmut Walcha (Archiv 1956, organ); Kenneth Gilbert (Harmonia Mundi 1989, harpsichord).

For WTC Book 2: the Book 1 cycles (Schiff, Gould, Hewitt, Richter, Pinnock, Suzuki) all include Book 2 in their coverage.

### 19.2 The Listening Window

This section takes four anchor movements from the Art of Fugue plus a brief WTC Book 2 movement. Timings vary substantially across instrumentations.

#### Contrapunctus 1 (about 3 minutes)

**0:00-0:30: The opening alto voice entry.** The alto voice enters with the D-minor theme, a slow austere phrase that establishes the work's character.

**About 0:30-1:00: Soprano enters with the theme transposed.** The soprano voice enters with the theme transposed (typically up a fifth); the two voices now sound together.

**About 1:00-1:30: Tenor and bass entries.** The tenor and bass voices enter in turn; the four-voice texture is built.

**About 1:30-3:00: Development and closing.** The fugue develops through episodes and additional theme-statements; the closing returns to D minor with the final cadence.

#### Contrapunctus 9 alla Duodecima in stylo francese (about 3 minutes)

The "Stretto fugue", Contrapunctus 9 features stretto (close-in-time imitative entries) as its principal compositional device. The "in stylo francese", "in the French style", refers to the French-overture-influenced character of the writing.

**0:00-3:00: The fugue.** The fugue is built on a faster-moving variant of the D-minor theme; the stretto entries appear throughout the movement, with voice-entries overlapping rather than being separated by episodes. The texture is contrapuntally dense; the stretto principle is the movement's principal expressive resource. Listen for the rapid voice-entries; the closely-spaced imitation is the stretto effect.

#### Contrapunctus 14 (the intended quadruple fugue, about 9 minutes, what survives)

**The first subject (the basic D-minor theme, transformed).** Contrapunctus 14 opens with a fugue on a transformed version of the basic D-minor theme. The opening 3-4 minutes develop this first subject.

**The second subject's introduction.** A second contrasting subject is introduced; the two subjects are developed together.

**The B-A-C-H signature theme (around 4-5 minutes into the movement).** The third subject, the B-A-C-H theme (B-flat, A, C, B-natural in note-spelling), is introduced. The three subjects are developed together. The B-A-C-H theme descends a half-step from B-flat to A, then leaps up a minor third to C, then descends a half-step again to B-natural: a compact, chromatically-wound four-note figure that spells the composer's name in German musical notation.

**The breaking-off.** The manuscript breaks off mid-phrase; the writing simply ends. When Contrapunctus 14 stops at bar 239, the fugue halts mid-phrase; no expected cadential resolution arrives. You hear an abrupt silence where the music should have continued, the final voice hanging without closure. Performances of Contrapunctus 14 typically end at the moment of the manuscript's interruption (some performances add a brief reconstructed ending, but the more-common practice is to stop where Bach stopped).

**The structural-emotional effect.** Contrapunctus 14's unfinished ending is one of the most-distinctive moments in any musical performance, and one of the few cases in the Western repertoire where a work's incompleteness has become essential to its character rather than a defect to be repaired. What you actually hear, in performance: the fugue has been developing through about nine minutes of increasing density. The first subject is the basic D-minor theme, transformed. The second subject enters; the two are combined. The third subject, the B-A-C-H signature, enters at about minute 5 — and at this point the listener who knows what is coming should feel the music gathering toward what should be its culmination. The three subjects are being combined; the fourth subject (the one Bach intended but never wrote) is approaching; the resolution should be enormous. Then the voice that has just stated the B-A-C-H theme reaches the end of its phrase, and the phrase is the last one written. The next bar would have continued. The next bar does not exist.

What you hear is the absence of the next bar. In a good performance the silence after is held — Savall's viol consort lets it run for several seconds before the recording fades — and the silence is the work's structural close. The traditional reception is that Bach died mid-composition over this fugue; modern scholarship questions the biographical narrative but the emotional effect of the silence is unchanged either way. The work asks the listener to register what does not arrive. The contrapuntal masterwork of Bach's lifetime, organized through the strict monothematic discipline of the Art of Fugue as a whole, ends with the composer's own name as the third subject of an intended quadruple fugue, and then ends. There is no rhetorical equivalent in the Western canon.

#### WTC Book 2 Prelude and Fugue in B♭ minor BWV 891 (about 9-10 minutes)

The WTC Book 2's anchor here is BWV 891 in B♭ minor — the cycle's contemplative-meditative summit, the late-Book-2 pair that pianists from Schiff to Pollini have singled out as the deepest pair in either book. The B♭-minor pair shows what twenty years of additional compositional development added to the prelude-and-fugue practice Book 1 (Chapter 10) had already established.

**Prelude (about 4-5 minutes).** A slow, deeply expressive prelude in B♭ minor. The texture is three-voice throughout (rather than the more typical four); the rhythm is slow and breathing, with long-breathed melodic phrases in the upper voice over a slowly-moving bass and a middle voice that fills harmonically. The harmonic motion is unusually chromatic for a Bach prelude — the bass passes through extensive chromatic side-steps; the upper voice traces a long contemplative arc with several climactic moments before resolving. The character is the late-Bach contemplative voice at full development; the prelude has been compared by performers to the slow movements of the late chamber music and the B Minor Mass's Confiteor (Chapter 14 §14.3) for its sustained meditative weight.

**Fugue (about 4-5 minutes).** A four-voice fugue on a slow, deeply chromatic subject. The subject is unusually long for a Bach fugue (about 8 bars before the second voice enters); the chromatic intervals within the subject force the fugue's harmonic development through distant keys. The four-voice texture builds gradually; the development uses inversion (the subject appearing upside-down) and stretto (close-in-time imitative entries) in the later passages. The closing pages are among the densest contrapuntal writing in either WTC book.

**What Book 2 added.** Compare the BWV 891 pair with the BWV 846 C-major pair from Book 1 (Chapter 10): same prelude-and-fugue genre, same key-cycle principle, same compositional voice, but at twenty years' later development. Book 1's C-major pair is the gentle introduction; Book 2's B♭-minor pair is the contemplative summit. The cycle as a whole — Book 1 + Book 2, 48 prelude-and-fugue pairs — shows the development of Bach's prelude-and-fugue practice across his mature life.

(For a more transparent late-period pair if BWV 891 feels too dense on first listening, the F♯-minor BWV 883 is the alternative — a three-voice fugue with a distinctive chromatic subject, about 7 minutes total, less monumental than BWV 891 but still characteristically late-Book-2.)

### 19.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The Art of Fugue's architectural plan

The Art of Fugue is the most-systematic exploration of fugal technique in any composer's output. The principal compositional principle: all fourteen fugues and the four canons are built on transformations of a single D-minor theme. The principle is strict, no theme outside the basic theme appears anywhere in the work; every contrapuntal voice in every movement is derived from transformations of the basic theme.

The fourteen fugues demonstrate, between them, essentially every standard fugal technique:

* **Simple fugues** (Contrapunctus 1-4): straightforward fugal treatments of the basic theme.
* **Counter-fugues** (Contrapunctus 5-7): fugues using inverted forms of the theme (where the theme's intervals are mirror-imaged, going up where the original goes down).
* **Double fugues** (Contrapunctus 8-11): fugues with two subjects, the basic theme plus a contrasting subject, developed together.
* **Mirror fugues** (Contrapunctus 12-13): fugues that can be played as written or with the entire score inverted (every voice's intervals mirror-imaged); each mirror-fugue exists in two complementary forms.
* **Quadruple fugue** (Contrapunctus 14): a fugue with four subjects intended; the surviving manuscript presents three of them (the basic theme, a contrasting subject, and the B-A-C-H signature) before breaking off mid-phrase, just as the fourth subject's entry would have prepared the combination of all four.

The four canons demonstrate canon types:

* **Canon at the octave** (Canon alla Ottava).
* **Canon at the tenth** (Canon alla Decima in Contrapunto alla Terza).
* **Canon at the twelfth** (Canon alla Duodecima in Contrapunto alla Quinta).
* **Canon by augmentation in contrary motion** (Canon per Augmentationem in Contrario Motu).

The architectural plan systematically covers the principal fugal and canonical devices. The work is conceptually a kind of fugal-technique textbook embodied as compositional masterpieces. No previous composer had produced anything quite comparable.

#### The instrumentation question

The Art of Fugue is in open score, written with each voice on its own staff but with no instrument specified. The work can be played on keyboard (harpsichord, piano, organ); by string quartet; by viol consort; by mixed instruments; by orchestra. Each instrumentation produces a different sound-character; none is canonical.

The principal arguments for various instrumentations:

* **Keyboard.** The compositional structure (the technical demands, the voice-ranges, the keyboard-idiomatic figuration) fits the keyboard well. The historical context (Bach was working on the WTC Book 2 in the same years) suggests keyboard as a plausible primary medium.
* **Viol consort.** The voice-ranges fit comfortably on the viol family (treble viol, alto viol, tenor viol, bass viol). The viol-consort sound was the Renaissance-and-early-Baroque polyphonic-ensemble sound; the Art of Fugue's Renaissance-style polyphony fits the consort sound naturally.
* **String quartet.** The voice-ranges fit the modern string quartet. The string-quartet sound has been an effective instrumentation tradition since the early twentieth century.
* **Mixed instruments.** Various combinations have been tried (strings + winds; different instruments for different voices).
* **Organ.** Some movements have been played on organ; the polyphonic-keyboard tradition fits the work.

For the listener: hearing the work in multiple instrumentations is rewarding. The Savall viol consort recording produces a distinctive sound that emphasizes the work's Renaissance-style polyphonic character; the keyboard recordings (Walcha on organ, Gould on piano-and-organ, Nikolayeva on piano, Aimard on piano) emphasize different aspects; the string-quartet recordings (Emerson) emphasize the chamber-music character.

#### The WTC Book 2

WTC Book 2 was compiled at Leipzig about 1742, twenty years after Bach completed Book 1 at Köthen. Like Book 1, Book 2 contains 24 prelude-and-fugue pairs in all 24 major and minor keys.

Book 2's character differs subtly from Book 1's. Bach was twenty years older when compiling Book 2; his compositional voice had matured further; the late-period concentration shows in some of the fugues' greater density. Book 2 contains some of Bach's most-elaborate fugal writing (the C-sharp minor fugue BWV 873 is in three voices but contrapuntally extremely dense; the F-sharp minor fugue BWV 883 has a distinctive chromatic subject; the B-flat minor fugue BWV 891 is a deeply meditative late fugue).

Book 2's compositional history is also more complex than Book 1's. Some of Book 2's preludes and fugues are revisions of earlier pieces (some date back to Köthen years; some were originally composed for other purposes and adapted); some are newly composed for the Book 2 collection. The compositional voice is uniformly mature, but the dating of individual pieces varies.

For the listener: Book 2 is the natural complement to Book 1 (Chapter 10). After hearing Book 1, Book 2 extends the listening into Bach's mature WTC voice. The principal recordings of Book 1 (Schiff, Gould, Hewitt, Richter, etc.) all include Book 2 as well.

#### Cross-piece links

The Art of Fugue and WTC Book 2 connect to many works across this volume.

To the WTC tradition (Book 1, Chapter 10; Book 2 within this chapter). The WTC's prelude-and-fugue practice and the _Art of Fugue_ 's monothematic fugue cycle are the bookends of Bach's keyboard-contrapuntal output.

To the late-period contrapuntal cluster: the _Musical Offering_ (Chapter 18, 1747, contemporaneous with Art of Fugue's late stages) and the Goldberg Variations (Chapter 17, 1741, the keyboard canon-exploration precursor). The three works together form Bach's late-period systematic contrapuntal exploration.

To the B Minor Mass (Chapter 14). The Mass's five-voice fugues are in the same fugal-writing tradition as the _Art of Fugue_ 's three- and four-voice fugues.

Chapter 20 §20.1 (the fugue subject and answer), §20.3 (the double, triple, and quadruple fugue), §20.4 (the canon), §20.9 (the chromatic harmony) will all collect threads the Art of Fugue exemplifies systematically. The Art of Fugue is the volume's central anchor for the synthesis of Bach's contrapuntal voice.

### 19.4 The Stories

#### The Art of Fugue's compositional history

The Art of Fugue's compositional history is reconstructable from the surviving autograph manuscripts plus the posthumous 1751 publication. The principal stages:

**c. 1740: Initial composition begins.** Bach apparently begins working on the Art of Fugue around 1740, after completing the WTC Book 2 (c. 1742) and during the same broader period as the Goldberg Variations (1741).

**c. 1742-46: Substantial composition.** The principal fugues and canons are composed across the mid-1740s. The autograph manuscript shows substantial work across these years.

**c. 1746-49: Continued composition.** The work continues to develop; Bach revises some movements; he adds others.

**1749-50: Final illness.** Bach's declining health affects the work's progress. Whether Contrapunctus 14 was set aside or whether Bach died mid-composition is the question discussed in §19.1.

**July 28, 1750: Bach's death.** The work is left in its unfinished state.

**1751: Posthumous publication.** Wilhelm Friedemann and Carl Philipp Emanuel Bach (with Altnickol's help) publish the Art of Fugue. The publication includes the unfinished Contrapunctus 14, a publisher's note about the unfinished state, and the chorale prelude "Wenn wir in höchsten Nöten sein" BWV 668 added as a closing piece.

**1751-1820s: The work is largely forgotten.** The 1751 publication was a financial failure; the work was too specialized for the broader market.

**1820s onward: The work is rediscovered.** As part of the broader nineteenth-century Bach revival, the Art of Fugue is rediscovered and gradually performed.

**20th century: The work enters the standard repertoire.** Recordings begin in the early-to-mid twentieth century; the work is performed regularly in concert; it becomes one of the canonical late-Bach works.

#### The B-A-C-H signature theme

The B-A-C-H signature theme, using the four notes B-flat (B in German notation), A, C, B-natural (H in German notation) to spell "BACH", is one of the most-celebrated musical signatures in Western music. Bach himself used the signature in several works (the Art of Fugue Contrapunctus 14 is the most-famous example); subsequent composers (Schumann, Liszt, Mendelssohn, Brahms, Schoenberg, Webern, Shostakovich, and many others) have used the B-A-C-H signature as a tribute to Bach.

In the Art of Fugue Contrapunctus 14, the B-A-C-H signature serves as the third subject of the (intended) quadruple fugue. Its appearance about halfway through the movement is the structural-poetic high point of the entire Art of Fugue, Bach's own name embedded as a contrapuntal voice within his most-comprehensive contrapuntal masterwork. The convergence of the work's compositional architecture (the quadruple fugue) with the composer's own signature (the B-A-C-H theme) is one of the most-celebrated symbolic moments in Bach's catalogue.

The shape of the BACH motif in note-letter terms:
    
    
       ┌─ B♭ ─ A ──────────────── C ─ B♮ ─┐
       │      ↓                 ↑ ↓       │
       │  half-step down   minor 3rd up   │
       │                   then half-step │
       │                   down to B♮     │
       └──────────────────────────────────┘
    

Four notes, played in succession, about 3 seconds at fugue tempo. The combination — chromatic descent, then a leap, then chromatic descent again — produces a distinctively wound-up shape that stands out against the surrounding diatonic counterpoint. Once you have the four notes in your ear, you can recognize the theme when it appears in the texture even when it is buried among other contrapuntal voices.

#### Recording history

The Art of Fugue's recording history is unusually varied because of the open-instrumentation question (§19.1 covers the principal recordings; Ch 21 surveys the broader history). For first orientation: sample at least two contrasting instrumentations — viol consort (Savall Astrée 1986) plus keyboard (Walcha Archiv 1956 organ, or Aimard DG 2008 piano). The string-quartet realization (Emerson DG 2008) is a third distinct sound-world worth hearing. WTC Book 2 recordings are extensions of the Book 1 cycles (Schiff, Gould, Hewitt, Richter, Pinnock, Suzuki).

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The Art of Fugue is built on the strict principle that every contrapuntal voice in every movement is derived from transformations of a single D-minor theme. Fourteen fugues and four canons, all built on the same theme. What does this kind of monothematic constraint give the work? Why might a composer impose such a constraint on himself, and what does the constraint produce compositionally that a freer approach would not?"_

* _"Contrapunctus 14, the intended quadruple fugue, breaks off mid-phrase just as the B-A-C-H signature theme (the third subject) has been introduced — before the fourth subject's entry that would have combined all four. The traditional reception treats this as Bach dying mid-composition; modern scholarship suggests Bach may have set the work aside rather than died over it. What does the difference between these two interpretations matter? Does it change the way you hear the unfinished ending?"_

* _"The Art of Fugue is in open score with no specified instrumentation. The work has been performed on keyboard, by string quartet, by viol consort, by orchestra, by mixed instruments, each producing a different sound-character. What does it mean for a major composition to lack a canonical instrumental version? How does each instrumentation contribute different things to the listening experience?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

Find the Hesperion XX / Jordi Savall recording of the Art of Fugue (or one of the alternatives listed in §19.1). Listen to Contrapunctus 1, Contrapunctus 9, and Contrapunctus 14 a second time.

  1. **Contrapunctus 1 (about 3 minutes).** Listen for the four voice-entries with the D-minor theme. Track the basic theme as it appears in each voice; this is the listening foundation for the entire Art of Fugue.

  2. **Contrapunctus 9 (about 3 minutes).** Listen for the stretto entries, voice-entries that overlap rather than being separated by episodes. The stretto principle is the movement's principal compositional device.

  3. **Contrapunctus 14 (about 9 minutes, what survives).** Listen through the work toward its unfinished ending. The first subject (transformed basic theme); the second subject's introduction; the B-A-C-H signature theme; the breaking-off mid-phrase. The silence after is the most-distinctive listening moment.

  4. **WTC Book 2 Prelude and Fugue in B♭ minor BWV 891 (about 9-10 minutes).** Listen for the prelude's chromatic bass and long-breathed upper voice; then for the fugue's slow chromatic subject and its four-voice contrapuntal development. The B♭-minor pair is Book 2's contemplative summit; comparing it with Book 1's C-major pair (Ch 10) shows what twenty years of additional compositional development added to the WTC voice.

### Going Deeper

**Listen** (alternative instrumentation): the Walcha organ Art of Fugue (Archiv 1956 or 1969); or the Emerson Quartet string-quartet recording (DG 2008); or the Aimard piano recording (DG 2008). Each instrumentation gives the work a different character.

**Listen** (cross-volume): the Musical Offering Ricercar a 6 (Chapter 18). The two late-period six-voice / six-subject contrapuntal masterworks pair instructively.

**Read** : Peter Williams, _J. S. Bach: A Life in Music_ (Cambridge University Press 2007), the chapter on the late works. About 50 pages covering the Art of Fugue, Musical Offering, and B Minor Mass.

**Read** (specialized): Joseph Kerman, _The Art of Fugue: Bach Fugues for Keyboard, 1715-1750_ (University of California Press 2005). About 200 pages on Bach's keyboard fugues across his career, including substantial Art of Fugue analysis.

## Chapter 20: Bach's Contrapuntal Voice — Applied to an Unfamiliar Work

The volume's previous nineteen chapters have introduced Bach's contrapuntal voice across roughly fifty anchor works, each demonstrating one or more of the principal compositional devices he developed. This synthesis chapter does two things. First, it collects the nine principal contrapuntal devices into a single compact inventory (§20.1), so that the reader has a reference. Second, and more important, it walks through a Bach work the volume has not anchored — Cantata BWV 78 "Jesu, der du meine Seele" opening chorus — using the listening skills the volume has built, to demonstrate that the synthesis is not a list of features to memorize but a working capability for engaging with unfamiliar Bach.

The point of the chapter is the second part. The first part is reference. If you remember nothing else from the synthesis, the §20.2 demonstration on BWV 78 should be where the working capability gets tested.

### 20.1 The Nine Contrapuntal Devices, Briefly

Each device is treated in detail in the chapters that introduce it. This is the compact reference:

  1. **The fugue subject and answer**. One voice announces a short musical idea (the subject); subsequent voices enter with the subject transposed up a fifth (the answer); when all voices have stated the subject, the exposition is complete and the fugue develops through episodes and re-statements. The volume's central anchors: Chapter 3 (Capriccio fugue), Chapter 10 (WTC fugues), Chapter 19 (Art of Fugue).

  2. **The chorale-prelude texture**. A Lutheran chorale tune appears as cantus firmus in one voice while contrapuntal voices weave around it. Central anchors: Chapter 6 (Orgelbüchlein), Chapter 11 (chorale cantata BWV 4), Chapter 12 (Leipzig chorale cantatas).

  3. **The double, triple, and quadruple fugue**. A fugue with two, three, or four subjects developed together. Central anchors: Chapter 6 (Passacaglia BWV 582 closing double fugue), Chapter 19 (Art of Fugue Contrapunctus 14, the intended quadruple fugue, surviving with three of the four subjects before breaking off mid-phrase).

  4. **The canon at various intervals**. Two voices imitate each other exactly with a fixed time-delay and interval. Central anchors: Chapter 17 (Goldberg's every-third-variation canon scheme rising from unison to ninth), Chapter 18 (Musical Offering's ten canons), Chapter 19 (Art of Fugue canons).

  5. **The stile antico**. Deliberate use of archaic Renaissance-polyphony writing — slow tempos, modal harmony, Gregorian-chant cantus firmus, uniform rhythms — for moments of particular solemnity. Central anchor: Chapter 14 (B Minor Mass Confiteor).

  6. **The permutation fugue**. A fugue in which the subject and countersubjects can be combined in multiple orderings, with each combination producing acceptable counterpoint. Central anchors: Chapter 10 (WTC fugues), Chapter 19 (Art of Fugue mirror fugues).

  7. **The Italianate concertante writing**. Vivaldian ritornello structure with strong rhythmic drive, brightly-major-key affect, alternation between full ensemble and soloist group. Central anchors: Chapter 8 (Brandenburg Concertos), Chapter 12 (cantata opening choruses), Chapter 15 (Christmas Oratorio celebratory openings).

  8. **The French-dance suite movements**. The stylized dance forms (Allemande, Courante, Sarabande, Gigue, plus optional gallantries) inherited from the French clavecin tradition. Central anchor: Chapter 9 (Cello Suites and Violin Partitas).

  9. **The chromatic harmony**. Harmonic motion using notes outside the diatonic scale of the prevailing key, especially descending chromatic bass lines for lament. Central anchors: Chapter 14 (B Minor Mass Crucifixus), Chapter 19 (Art of Fugue Contrapunctus 14 with the B-A-C-H chromatic theme).

The devices overlap. A typical mature Bach choral movement uses several at once — the cantus-firmus chorale tune as compositional foundation, fugal voices around it, chromatic harmony at moments of textual weight, Italianate ritornello framing. The nine devices are not separable techniques but a unified compositional language. The next section demonstrates this on a specific unfamiliar work.

### 20.2 Demonstration: Cantata BWV 78 "Jesu, der du meine Seele" Opening Chorus

The Cantata BWV 78 "Jesu, der du meine Seele" ("Jesus, by whom my soul") is a chorale cantata from Bach's 1724-25 Leipzig chorale-cantata cycle, composed for the 14th Sunday after Trinity 1724. The volume has not anchored it directly. It is, however, one of the major chorale cantatas of the cycle — a work the reader who has worked through Chapter 11 (BWV 4) and Chapter 12 (BWV 140, 147, 80, 82) is now equipped to encounter.

The opening chorus (about 7 minutes; the cantata as a whole runs about 22 minutes) is the demonstration. Find a recording — Suzuki BIS 1996, Gardiner Soli Deo Gloria 2000, or Herreweghe Harmonia Mundi 1997 are all good options — and listen through with the following walkthrough.

**The chorale tune**. Before listening to the cantata, find a hymnal recording of the chorale "Jesu, der du meine Seele" (text and tune by Johann Rist 1641, in the chorale collections Bach worked with at Leipzig). Hear the chorale sung plainly. The tune is in a minor mode, with a slow contemplative shape; the text is a penitential prayer. Five minutes with the chorale tune in plain hymnic version makes the cantata's opening chorus a dramatically different listening experience.

**0:00-0:30: Listen for the descending chromatic bass**. The opening chorus is built on a descending chromatic bass line in the continuo — the same lament-tradition device that the Crucifixus of the B Minor Mass uses (Chapter 14 §14.3). The bass descends step by step through chromatic half-steps, repeating across the movement's structure. You should recognize this device immediately from your Crucifixus listening. Apply: §20.9 chromatic harmony.

**0:30-2:30: Listen for the chorale tune as cantus firmus in the soprano**. As the chorus enters, the soprano voice takes the chorale tune in long sustained notes — this is the cantus-firmus principle you encountered in the Orgelbüchlein chorale preludes (Chapter 6) and the chorale cantata BWV 4 (Chapter 11). The chorale tune is slow, ornamented, but unmistakably the same tune you heard in the hymnal version. The lower voices (alto, tenor, bass) weave contrapuntal lines around the cantus firmus while the chromatic bass continues underneath. Apply: §20.2 chorale-prelude texture.

**Throughout: Listen for the contrapuntal voices in the alto, tenor, and bass**. The lower three voices are not just harmonic filler; each carries its own independent melodic line, weaving around the cantus-firmus chorale in the soprano. The four-voice texture is what you built listening capability for on "Sheep May Safely Graze" (Chapter 2) and the WTC fugues (Chapter 10) — the same skill, now applied at larger scale and with the chromatic-bass foundation. Apply: §20.1 fugue subject and answer (the lower voices use fugal-imitative writing, even though the work as a whole is not strictly a fugue).

**Throughout: Listen for the orchestral instruments doubling and adding**. The strings double the chorus's lines while the oboes provide independent obbligato figures. The orchestration is partly French-style (the dotted-rhythm phrasing in some passages) and partly Italianate (the rhythmic drive carries the chorale-cantata texture forward through the chromatic bass).

**About 5:00-7:00: The closing buildup**. The chorus's voices intensify as the chorale tune approaches its final phrase. The chromatic bass continues; the harmonic motion becomes more dense; the four-voice texture reaches its fullest density. The cadence arrives on the chorale's expected tonic.

What you have just done: applied four of the nine contrapuntal devices (§20.1 fugue subject, §20.2 chorale-prelude texture, §20.7 Italianate writing in the orchestral framing, §20.9 chromatic harmony) to a single Bach movement you had not heard before, identifying each device's contribution to the texture, and tracking the cantus-firmus chorale tune through the polyphonic weaving. This is the working capability the volume's Thread B has been building.

If the BWV 78 opening chorus is now intelligible as a compositional argument — chorale + chromatic bass + fugal-imitative lower voices + orchestral framing — then the synthesis has worked. You can apply the same listening discipline to any Bach work you encounter. The catalogue is approachable.

### 20.3 Bach's Contrapuntal Voice as a Unified Language

A note on what holds these nine devices together. When you hear a major Bach sacred work at its most characteristic — a B Minor Mass Kyrie, a Leipzig Passion opening chorus, a late chorale cantata, the BWV 78 opening you have just walked through — you are likely hearing several features at once: the fugue subject moving through four, five, or six voices in systematic imitation (§20.1); a chorale tune sustained as cantus firmus in one of those voices (§20.2); chromatic harmony in the bass providing emotional depth and structural weight (§20.9); the Italianate drive of the ritornello framing the whole (§20.7); the stile antico invoked at moments of maximum theological solemnity (§20.5). The individual features are not separable ornaments; they are the woven fabric. Listening for any one of the nine, you hear the others alongside it.

The unity is partly what makes Bach Bach. Other composers used many of these devices individually — the canon, the chorale prelude, the fugue, the chromatic bass — but few combined them as comprehensively and as habitually. The contrapuntal voice that the volume's Thread B has been building toward is not a list of techniques but this combinatorial fluency: any one device by itself is finite; the combinations produce the depth.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The chapter argues that the nine contrapuntal devices are not nine separable techniques but a unified compositional language. Listen again to one of the volume's anchor works (the BWV 4 opening chorus, or the WTC Book 1 C-major fugue, or the Crucifixus of the B Minor Mass) and try to identify how many of the nine devices are operating simultaneously. Does the unity feel right, or do you experience the devices as more separable than the chapter suggests?"_

* _"Try the same demonstration the chapter walks through on BWV 78 with a different unfamiliar work — perhaps a different chorale cantata (BWV 62 'Nun komm, der Heiden Heiland' is a recommended next attempt), or one of the Brandenburg Concerto movements the volume did not anchor in detail, or one of the WTC Book 2 fugues. How well does the listening capability transfer? Where do you find yourself reaching the limits of what the volume has built?"_

* _"The chapter argues that the working capability — the ability to apply the contrapuntal-listening discipline to any unfamiliar Bach work — is the synthesis's actual value, more than any catalogue of features. Does this match your experience of the volume? What other Bach work would you want to try the listening capability on next?"_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

The chapter's central exercise is the BWV 78 walkthrough in §20.2. Do it once carefully, with the chorale-tune hymnic version heard first, then the cantata opening chorus with the four contrapuntal devices in mind. The walkthrough is the synthesis test; if you can do it, the volume has worked.

If you want a second demonstration: BWV 62 "Nun komm, der Heiden Heiland" (the chorale cantata for Advent 1 1724) makes a good companion piece. Different chorale, different liturgical season, same compositional principles.

### Going Deeper

**Read** (the synthesis-relevant scholarship):

* **Robert L. Marshall** , _The Music of Johann Sebastian Bach_ (Schirmer 1989) — the work-by-work analytical reference.
* **Christoph Wolff** , _Johann Sebastian Bach: The Learned Musician_ (Norton 2000) — the biographical-and-compositional reference.
* **Peter Williams** , _J. S. Bach: A Life in Music_ (Cambridge 2007) — the alternative biography with substantial musical-compositional analysis.

**Listen** (the late-period synthesis works):

* The complete Art of Fugue (Savall, Walcha, or Aimard) — the most-systematic exploration of contrapuntal devices.
* The complete Musical Offering (Savall, Goebel, Pinnock) — the ten canons demonstrate canon types systematically.
* The complete Goldberg Variations (Gould 1955 or 1981) — the canon-at-rising-intervals scheme is the most-extensive keyboard exploration of the device.

These three late-period works together demonstrate the full range of Bach's contrapuntal voice.

## Chapter 21: Recording History — What the Choices Mean

The Bach catalogue has been recorded more extensively than that of any other Western composer. Hundreds of versions exist of the major works; multiple complete-cantata cycles exist; the Goldberg Variations alone have produced something like 200 published recordings. The first impulse of a chapter on recording history is to inventory the principal traditions and their leading figures, but that work has been substantially done in the per-chapter Alternative recordings sections of Chapters 4-19, and a roll-call here would mostly repeat. This chapter does something different: it tries to identify what the principal interpretive choices actually mean, where the divisions in performance practice come from, and where a listener has to decide rather than just follow consensus.

### 21.1 The Fundamental Divide: Modern Instruments vs HIP

The largest single division in Bach performance practice across the past fifty years has been between the modern-instrument tradition that dominated the mid-twentieth century (Karl Richter, Otto Klemperer, Karl Münchinger, Helmut Walcha on organ) and the Historically Informed Performance (HIP) movement that began in the 1970s and has been the dominant scholarly-performance tradition since the late twentieth century (Nikolaus Harnoncourt, John Eliot Gardiner, Trevor Pinnock, Philippe Herreweghe, Ton Koopman, Masaaki Suzuki).

The modern-instrument tradition uses contemporary symphony-and-choral forces. A typical Richter cantata reading from the 1960s involved 80-100 choir voices and 40-50 modern-instrument players; the resulting sound is full, warm, weighty. The HIP tradition reconstructs the smaller forces and period instruments Bach actually used. A typical Suzuki cantata reading involves perhaps 18-24 voices and 12-15 period instruments; the sound is leaner, drier, sharper.

What each gets and loses:

The modern-instrument tradition gets emotional weight and choral mass. Klemperer's 1961 St. Matthew Passion at 3 hours 40 minutes is genuinely monumental in a way no HIP reading is — the slow tempos and full forces convey a kind of monumental seriousness that the leaner HIP readings cannot. Richter's complete cantata cycle (Archiv 1958-1978) presents the cantatas as substantial concert works with cathedral-scale forces. The reading gains in emotional impact what it loses in textural transparency.

The HIP tradition gets contrapuntal clarity and (arguably) historical accuracy. When Gardiner's smaller forces sing a Bach cantata fugue, you can actually hear the individual contrapuntal voices in a way that Richter's larger choir does not allow. The period instruments (the natural trumpet, the oboe d'amore, the viola da gamba, the harpsichord continuo) produce timbres that the modern-instrument equivalents replace with smoother, more homogeneous sounds. What the HIP reading loses is the emotional accumulation that the larger forces provide.

Neither tradition is obviously correct. The historical-authenticity argument that has dominated scholarly performance for fifty years is partial: even the most scrupulous HIP reading is a 21st-century construction of what a 1730 Leipzig performance might have sounded like, not a recording of one. The contemporary listener who cares about Bach should hear both — Richter's St. Matthew Passion of 1958 and Gardiner's of 1988, side by side on the same Passion, with the contrast informing rather than settling which is better.

### 21.2 The Bach Cantata Pilgrimage (2000)

John Eliot Gardiner's Bach Cantata Pilgrimage of 2000 is the most-ambitious single Bach-performance project of the modern era and deserves separate treatment. Across the year 2000 Gardiner conducted the Monteverdi Choir and English Baroque Soloists in performances of all of Bach's sacred cantatas — over 200 works — at churches around the world, with each Sunday's cantata performed on the corresponding Sunday of the liturgical year on which Bach himself would have performed it. The Pilgrimage was a kind of liturgical-archaeological exercise as much as a music-making project: the cantatas were placed back into something approximating their original setting (a Sunday morning service in a Lutheran church) rather than performed as detached concert works.

The recordings were released over the following decade on Soli Deo Gloria; the cycle is one of the major complete-cantata documents. What sets it apart from the other cycles (Harnoncourt-Leonhardt, Richter, Rilling, Koopman, Suzuki) is the contextual restoration. Where Suzuki records in a Tokyo studio with the Bach Collegium Japan, Gardiner records in an actual Lutheran church on the appropriate Sunday with the congregation present. The recordings have the acoustic and the occasional rough edges of live performance; they also have an experiential dimension that pure studio recording cannot produce. Hear at least one cantata in the Pilgrimage version (the Cantata BWV 4 from Easter Sunday 2000, recorded at the Georgenkirche in Eisenach where Bach was baptized in 1685, is the canonical example) to register what the restoration adds.

### 21.3 The Two Glenn Goulds

Glenn Gould's two Goldberg Variations recordings — CBS 1955 mono and CBS 1981 stereo — are the special case of recorded Bach interpretation. The same pianist recorded the same work twenty-six years apart and produced two substantially different readings. The 1955 reading is fast (about 38 minutes); the 1981 reading is slow (about 51 minutes); the slow variations in particular take dramatically different tempos. The 1981 Variation 25 ("Black Pearl") runs about 6 minutes 30 seconds, among the slowest readings of any pianist; the 1955 takes the same variation in about 4 minutes 30 seconds.

The two recordings constitute the clearest available demonstration that interpretive choice is not the same as work fidelity. Both recordings are Gould; both are the Goldberg Variations; both are credible readings; they are not the same reading. The implication for the broader recording history is that "Bach the work" and "Bach the recording" are different things. A listener engaging seriously with the Goldberg Variations should hear both Gould readings as a unit — the comparison is part of what the work has come to mean in the twentieth-and-twenty-first-century reception.

Gould's 1981 was completed in April-May 1981; he died in October 1982, eighteen months later. The 1981 Goldberg is often treated as his final compositional-interpretive statement. Whether the slower late tempos reflect age, maturity, or different interpretive priorities is debated; what is clear is that the second Gould Goldberg is not simply a "better" version of the first. It is a different reading of the same notes.

### 21.4 Differentiating Among the HIP Cantata Cycles

The HIP cantata recording tradition has produced four major complete cycles in the past half-century: Harnoncourt-Leonhardt (Teldec 1971-1990), Gardiner (Soli Deo Gloria 2000), Koopman (Erato/Antoine Marchand 1994-2005), and Suzuki (BIS 1995-2013). The cycles overlap substantially in repertoire (they all cover the surviving ~200 sacred cantatas) but differ in interpretive emphasis. A listener who wants to build a serious cantata library can pick one cycle for breadth and supplement with selected single-cantata recordings; the question is which cycle suits her.

The Harnoncourt-Leonhardt cycle (the first complete HIP cantata cycle) uses boy choirs throughout for the soprano and alto parts, in approximate reconstruction of the Thomanerchor's 1730 practice. The boy-voice sound is unfamiliar to modern ears used to women's soprano-and-alto voices; some listeners find it revelatory of the original Lutheran-Leipzig sound, others find it under-resourced. The cycle is foundational; some of the individual readings now feel dated.

The Gardiner Pilgrimage uses adult mixed voices in his Monteverdi Choir; the recordings are dramatically projected (Gardiner's general interpretive temperament) and benefit from the church-acoustic contextual restoration. The cycle is the most-engaged with the dramatic possibilities of the cantata genre; some readings sacrifice contemplative depth to dramatic projection.

The Koopman cycle is energetic and well-articulated, with a slightly looser interpretive temperament than Gardiner's. The Amsterdam Baroque Orchestra sound is bright; the choir is small and clear.

The Suzuki cycle is the most-recent complete cycle and is often singled out for its clear contrapuntal articulation. The Bach Collegium Japan sound is restrained; the readings tend to favor textural clarity over dramatic projection. For listeners who want to hear the cantatas' contrapuntal architecture most clearly, the Suzuki cycle is often the recommended choice. Some listeners find the readings emotionally restrained.

For a single cycle: Suzuki for the most-balanced contemporary HIP standard; Gardiner Pilgrimage for the contextual restoration; Harnoncourt-Leonhardt for the historical foundation; Koopman for the bright energetic alternative. Most serious cantata listeners eventually accumulate selections from several cycles; the cycles are sufficiently different in interpretive temperament that the choice between them is not trivial.

### 21.5 Practical First-Recording Recommendations

For a listener building a personal Bach library, the following recommendations cover the major works the volume has anchored. The per-chapter Alternative recordings sections (Chapters 4-19) give the wider field; this list is the recommended first acquisition.

**Cantatas** : Gardiner Pilgrimage (Soli Deo Gloria 2000) for HIP with contextual restoration; Suzuki (BIS 1995-2013) for the contemporary HIP standard.

**Passions** : Gardiner St. Matthew (Archiv 1988) and Herreweghe St. John (Harmonia Mundi 1989) for the HIP standard; Klemperer St. Matthew (EMI 1961) for the modern-instrument monumental alternative.

**B Minor Mass** : Gardiner (Archiv 1985) for HIP; Klemperer (EMI 1967) for the modern-instrument alternative.

**Christmas Oratorio** : Gardiner (Archiv 1987) for HIP; Jacobs (Harmonia Mundi 1997) for the more theatrical alternative.

**Brandenburg Concertos** : Pinnock (Archiv 1982) for HIP; Richter (Archiv 1967) for the modern-instrument alternative.

**Cello Suites** : Casals (EMI 1936-39) as the historical foundation; Yo-Yo Ma 1998 (Sony) for the contemporary modern-instrument reading; Bylsma 1992 (Sony) for the period-instrument alternative.

**Violin Sonatas and Partitas** : Szeryng (DG 1967) for modern instruments; Podger (Channel Classics 1999) for HIP.

**Well-Tempered Clavier** : Schiff (Decca 1984-85 + 1985-87) for piano; Pinnock (Hänssler 2003-05) for harpsichord; Gould (CBS 1962-63 + 1969-71) for the modernist-contrarian alternative.

**Goldberg Variations** : Gould 1955 and Gould 1981 (both CBS) as the essential pair; Hantaï (Mirare 1993 or 2003) for harpsichord; Perahia (Sony 2000) for a contemporary lyrical piano alternative.

**Organ works** : Walcha (Archiv 1956-71 stereo) for modern instruments; Koopman (Challenge Classics 1990s-2000s) for HIP.

**Art of Fugue** : Savall (Astrée 1986) for the viol-consort reading; Walcha or Aimard for the keyboard reading; sample at least two contrasting instrumentations.

**Musical Offering** : Savall (Alia Vox 2001) or Goebel (Archiv 1979/1984).

For a starter library of ten recordings (one per major work category): Goldberg Gould 1955; Cello Suites Casals 1936-39; St. Matthew Passion Gardiner 1988; B Minor Mass Gardiner 1985; Brandenburgs Pinnock 1982; Violin Sonatas/Partitas Szeryng 1967; WTC Book 1 Schiff 1984-85; selected cantatas from Gardiner Pilgrimage; organ works selections from Walcha; Art of Fugue Savall 1986. These ten cover the principal compositional centers of the catalogue.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The chapter argues that the modern-instrument tradition and the HIP tradition each get something the other loses — the modern instruments get emotional weight and choral mass; the HIP gets contrapuntal clarity and historical reconstruction. Listen to the same work in both traditions (the St. Matthew Passion opening chorus is the recommended comparison: Klemperer 1961 vs Gardiner 1988). Which do you actually prefer? What does your preference tell you about what you want from the music?"_

* _"Glenn Gould's 1955 and 1981 Goldberg Variations are the clearest case of the same pianist recording the same work twice with substantially different interpretive choices. The chapter argues that the two recordings together constitute the work in the modern reception, not either alone. Is this right? Or is one of the two recordings closer to the truth of the work?"_

* _"The Bach Cantata Pilgrimage of 2000 restored the cantatas partly to their original liturgical context — actual Lutheran churches, the corresponding Sunday of the year, in some sense the original performance occasion. The restoration is incomplete (modern audiences and modern performance values still operate) but it is closer than concert-hall performance. What does the contextual restoration give the work that pure concert performance does not? And what does the modern listener bring that the original Leipzig congregation did not?"_

## Chapter 22: Bach Reception — The Bach Each Era Constructed

The reception of Johann Sebastian Bach from his death in 1750 to the present is sometimes told as a linear story: forgotten for half a century, rediscovered by Mendelssohn in 1829, then progressively recognized as one of the supreme figures of Western art music. The linear telling is convenient but misleading. The truer story is one of successive reinventions: each generation has constructed a Bach answering what its moment found useful — sometimes from the same surviving documents, sometimes by emphasizing different works, sometimes by reading different meanings into the same notes. The composer who served as the foundational figure of nineteenth-century German musical nationalism is not quite the composer Albert Schweitzer's 1908 _J. S. Bach_ presented as a Christian-mystical genius, and neither of those is quite the working Lutheran cantor that the late-twentieth-century HIP movement insisted Bach actually was. All three readings draw on Bach's documented life and surviving music; all three are partly the music and partly the era looking through the music.

This chapter walks through the principal reinventions. The aim is not to argue that any one is the correct Bach — they are all partial — but to make visible the construction so that the contemporary listener can ask which Bach she is listening to when she turns on a recording in 2026.

### 22.1 Bach as Bach Saw Himself (Leipzig, 1750)

What we know of Bach's self-conception, mostly from his correspondence and the contractual documents of his Leipzig employment, suggests a relatively modest professional identity. He was a Lutheran cantor and Kapellmeister, paid to compose and direct music for specific liturgical occasions, responsible for teaching choir-school boys, periodically negotiating with the Leipzig city council about pay and prerogatives. He held a Saxon court title — _Compositeur_ at the Royal Court Chapel at Dresden — that he used in his published works' title pages because the title strengthened his hand against the Leipzig council. He took on private students. He published carefully chosen works (the _Clavier-Übung_ series, the Goldberg Variations, the Musical Offering) under his own direction. He was respected within central-German musical circles and known by reputation across the Lutheran north, but he was not internationally famous in the way that Telemann or, later, his own son Johann Christian Bach in London would be.

The C. P. E. Bach Obituary of 1750-51 is the principal contemporary biographical document. It records the family-musician dynasty, the apprenticeship pilgrimages of the young Bach to Hamburg and Lübeck, the Weimar imprisonment, the Köthen and Leipzig careers, the late contrapuntal works. It does not yet construct Bach as a supreme figure. He is presented as the most-learned contrapuntist of his generation — but generations move on, and the obituary itself acknowledges that the _Empfindsamer Stil_ his sons were now writing in was the future.

### 22.2 The Forgotten Bach (1750-1800)

The fifty years after Bach's death were not a period of total disappearance, but they were a period of substantial obscurity outside specialist circles. The principal reasons:

The shift in musical taste was already underway in Bach's lifetime. Carl Philipp Emanuel Bach (1714-1788) and Johann Christian Bach (1735-1782) were the central-German musical sons of the new style — clearer melodically, less dense contrapuntally, more emotionally direct in the _Empfindsamer_ manner. By the 1770s the contrapuntal-baroque idiom was old-fashioned; J. S. Bach was old-fashioned along with it.

Most of Bach's works were unpublished. The cantatas existed only as performance materials in Leipzig church archives and in the Bach family's possession; the Passions existed only in performance copies; even the B Minor Mass had never been performed complete. Without published scores, the works could not enter wider European performance.

The cantata genre was inseparable from its local liturgical context. A work written for the third Sunday after Trinity 1725 at the Thomaskirche, with the day's chorale and the day's Gospel reading as its compositional and theological foundation, could not simply travel to a Berlin or Vienna concert hall in 1780.

But the obscurity was incomplete. Mozart studied Bach keyboard works at Baron van Swieten's Vienna circle from 1782 (the Mozart volume covers this). Beethoven studied the WTC under Neefe in Bonn in the 1780s (the Beethoven volume covers this). Both engagements left marks on their compositional voices. The narrow continuity through specialist musicians preserved enough Bach knowledge across the obscure decades to make the nineteenth-century revival possible.

### 22.3 The German-Nationalist Bach (Forkel 1802, Mendelssohn 1829)

When Johann Nikolaus Forkel published _Über Johann Sebastian Bachs Leben, Kunst, und Kunstwerke_ in 1802, Germany was in the middle of the Napoleonic occupation. The book is the foundational text of modern Bach reception, but it is also unambiguously a political-cultural document. Forkel framed Bach as the foundational figure of German musical heritage — the proof that the German tradition had its own supreme art-music figure who did not require French or Italian validation. The framing was, in 1802 Germany, urgent.

The Forkel framing carries through most of the nineteenth-century reception. When Mendelssohn revived the _St. Matthew Passion_ in Berlin on March 11, 1829 (Chapter 13 §13.1 describes the performance itself), the cultural meaning was not just "let us hear this beautiful music again" but "let us recognize the German musical heritage to which we belong." The 1850 founding of the Bach Society in Leipzig, on the centenary of Bach's death, and the Bach-Gesellschaft complete-works edition project of 1851-1900, were a continuation of the same nationalist-cultural project — the German nation creating the institutional apparatus to canonize its supreme musical figure.

Modern readers should be aware that Forkel's biography includes anecdotes that subsequent scholarship treats cautiously: the moonlight-copying story of the young Bach at his brother's house (Chapter 3); the precise Goldberg-Keyserling commission narrative (Chapter 17); some details of the Frederick the Great visit (Chapter 18). The anecdotes have the texture of polished family-tradition narratives Forkel collected from Bach's surviving sons fifty years after the events. They have shaped Bach reception for two centuries despite their uncertain factual basis — partly because they are good stories, partly because they served the nineteenth-century construction Forkel was building.

### 22.4 The Supreme-Architect Bach (19th Century)

Across the nineteenth century, as the BG edition gradually made the catalogue available, the dominant interpretive frame became Bach as the supreme architect — the mathematical-structural-contrapuntal master who had pushed the Western music tradition to a kind of objective perfection. This reading found particular support in the late contrapuntal works: the Goldberg Variations, the Musical Offering, the Art of Fugue. The works' architectural precision (the every-third-variation canon scheme in the Goldberg; the systematic canon-types in the Musical Offering; the monothematic constraint of the Art of Fugue) lent itself to the architect-Bach reading.

The 19th-century supreme-architect Bach was substantially constructed. The contrapuntal density and architectural rigor of the late works is real; the suggestion that this is what Bach principally was — rather than one mode he worked in alongside the cantatas, the Passions, the keyboard miniatures, the chamber music for Köthen, the pedagogical pieces for his sons — is a selective emphasis. But the emphasis became the dominant frame for nearly a century. It shapes the still-living cliché that Bach is mathematical, austere, brain-music — a cliché Chapter 1 §1.4 Habit 1 spent some effort dismantling.

The supreme-architect Bach also enabled the cross-disciplinary engagement with Bach as a model of structural perfection in fields beyond music. The 20th-century convergence with mathematics and logic — most visibly in Douglas Hofstadter's _Gödel, Escher, Bach: An Eternal Golden Braid_ (1979), which placed the Musical Offering's canons alongside Gödel's incompleteness theorems and Escher's drawings as exemplars of self-referential structure — was a late development of this 19th-century inheritance.

### 22.5 The Mystical-Religious Bach (Schweitzer 1908)

Albert Schweitzer's _J. S. Bach_ (1908 German, 1911 English) was the early-20th-century counter-construction to the supreme-architect reading. Schweitzer, who was a theologian and a working organist as well as a polymath, presented Bach as a religious-musical mystic — the Lutheran cantor whose compositional voice was essentially the expression of Christian piety in music, with specific melodic and harmonic devices functioning as symbolic carriers of theological meaning. Schweitzer's reading was substantially shaped by his own theological commitments; the "musical symbolism" he documented in detail (specific melodic figures representing specific theological concepts) was as much projection as discovery.

Modern scholarship has largely moved away from the strongly mystical reading. The specific musical-symbolism arguments are now treated as Schweitzer's interpretive overlay rather than as recovered Bach intentions. But the broader claim — that Bach was a religious composer whose work cannot be detached from its Christian theological foundation — remains alive and is the dominant frame in some performance and listening communities even today. Chapter 16 surveyed where the present volume stands (between Schweitzer and Gardiner, closer to Gardiner): the religious commitments are real but the works function as music without confessional decoding.

### 22.6 The Working-Musician Bach (HIP, 1970s onward)

The Historically Informed Performance movement of the 1970s onward (Chapter 21 surveys the recordings themselves) carried a particular interpretive frame: Bach as a working musician within specific historical conditions, performing on specific instruments with specific tuning systems for specific liturgical and chamber-music occasions. The HIP construction is a deliberate corrective to both the 19th-century supreme-architect Bach and the Schweitzer mystical-religious Bach — a reading that emphasizes the working professional rather than the supreme artist or the religious mystic.

Nikolaus Harnoncourt's writings (_Baroque Music Today_ , 1982) make the case explicitly: Bach was not the timeless universal figure of the nineteenth-century reception; he was a working German Lutheran cantor whose music was designed for performance on the instruments of his time, in the acoustic conditions of his churches, by ensembles of the size he actually commanded. To hear the music as he composed it requires reconstructing those conditions. The argument has substantially reshaped Bach performance practice across the past fifty years.

The HIP Bach has its own selective emphasis. The reading privileges what is recoverable about historical performance practice and tends to subordinate questions about what the music means in contemporary contexts. A performer who carefully reconstructs the Thomanerchor's 1730 voice sizes and the Stadtpfeifer's wind-instrument tunings is implicitly committed to the view that this reconstruction is what matters most — that the music was at its truest in its historical context and what we should aim for is recovery of that context. This is one defensible interpretive position. It is not the only one.

### 22.7 The Catalogue Bach (Wolff 2000)

Christoph Wolff's _Johann Sebastian Bach: The Learned Musician_ (Norton 2000) represents the contemporary scholarly synthesis: Bach as the polymath master of the entire keyboard-organ-vocal-instrumental output, with his career organized through the six-period Weimar/Köthen/Leipzig framework this volume has used as its biographical spine. The Wolff Bach is the catalogue Bach — the figure whose stature emerges from the substantial size and range of the surviving works rather than from any specific theological or architectural claim.

The Wolff framing is now the default scholarly position. Most contemporary Bach scholarship operates within it. The framing is biographically substantial — the documentary work behind Wolff's biography is genuinely impressive — and avoids the more obvious projections of the earlier constructions. But it too has its emphases: the architectural arc of the six-period framework imposes more coherence on Bach's actual career trajectory than the documentary evidence necessarily supports; the catalogue-comprehensive scale of the treatment can subordinate individual works to a totalizing picture; the "learned musician" label, which Wolff means as a compliment to Bach's polymath range, can flatten the differences between the contemplative organ chorale prelude and the festive secular cantata.

### 22.8 Which Bach Are We Listening To?

The contemporary listener inherits all of these constructions, and they are not always compatible. When you play Glenn Gould's 1955 Goldberg Variations, you are hearing a substantially supreme-architect Bach — Gould's interpretive choices foreground contrapuntal architecture over melodic-harmonic warmth. When you play John Eliot Gardiner's Bach Cantata Pilgrimage recordings, you are hearing a working-musician HIP Bach — recorded in churches on the corresponding Sundays of the liturgical year, with the institutional context partly reconstructed. When you read Schweitzer alongside the recordings, you are filtering the music through a still-living mystical-religious frame. When you read Wolff, you are framing the works as components of a coherent catalogue.

These are not just different recordings or different books; they are different Bachs. The differences are not all reconcilable. The reader of this volume — who has been carried through the catalogue under what is essentially a Wolff biographical frame, with HIP performance recommendations and a deliberately-cautious theological frame closer to Gardiner than to Schweitzer — should be aware that the frame has shaped what she has read.

The reception trajectory is not closed. The 2020s and beyond will continue to develop new readings — possibly returning to Schweitzer's theological emphasis, possibly pursuing further the HIP construction toward maximally-specific historical reconstructions, possibly developing new framings none of the previous can predict. What is clear is that the contemporary listener is herself part of the reception process. The Bach she hears is partly her own.

### 22.9 The Voyager Golden Record (1977) as a Coda

A final document worth pausing over. In 1977 the Voyager 1 and 2 spacecraft were launched into interstellar trajectory, each carrying a gold-plated phonograph record containing 27 musical selections totaling about 90 minutes — a representative-musical statement of humanity for any extraterrestrial intelligence that might encounter the spacecraft in the indefinite future. Three of the 27 selections are Bach: Brandenburg Concerto No. 2 first movement (Munich Bach Orchestra / Karl Richter); Partita No. 3 for solo violin BWV 1006 Gavotte en Rondeaux (Arthur Grumiaux); WTC Book 2 Prelude and Fugue No. 1 in C major BWV 870 (Glenn Gould). Bach is the most-represented composer on the record.

The Voyager selection committee, led by Carl Sagan and Ann Druyan, was operating under multiple constraints. Bach got three slots; Beethoven got two (the Fifth Symphony first movement and the Cavatina from String Quartet Op. 130); Mozart got one (an aria from _The Magic Flute_); Stravinsky got one (_The Rite of Spring_ opening). The choice to put Bach at the apex of the Western art-music representation reflects the late-20th-century consensus that Bach is the central figure of Western musical heritage. The choice was also a particular construction — the selections emphasize the architectural-instrumental Bach (the Brandenburg concerto-grosso; the polyphonic-illusion violin partita; the keyboard prelude-and-fugue) over the cantata-Passion-mystical-religious Bach. The Voyager Bach is more the 19th-century supreme-architect Bach than the Schweitzer Bach or the HIP working-musician Bach.

The record is now in interstellar space at distance growing perpetually. Whatever Bach reception continues to develop in the next centuries on Earth, the Voyager Bach is fixed: three specific recordings of three specific works, traveling away from any further reception forever.

### Conversation Seeds

* _"The chapter argues that there is no single 'true' Bach but a sequence of constructions — the Lutheran cantor, the supreme architect, the religious mystic, the working musician, the learned polymath. Which of these readings feels most accurate to the music you have heard in this volume? Or do all of them feel partial, and what is the construction that might capture what each misses?"_

* **One-sentence stake.** If you had to name the Bach you actually heard across this volume — not the Bach the chapters described, but the one your ear formed — what single sentence would it be?

The selection committee's choices emphasized the architectural-instrumental Bach (the Brandenburg concerto, the violin partita, the WTC) over the cantata-Passion-mystical Bach. If you were on a 2026 selection committee for an updated record, which Bach work would you choose, and which Bach would your choice represent?"_
