## Chapter 23: Where to Go Next

The previous twenty-two chapters have walked through roughly fifty Bach works across the 65-year career, building the listening skills the volume's Thread B has developed and providing the biographical, institutional, theological, and reception-historical context the music requires. This closing chapter is intentionally short. It is the practical handoff: a tight reading list, a tight first-recordings list, the preview of subsequent _Close Listening_ volumes, and the final pointer to the reader's continuing engagement.

### 23.1 The Recommended Reading

A reader who wants one book to read alongside or after the volume: **John Eliot Gardiner,_Bach: Music in the Castle of Heaven_ (Knopf 2013)**. About 670 pages. The contemporary performer-biographer voice; accessible, direct, opinionated; the book has had wide readership beyond academic specialists. Recommended as the primary single-book complement to this volume.

For a biographical companion: **Christoph Wolff,_Johann Sebastian Bach: The Learned Musician_ (Norton 2000)**. About 600 pages. The contemporary single-volume biography in English; archival and dense; the reference framework this volume has used throughout.

For the cantata catalogue: **Alfred Dürr,_The Cantatas of J. S. Bach_ (Oxford 2005 English edition)**. About 1,000 pages, work-by-work. Reference rather than sustained read; consult when working through specific cantatas.

For the Passions: **Daniel R. Melamed,_Hearing Bach's Passions_ (Oxford 2005)**. About 200 pages.

For the organ works: **Peter Williams,_The Organ Music of J. S. Bach_ (Cambridge 2003, second edition)**. About 800 pages; reference, not sustained read.

For the Bach family context: **Karl Geiringer,_The Bach Family: Seven Generations of Creative Genius_ (Oxford 1954)**. About 500 pages. Dated in some particulars but remains the standard treatment.

For the theological-mystical Bach as a historical document: **Albert Schweitzer,_J. S. Bach_ (1908 German, 1911 English, Newman translation)**. About 800 pages. Now over a century old; read as a document of how Bach was heard a century ago rather than as a current interpretive guide.

A practical recommendation: start with Gardiner (one book). If you find yourself wanting more, add Wolff (two books). If you find yourself working through the catalogue, add Dürr (cantata reference) and Melamed (Passion reference). Most listeners do not need the more-specialized texts.

### 23.2 First Recordings to Live With

Chapter 21 §21.5 gives the full recommendations by work category. For a starter library of ten recordings covering the principal compositional centers:

  1. **Goldberg Variations** , Glenn Gould 1955 (CBS).
  2. **Cello Suites** , Pablo Casals 1936-39 (EMI).
  3. **St. Matthew Passion** , John Eliot Gardiner 1988 (Archiv).
  4. **B Minor Mass** , Gardiner 1985 (Archiv).
  5. **Brandenburg Concertos** , Trevor Pinnock 1982 (Archiv).
  6. **Violin Sonatas and Partitas** , Henryk Szeryng 1967 (DG).
  7. **Well-Tempered Clavier Book 1** , András Schiff 1984-85 (Decca).
  8. **"Sheep May Safely Graze" and selected cantatas** , Gardiner Bach Cantata Pilgrimage (Soli Deo Gloria 2000, any volume).
  9. **Organ works selections** , Helmut Walcha 1956-71 (Archiv).
  10. **Toccata and Fugue in D minor BWV 565** , included on most Walcha selections; also available in countless other recordings.

These ten map to the seven-work starter list from Chapter 1 §1.4 Habit 6 — Goldberg, Brandenburg, Cello Suite 1 Prelude (within the Casals set), Sheep May Safely Graze (within the Gardiner cantatas), WTC C-major Prelude (within Schiff WTC Book 1), St. Matthew Passion, BWV 565 — plus the B Minor Mass, the Violin Sonatas and Partitas, and the organ works selections, which fill out the catalogue's principal centers.

For a more comprehensive library: add the contrasting Glenn Gould 1981 Goldberg; add the St. John Passion (Herreweghe 1989); add the Christmas Oratorio (Gardiner 1987); add the Art of Fugue (Savall 1986). A library of fourteen recordings covers essentially everything the volume has anchored.

### 23.3 Preview of Subsequent Close Listening Volumes

The _Close Listening_ series will continue with additional single-composer volumes. The planned next volumes:

**Schubert**. Franz Schubert (1797-1828), the great Viennese composer of the early Romantic period. The volume will follow Schubert's compressed 31-year career: the late piano sonatas (D. 894, 958, 959, 960), the string quintet D. 956, the great song cycles (_Die schöne Müllerin_ , _Winterreise_ , _Schwanengesang_), the symphonies (especially the Unfinished and the Great C major), and the late chamber works.

**Brahms**. Johannes Brahms (1833-1897), the late-Romantic composer who built his work on substantial study of the Baroque and Classical traditions. The volume will cover the four symphonies, the two piano concertos, the violin concerto, the late piano works (Op. 116-119), the chamber music (string quartets, piano trios, clarinet works), the choral-orchestral works (German Requiem, Schicksalslied, Alto Rhapsody), and the lieder.

**Tchaikovsky**. Pyotr Ilyich Tchaikovsky (1840-1893). The six symphonies (especially 4-6), the piano concertos and violin concerto, the ballets (Swan Lake, The Nutcracker, The Sleeping Beauty), the operas (Eugene Onegin, The Queen of Spades), and the chamber and choral works.

Beyond these three, the series is planned to extend further into single-composer volumes on Mahler, Wagner, Debussy, Stravinsky, Shostakovich, and others.

### 23.4 The Closing Handoff

The reader who has worked through the volume has built substantial listening capability: the four-voice listening (Chapter 2), the cantus-firmus listening (Chapters 6 and 11 onward), the fugue-subject-and-answer listening (Chapters 3, 10, 19), the canon-at-the-interval listening (Chapters 17, 18, 19), the polyphonic-illusion listening (Chapter 9), the variation-over-fixed-bass listening (Chapters 6, 9, 14, 17), the chromatic-harmony recognition (Chapters 14, 19), and the stile antico recognition (Chapter 14). The skills together let you engage with any Bach work and follow its compositional argument with informed attention.

The six listening habits Chapter 1 asked you to reconsider have each been addressed across the volume. Bach is not brain-music; the contrapuntal voice you have heard across twenty-three anchor chapters is the vehicle for emotion, not a substitute for it. The cantatas are not all alike; you have heard the chorale cantata, the dialogue aria, the solo cantata, and the Passion-cantata in their specific forms. Bach was not always the universal canonical figure; Chapter 22 traced the fifty-year obscurity and the century-long construction of the modern reception. A Lutheran framing is not required to engage deeply; the music functions across religious and secular contexts. Fugue is now accessible to you. As for where to start: you started in Chapter 1, and the catalogue is yours from here.

The volume has covered about fifty anchor works from a surviving catalogue of roughly 1,100 BWV-numbered works — perhaps 20% of what is there. The unfamiliar works are now approachable. You can listen to a Bach cantata you have not heard before and follow its structure; you can listen to a Bach keyboard fugue and identify the subject's voice-entries; you can listen to a chorale prelude and identify the chorale tune as cantus firmus; you can listen to a variation-set and track the fixed bass underneath.

The reception is alive and continuing. New recordings come out every year; new scholarship is published; new performance traditions develop. Your engagement with Bach is part of this continuing reception; what you bring to your listening — your knowledge of the catalogue, your interpretive sensibilities, your specific cultural-personal context — becomes part of the broader picture of how Bach is heard in the contemporary world.

The volume has been your companion through the introduction. From here, the catalogue is yours.

* * *

_This is the closing chapter of Close Listening: Bach. The next volume in the series, Close Listening: Schubert, will follow._
